s much to make plain the high esteem that I have

A          for your respectable person as to thank you in the

name of the Americans, I dedicate to you the

present work: which, though not worthy of your singular merit, is nevertheless the thing that I hold dearest.

It is a source of regret to me to have once been thought opposed, merely because of some difference

A2                    of [p. 4] of opinion, to so celebrated an author, who on so many counts has won the esteem of all men of letters. The Americans, moreover, are exceedingly indebted to you, having found in you a defender no less illustrious for your birth than distinguished for your important offices, and above all most eminent for your brilliant writings: (*) one who, amid the thorniest affairs of State, has known how to find time to study the history of America with care, and has had the courage to defend those despised nations against so many renowned Europeans declared to be their enemies and persecutors.

I hope that this work of mine, composed likewise to dispel the errors published in Europe against America, will be welcome to you as a token of my respect and of the gratitude of the Americans toward you.

Dissertations

(*) The works published by Count Carli are: 1. Della spedizione degli Argonauti. 2. Della Teogonia d’Esiodo. 3. Delle monete, e della istituzione delle zecche in Italia. 4. L’uomo libero. 5. Le lettere americane, of which two volumes have been published, and the third will not be long in being published. 6. A dissertation published as early as 1745 against the common prejudices concerning witchcraft, household ghosts, charms, etc. All works full of exquisite erudition.

[p. 5]

DISSERTATIONS O N T H E L A N D, O N T H E A N I M A L S , A N D ON THE INHABITANTS OF MEXICO

In which the Ancient History of that country is in part confirmed, many points of Natural History are illustrated, and a great many errors put

about concerning America by some celebrated modern Authors are

refuted.

## TO THE READER

hese Dissertations that we now bring to light are

T          necessary, and not merely useful, to illustrate the an‐

cient History of Mexico and to confirm the truth of

many things contained in it. The first dissertation is necessary to supply the lack of information concerning the first peopling of that new World. The second, though tedious, is not to be omitted, so that the foundations of our Chronology may be known, and it will be useful to whoever in the future shall wish to write the history of Mexico. All the others likewise are necessary to turn aside incautious readers from those deceptions in which they will have been entangled by that great throng of modern authors who, without sufficient knowledge of it, have set about writing of the land, the animals, and the men of America.

For how many, in reading, for example, the work of the Researcher (*), will not fill their heads with a thousand unseemly ideas, contrary to the truth of my History? He is a philosopher

of

(a) M. de Pauw, in the work entitled Recherches philosophiques sur les Américains. [p. 6] of the fashionable sort, and erudite, especially in certain matters in which it would be better for him to be ignorant, or at least not to speak of them. He seasons his discourses with buffoonery and with slander, ridiculing whatever is respectable in the Church of God, and biting at whoever comes before him in his researches, without any regard for truth or for innocence. He decides boldly and in a magisterial tone, cites the writers on America every three words, and protests that his work is the fruit of ten years of labor. All this makes the author highly commendable in the eyes of many readers of our philosophical age. His slander, the contempt with which he speaks of the most venerable Fathers of the Church, the scorn that he pours on the Roman Pontiffs, on sovereigns, and on the Religious Orders, and the little account that he shows he makes of the sacred books, instead of diminishing his authority, may increase it in an age in which more errors have been published than in all past ages, in which people write with freedom and lie with shamelessness: he is not esteemed who is not a philosopher, nor is he reputed a philosopher who does not mock at Religion and adopt the language of impiety.

The argument of M. de Pauw’s work is to persuade the World that in America Nature has altogether degenerated in the elements, in the plants, in the animals, and in men. The land, encumbered with high mountains and cliffs, and in the plains flooded with dead and spoiled waters, or covered with vast forests so thick that the rays of the sun cannot penetrate them, is, he says, generally very barren, and more abundant in poisonous plants than all the rest of the World. The air is unwholesome, and much colder than that of the old continent. The climate is contrary to the generation of animals. All the animals proper to those countries were smaller, more deformed, weaker, more cowardly, and more dull-witted than those of the Old World; and those that were transported there from elsewhere soon degenerated, just as did all the plants of Europe that were transplanted there. Men scarcely differed from the beasts except in figure: but even in this many traces of their degeneration are to be seen:

the [p. 7] the olive color, the head too hard and armed with thick hair, and the whole body entirely devoid of hair. They are ugly and weak, and subject to many strange diseases caused by the unwholesome climate. But however such their bodies may be, their souls are even more imperfect. They are so lacking in memory that today they do not remember what they did yesterday. They do not know how to reflect, or to order their ideas, nor are they capable of improving them, nor even of thinking, because nothing but thick and viscous humors circulate in their brains. Their will is insensible to the stimuli of love and of every other passion. Their sloth keeps them immersed in savage life. Their cowardice was made plain in the conquest. Their moral vices corresponded to these physical defects. Drunkenness, lying, and pederasty were common in the islands, in Mexico, in Peru, and throughout the new continent. They lived without laws. The few arts known to them were too crude. Agriculture was among them wholly abandoned, their architecture very wretched, and their instruments more imperfect still. In the whole new World there were no more than two cities, Cuzco in South America and Mexico in North America, and these two were nothing but two miserable hamlets, etc.

This is a slight sketch of the monstrous portrait that M. de Pauw draws of America. I do not set it out in full, and I leave aside also what other authors, ill informed or prejudiced like him, have made of it, because I have no patience to copy so many absurdities. Nor do I claim to make an apology for America and the Americans, because that would require a very voluminous work. To write an error or a lie two lines suffice, and to refute it two pages are sometimes not enough, nor even two sheets: what then would be needed to refute so many hundreds of errors? I, however, wish to refute only those that are opposed to the truth of my History. I have chosen the work of M. de P., because in it, as in a cesspool or sewer, all the filth has been gathered, that is, the errors of all the others. If at times my expressions should seem

A2                      a [p. 8] a little too strong, it was because I judge it not fitting to use gentleness toward a man who insults the whole New World and the most respectable persons of the Old World.

But although the work of M. de P. is the principal target at which I direct my blows, I shall nonetheless have to deal with other authors, and among them with M. de Buffon. I have a great esteem for this celebrated author, and I reckon him the most diligent, the most acute, and the most eloquent naturalist of our century; indeed I believe that there has not been until now in the World another who has made the animals better known than he; but since the subject of his work is so vast, it is no wonder that he sometimes erred, or forgot what he had already written, especially with regard to America, where Nature is so varied: so that neither such errors nor the reasons that we shall adduce against them will be able in any way to prejudice the great reputation that he enjoys among all the men of letters of the World.

In comparing as I do the one continent with the other, I do not claim to make America appear superior to the Old World, but only to show the consequences that can naturally be deduced from the principles of those authors whom I oppose. Such parallels are too odious, and to boast in a partisan way of one’s own country above all others seems more fitting for quarreling children than for learned men in dispute.

In the citations of the History of Quadrupeds of Count de Buffon I have made use of the edition made at Paris in the royal press, in thirty-one small volumes in 12mo, and finished in the year 1768. In those of the Recherches of M. de Pauw I have made use of the London edition of 1771, in three volumes, with the refutation of it by Dom Pernety and the reply of M. de P.

Dissertation

[p. 9]

## D I S S E RTAT I O N I 

## ON THE PEOPLING OF AMERICA, AND

PA RT I C U L A R LY O N T H AT O F M E X I C O.

S   carcely will one find in History a problem more difficult to solve

than that of the peopling of America, nor one on which there is a greater variety of opinions. It may be said that they are as many as were those of the ancient philosophers concerning the Supreme Good. Now I do not intend to examine them all, because that would be a fruitless labor; nor do I claim to establish a new system, since there are no foundations on which to rest it. I wish only to set forth my conjectures and submit them to the judgment of learned men, because it seems to me that they will not be altogether useless; but in order to treat the subject with the clarity and precision that are fitting, I shall divide the general question into several articles, and declare my views in a number of conclusions.

§ I.

At What Time Did the Peopling of America Begin?

Vetancurt (*) and other authors were persuaded that the New World began to be peopled before the Flood. This could certainly have happened, because the space of 1656 years that elapsed from the creation of the first men to the Flood, according to the chronology of the Hebrew text of Genesis and of our Vulgate, and much more that of 2242, or else of 2262 years, according to the reckoning of the Septuagint, was without doubt sufficient to people the whole world, as some have already demonstrated: at least

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                     B        after

(*) In the Teatro mexicano, pt. 2, treatise 1, ch. 1. [p. 10] after ten or twelve centuries some families of those that went spreading toward the more Eastern parts of Asia could have passed over to that part of the world which today we call America, whether it was, as I believe, joined to the other, or separated from it by a small strait of sea. But how is one to prove that America was in fact peopled before the Flood, as those authors would have it? Because in America, they say, there were once Giants, and the epoch of the Giants was antediluvian. (a) Because God, others will say, created the earth only so that it should be inhabited, (b) and it is not likely that, having created America to that end, he would have wished to leave it so long without inhabitants, especially since he had commanded the first men to multiply and fill the earth. (c) But even if we grant to those authors that the sacred text in which mention is made of the Giants must be understood in the common sense, that is, of men of extraordinary height and bulk, and even if we do not doubt that such men existed in the New World, as we have said elsewhere, in spite of Messrs. Sloane, (d) de P., and others, who believe nothing but what they are accustomed to see, this would nevertheless do nothing to confirm that

opinion;

(a) Now giants were upon the earth in those days. Gen. 6.

(b) God himself that formed the earth, and made it … he created it not in vain: he formed it to be inhabited. Isa. 45.

(c) Increase and multiply, and fill the earth. Gen. 1.

(d) The paper of Hans Sloane, an Englishman, in which he strives to prove that the great bones found in America were those of elephants, etc., and not of giants, may be read in the Mémoires of the Royal Academy of Sciences of Paris for the year 1727. Besides what we said against such an opinion in bk. I of the History, we add here the testimony of Dr. Hernández, an eyewitness, sincere and well informed: “A great many bones of giants of no common size,” he says, “have been found in these days, both among the Texcocans and among the people of Toluca.” “These things,” he adds, “are too well known for anyone to be able to deny them credence; and yet I am not unaware that many judge that many things cannot happen until they have happened. So true and indubitable is what our Pliny said: that the power and majesty of nature lack credence at every moment.” Treatise 1, On the Quadrupeds of New Spain, ch. 32. If in the excavations made in America only detached and scattered bones had been found, it might reasonably be believed that they had belonged to some large beasts; but since human skulls and entire skeletons have been found, there is no longer any room for Sloane’s conjectures. See what Acosta relates in bk. 7, ch. 3 of his History [p. 11] opinion; for the sacred books themselves inform us of some Giants after the Flood, such as Og king of Bashan, (e) and those five Gethites of whom mention is made in the books of Kings. From these Giants, mentioned in Holy Scripture by reason of their relation to the history of the Hebrews, we may conjecture that there were many others, both in Palestine and in other countries, of whom the knowledge did not matter to the Sacred historians. The text of Isaiah proves nothing in favor of that opinion; for although God created the earth in order that it should be inhabited, no one can guess the time fixed by him for the execution of his divine counsels.

The traveler Gemelli says, citing certain ancient paintings of the Mexicans, (f) that the city of Mexico was founded in the year II Calli, corresponding, he says, to the year 1325 of the creation of the World, that is, more than three hundred years before the Flood; but this enormous absurdity was not an error of his mind, but a slip of his pen, as is clearly seen in reading the whole context of his narrative: hence the slanderous Researcher wrongly reproaches him with it, and charges the same error to the most distinguished Sigüenza also, whereas we are sure that this learned Mexican was of a very different opinion. It is indeed true that the city of Mexico was founded in the year II Calli, and that this year was 1325; not, however, of the creation of the World, but of the common era of Christianity. The aforesaid traveler, instead of writing this, wrote that other.

Besides, it is useless to inquire whether America was peopled

B2                                        before

concerning the gigantic skeleton dug up in 1586 at Jesús del Monte, a villa of the Jesuits of Mexico near that capital, when he was living there. See also what Zárate, a learned and distinguished writer of the History of Peru, says in bk. 1, ch. 5, concerning the human bones and skulls dug up in his time at Puerto Viejo, a district of the province of Guayaquil. See what the most truthful Bernal Díaz relates of the bones presented by the Tlaxcalans to Cortés, etc. etc.

(e) Torrubia, in his Apparato alla storia naturale di Spagna, fell no fewer than three times into the gross error of making Og antediluvian, and states expressly that he was drowned in the Flood.

(f) Giro del mondo, vol. 6. [p. 12] before the Flood: for, on the one hand, since this cannot be guessed, and on the other since it is most certain that in the Flood all men perished, it is always necessary, after that general inundation, to seek new settlers for America. I know well that some authors confine the Flood within the bounds of a part of Asia; but I know also that such an opinion agrees ill with the truth of the sacred books, (g) with the tradition of the Americans themselves, (h) or with physical observations.

Dr.

(g) All the high mountains under the whole heaven were covered. The water was fifteen cubits higher than the mountains which it had covered. Gen. 7. It seems that God suggested these words to the sacred writer in order to refute the cavils of the Unbelievers; for it is not easy to find other expressions more suitable to signify the universality of the Flood. But even if that sacred text were to be understood only of the mountains of Palestine and of other countries not too far from it, as some claim, I in truth cannot understand how the water, given the laws of Nature concerning the equilibrium of liquids, could rise fifteen cubits above the high mountains of those countries without flooding all of Asia, Africa, Europe, and America too. If, then, the Flood was not universal, why command the construction of the ark, when the family of Noah could have withdrawn from the inundation at its ease by betaking itself to other countries where that calamity was not going to occur? Why have some individuals of every species of quadrupeds, birds, and reptiles shut up in the ark in order to preserve the species on the surface of the earth? That seed may be saved upon the face of the whole earth. Gen. 7. Since the species of animals would remain in very many other countries that were not to be flooded, such a precaution would have been superfluous and ridiculous, especially with regard to birds. For these and other such reasons we must conclude that those who, while holding the authority of the sacred books to be divine, nevertheless deny the universality of the Flood, ought out of charity to be taken to some hospital.

(h) Since God wished to make his justice respected by the posterity of Noah, and to confound the incredulity of Mortals, he ordained that besides the testimony of the sacred scriptures, and the marine bodies left in great abundance on the mountains as eternal monuments of the Flood, there should also be preserved the memory of that fearful and general punishment among the American nations. These, in fact, without having any knowledge of the holy books, or any commerce with the nations of the old continent, nevertheless had knowledge of the Flood, as is attested by Gómara, Acosta, Herrera, García, Martínez, Torquemada, Sigüenza, Ixtlilxochitl, and all those authors who made diligent researches on this matter. The Toltecs, the Acolhuas, the Tarascans or Michoacans, the Mexicans, the Mixtecs, the Tlaxcalans, the Chiapanecs, and other nations preserved the tradition of the Flood, and had it represented in their paintings. All believed that it had been universal, and that all men had been drowned, except one man and one woman, or else one family. This is a fact of which one cannot [p. 13] Dr. Sigüenza believed the peopling of America to have begun not long after the dispersion of the peoples. As we do not have the manuscripts of this most distinguished Mexican, so we are ignorant of the foundations of his opinion, which is, besides, quite in agreement with the tradition of the Chiapanecs, of which we shall speak further on. Other authors, on the contrary, believe that peopling too recent, because the writers of the History of the Mexicans and of the Peruvians found among these nations no memory of their particular events that went beyond eight centuries. But these authors confuse the peopling of Mexico proper, made by the Chichimecs and by the Aztecs, with that which their Ancestors had made many centuries earlier in the northern countries of America, and they do not know how to distinguish the Mexicans from the other nations that occupied that country before them. Who knows, for example, when the Otomis, the Olmecs, the Cuitlatecs, and the Michoacans entered the country of Anahuac? It is no wonder that some writers on Mexico could not find a memory more ancient than eight centuries; for besides the loss of the greater part of the historical monuments of those nations, of which we have made mention so many times, they, not knowing for the most part how to reconcile the Mexican years with ours, went astray to such a degree that they fell into many gross anachronisms; but those who procured a greater abundance of ancient and select paintings, and had greater sagacity in tracing out the chronology of those peoples, such as Sigüenza and Ixtlilxochitl, certainly found much more ancient memories, and made use of them in their most estimable writings.

I do not in truth doubt that the peopling of America is most ancient, and much more so than it seems to European authors. 1. Because the Americans lacked certain arts and inventions, such as, for example, that of making use of wax, or of

oil

doubt without rashness. See what we have said on this matter in the History, and what we shall say further on. Father Acosta says that all the Indians had knowledge of the Flood; but this must be understood of those who lived in society. [p. 14] oil for making light, which, being on the one hand very ancient in Asia and in Europe, are on the other very useful, not to say necessary, and once learned are never abandoned. 2. Because the civilized nations of the new World, and particularly those of Mexico, preserved in their traditions and in their paintings the memory of the Creation of the World, of the Flood, of the building of the tower of Babel, of the confusion of tongues, and of the dispersion of Peoples, as we have said in the History, and as the aforesaid authors attest, though altered with some fables, and had no knowledge of the events that occurred later in Asia, in Africa, and in Europe, although many of them were so great and so notable that they could not easily be effaced from their memory. 3. Because neither among the Americans was there any knowledge of the peoples of the old continent, nor among these has any trace been found of a passage made by those nations to the new World. These reasons make our opinion not certain, indeed, but very probable. (*)

§ II.

## Who Were the Settlers of America?

Those freethinkers who do not recognize in the holy books the stamp of supreme truth, or do not make much account of them, say that the Americans do not derive their origin from Adam and Noah, and believe, or pretend to believe, that as God created Adam in order to be the Father of the Asiatics, so he made

other

(*) A certain modern author asserts that the peopling of America is more ancient than the use of iron, because such use was not found among the Americans. But this opinion is undoubtedly false; for the invention of iron was earlier than the Flood. Of Tubalcain, sixth descendant of Adam, it is said in chapter 4 of Genesis that he was a Smith, and wrought every sort of work in iron and copper: Sella bore Tubalcain, who was a hammerer and artificer in every work of brass and iron. Now who will believe America peopled before Tubalcain? The Americans did not use iron, perhaps because in the northern countries of America, where they first settled, they did not find the mines, and then the memory of that metal was lost among them. [p. 15] other men, after or before him, that they might be Patriarchs of the Africans, the Europeans, and the Americans. This is not opposed, says a modern author, (i) to the truth of the sacred books; for although Moses makes no mention of any other first Patriarch but Adam, this was because he did not set about writing the History of the other peoples, but only that of the Israelites. But besides that this stale system openly contradicts venerable tradition, the holy scriptures, (k) and the common belief of the Catholic Church (which matters little to philosophers of that stamp), it has also been refuted by the tradition of the Americans themselves, who both in their paintings and in their songs called themselves descendants of those men who escaped the general inundation. The Toltecs, the Acolhuas, the Mexicans, the Tlaxcalans, the Tarascans, the Mixtecs, the Chiapanecs, and other peoples were all in agreement on this point: all said that their ancestors had come from elsewhere into those countries; they pointed out the road taken by them, and even preserved the names, whether true or supposed, of those first progenitors of theirs who after the confusion of tongues separated from the rest of men.

Monsignor Francisco Núñez de la Vega, Bishop of Chiapas, says in the Preface to his Constituciones sinodales that in the visitation of his diocese made by himself toward the end of the last century he found many ancient Calendars of the Chiapanecs, and an old manuscript in the language of that country made by the Indians themselves, in which it was said, according to their ancient tradition, that a certain Votan (*) took part in the building of that grand edifice which was made by order of his grandfather to ascend to heaven: that there each people was given its own language

and

(i) The author of a wretched little work entitled Le Philosophe douceur, printed at Berlin in the year 1775.

(k) These three are the sons of Noe: and from these was all mankind spread over the whole earth. Gen. 9. And hath made of one all mankind to dwell upon the whole face of the earth. Acts 17. The common origin of all men derived from Adam and from Noah cannot be expressed in more significant words.

(*) Votan is the principal of those twenty illustrious men who communicated their names to the twenty days of the Chiapanec month. [p. 16] and that the same Votan was charged by God with making the division of the lands of Anahuac. The aforesaid prelate adds that there was in his time in Teopisca, a large place in that Diocese, a family of the surname Votan, who believed themselves descended from that ancient settler. I do not claim to make the peopling of America believed so ancient on the strength of that tradition of the Chiapanecs, but only to show that the Americans considered themselves descended from Noah.

Of the ancient Indians of Cuba several historians of America relate that, when they were questioned by the Spaniards about their origin, they answered that they had learned from their elders that God created the heavens, the earth, and all things: that an old man, having had a presentiment of a great inundation, with which God wished to punish the sins of men, built himself a great canoe and embarked in it with his family and with many animals: that, the inundation having then abated, he sent out the raven, which, because it found carrion to feed on, never returned to the canoe: that shortly after he sent the dove, and this soon returned bearing in its beak a twig of Hoba, a certain fruit-bearing tree of America: that when the old man saw the land dry, he disembarked, and having made wine from wild grapes, became drunk and fell asleep: that then one of his sons mocked at his nakedness, and another son piously covered him: that on awaking he blessed the latter and cursed the former: finally that they drew their origin from the cursed son, and therefore went almost naked: that the Spaniards, since they were well clothed, perhaps descended from the other.

The Mexicans called Noah Coxcox and Teocipactli, and the Michoacans Tezpi. These said “that there was a great Flood, and that Tezpi, in order not to be drowned, embarked in a vessel built in the fashion of an ark with his wife, his children, and various animals, and several seeds of fruits, and that, the water having abated, he sent out that bird which has the name of Aura, which stayed to eat the dead bodies, and then sent other birds, which did not even return

except [p. 17] except that little bird (the hummingbird), so much prized by them for the variety of colors of its feathers, which brought him a branch,” (l) and from this family all believed that they drew their origin. If, then, we have regard either to the sacred books or to the tradition of the Americans, we must seek among the posterity of Noah the settlers of the new World.

But who were they? Which of the sons of Noah was the stock of the American nations? Dr. Sigüenza and the most gifted Mexican, Sor Juana Inés de la Cruz, believed, or at least conjectured, that the Mexicans and other nations of Anahuac were descended from Nephtuim, son of Mesraim and grandson of Ham. The Chevalier Boturini was of the opinion that they descended not only from Nephtuim but also from his five other brothers. The very learned Spaniard Arias Montano was persuaded that the Americans, and the Peruvians in particular, belonged to the posterity of Ophir, fourth descendant of Shem. The reasons of this author are so weak and insufficient that they do not deserve mention. Those of Sigüenza we shall discuss further on.

The other authors, who have not chosen to advance in their researches to so remote an antiquity, have sought in various countries of the World the origin of the Americans. Their opinions are so many and so diverse that it is not easy to number them. One thinks he has found the Progenitors of the Americans in Asia, another in Africa, another in Europe. Among those who imagine they have found them in Europe, it seemed to some that they were the Greeks, to others the Romans, to others the Spaniards, to others the Irish, to others the Courlanders, and to some even the Russians. Among those who deem them to have originated

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                  C                         from

(l) Herrera, Dec. 3, bk. 3, ch. 10. See this author in Dec. 4, bk. 1, ch. 2, concerning what the Indians of Tierra Firme said of their origin. See also the same Herrera, Torquemada, and others concerning the tradition that existed among the Haitians, that is, the inhabitants of the island of Hispaniola. Of the tradition of the Mexicans, the Acolhuas, and the Tlaxcalans we have treated in bk. II of our History. Of that of the Toltecs mention is made by Boturini, as also by Torquemada and others. Of that of the Mixtecs García wrote in his learned treatise on the origin of the Indians. [p. 18] from Africa, some make them descend from the Egyptians, some from the Carthaginians, some from the Numidians. But there is no greater variety of opinions than among those who believe that the peopling of America is to be ascribed to Asia. The Israelites, the Canaanites, the Assyrians, the Phoenicians, the Persians, the Tartars, the East Indians, the Chinese, the Japanese, all have their advocates among the historians and philosophers of these last two centuries. Some, moreover, not content with seeking the aforesaid settlers in the known countries of the World, draw out from beneath the waters of the Ocean, or from imaginary spaces, the famous island of Atlantis, in order to send colonists from it to America. But this is little: for there are authors who, so as not to wrong any people, believe the Americans descended from all the nations of the World. (m)

The cause of so great a variety, and of such an extravagance of opinions, has been the persuasion that, to believe one nation born of another, nothing more was needed than to find some affinity in a few words of their languages, and some resemblance in their rites, customs, and usages. Such too are the foundations of almost all the opinions mentioned, collected already, and illustrated with a great abundance of erudition, by the Dominican García, and by those learned Spaniards who reprinted his work with new additions, in which anyone who wishes may see them; for I should think I was wasting my time in refuting them.

But I cannot refrain from mentioning the opinion of Dr. Sigüenza, adopted also by the most distinguished French Bishop Pierre Daniel Huet, because it seems to me the best founded. Sigüenza, then, was persuaded that the nations that peopled the Mexican empire belonged to the posterity of Nephtuim, and that their progenitors, departing from Egypt not long after the confusion of tongues, set out toward America. The reasons on which he based such a view are found only hinted at in the Biblioteca mexicana. We should like to see them set out with all that force and with all that erudition with which they will undoubtedly have been written by that most learned

Author

(m) This improbable opinion was that of García and of Vetancurt.

[p. 19]

Author; but since we are deprived of his most estimable writings, we shall do no more than hint at them, as Dr. Eguiara did in the aforesaid Biblioteca.

Such reasons are reduced, as far as appears, to the conformity of those American nations with the Egyptians in the practice of pyramidal edifices and of hieroglyphics, in the way of computing time, in dress, and in certain customs; and to this he will perhaps have added the likeness of the Teotl of the Mexicans to the Theuth of the Egyptians, which led Monsignor Huet to the same opinion as Sigüenza, though by a different road. If this thought is proposed as a conjecture, I shall not contradict it; but if it is claimed that it is a truth that can be affirmed, those reasons do not appear to me sufficient.

Sigüenza wished the sons of Nephtuim to have gone out from Egypt toward America not long after the confusion of tongues; hence he ought to make the comparison of the customs of the Americans with those of the first Egyptians, not with those of their descendants, who inhabited Egypt many years afterward, and from whom the Americans are not believed to be descended. Now who will be persuaded that the Egyptians immediately after the dispersion of peoples began to build pyramids and to make use of hieroglyphics, and that from then on they had already ordered their years and months in the form in which they afterward had them? All these things were undoubtedly later than that epoch. Nor was it necessary to have seen the pyramids of Egypt in order for the idea of building such edifices to occur to the Americans: for to that end it sufficed to see the mountains that are upon the earth. Anyone who wishes to build a lofty edifice to immortalize his name will easily think of making it pyramidal; because there is no other edifice that can be raised to such a height at less expense, since the higher it rises the less quantity of materials it requires. Besides, the Mexican edifices were altogether different from those of the Egyptians. The latter were true pyramids; the former were not, but were structures composed for the most part of three, four, or five square or oblong bodies, of which the upper had less breadth than the lower: those of the Egyptians

C2                     were [p. 20] were for the most part hollow; those of the Mexicans solid: these served as bases for their Sanctuaries; those, as sepulchers of kings. The temples of the Mexicans, and of the other nations of Anahuac, were of so rare a kind that I do not know that they have been in use among any nation of the World: so that they must be considered as an original invention of the Toltecs, or of other settlers more ancient than they.

In the way of computing time the Mexicans were more like the Egyptians (I speak of the later Egyptians, not of the first, of whose method nothing is known). The Egyptian year was solar, of 365 days, like that of the Mexicans: both the one and the other contained 360 days in their months, and as the Egyptians added five days to their last month Mesori, so the Mexicans did to their month Izcalli: in which they agreed both with the Egyptians and with the Persians; but for the rest there was a great difference between the one and the other. The Egyptian year consisted of twelve months, and the months of thirty days; the Mexican year (*) was composed of eighteen months, and the months of twenty days. The Egyptians, like very many other nations of the old continent, counted by weeks; the Mexicans by periods of five days in civil matters, and of thirteen days in what concerns Religion.

The Mexicans made use, like the Egyptians, of hieroglyphics; but how many other nations have not likewise made use of them to conceal the mysteries of their Religion? And if the Mexicans learned hieroglyphics from the Egyptians, why did they not also get from them the use of letters? Because letters, it will be said, were invented after their separation; but how is it known that before they separated hieroglyphics had already been discovered?

The dress of the first Egyptians will probably have been the same as that of the other sons and descendants of Noah: at least there is no reason to make us believe the opposite. Concerning the political customs, moreover, of those first men we know nothing. The most ancient Egyptians of whom we have certain records were those who

lived

(*) I speak of the religious year of the Mexicans; for of their civil and astronomical year we know nothing. [p. 21] lived in the time of the Patriarch Joseph. Now if we wish to compare their usages, indicated in the sacred books, with those of the Mexicans, instead of their alleged sameness we shall find their diversity. Finally I do not claim to demonstrate the opinion of Sigüenza false, but only to show that it is not a truth of which one can be certain.

The extravagant Researcher says that the Mexicans derive their origin from the southern Apalachites; but he neither alleges nor can allege any reason that makes such a paradox probable or likely: and even were it true, the difficulty would still stand concerning the origin of the Apalachites themselves. It is true that for that bold author there is no difficulty, because at times he gives to understand that the preposterous system of the Frenchman La Peyrère does not displease him.

As for my own opinion, it seems well to set it out in the following conclusions.

I. The Americans descend from various Nations, or from various families dispersed after the confusion of tongues. No one who has any knowledge of the multitude and the extreme diversity of the American languages can doubt this truth. In Mexico I have numbered thirty-five of those that are known so far. In South America there are many more. At the beginning of the last century the Portuguese counted as many as a hundred and fifty on the Marañón. It is true that among some of these languages one perceives such an affinity as at once shows that they are born of one and the same mother, as the Eudeve, the Opata, and the Tarahumara in North America, and the Mocobi, the Toba, and the Abipona in South America; but there are many others much more different from one another than Hebrew and Illyrian. I can affirm without danger of deceiving myself that there will be found, neither among the living nor among the dead languages of Europe, two more different from one another than the Mexican, the Otomi, the Tarascan, the Maya, and the Mixtec, five dominant languages in different provinces of Mexico. So that it would be a great absurdity to say that such American languages were different dialects of one mother language. How is it

possible [p. 22] possible that a nation should alter its primitive language to such a degree, or multiply it into so many dialects so different from one another, that even after many centuries they would not have many words common to all, or at least that there should not be some affinity, or that some trace of their origin should not remain?

Who can ever believe what is read (*) in the History of Father Acosta? Namely, that the Aztecs, or Mexicans, having arrived after their long pilgrimage in the kingdom of Michoacán, wished to settle there, attracted by the pleasantness of the land; but since the whole body of the nation could not remain there, their god Huitzilopochtli consented that some should remain, and even suggested to the others the way of doing it, ordering them that when those who were to remain bathed in the lake of Pátzcuaro, they should steal their clothes and promptly flee to continue their journey: that those who were bathing, seeing themselves deprived of their clothes, and mocked by their companions, conceived such disdain that they resolved not only to remain there but even to adopt a new language, and that hence the Tarascan language had its origin. Still more incredible is the account adopted by Gómara and by several historians; namely, that from an old man called Iztac Mixcoatl and from his wife Ilancueitl were born six sons, all of different language, called Xelhua, Tenoch, Olmecatl, Xicalancatl, Mixtecatl, and Otomitl, who were the progenitors of as many nations that peopled the country of Anahuac. This allegory, by which the Mexicans meant to signify that all those nations drew their origin from a common stock, was changed into a fable by the aforesaid authors, because misunderstood.

II. The Americans do not derive their origin from any people now existing in the old World, or at least there is no reason to affirm it. This conclusion rests on the same reason as the preceding; for if the Americans descended from any of those peoples, their origin could be traced

by

(*) Natural and Moral History of the Indies, bk. 7. Father Acosta does not show that he consents to that fabulous account of the Mexicans; but neither does he contest it. [p. 23] by some trace remaining in their languages, despite the antiquity of their separation; but such a trace has not been able to be found up to now, although many authors have sought it with great zeal, as may be seen in the work of the Dominican García. I have compared at great length the Mexican language, and other American ones, with several others, both living and dead, of the old continent, and I have not been able to find any affinity between the one and the others. The likeness of the Teotl of the Mexicans to the Theos of the Greeks has induced me at times to compare these two languages; but I have always found in them a great diversity. This argument is more effective with regard to the Americans because of their firmness and constancy in retaining their languages. The Mexicans preserve their language among the Spaniards, and the Otomis retain their difficult speech among the Spaniards and the Mexicans, even after two centuries and a half.

If the Americans descend, as I believe, from various families dispersed after the confusion of tongues, and from then on separated from those others that peopled the countries of the old continent, in vain will authors labor to seek in the languages or in the customs of the Asiatic peoples the origin of the settlers of the new World. I do not doubt, in view of what the sacred books tell us, that after the posterity of Noah had been sufficiently multiplied there was an express order of God for the families to separate, and for each to betake itself to people the country assigned to it. Moses in his divine canticle speaks thus to the people of Israel: “Remember the days of old, and consider each of the past generations: ask your Father, and your ancestors, and they will tell you that when the Most High divided the peoples, and separated the sons of Adam, he fixed the bounds of the peoples (of Palestine) according to the number of the sons of Israel:” (n) wherein the Lord is shown in the act of apportioning the families, and of prescribing the limits of the countries that they were to occupy.

Those

(n) Remember the days of old, think upon every generation: ask your father, and he will declare to you, your elders, and they will tell you. When the Most High divided the nations, when he separated the sons of Adam, he appointed the bounds of peoples according to the number of the children of Israel. Deut. 32.

[p. 24]

Those men who undertook the building of the tower of Babel encouraged one another to work at that construction with these words: “Come,” they said, “let us build ourselves a city and a tower whose top may touch the heavens, and let us make our name famous before we are scattered over all the earth.” (o) They knew, then, that they were to be dispersed through all the lands; and God, because by such a building they were opposing his counsels, or at least delaying the execution of his orders concerning the peopling of the earth, confounded their language, and so contrived to separate them and to scatter them from that place through all the lands. (p) Hence it is likely that Noah, a venerable old man, revered by all as a father, who survived the Flood by three hundred and fifty years, assigned to each family its district according to the instruction he had received from God; for otherwise the division could not have been carried out without bloody wars, each one wishing to enjoy his native country without exposing himself to many perils and misfortunes in unknown lands. This opinion of mine becomes still more likely from the tradition of the Chiapanecs concerning the peopling of Anahuac by Votan, of whom we have already spoken. Yet it must not be thought that those very persons who separated from the other families at Babel actually peopled, as the Chiapanecs said, the lands of America; rather it was their descendants, as those first families went on little by little toward that region and multiplied in the course of their journey.

## From

(o) Come, let us make us a city and a tower, the top whereof may reach to heaven; and let us make our name famous before we be scattered abroad into all lands. Gen. 11.

(p) And so he scattered them from that place into all lands. Ibid.

[p. 25]

## § III

From What Quarter, and How, the Settlers and the Animals Passed into America.

This is the other point, and the more difficult one, in the problem of the peopling of America, and in it, as in the other, the opinions of the authors are very diverse. Some ascribe the peopling of the New World to certain Phoenician merchants who, sailing the Ocean, came upon it by chance. Others imagine that those same peoples whom they suppose to have passed from the old continent to the island of Atlantis thence easily made their way to Florida, and from that vast country went spreading throughout all America. Others believe that they passed there from Asia by the Strait of Anian, and still others that they crossed from the northern regions of Europe by some arm or other of the frozen sea.

Father Feijoo, a Spanish Benedictine, offered a few years ago to propose to the literary world a new system. (q) And what is this new system? That America was once joined on its northern side to the old continent, and that by that way men and beasts passed over. But this opinion is as old as Father Acosta, who published it in his History of America 144 years before Father Feijoo; (r) besides which, it is not enough to resolve all the difficulties that attend the passage of the beasts, as we shall see below.

Count de Buffon, in spite of his great intelligence and his prolix exactness, openly contradicts himself on this point. He supposes both continents to have been united by way of Eastern Tartary, and affirms that by that way the first settlers passed into America, together with all those beasts which are found there common to the one World and the other, such as the bison (called Cibolos in Mexico), the wolves, foxes, martens,

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                       D           deer,

(q) Teatro crítico universal, vol. 5, disc. 14.

(r) Natural and Moral History of the Indies, bk. 1, chs. 20 and 21. [p. 26] deer, fallow deer, and other such quadrupeds, to which a cold climate is congenial, but that there could not have been in America lions, tigers, camels, elephants, giraffes, nor any of those seventeen species of monkeys that are found in the old continent; and, to put it in a few words, no quadruped proper to a warm clime could have been common to both continents, because it was not capable of withstanding the cold of the northern countries through which it had to pass from the one World to the other. This he repeats incessantly throughout his Natural History, and with such firmness that for this reason alone he banishes from America the gazelles, the goats, and the rabbits. He reckons as properly American quadrupeds only those that live solely in the warm countries of that New World, among which he counts the thirteen or fourteen species of American monkeys, divided by him into the two classes of Sapayùs and Sagoini: of these, he adds, none was in the old continent, just as there is in the new continent none of the seventeen species of the old. What, then, was the origin of these and other properly American quadrupeds? This doubt, which occurs often in the Natural History of that great philosopher, remains undecided until the next-to-last volume of the history of the quadrupeds, in which, speaking as a good Catholic, he reasons thus: (f) “Since it cannot be doubted that all animals in general were created in the old continent, one must admit the passage from this into the other continent, and suppose at the same time that these animals (the fallow deer, the roe deer, and the skunks), instead of having degenerated, like the others, in the New World, have on the contrary been perfected there, and by the suitability of the climate have surpassed their own nature. … The fact that so many animals have been found in the New World that cannot be referred to any of the Old World shows sufficiently that the origin of these animals proper to the New World must not be ascribed to simple degeneration. However great and effective we may choose to suppose the effects, we shall never be able to convince

ourselves,

(f) Histoire nat., vol. 29. Discours sur la dégénération des animaux. [p. 27] ourselves, with any appearance of reason, that these animals were originally the very same as those of the old continent. It is without doubt more in accord with reason to believe that the two continents were once contiguous or continuous, and that those species that had withdrawn into the regions of the New World, because they found there a sky and a land more congenial to their nature, were shut in there and separated from the others by the irruptions of the seas that divided Africa from America. (t) This cause is natural, etc.” From this discourse of Count de Buffon it follows: 1. that there is no properly American animal, since all went there from the old continent, where they were created; 2. that the argument based on the nature of animals averse to the cold is worth nothing to prove that they could not have passed into the new continent, because those that by their nature could not make the passage through the northern countries could make it by that part where America was once united to Africa, as that author believes; 3. that wherever the Sagoini and the Sapayùs passed into the new World, there elephants, camels, giraffes, lions, tigers, etc., could likewise have gone.

Leaving aside other opinions, which do not deserve to be mentioned, I shall set out my view in a number of conclusions, not in order to establish, as I have protested, a new system, but to supply materials to other and better minds, and to illustrate some points of my History.

I. Men and animals passed into America from the old continent. This truth is founded in the sacred books. Moses himself, who declares Noah the common stock of all

D2                         men

(t) I beg readers to compare what Count de Buffon says here about the former union of Africa and America with what he writes in vol. 18, where he speaks of the lion: “The American lion,” he says, “cannot descend from the lion of the old continent, because, as the latter does not live except between the tropics, and nature having closed to it, as it appears, all the roads to the north, it could not pass from the southern parts of Asia and Africa into America, since these two continents are separated by immense seas: hence it must be said that the American lion is an animal proper and peculiar to the New World.” [p. 28] men after the Flood, says expressly that in that general inundation of the earth all its quadrupeds, all its birds, and all its reptiles perished, except a few individuals who were saved in the ark to restore the species. The repeated expressions that the sacred historian uses to signify universality do not permit us to doubt that all the quadrupeds, reptiles, and birds that are in the world today descend from those few individuals whom God withdrew from the general inundation; otherwise the diligence employed in shutting those animals, and particularly the birds, in the ark to preserve the species would have been superfluous, as we have noted above, and even ridiculous, and a folly not unlike that of the daughters of Lot, who, because they saw the cities of Sodom and Gomorrah burning, persuaded themselves that all men had perished and that they alone had remained to preserve the human species on the earth.

II. The first settlers of America could have passed there in boats by sea, or on foot by land, or over the ice. 1. They could have passed in boats, either carried off by chance by a strong wind, or deliberately, if the strait of sea that separated the one continent from the other was perhaps small. There is no doubt that those settlers could have reached the New World in the same manner in which, many centuries afterward, there was carried to it that sailor, or pilot, to whom, according to many authors, Columbus owed the first information that urged him on to his glorious and memorable discovery. (u) 2. They could have passed on foot by land, supposing, as we shall presently see, the union of both continents. 3. They could also have made that passage on foot over some strait of frozen sea. No one is unaware how great and how lasting are the ice-fields of the northern seas. It would not be surprising, then, that a strait

of

(u) Some authors affirm that the sailor who gave Columbus word of the new countries of the West was from Andalusia; some say he was a Biscayan; some make him a Portuguese. Others, again, deny the fact. Whatever the case, it is certain that in history we have not a few examples of ships swept away by the winds and carried many degrees from the course they were keeping. It is enough to read the examples given by Pliny in bk. 2, ch. 67, and in bk. 6, ch. 22, of his Natural History. [p. 29] of sea between the two continents should freeze to such a degree as to hold them united for some months, and men should pass over it, either seeking new countries or pursuing some wild beast. Here we are not discussing what in fact happened, but only what could have happened.

III. The progenitors of the nations that peopled the country of Anahuac (of which alone we are now speaking) passed from the northern countries of Europe into the northern countries of America, or rather from the more eastern countries of Asia into the more western countries of America. This conclusion is founded on the constant and general tradition of all those nations, who unanimously said that their progenitors were people who had come to Anahuac from the countries lying to the north and to the northwest. Such a tradition was confirmed by the remains of some very ancient edifices built by those nations on their pilgrimage, of which we made mention in bk. 2 of the History, and by the common belief of the northern peoples. Besides what we have said elsewhere, we have in Torquemada and Vetancurt a very clear document. On a journey that the Spaniards made in the year 1606 from New Mexico to the river which they called the Tizón, six hundred miles from that province toward the northwest, they found some large edifices, and came upon some Indians who spoke the Mexican language, from whom they learned that some days’ journey from that river toward the north lay the kingdom of Tollan, and many very large settlements, from which had come those who peopled the Mexican empire, and that those same settlers had built those and other edifices. In fact all the peoples of Anahuac affirmed that toward the northwest and toward the north lay the kingdoms and provinces of Tollan, Teoacolhuacan, Amaquemecan, Aztlan, Tehuajo, Copalla, etc. (all Mexican names), whose discovery, if in future the settlement of the Spaniards advances in those parts, will bring great light to the ancient History of Mexico. The Chevalier Boturini attests that in the ancient paintings of the Toltecs the pilgrimage of their ancestors was represented through Asia and through the northern countries

of [p. 30] of America as far as their settling in the kingdom of Tollan, and he even offers to point out in his General History the route they took on their journey; but as he had not the leisure to compose the History he was planning, we cannot say more on this subject.

Now, since those countries in which the progenitors of those nations settled long ago lie toward that quarter where the most western coast of America comes so near to the most eastern coast of Asia, it is probable that by that same quarter they passed from the one continent to the other, either in boats, if there was then that strait of sea which there is today, as appears from the recent discoveries of the Russians, or on foot, if it was all one continent, as we shall see below. The traces that those nations went on leaving lead us to that strait, which is without doubt the very one that travelers discovered in the sixteenth century and called the Strait of Anian. (x)

As for the other nations of America, since no tradition is found among them concerning the quarter by which they passed to the New World, we can affirm nothing. It may be that all of them passed where the progenitors of the Mexicans went, and it may also be that they passed by another quarter very different. I conjecture that the progenitors of the nations that peopled South America made their way there by that quarter by which, as we shall shortly say, the beasts proper to warm countries passed, and that the progenitors of those nations that inhabited all those countries that lie from Florida to the northernmost part of America passed there from the north of Europe. The diversity of character that is seen in the three aforesaid classes of Americans, and the situation of the countries they occupied, make me suspect that they had different origins, and that their progenitors made their way there by different quarters; but this is a mere suspicion and a conjecture.

Some

(x) In the geographical maps of America published in the last century the Strait of Anian is commonly seen described, though with no little variety. For some years past it began to be omitted, because it was thought fabulous; but after the discoveries of the Russians some geographers have begun to restore it.

[p. 31]

Some assign another quarter for the passage of the first settlers, and that is the island of Atlantis, whose existence, impugned by Father Acosta, was upheld by Dr. Sigüenza, as appears from Gemelli’s account, and has most recently been advanced with great abundance of erudition by the distinguished author of the Lettere americane. If in the account that Plato gives of that island in the Timaeus there were not a number of fables intermingled, the authority of so grave a philosopher might perhaps induce us to assent. Leaving this dispute, then, to others, let us come to the most difficult point of our problem.

IV. The quadrupeds and reptiles of the New World passed there by land. This truth will be made plain by demonstrating the improbability or unlikelihood of the other opinions. The great Doctor of the Church, Augustine, was of the opinion that the wild beasts and noxious animals that are on the islands could have been transported there by Angels; just as it may be believed that it was by the same Angels that the assembling of the animals was done at the place where Noah’s ark had been built; for it was not possible for men to gather together the wild beasts straying through the woods, and the birds that flew scattered in such diverse regions, if they themselves had not been transported there by Angels, or else induced by a certain inclination instilled in them by the Creator. But this solution, although it cuts off altogether every difficulty in the passage of the wild beasts to the New World, would not be welcome in the age in which we live, nor ought we to avail ourselves of it unless we had recognized all the other expedients as useless for saving the truth of the sacred books.

The same holy Doctor suggests (y) three other solutions

to

(y) “They may indeed be believed to have crossed to islands by swimming, but only to the nearest ones. … But if men captured them and brought them along with them, and in that way established those kinds where they dwelt, out of zeal for hunting, it is not incredible; although it is not to be denied that by command of God, or by his permission, they might also have been transported by the work of Angels. But if they sprang from the earth in accordance with the first origin, when God said, Let the earth bring forth the living creature, it appears more clearly that it was not so much for the sake of restoring the animals as for the sake of prefiguring the various nations, on account of the sacrament of the Church, that all the kinds were in the ark, if on islands to which they could not cross the earth produced many animals.” Augustine, bk. 16, De civitate Dei, ch. 7. [p. 32] to resolve that difficulty. The wild beasts, he says, could have passed to the islands by swimming; they could have been transported there by men, to have game; and they could also have been formed there out of the earth, as they were made in the beginning of the World. But none of these solutions suffices to resolve the difficulties that lie in the passage of the wild beasts to the New World; for as to the first, it is certain that however narrow one may wish the stretch of sea that separated the two continents to have been, it is not credible that so many animals, which are not destined to lead their life in the water nor are accustomed to swimming, would have dared to cross it. It is quite true that some could have passed by swimming, as the wild boars of Corsica pass into France; but who would believe as much of the American monkeys, which are wholly unable to swim, or of the Perico ligero, that is, the sloth, which is so slow and moves with such difficulty? Besides, what could have induced so many beasts to leave the land and abandon themselves to the perils of the sea?

It is no less incredible that those animals were carried by men on ships, especially if their arrival on the coasts of America is supposed to have been accidental. Even in the case of undertaking that voyage deliberately, they might have taken with them some squirrels and some curious little monkeys for their amusement, some rabbits, hares, and techichi, so that after multiplying they might serve for their sustenance, and some deer, fallow deer, martens, and even tigers to clothe themselves in their skins; but to what end carry wolves, foxes, beech martens, cojoti, tlalcojoti, pumas, or American lions, and other such beasts, which instead of bringing them any usefulness are so harmful to them? For the hunt? But could they not have such recreation without any harm, and with great advantage, in deer, fallow deer, wild goats, rabbits, hares, and other less ferocious animals? And if the first settlers of America are supposed to have been so foolish as to wish to carry to their new countries those so harmful beasts in order to hunt them, at least they will not have been so mad as to resolve to carry so many species of serpents in order to have the pleasure of killing them.

As

[p. 33]

As regards the third solution, namely that God created the animals in America as he had created them in Asia, that would doubtless cut off the difficulty altogether, were it not opposed to the sacred books. If God was to create those species after the Flood, why did he give Noah the order to keep in the ark a certain number of individuals of all the quadrupeds, of all the reptiles, and of all the birds, so that the species might not perish? That seed may be saved upon the face of the whole earth. If this text is to be understood only of the animals of the old continent, and not of those of the new, likewise that other text, in which it is said that from the three sons of Noah all mankind was propagated, from these was all mankind spread over the whole earth, might be understood only of the settlers of Asia, Africa, and Europe, and not of those of America; whence we should have to take refuge in the absurd system of Isaac de la Peyrère, or in another of the same sort. I, at least, cannot distinguish that super faciem universae terrae of the first text from that super universam terram of the second.

There remains still another expedient for the passage of the beasts, and it is the very one that we noted above in speaking of men. Someone might imagine that the beasts passed over some strait of frozen sea; but who can persuade himself that several species of very voracious beasts would have betaken themselves to those regions, bare of everything that could serve for their sustenance, and that others, whose nature is exceedingly averse to the cold, would have dared to advance in the rigor of winter to those frozen places?

Now, since it is not probable that the beasts of the New World passed there by swimming, or over the frozen sea, nor that they were transported there by men or by Angels, nor that they were created there anew by God, we must believe that the quadrupeds, as well as the reptiles, that were found in America passed there by land, and consequently that both continents were then united. This has been the opinion of Acosta, of Grotius, of Buffon, and of other great men. I

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.           E                       am [p. 34] am far from adopting the system of Count de Buffon in its whole extent. This philosopher, for all his eloquence and erudition, will never persuade me that all that is now land was once the bed of the sea. I shall never be able to believe that the old continent (and I say the same of the new) was ever subject to a general inundation distinct from Noah’s Flood and more lasting than it. All the arguments of Count de Buffon do not suffice to persuade us of such an opinion, which seems little consonant with the sacred books, in whose history it is shown that at least a part of Asia was peopled from the creation of the first men to the universal Flood, and from the time the earth dried until some years after the death of our divine Redeemer. In the series of the forty centuries and more comprised in the narrative of sacred scripture there is found no gap in which that alleged inundation can be accommodated. As for the new continent, there is no reason capable of inducing us to believe that any general inundation took place there other than that of Noah, as we shall demonstrate in our third dissertation.

But there is no doubt that our planet has been subject to very great vicissitudes since the Flood. The histories, ancient and modern, confirm the truth that Ovid sang in the name of the philosopher Pythagoras:

I have seen what was once the most solid land become sea;

I have seen lands made out of the sea. (*)

Now some lands are plowed where once one sailed, and on the contrary today ships furrow where the plow once furrowed. Earthquakes have swallowed up many lands, and others have been raised by subterranean fires. (y) Rivers have formed new lands with their mud; the sea, withdrawing from some coasts, has lengthened the land on that side, and, advancing elsewhere with its irruptions, has shortened it: it has separated some

lands

(*) Metamorphoses, bk. XV.

(y) “Lands are born also in another way, and suddenly emerge in some sea, as though nature were making things even with herself, and what a chasm has swallowed up in one place she gives back in another.” Pliny, Natural History, bk. 2, ch. 86. [p. 35] lands that were united, and has formed new straits and gulfs. We also have examples of all these revolutions in past centuries. Sicily was once joined to the continent of Naples, as Euboea (today Negroponte) to Boeotia. Diodorus, Strabo, and other ancient authors say the same of Spain and Africa, and affirm that by a violent irruption made by the Ocean into the land that lay between the mountains Abila and Calpe, that communication was broken, and the Mediterranean Sea was formed. Among the Ceylonese there is a tradition that such an irruption of the sea separated their island from the peninsula of India. The Malabars believe the same with regard to the Maldive islands, and the Malays with regard to that of Sumatra. It is certain, says Count de Buffon, that in Ceylon the land has lost thirty or forty leagues of terrain, which the sea has taken from it, and, on the contrary, at Tongres, a place in the Low Countries, the sea has yielded more than thirty leagues to the land. The northern part of Egypt owes its existence to the inundations of the Nile. (A) The earth that this river has brought from the inland countries of Africa and deposited in its inundations has formed a soil more than twenty-five braccia deep. Likewise, adds the aforesaid author, the province of the Yellow River in China and that of Louisiana were formed only of the mud of rivers. Pliny, Seneca, Diodorus, and Strabo report innumerable examples of such revolutions, (B) which I pass over, lest this dissertation

## E2                        seem

(A) Pharos, or Farion, an island of Egypt, which, as Homer indicates in the Odyssey, was a day and a night’s sail distant from the northern land of Egypt, was so near to it in the time of the celebrated Cleopatra that it lay barely seven stadia off; for such was the length of the bridge that that queen had the Rhodians build to give communication between that island and the continent. Herodotus, Aristotle, Seneca, Pliny, and other ancient authors make mention of this notable increase of the land of Egypt.

(B) See in particular Pliny in bk. 2 of the Natural History, and Seneca in bk. 6 of the Natural Questions. Pliny reckons among the new islands that appeared in the sea, formed by the upheaval of the land, those of Rhodes, Delos, Anaphe, Nea, Alone, Hiera, Thera, Therasia, and, in his own times, that of Thia. Among the islands formed by earthquakes he puts Sicily, separated from the continent of Italy by an interval of twelve miles, Cyprus detached from [p. 36] seem too prolix and burdened, as also many modern revolutions that are read in the Theory of the Earth of Count de Buffon and in other authors. In our America, all those who have observed with philosophic eyes the peninsula of Yucatán do not doubt that its terrain was once the bed of the sea; and conversely, in the Bahama channel several indications are seen that the island of Cuba was once joined to the continent of Florida. In the strait that separates America from Asia are found very many islands, which will probably be those mountains that were in that stretch of land which we believe to have been swallowed up by some earthquakes: which is rendered more likely by that multitude of volcanoes that we know to be in the peninsula of Kamchatka. We also conjecture that the sinking of that land, and the separation of the two continents, were caused by those great and extraordinary earthquakes of which the histories of the Americans make mention, and of which they made an epoch almost as memorable as that of the Flood. The histories of the Toltecs fix such earthquakes in the year 1 Tecpatl; but as we do not know of what century that was, neither can we guess the time at which that great calamity came. If a great earthquake were to swallow up the isthmus of Suez, and there were then as great a scarcity of historians as there was in the first centuries after the Flood, after three or four hundred years it would be doubted whether Asia had once been joined on that side to Africa, and some would boldly deny it.

V. The quadrupeds and reptiles of America passed from the one continent to the other by different quarters. Among the American beasts there are some whose nature is extremely averse to the cold, such as the monkeys, the tapirs, the crocodiles, etc. There are others, again, whose natural inclination leads them to icy countries, such as the martens, the reindeer, and the gluttons. Now neither could these

go Syria, Euboea from Boeotia, Atalanta and Macris from Euboea, Besbicus from Bithynia, and Leucosia from the promontory of the Sirens. Among the lands submerged in the sea he mentions the island of Ceos, in which thirty miles of land were swallowed up with an incredible slaughter of the inhabitants, etc. [p. 37] go to America through the torrid zone, nor those through the cold zones; for great violence would have to be done to their inclination, and they would without doubt die on the way. The monkeys that are in New Spain passed there without doubt from South America. (C) The center of their population is the countries lying under the equator, and between it and the fourteenth or fifteenth degree of latitude: in proportion to the distance from the equator their number goes for the most part diminishing, and beyond the tropics there are none, save in some region which for certain particular circumstances is as hot as the equatorial countries. Who, then, can persuade himself that such beasts made their way to the New World through the rigorous climate of the north? Someone might say that it is not unlikely that they were transported by men, being so much prized for their antics and for their ridiculous imitation of Man; but besides that the argument we make on the monkeys can be made on other quadrupeds, which have no value to make them sought after, and indeed many bad qualities to make them shunned, it is not credible that men would have wished to take with them individuals of so many species of monkeys as there are in America, and much less of some which, instead of being graceful, are on the contrary of an ugly aspect and a fierce disposition, like those they call Zambos; and in case men had resolved to bring to the New World two individuals at least of every species, these certainly could not arrive there either by the seas or by the countries of the north, however much their handlers might strive to defend them from the cold. They would therefore have had to transport them from the warm countries of the old continent to the equally warm countries of the new, over a sea lying under a climate not unlike that of the country native to those quadrupeds

namely,

(C) Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl, an Indian well instructed in the antiquities of his nation, says in his Universal History of New Spain that there were no monkeys in the land of Anahuac; that the first that were seen there came from the south after the epoch of the great winds. The Tlaxcalans, changing this event into a fable, said that the world once ended with a wind, and that those few men who survived were transformed into monkeys. [p. 38] namely, either from the southern countries of Asia to the southern ones of America by the Indian and Pacific seas, or from the western countries of Africa to the eastern ones of America by the Atlantic Ocean. If, then, men transported those beasts from the one World to the other, this was done by one of those seas. But was this voyage accidental, or made on purpose? If accidental, how and why did they take with them so many animals? If it was made on purpose, and with a deliberate resolve to pass from the one World to the other, who gave them information of it? Who showed them the situation of those countries? Who pointed out to them the way? How did they dare to cross so great a sea without a compass? In what ships? If these arrived there happily, why did no memory of their construction remain among the Americans?

Besides, crocodiles, beasts which require a warm or temperate climate and live alternately on land and in fresh water, are common in the torrid zone of the New World. Now by what way did these beasts pass? Certainly not by the north, because their nature is exceedingly averse to the cold. Nor were they transported by men; for where were men ever seen so foolish as to wish to embark crocodiles with them, to bring them to those countries where they were going to settle? Nor can it be said that they passed by swimming; for it is not possible that they would have moved away, swimming through the brackish waters of the Ocean, almost two thousand miles from the banks of the rivers or lakes in which they had been reared, and where they enjoyed the company of the other individuals of their species.

No other expedient remains, then, than to admit the ancient union of the equatorial countries of America with those of Africa, and the continuation of the northern countries of America with those of Europe or of Asia: the latter for the passage of the beasts proper to cold climes, and the former for the passage of the quadrupeds and reptiles proper to warm climes. By the reasons adduced so far we are persuaded that there was once a great tract of land that joined the part now most eastern of Brazil to the most western part of Africa, and that all this space of land was perhaps swallowed up by some great

earthquakes [p. 39] earthquakes, only some remnants of it remaining in the islands of Cape Verde, Fernando de Noronha, Ascension, St. Matthew, and others, and in the many shoals recognized by several travelers, and particularly by M. de Buache, who sounded that stretch of sea with the greatest diligence. (D) These islands and shoals will probably have been the highest parts of that submerged continent. Likewise we believe that the part of America now most western was once joined by means of a smaller continent to the most eastern part of Tartary, and perhaps America was also joined by way of Greenland to other northern countries of Europe.

The supreme respect that we bear to the sacred books obliges us to believe that the quadrupeds and reptiles of the New World descend from those individuals that escaped in Noah’s ark from the universal Flood, and the reasons adduced so far, with others that we omit so as not to be tedious, persuade us that such beasts passed by land, and by different quarters, into the new continent. All other systems are subject to the gravest difficulties; in ours there are some, but not altogether insuperable. The greatest consists in the apparent unlikelihood of so great an earthquake as would swallow up a stretch of land of more than fifteen hundred miles, such as in our supposition was that which joined Africa to America, and sink it as deep as is the depth observed in some places of that sea. But besides that we do not ascribe that stupendous revolution to a single earthquake, there being moreover in the bowels of the earth so many masses of combustible materials that the inflammation of some could rapidly communicate itself to others (in the same way in which Gassendi explains the propagation of lightning), and the violent rarefaction of the air contained within those natural mines could all at once shake, agitate, and sink

a

(D) M. de Buache presented in the year 1737 to the Royal Academy of Sciences of Paris the hydrographic charts of that sea made according to his observations, which were examined and approved by the Academy. The distinguished author of the Lettere americane gave in his vol. 2 an abridgment of those charts. [p. 40] a tract of land of two or three thousand miles. This is neither impossible nor unlikely, nor are examples of it lacking in History.
