The earthquake that was felt in Canada in the year 1663 swallowed up a chain of mountains of solid rock more than three hundred miles long, leaving all that great stretch of land changed into an immense plain. How great, then, must have been the convulsion caused by those extraordinary and memorable earthquakes of which the American histories make mention, and with which they believed the World ended?

It may also be objected to our system that, if the beasts passed by land from the one continent to the other, it is not easy to guess the reason why some species passed there without a single individual remaining in the old continent, and why, on the contrary, some entire species remained in the old continent without any individual of them passing to America. Why did the fourteen species of monkeys that are found today in America pass, and not the seventeen species that Count de Buffon enumerates in Asia and Africa, all being of the same climate and having likewise freedom and ease to pass? Why did the sloths pass, and not the gazelles, which are so swift? If, moreover, from Armenia, where Noah’s Ark came to rest, the beasts set out toward America, the species destined for the equatorial countries of the New World must without doubt have made a journey of six thousand miles, going from Armenia through Mesopotamia and Syria to Egypt; thence through the center of Africa to the supposed tract of land that joined both continents, and from that finally to Brazil: and though as regards other beasts no difficulty appears in making that journey in ten, in twenty, or in forty years, nevertheless as regards the sloths one cannot understand how they could accomplish it even in six centuries, walking constantly. If we give credit to Count de Buffon, the sloth cannot advance in an hour more than a perch, or six royal feet of Paris: whence, to make that journey of six thousand miles, it would need some six hundred and eighty years, and even more, if we believe what Maffei, Herrera, and Piso wrote,

who [p. 41] who affirm that that wretched quadruped can scarcely make in fifteen days, or in two weeks, a stone’s throw.

This is what can be said against our system; but some of the aforesaid arguments have greater force against the other opinions, except against that which employs Angels in the transport of the beasts. If men were those who transported the beasts, why in place of wolves and foxes did they not carry horses, oxen, sheep, and goats? How did they not leave behind even a single individual of several species in the old continent? If it is maintained that such beasts passed by swimming, then the difficulty of the sea voyage is added to that of the land one. If all the beasts are made to pass, even those of South America, by the north, then instead of making a journey of six thousand miles they would have had to make another of more than fifteen thousand, for which journey the sloth would need 1740 years.

We reply, then, to the aforesaid objections: 1. that, since not all the quadrupeds of the earth have yet been identified, we cannot know how many there are in the one and the other continent, and how many are lacking. Count de Buffon enumerates only two hundred species of quadrupeds. M. Valmont de Bomare, who wrote shortly after that author, enumerates two hundred and sixty-five; but to tell the truth no one is capable of counting them all; for nothing is known of the quadrupeds of some inland regions of Africa, of a great part of Tartary, of the country of the Amazons, of northern Louisiana, of the lands lying north of the Colorado river, of the country of the Apaches, of the Solomon Islands, of New Holland, etc., which countries are a considerable part of our globe. Nor is it a wonder that no knowledge is had of the beasts of those unknown countries, since of those of countries well known, and inhabited by the Europeans for two hundred and sixty years, the zoologists do not have the information required to write a complete history of the quadrupeds. Count de Buffon, although he is the man most instructed in this matter, omits also

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.         F                   some [p. 42] some quadrupeds of Mexico, expatriates others from their proper country, and confuses others, as we shall show in the dissertation on animals. But as regards the beasts that certainly were lacking in America, such as the elephants, the camels, and the horses, several reasons can be adduced for such a lack. It may be that those beasts did in fact pass to the New World and then perished there, either killed by wild beasts or exterminated by some epidemic that befell them. It may also be that they never passed to America. Some, such as elephants and rhinoceroses, whose multiplication is very slow, stayed in the southern countries of Asia and Africa, because they found there a climate suited to their nature, good pastures, and a great space of land in which to live comfortably: so that they had no need to leave those regions to procure their food elsewhere. It is true that many authors have persuaded themselves that the great bones dug up near the Ohio River, and in other places of America, were of elephants, which would demonstrate their ancient existence on that continent; but as modern zoologists do not agree about the species of quadruped to which such bones belonged, no argument can be drawn from them against us. (E) Finally, other beasts perhaps did not pass into the New World because men detained them. I do not doubt that, after the family of Noah came out of the ark, it kept in its power the cows, sheep, and goats, reduced to herds and flocks, to take from them both clothing, after the example of their ancestors, and sustenance, in accordance with the permission given by God after the Flood. In proportion as men went on multiplying, their possessions went on likewise increasing in Armenia, Chaldea, Syria, Persia, and Egypt, in which regions there remained,

as

(E) Müller said that those bones had belonged to certain very great quadrupeds, which he calls Mammouts. Count de Buffon, trusting him too much, calculated that the said quadrupeds were six times larger than elephants. Others believed that they were bones of hippopotamuses, others that they were of sea beasts; and others finally that they belonged to other quadrupeds, unknown and extinct. But there is no doubt that many of those bones were of giants, as we said in bk. 1 of our History. [p. 43] as is to be believed, confined in those first times under the care of the firstborn of the families, the herds and flocks; while the other quadrupeds, which were at liberty, fleeing from men, betook themselves to countries not yet peopled, and some, seeking the climate and the food suited to their nature, will have made their way toward America. Meanwhile many families destined to people various countries of the earth, foreseeing their separation, and wishing to leave to posterity a clear monument of their magnificence, undertook the building of the city and the tower of Babel. God confounded their language in order to compel them to betake themselves to the countries assigned to them, and they, constrained by the command and the punishment of Heaven, set out upon different roads. The progenitors of those who were destined to people America either did not bring with them flocks and herds, because they could not have them, or, having drawn them from Chaldea, used them up for lack of provisions in their long pilgrimage. It is certain that not one of those beasts which from the first centuries have been under the care and direction of men in the Old World was found in the New: which seems a clear indication that the animals passed into the New World by their own instinct, and were not transported there by men. What we say of cows, sheep, and goats, we may also conjecture of asses and horses, since we need not doubt that these animals too were reduced to servitude immediately after the Flood. But however that may be, the argument drawn from the passage of some beasts, and not of others, proves nothing against our system.

As for the calculation mentioned above, concerning the time the sloth needed to make its way from Armenia to Brazil, we find no difficulty in it. Even had it needed a thousand years, it could at last arrive in the New World, in case the two continents remained joined all that time: a supposition that can be proved false neither by history nor by reason; but neither is there any reason that obliges us to admit such a calculation. The same Count de Buffon protests that the authors have exaggerated the slowness of the sloth, and M.

F2              Daubenton

[p. 44]

Daubenton recognized that it was not so slow as the tortoise. Besides, since this beast is not harmful, but rather deserving of compassion, it could have been helped by men, and carried from one country to another.

Such are my sentiments concerning the peopling of America, which I submit to the judgment of wise and Christian Doctors; not, however, to that of certain unbelieving and capricious philosophers, who neither respect divine authority nor care for human traditions, nor are willing to listen to reason.

Dissertation

[p. 45]

D I S S E RTAT I O N I I . O N T H E P R I N C I PA L E P O C H S O F T H E H I S T O RY

O F M E X I C O.

T    he great variety that we find among the authors concerning the

chronology of the History of Mexico obliges us to examine at length the epochs of the principal events. Had we done so in the body of the History, it would have been necessary to interrupt the thread of the narrative with thorny disputes. Had we done it, as we wished, in the notes, these would have grown excessively long. The variety of opinions among the historians arose, as it appears, from their not having adjusted the Mexican years to ours. I have labored with great diligence to trace out the truth, and in large part I think I have succeeded, as I shall show in the present dissertation, which will no doubt be tedious to those who have no interest in the clarification of these points of chronology.

§ I.

On the Epoch of the Arrival of the Toltecs and of Other Nations in the

Country of Anahuac.

In the present dissertation we do not treat of the first settlers, of whom we have already spoken, but only of those nations that figure in some way in our History. Now, first of all, the authors disagree about the order of the arrival of these nations; for the Chichimecs, for example, who according to Acosta, Gómara, and Sigüenza were the first to arrive in that country, were according to Torquemada the third, and according to Boturini the fourth. Their disagreement as to the time of the arrival of each nation is no less, as we shall see.

No

[p. 46]

No one doubts that the Toltecs were very ancient. From the histories of the Chichimecs themselves it is evident that they did not arrive in Anahuac until after the ruin of the Toltecs, whose buildings they recognized on their journey, and whose remains they found on the shores of the Mexican lakes and in other places. On this point Torquemada, Vetancurt, and Boturini are in agreement. Herrera, Acosta, and Gómara make no mention of the Toltecs, perhaps because those authors whom they used omitted the accounts of that nation, since these were scanty and obscure.

Concerning the time of their arrival in Anahuac, Torquemada says in bk. 3 of his History that it took place in the year 700 of the common era; but from what he wrote in bk. 1 it is deduced that it happened about 648. The Chevalier Boturini makes them almost a century more ancient, since he believed Ixtlalcuechahuac, the second king of that nation, to be reigning in Tula in the year 660. From their paintings we know that they departed from Huehuetlapallan in the year I Tecpatl; that after wandering a hundred and four years they settled first in Tollantzinco and then in Tula; and that their monarchy, begun in the year VII Acatl, lasted three hundred and eighty-four years. After comparing these epochs of the Toltecs with those of the Chichimecs, their successors, I have become persuaded that their departure from Huehuetlapallan occurred in the year 544, and that their monarchy began in the year 667. Whoever wishes to continue, going back toward that time, the series of Mexican years matched with Christian ones, which we set out at the end of the second volume, will find that the year 544 of the common era was I Tecpatl, and that the year 667 was likewise VII Acatl. Besides, there is no reason to move these epochs earlier, nor can they be put later without upsetting those of the later nations. Now, since this monarchy began in the year 667 and lasted 384 years, its end, and the overthrow of the Toltecs, must be fixed in the year 1051.

Between the ruin of the Toltecs and the arrival of the Chichimecs Torquemada allows no more than nine years; but this cannot be, because the Chichimecs found, as the same author says,

the [p. 47] the buildings of the Toltecs in ruins, and it is not likely that they were ruined in only nine years. Besides, the beginning of the Chichimec monarchy cannot be fixed in that century without increasing the number of those kings, or without prolonging their lives beyond measure, as Torquemada does. Who will be capable of believing that Xolotl reigned 113 years and lived 200? That Nopaltzin his son lived 170, that Techotlala his great-grandson reigned 104, and that Tezozomoc his descendant reigned in Azcapotzalco 160 years, or 180? It is true that a man of robust constitution, aided by sobriety in diet and by so mild a climate as that of Mexico, could reach so advanced an age, and in the history of that country the examples are not too rare of men who have prolonged their lives beyond the regular term of mortals. Calmecahua, one of the Tlaxcalan captains who helped the Spaniards in the conquest of Mexico, lived 130 years. Pedro Nieto, a Jesuit, died in 1636 at the age of 132. Diego Ordóñez, a Franciscan, died at Sombrerete at the age of 117, (*) preaching to the people until the last month of his life. A long catalogue could be made of those who, both in the two past centuries and in our own day, have passed the age of a hundred in those countries. Particularly among the Indians those are not so rare who reach ninety and a hundred years, keeping to extreme old age their black hair, their teeth firm, and their sight good; but since so few have been those who, after the twenty-third century of the world, have prolonged their lives to the age of 150, who are regarded as so many prodigies, we cannot assent to the extravagant chronology of Torquemada, resting perhaps on the authority of some painting or writing of the Texcocans, especially since the same author confesses that those nations did not keep great account of the years. We therefore believe without hesitation that the arrival

of

(*) Diego Ordóñez lived 104 years in religion and almost 95 in the priesthood. In his last sermon he took leave of the people of Sombrerete with those words of St. Paul: I have fought a good fight, I have finished my course, etc. [p. 48] of the Chichimecs in Anahuac took place in the twelfth century, and in all likelihood about the year 1170.

Hardly eight years had passed since Xolotl, the first Chichimec king, had established himself at Tenayuca, when new peoples arrived there, led, as we have said in the History, by six chiefs. I do not doubt that these new peoples were the six tribes of the Xochimilcas, the Tepanecs, the Colhuas, the Chalcas, the Tlahuicas, and the Tlaxcalans, separated from the Mexicans at Chicomoztoc, and arrived in the Valley of Mexico not all together but with some interval of time, and in the order we have indicated. It is certain that when they arrived there, a few years after the Acolhuas, they found the city of Azcapotzalco already founded by the Tepanecs, and that of Colhuacan by the Colhuas. It is known, moreover, that these tribes arrived in that country after the Chichimecs: therefore their arrival took place in that interval which lay between the arrival of the Chichimecs and that of the Acolhuas. Now there is no record of other peoples arriving in Anahuac at that time, except those led by the aforesaid six chiefs: these, then, were those six tribes of Nahuatlacas, namely the Xochimilcas, the Tepanecs, the Colhuas, etc., each led by its own chief. Father Acosta makes these tribes almost three centuries more ancient, since he says that they reached the shores of the Mexican lake in the year 902, after a pilgrimage of eighty years; but this chronology does not agree well with the History, from which it is evident that when Xolotl arrived in the Valley of Mexico with his colony of Chichimecs, he found the shores of that lake unpeopled, and the arrival of this colony could not have taken place before the middle of the twelfth century, according to what we have said above.

The year of the arrival of the Acolhuas is not known; but I do not doubt that it was toward the end of the twelfth century; for they arrived a few years after the arrival of those six tribes, and moreover it is evident from the same History that Xolotl survived their arrival by some years.

The last nation, or tribe, to arrive in Anahuac was that of the Mexicans. Among so many historians consulted by me I have not

found [p. 49] found even one who is of the contrary opinion, except Vetancurt, who places the Otomis after the Mexicans. Father Acosta fixes the arrival of the Mexicans at the shores of the Mexican lake in the year 1208; for he affirms that they arrived there 306 years after the Xochimilcas and the other tribes of the Nahuatlacas, whose arrival he believes to have been in 902. Torquemada, according to the calculation made by Vetancurt on his account, puts the arrival of the Mexicans at Chapultepec in the year 1269. An anonymous Mexican history cited by the Chevalier Boturini puts the arrival of that tribe at Tula in the year 1196, and on this epoch several Indian historians seem to agree. Besides, this chronology agrees perfectly with all the other epochs; hence we have adopted it as the most probable and almost certain. This being supposed, one must say that the Mexicans arrived at Zumpango in the year 1216 and at Chapultepec in the year 1245; for it is known that they stayed nine years at Tula, and eleven at Tepexic and in other places before arriving at Zumpango; that they remained seven years at Zumpango, and twenty-two in other places before going to Chapultepec. After having been seventeen years at Chapultepec, they passed to Acolco in 1262, where they stayed 52 years, and from there they were led as slaves to Colhuacan in 1314.

As for the Otomis, there is great variety among the historians. Some confuse them with the Chichimecs, such as Acosta, Gómara, and most of the Spanish authors. Torquemada in bk. 1 distinguishes them expressly; but in other places in his History he seems to confuse them. Vetancurt, after copying Torquemada’s narrative in all that concerns the Toltecs, the Chichimecs, and the other nations, says, speaking of the reign of Chimalpopoca, third king of Mexico, that in his time the Otomis arrived in Anahuac and settled chiefly at Xaltocan. This anecdote of Vetancurt’s is not to be despised; for he undoubtedly took it from the writings of Sigüenza, since he is not accustomed to depart from Torquemada except to follow that learned Mexican; but he errs in the chronology, since he fixes the arrival of the Otomis

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.         G                        in [p. 50] in the year VI Tecpatl, which he believes to have been 1381. He was certainly mistaken; for, as appears from our chronological table placed at the end of the second volume, the year 1381 was not VI Tecpatl but VI Calli, nor was Chimalpopoca then reigning, but Acamapichtli, as we shall see below. If the arrival of the Otomis in the Valley of Mexico (not in the country of Anahuac, where they had settled many centuries before) took place in the year VI Tecpatl, and under the reign of Chimalpopoca, it must without doubt have been in the year 1420. That no mention is made of the Otomis before this epoch, and that they were found by the Spaniards less civilized than the other nations, scattered through several provinces and in some isolated places, and surrounded by other nations of a different language, leads us to believe that precisely at that time they began to live in society under the domination of the Tepanecs, and afterward under that of the Mexicans and the Tlaxcalans. I am persuaded that, because they found the land occupied by the other nations, they could not, like the others, settle all in one country, although the greater part of that nation peopled the tract of land that lies to the northwest and north of the capital, as being nearer to the country where they had previously lived scattered like wild beasts.

The reason why the Otomis were confused by many historians with the Chichimecs may be taken from the same history. When the ancient Chichimecs were made civilized by the Toltecs and the Nahuatlacas, many families of that nation abandoned themselves to the savage life in the country of the Otomis, prizing the exercise of the hunt more than the labors of agriculture. These retained the name of Chichimecs, and the others, brought to civilization, began to be called Acolhuas, honoring themselves with the name of a nation that was esteemed the most civilized. Of the Otomis, then, those who adopted civil life kept the name of Otomis, by which they are known in history; but the others, who scattered in the woods and mingled with the Chichimecs, and would not give up their barbarous liberty, were called by many Chichimecs, from the name of that famous nation: whence some

writers [p. 51] writers, speaking of these barbarians, who for more than a century after the conquest of Mexico gave the Spaniards a great deal of trouble, distinguish the Mexican Chichimecs from the Otomi Chichimecs; because the former spoke the Mexican language, and the latter the Otomi, according to the nation from which they drew their origin.

From all that we have said so far we can conclude, with the greatest probability that is possible in a subject so obscure, that the order and time of the arrival of those nations in Anahuac was this:

The Toltecs in the year 648.

The Chichimecs about the year 1170.

The first Nahuatlacas about 1178.

The Acolhuas about the end of the twelfth century.

The Mexicans arrived at Tula in the year 1196, at Zumpango in the year 1216, and at Chapultepec in the year 1245.

The Otomis entered the Valley of Mexico, and began to be brought to civil life, in the year 1420.

I know well that the Tepanecs boasted of their city of Azcapotzalco as so ancient that, according to Torquemada, they counted 1561 years from its foundation to the beginning of the last century; so that they reckoned it founded immediately after the death of our divine Redeemer; but the opposite is evident from the histories of the other nations, which make the Tepanecs little more ancient than the Mexicans in Anahuac, and also from the very series of the lords of Azcapotzalco, whose portraits were preserved down to our day in an ancient building of that city. They counted no more than ten lords from the foundation of their city to the memorable ruin of their state, caused by the combined arms of the Mexicans and the Acolhuas, which happened, as we shall see, in the year 1425; so that one would have to give each lord a hundred and forty years of government to make up that sum.

The Totonacs, for their part, said they were more ancient than the Chichimecs; for to boast of antiquity is a weakness common to all nations. They related, then, that having at the beginning settled for some time on the shores of the

G2                      lake

[p. 52]

Lake Texcoco, they thence betook themselves to people those mountains, which took from them the name of Totonacapan; that there they were ruled by ten lords, each of whom governed the nation for exactly eighty years, until the Chichimecs, arriving in Anahuac in the time of Xatoncan, second lord of the Totonac nation, subjected them to their dominion, and that afterward they were finally subjected to the Mexican kings. Torquemada, who reports this account of the Totonacs in bk. 3 of his Monarquía indiana, adds that this is certain, and proved by authentic histories worthy of belief; but whatever he may say, it is certain that the time of the arrival of that nation in Anahuac is not known, nor can it be known, and that the tale of the ten lords who governed the nation exactly eighty years is good only for amusing children.

The time at which the Olmecs and the Xicalancas arrived is still less known. The Chevalier Boturini says that he could find no painting or record concerning these nations; nevertheless he believes them more ancient than the Toltecs; but however that may be, it is indubitable that they were most ancient.

We make no mention here of the other nations, because their antiquity is absolutely unknown; but we do not doubt, in view of what we have set forth elsewhere, that the Chiapanecs were among the most ancient, and perhaps the first of all the nations that peopled the country of Anahuac.

§ II.

On the Correspondence of the Mexican Years to Ours, and on the Epoch of the

Founding of Mexico.

All the writers, both Mexican and Spanish, who have mentioned Mexican chronology are in agreement concerning the method those nations had of counting their centuries and their years, which I have set out in bk. VI of the History, and in the tables placed at the end of vol. II. Whenever, then, the correspondence of a Mexican year with

any [p. 53] any of the Christian years is found, the correspondence of all the others will at once be known. If I know, for example, that this year 1780 is, as indeed it is, II Tecpatl, I am sure that 1781 is III Calli, 1782 is IV Tochtli, etc. All the difficulty consists in finding a Mexican year whose correspondence to some Christian year is altogether certain and indubitable; but we find this difficulty already overcome, since we are sure, no less from the paintings of the Indians than from the testimony of Acosta, Torquemada, Sigüenza, Vetancurt, and Boturini, that the year 1519, in which the Spaniards entered Mexico, was I Acatl, and consequently that the year 1518 was XIII Tochtli, the year 1517 XII Calli, etc. So that one cannot doubt the exactness of our chronological table placed at the end of vol. II, in what concerns the correspondence of the Mexican years to the Christian. Those authors who differ from it erred in the calculation, and contradicted themselves. Vetancurt, to make us understand the manner of reckoning years that the Mexicans had, presents us with a table of the Mexican years matched with the Christian years from the year 1663 to 1688; but this table is wrong from head to foot, because the author supposes the year 1663 to have been I Tochtli, which is shown to be false by continuing our table to that year. He affirms that 1507 was a secular year: this error admitted, he cannot fail to err throughout his chronology. If the year 1519 was I Acatl, as he supposes with the other writers, we shall find, going back in our table, that the secular year was not 1507, but 1506. To confirm his chronology he alleges the testimony of his friend and countryman Dr. Sigüenza, who says he had found that the year 1684 was IX Acatl. If this were so, his calculation would doubtless be right; but although we do not doubt his truthfulness in the citation of Sigüenza, we have reason to believe that this learned Mexican corrected his chronology: nor could he do otherwise, knowing, as in fact he knew, that the year 1519 was I Acatl, a certain principle, on which all Mexican chronology must rest, and from which is clearly deduced the year

1684

[p. 54]

1684 was not IX Acatl, but X Tecpatl. Torquemada, in discoursing in bk. 3 about the Totonacs, speaks of a noble of that nation who was born in the year II Acatl, and that the year before, 1519, in which the Spaniards had arrived in that country, was among the Mexicans I Acatl. When Torquemada wrote this he was either weighed down with sleep or distracted by another thought; for he knew well, as we all know, that the year which among the Mexicans follows I Acatl is not II Acatl but II Tecpatl, and such was the year 1520 of which he speaks.

Supposing, then, that the year 1519 was I Acatl, and the relation of the Mexican years to the Christian being known, it is not difficult to trace the epoch of the founding of Mexico. All the historians who have consulted the paintings of the Mexicans, or have informed themselves orally from them, agree in saying that that famous city was founded by the Aztecs in the fourteenth century of Christianity; but they differ as to the year. The Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection fixes the founding in the year 1324; Gemelli, following Sigüenza, in 1325; Sigüenza as cited by Vetancurt, and an anonymous Mexican cited by Boturini, in 1327, (a) Torquemada, according to the calculation made by Vetancurt on his account, in 1341, and Enrico Martínez in 1357. The Mexicans put this founding in the year II Calli, as is seen in the first painting of the Mendoza Collection, and in others cited by Sigüenza. Since it is certain, then, that that city was founded in the fourteenth century, and in the year II Calli, this could not have been in 1324, nor in 1327, nor in 1341, nor in 1357, because none of these years was II Calli. If we go back from the year 1519 to the fourteenth century, we shall find in it two years II Calli, namely 1325 and 1377. Now in the latter year certainly this founding did not take place; because then it would be necessary to shorten too much the reigns of the Mexican monarchs, contradicting the

chronology

(a) The testimony of the anonymous Mexican is found in a copy of an ancient painting made in the year 1631. [p. 55] chronology of the ancient paintings. There remains, then, no other recourse than to say that that famous capital was founded in 1325 of the common era: and this was without doubt the opinion of Dr. Sigüenza; for Gemelli, who had in this matter no other instruction than what was given him by that Mexican man of letters, puts this founding in the year 1325, which he says was II Calli. (b) If he was previously of another opinion, he afterward changed it, perceiving that it did not agree well with that certain principle, that the year 1519 was I Acatl.

## § III

On the Chronology of the Mexican Kings.

It is difficult to make clear the chronology of the Mexican kings, because of the disagreement of the authors. We shall use some certain points to trace the uncertain ones. To give readers some idea of the variety of opinions it is enough to cast one’s eyes on the following table, in which we indicate the year in which, according to Acosta, the Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection, and Sigüenza, each of the kings began to reign. (c)

Acosta      The Interpr.            Sigüenza

Acamapichtli.       1384.       1375.              May 3, 1361.

Huitzilihuitl.      1424.       1396.              April 19, 1403.

Chimalpopoca.       1427.       1417.              Feb. 24, 1414.

Itzcoatl.           1437.       1427.              . . . . . 1427.

Moctezuma I.        1449.       1440.              Aug. 13, 1440.

Axayacatl.          1481.       1469.              Nov. 21, 1468.

Tizoc.              1477.       1482.              Oct. 30, 1481.

Ahuitzotl.          1492.       1486.              April 13, 1486.

Moctezuma II.       1503.       1502.              Sept. 15, 1502.

Acosta,

(b) We have pointed out elsewhere Gemelli’s mistake in having written: the year 1325 from the creation of the world, instead of writing: of the common era.

(c) The years put in the table according to the Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection are those that are read in the edition of Tedi Purchas, which we have not been able to find.

[p. 56]

Acosta, and following him Enrico Martínez and Herrera, differ from the other authors not only in the chronology but also in the order of the kings, putting Tizoc on the throne before Axayacatl, whereas the contrary is evident no less from the testimony of the Mexicans than from that of the other Spanish authors. Gómara muddles the reigns of the lords of Tula with those of the kings of Colhuacan and of the Mexican kings; Torquemada gives the years of the one and of the others, and his chronology differs from that of the other authors. Solís makes Moctezuma II the eleventh of the Mexican kings; but I do not know whence he drew so strange an anecdote. M. de Pauw, to demonstrate his extravagance in this too, counts no more than eight kings of Mexico; but it is altogether certain and indubitable that the Mexicans had eleven kings, namely those nine mentioned above, and after them Cuitláhuac and Cuauhtémoc. Some authors would not count these last two among the kings, because they reigned a short time; but, having been lawfully elected and peaceably accepted by the nation, they have as much right to be counted among the Mexican kings as all their predecessors. Acosta says that he does not mention them because they had nothing of kings but the name, since in their time almost the whole kingdom was already subject to the Spaniards; but this is absolutely false, because when Cuitláhuac was elected, the Spaniards had under them nothing but the province of the Totonacs, and these were allies rather than subjects. When Cuauhtémoc was elected, they had added to that province the states of Cuauhquechollan, Itzocan, Tepeaca, Tecamachalco, and a few places in those surroundings; but all these states, compared with the rest of the Mexican empire, were less than Bologna is in comparison with the whole Papal State.

To trace the chronology of these eleven kings it is necessary to use another method, beginning with the last and continuing in retrograde order to the beginnings of the monarchy.

Cuauhtémoc. This king ended his reign on August 13, 1521, having been made prisoner by the Spaniards,

and [p. 57] and the court of Mexico conquered. The day of his election is not known; but from Cortés’s account it is deduced that he was elected in October or November of the year before: so that he could not reign more than nine or ten months.

Cuitláhuac. This king, successor to his brother Moctezuma, ascended the throne in the first days of July 1520, as is deduced from Cortés’s account. Some Spanish authors say that he reigned no more than forty days; others affirm that he reigned sixty; but from what Cortés says he heard from a Mexican officer in the war of Cuauhquechollan, it is perceived that that king was still alive in October. We therefore do not doubt that his reign was of at least three months.

Moctezuma II. It is known that he reigned 17 years and a little more than nine months, and that he began to reign in September 1502, and died in the last days of June 1520. The reason why some authors put the beginning of his reign in 1503 was that they knew he had reigned seventeen years, and did not take account of the nine months more.

Ahuitzotl. Acosta gives this king eleven years of reign, Martínez twelve, Sigüenza sixteen, and Torquemada eighteen. I believe that we can trace the years of his reign and the time of his exaltation from the epoch of the dedication of the great temple. This took place without doubt in 1486: on which several authors agree. On the other hand it is evident that King Tizoc having barely begun this edifice, Ahuitzotl continued it and brought it to completion, and this he could not do in the same year in which he began to reign, nor even in two or three years, such an edifice being as vast as we know. Nor could he in so short a time wage the war he waged, in so many countries so distant from one another, and procure that astonishing number of prisoners who were sacrificed at that great festival. We therefore believe that the beginning of his reign cannot be fixed after the year 1482, nor can it be put earlier without upsetting the epochs of his predecessors, as we shall see below. Having then begun

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.       H                      to [p. 58] to reign in 1482, and having ended in 1502, we must give him nineteen years and some months, or almost twenty years of reign.

Tizoc. No one doubts that the reign of this monarch was very brief, and there is none among the authors who gives him more than four and a half years on the throne. We can deduce the time of his reign, and that of his predecessor also, from that of Nezahualpilli, king of Acolhuacan; for since this king was so celebrated, and had so many historians at his court, we have certain information of his reign. Nezahualpilli died in 1516 after having reigned in Acolhuacan forty-five years and some months: hence the beginning of his reign must be fixed in 1470. It is known, moreover, that the eighth year of Nezahualpilli was the first of Tizoc: so that the latter must have begun his reign in 1477, and must have reigned four and a half years, as several historians say. Torquemada says that he reigned less than three; but this author openly contradicts himself, no less in this than in other articles of his chronology; for, adopting as he does the aforementioned calculation for the reign of Nezahualpilli, and giving less than three years to the reign of Tizoc, he ought to have fixed his death in 1480, and consequently given Ahuitzotl not eighteen years but twenty-two of reign.

Axayacatl. It is known that this king began to reign six years before Nezahualpilli, that is, in the year 1464, and that he ended, according to what we have said, in 1477, in which his successor Tizoc ascended the throne. From this it is deduced that he reigned thirteen years, as Sigüenza and other historians affirm. Acosta does not give him more than eleven years, nor the Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection more than twelve. The most probable is that the thirteen years were not completed.

Moctezuma I. All affirm that this famous king completed twenty-eight years on the throne; but some give him one year more; because these count as a full year those months that he reigned beyond twenty-eight years, which were neglected by the others. He began to reign, then, in 1436, and ended in

1464

[p. 59]

1464. In his time the toxiuhmolpia, or secular year, was celebrated, not in the sixteenth year of his reign, as Torquemada would have it, but in the eighteenth, that is, in 1454.

Itzcoatl. Almost all the historians give thirteen years of reign to this great king; only Acosta and Martínez give him twelve. The reason for such a difference will have been the same as that mentioned above, namely that since Itzcoatl had not completed thirteen years on the throne, Acosta and Martínez took no account of those months over the twelve years, and the others counted them as if it had been a full year. He began to reign in 1423; he could not begin either sooner or later; because he ascended the throne a year after Maxtla usurped the crown of Acolhuacan. Maxtla reigned three years, and ended together with the kingdom of the Tepanecs. The following year, that is, three years after Itzcoatl had begun to reign, Nezahualcoyotl was restored to the throne of Acolhuacan, which the Tepanecs had usurped from him. It is known, moreover, that Nezahualcoyotl reigned forty-three years and some months, and therefore, since he ended in 1470, it seems that the beginning of his reign must be fixed in 1426, the ruin of the Tepanecs in 1425, the beginning of the reign of Itzcoatl in 1423, and that of the tyranny of Maxtla in 1422.

Chimalpopoca. This unfortunate king was confused by Acosta, Martínez, and Herrera with his nephew Acolnahuacatl, son of Huitzilihuitl: whence these authors make Chimalpopoca ascend the throne at only ten years of age, and make him die soon afterward at the hands of the Tepanecs; but the opposite is evident from the paintings and accounts of the Indians, cited by Torquemada, and in part seen by us. Sigüenza fell through inadvertence into a contradiction; for he says that Chimalpopoca was the younger brother, as in fact he was, of Huitzilihuitl: of this king he affirms that he began to reign at eighteen years of age, and that he reigned little less than eleven: so that he must have died not yet having reached twenty-nine years of age, and Chimalpopoca, who immediately succeeded him, should have been at most twenty-eight when he began to reign; nevertheless Sigüenza makes him ascend the throne at forty years and more. In the Mendoza

H2              Collection

[p. 60]

Collection no more than ten years of reign are given to this king. Torquemada and Sigüenza give him thirteen, and this is without doubt the most probable, in view of the series of his actions and events; but Vetancurt, following Torquemada, has on this point some notable anachronisms. He puts the election of Chimalpopoca in the time of Techotlala, king of Acolhuacan: let us suppose that this was in the last year of that king: Techotlala was succeeded by Ixtlilxochitl, who reigned seven years: Ixtlilxochitl was succeeded by Tezozomoc, who tyrannized over that empire nine years, and he was succeeded by Maxtla, in whose time Chimalpopoca died. According to these principles, adopted by Torquemada and by Vetancurt, one must give Chimalpopoca at least sixteen years of reign, resulting from the seven of Ixtlilxochitl and the nine of Tezozomoc; which is opposed to their own chronology and to that of the other historians. If then we wish to combine the chronology of the kings of Mexico with that of the kings of Tlatelolco according to the calculation of the aforesaid authors, there will hardly remain to us nineteen years to apportion between the two kings Chimalpopoca and Itzcoatl, as we shall see below. Since thirteen years, then, must be counted in the reign of Chimalpopoca, according to the opinion of the greater part of the historians, we must fix the beginning of his reign in 1410. Maxtla succeeded Tezozomoc his father a year before the death of Chimalpopoca, that is, in 1422. Tezozomoc held the crown of Acolhuacan nine years: having died, then, in 1422, he began his tyranny in 1413. As for Ixtlilxochitl, lawful king of Acolhuacan, we know that he reigned seven years, until in 1413 his life was taken from him, together with the crown, by the tyrant Tezozomoc: he began to reign, then, in 1406.

Huitzilihuitl. The opinions of the writers are too diverse concerning the number of years this monarch reigned. Sigüenza says that it was ten years and ten months. Acosta and Martínez give him thirteen, the Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection twenty-one. Torquemada testifies that among the Mexican historians whom he saw, some give him twenty-two years, and others

twenty-six [p. 61] twenty-six; but I do not doubt that the true number of years is that indicated by the Interpreter of the Mendoza Collection; because we know from the historical paintings of the Mexicans that the thirteenth year of this king was a secular year, which, in view of what is seen in our chronological table placed at the end of the second volume, could be no other than 1402: he began to reign, then, in 1389. Having died, then, in 1410, as appears from what we have said about the reign of Chimalpopoca, we must count in the reign of Huitzilihuitl twenty-one years.

Acamapichtli. The chronology of the preceding kings being supposed, and the epoch of the founding of Mexico established, we have little to do with regard to this king. Torquemada affirms that the paintings and manuscript histories of the Mexicans fix the election of Acamapichtli in the twenty-eighth year of the founding of Mexico. He was elected, then, in 1352, or at the beginning of the year 1353, and his reign will have been of thirty-seven years, or little less. The interregnum that there was after the death of this king was, according to Sigüenza, of four months, whereas all the others were hardly of a few days.

§ I V.

On the Epochs of the Events of the Conquest.

It is not very difficult to trace the epochs of the events of the conquest, because we find them for the most part indicated by the Conqueror Cortés in his letters to Charles V; but since there are several anachronisms among the Spanish historians, either because they did not consult those letters, or because they did not take care to learn on what days the movable feasts of those years fell, which Cortés sometimes uses, it is necessary to fix some points of chronology, leaving aside others of less importance in order to spare the readers tedium.

The arrival of Cortés’s fleet on the coast of Chalchicuecan took place, as all know, on Holy Thursday of 1519.

This

[p. 62]

This was on April 21, because Easter fell that year on the 24th. The entry of the Spaniards into the city of Tlaxcala took place not, as Herrera and Gómara say, on September 23, but on the 18th, as Bernal Díaz, Vetancurt, and Solís affirm: which can be shown by calculating, according to Cortés’s account, the days the Spaniards stayed in Tlaxcala and in Cholula, and those they spent on their journey as far as Mexico. Bernal Díaz says that before entering Tlaxcala they stayed twenty-four days in the lands of that republic, and then twenty in that city, as is also evident from Cortés’s letter. They entered Cholula on October 14, and Mexico on November 8. Six days later King Moctezuma was made prisoner, as Cortés himself affirms. This general remained in that capital until the beginning of May of the following year, at which time he went to Cempoala to oppose Narváez. He attacked him there, and won the victory over that enemy of his on the Sunday of Pentecost, which that year (1520) fell on May 27. The uprising of the Mexicans, caused by the violence of Alvarado, took place at the great feast of the month Toxcatl, which began that year on May 13. Cortés returned to the capital after his victory on June 24, as everyone knows. In the account of the events that occurred in the last days of June and the first of July I find confusion, and anachronisms, among the historians. I have followed the letters of Cortés, which contain the most authentic account of the conquest.

The death of Moctezuma seems to have occurred on June 30; for he died, as Cortés testifies, three days after receiving the blow of the stone: he received it while those two war machines were being built, of which we make mention in the History; and they were built on the night of June 26 and on the following day, as is deduced from Cortés’s account. This death cannot be placed either after or before the 30th without upsetting the series of events.

Let us fix on July 1 the Sad Night, that is, the one in which

the [p. 63] the Spaniards came out defeated, because Cortés puts seven days on their journey from Mexico to the lands of Tlaxcala, and affirms that they entered them on July 8. Bernal Díaz and Vetancurt say that the Spaniards left Mexico on the 10th and entered the dominions of that republic on the 16th; but on this point more faith is due to Cortés. The events that occurred from June 24 to July 1 will seem many for so short a time; but it is no wonder that in circumstances of such straitness and of such great danger actions multiplied, as they made a last effort to escape with their lives.

The war waged by the Spaniards in Cuauhquechollan took place in the month of October, as appears from Cortés’s account. This epoch is important to us for knowing the time that Cuitláhuac reigned; for a Mexican captain, from whom Cortés learned the state of the court, gave him an account of the measures taken at that time by that king against the Spaniards. Those who maintain that that king reigned no more than forty days reject that information as false; but since they allege no reason to convince us of its falsity, we ought to believe it.

As to the day on which the siege of Mexico began, and the time of its duration, the authors commonly err. These for the most part say that the siege lasted ninety-three days; but they did not make their calculation exactly; for Cortés held the review of his troops in the great square of Texcoco, and assigned the place that the three divisions of the army were to occupy in that siege, on the Monday of Pentecost of the year 1521. Now even if we supposed, contrary to the truth of History, that on that very day of the review the siege was begun, there would not be ninety-three days but only eighty-five; because that Monday fell on May 20, and everyone knows that the siege ended with the capture of the capital on August 13. If they count as the siege the hostilities carried on by the Spaniards in the cities of the lake, they ought to fix the beginning of such a siege in the first days of January, and count not ninety-three days but seven months. Cortés, who

on [p. 64] on this point deserves more credence than any other historian, says expressly that the siege began on May 30, and lasted seventy-five days. It is true that the same letter of Cortés could have caused that error; for in it one is given to understand that on May 14 the divisions of Alvarado and Olid were already in Tacuba, whence the siege began; but there is a manifest error in the figures; for it is certain that those two captains did not go to Tacuba before the review of the troops, and we know from Cortés and from the other historians that this was held on the Monday of Pentecost, May 20.

Torquemada says in bk. 4, ch. 46 that the Spaniards entered Mexico for the first time on November 8; but in ch. 14 of the same book he affirms that this entry occurred on July 22: that they stayed there 150 days, 95 of them in friendship with the Mexicans, and 40 in war, which was caused by the slaughter made there by Alvarado at the feast of the month Toxcatl, corresponding, as he believes, to our April, etc. The heap of anachronisms, errors, and contradictions that the aforesaid author has in the cited chapter suffices to give an idea of his preposterous chronology. We persuade ourselves that the diligence we have used in clearing up such points has made us avoid, if not all, at least many errors.

Dissertation

[p. 65]

## D I S S E RTAT I O N I I I 

O N T H E L A N D O F M E X I C O.

hoever reads the horrible description that some

W               Europeans give of America, or hears the insulting con‐

tempt with which they speak of its land, its climate, its plants, its animals, and its inhabitants, will at once be persuaded either that fury and rage have armed both their pens and their tongues, or else that the New World is truly an accursed land, destined by Heaven to be the torment of evildoers. If we believe M. de Buffon, America is a wholly new country, scarcely emerged from under the waters that had flooded it: (*) a continual swamp in its plains; an uncultivated land covered with forests, even after it has been peopled by the Europeans, more industrious than the Americans, or else encumbered with inaccessible mountains, which leave only a small space of ground for the cultivation and habitation of men: an unhappy land under a niggardly sky, in which all the animals transported from the old continent have degenerated, and those that were native to its climate are small, deformed, feeble, and deprived of arms for their defense. If we are to believe M. de P. (who in great part copies the sentiments of M. de Buffon, and where he does not copy them multiplies and magnifies the errors) “America has been generally, and is even today, a country too sterile, in which all the plants of Europe have degenerated, except the aquatic and succulent ones: its stinking soil bears a greater number of poisonous plants than all the other parts of the world… Its land, either encumbered with mountains or covered with forests and swamps, shows nothing but an immense and sterile desert: its

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.            I                    climate

(*) Natural History, vol. 6. [p. 66] climate too contrary to the greater part of the quadrupeds, and above all pernicious to men, who are brutalized, weakened, and corrupted in a surprising manner in all the parts of their organization.” (*)

The chronicler Herrera, although otherwise so judicious and moderate, nevertheless, when he sets about comparing the sky and the land of Europe with those of America, shows himself so ignorant even of the first elements of Geography, and breaks out into such absurdities, that they could not be tolerated even in a child: Our hemisphere, he says, is better than the new one in respect of the sky. Our pole is more adorned with stars, because it has the north at 3½ degrees, with many shining stars. Wherein he supposes 1. that the southern hemisphere is new, whereas it has been known for so many centuries in Asia and in Africa; 2. that all America belongs to the southern hemisphere, and that northern America does not look to the same pole and the same stars as the Europeans. We have, he adds, another pre-eminence, that the Sun lingers seven days longer toward the Tropic of Cancer than toward that of Capricorn: as if the excess of the Sun’s stay in the boreal hemisphere were not the same in the new continent as in the ancient. It seems that our good chronicler had persuaded himself that the greater love which that planet bears to beautiful Europe is the cause of its lingering longer in the boreal hemisphere. A gallant thought, and worthy of a French poem! And hence it comes, our chronicler continues, that the Arctic part is colder than the Antarctic, because it enjoys less of the Sun; but how can there be less of the Sun in the Arctic part, when this planet lingers seven days longer in the boreal hemisphere? Our land extends more from west to east, and is therefore more convenient for human life than the other, which, narrowing from west to east, widens too much from one pole to the other; for the land that stretches from west to east is at a more equal distance from the cold of the north and the heat of the south. But

if

(*) Rech. philosoph., pt. 1. [p. 67] if the north is the region of cold and the south that of heat, as our chronicler would have it, the equatorial countries will, by his principles, be beyond question the most convenient for human life, as being equally distant from the north and from the south. In the other hemisphere, our author concludes at last, there were no dogs, nor asses, nor sheep, nor goats, etc. There were no lemon trees, nor orange trees, nor fig trees, nor quince trees, etc. (*)

These and other such absurdities of several authors are the effects of a blind and excessive patriotism, which makes them conceive certain imaginary pre-eminences of their own country over all the others in the world. It would not be difficult for us to set against their invectives against America the great praises that many most illustrious Europeans, better informed than they, have written of those countries; but besides that this would be foreign to our purpose, it would also be tedious to the readers: so we shall content ourselves with examining in this dissertation what these men have written against the land of America in general, or against that of Mexico in particular.

§ I.

On the Alleged Inundation of America.

Almost all that Messrs. de Buffon and de P. write against the land of America, concerning its plants, its animals, and its inhabitants, rests upon the supposition of a general inundation distinct from that which occurred in the time of Noah, and much more recent, because of which that whole vast country remained a long time under water. From this recent inundation arise, according to M. de Buffon, the malignity of the climate of America, the sterility of its soil, the imperfection of its animals, and the coldness of the Americans. “Nature had not had time to put into execution its

I2                    designs

(*) Herrera, Dec. I, bk. 1, ch. 5. [p. 68] designs, nor to assume its whole extent.” From the lakes and swamps left by that inundation originates, according to M. de P., the excessive humidity of that air, and the humidity is the cause of the infection of the atmosphere, of the extraordinary multiplication of insects, of the irregularity and smallness of the quadrupeds, of the sterility and stench of the soil, of the infecundity of the women, of the abundance of milk in the breasts of the men, of the dull-wittedness of the Americans, and of a thousand other extraordinary phenomena, which he, from his study in Berlin, has observed better than we who were so many years in America. These two authors, although they agree about the aforesaid inundation, nevertheless disagree about the time; for M. de P. believes it far more ancient than M. de Buffon.

Now this supposition of theirs is ill-founded, and the alleged inundation of the New World is a chimera. M. de P. strives to support it on the testimony of Father Acosta, on the almost infinite number of lakes and swamps, on the veins of heavy metals found almost at the surface of the earth, on the marine bodies found heaped up in the lowest inland places, on the destruction of the great quadrupeds, and finally on the unanimous tradition of the Mexicans, the Peruvians, and all the savages who live from the Magellanic land to the river St. Lawrence, who all with one accord attest that their ancestors dwelt on the mountains throughout the whole of that time, when the valleys were flooded.

It is indeed true that Father Acosta, in bk. 1, ch. 25 of his History, doubts whether what the Americans said of the flood ought to be understood of that of Noah, or rather of some other particular one that occurred in their land, like those of Deucalion and Ogyges in Greece; and he seems also to incline toward this opinion, which he says was that of some experienced men; but reasoning in bk. 5, ch. 19 on the conquests of the first Incas, he shows that he firmly believed this ought to be understood of the Flood of Noah: “The pretext,” he says, “with which they conquered (the Incas) and made themselves masters of the land was to feign that after the universal flood (of which

all [p. 69] all those Indians had knowledge) they had peopled the world anew, seven of them issuing from the cave of Pacaritambo, and that therefore all other men ought to render homage to them, as to their progenitors.” Father Acosta recognized, then, that that tradition of the Americans was beyond doubt that of the universal flood, and that the fables with which it had been disfigured had been invented by the Incas to establish their empire. What would that author say if he had had in favor of that general tradition those documents that we have? The Mexicans, as their own historians affirm, and as we say elsewhere, made no mention of the flood without likewise recalling the confusion of tongues and the dispersion of peoples, and these three things they represented in a single painting, as is seen in the one that the most illustrious Sigüenza had from Señor Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl, and the latter from his most noble ancestors, a copy of which we have given in our History. The same tradition was found among the Chiapanecs, the Tlaxcalans, the Michoacans, (a) the Cubans, and the Indians of Tierra Firme (b) with the expression that some men with some animals were saved from the flood in a boat, and had first set free a bird, which did not return to the boat because it gave itself to eating carrion, and then another, which returned with a green branch in its mouth; which makes it evident that they were speaking of no other flood than the one that inundated the whole earth in the time of the Patriarch Noah. All the circumstances with which this universal and most ancient tradition was found altered among some American nations either were allegories, like those of the seven caves of the Mexicans to signify the seven principal nations that peopled the country of Anahuac, or fictions of ignorance

or

(a) See what we have said in § 2 of Diss. I, as also Herrera in Dec. 3, bk. 3, ch. 10, in Dec. 4, bk. 1, ch. 11, and in other places, Torquemada, García, Boturini, etc.

(b) Herrera mentions the tradition that existed among the Indians of Tierra Firme in Decade 4, bk. 1, ch. 11. Of that which existed among the Tlaxcalans, the Chiapanecs, and the Cubans we have spoken elsewhere. [p. 70] or of ambition. None of those nations believed that men had escaped upon the mountains, but in a boat, and if by chance there was any that believed otherwise, this was without doubt because the tradition of the flood, after so many centuries, had been altered. It is therefore absolutely false that there was a unanimous tradition of a peculiar inundation of America among all those peoples who inhabited from the Magellanic land to the river St. Lawrence.

The lakes and swamps, which seem to Messrs. de Buffon and de P. indubitable traces of the alleged inundation, are undoubtedly effects of the great rivers, the innumerable springs, and the most abundant rains of America. If those lakes and swamps had been made by that ancient inundation, and not by the causes we have assigned, they would long since, after so many centuries, have been consumed and dried up by the continual evaporation caused by the heat of the Sun, especially under the torrid zone, or at least would have diminished considerably; but such a diminution is not observed, except in those lakes from which human industry has diverted the rivers and torrents that discharged into them, as in those of the Mexican valley. I have seen and observed the five principal lakes of New Spain, which are those of Texcoco, of Chalco, of Cuitzeo, of Pátzcuaro, and of Chapala, and I am sure that they were neither formed nor are preserved except by the copious waters of the rains, the rivers, and the springs. All the world knows that there are no rains more copious and more torrential, nor rivers greater, than those of America. Why then invent inundations, when we have to hand causes more natural and more certain? If lakes were an argument for an inundation, we ought to believe it to have occurred rather in the old than in the new continent; for all the lakes of America, even including those of Canada, which are the largest, are not comparable to the Black, White, Baltic, and Caspian Seas, which, though commonly called seas, are nevertheless, according to M. de Buffon, true lakes, formed by the rivers that empty into them. If to these are added the lakes of Geneva, Onega, Pskov,

and [p. 71] and many others, and very large ones, of Russia, of Tartary, and of other countries, (*) we shall soon perceive how much those who have so exaggerated the American lakes had forgotten their own continent. The lake of Chapala, which on geographical maps is seen honored with the magnificent name of Mar Chapallico, which I have seen and coasted three times, has scarcely a hundred miles of circumference. Now if the rivers Don, Volga, Dnieper, Danube, Oder, and others of the old continent, though much less large than the Marañón, the Río de la Plata, the Magdalena, the St. Lawrence, the Orinoco, the Mississippi, and others of the New World, are nevertheless amply sufficient, as M. de Buffon says, to form those lakes so great that they have always been believed to be seas, what wonder is it that the greatest rivers of America make smaller lakes and swamps? M. de P. says that “these lakes seem to be reservoirs of waters, which have not yet been able to escape from those places once flooded by a violent agitation impressed upon the whole terraqueous globe. The very numerous volcanoes of the Cordilleras, or American Alps, and of the rocks of Mexico, and the earthquakes that are incessantly felt now in one, now in another part of those Alps, show that that land is not yet at rest in our days.” But if that violent agitation was general throughout the whole terraqueous globe, why were the lands of Peru and of Mexico inundated, being, as in fact they are, and as Messrs. de Buffon and de P. confess, extremely elevated above the surface of the sea, and those of Europe not inundated, being much lower? Whoever has observed the stupendous elevation of the inland countries of America will never be able to persuade himself that the water could rise so as to cover them without inundating all of Europe likewise. For the rest, we could say in like manner that Vesuvius, Etna, Hecla, and the very many volcanoes of the Moluccas, of the Philippines, and

of

(*) M. de Bomare counts thirty-eight lakes in the cantons of the Swiss, and says that into that of Haarlem ships of the line enter. The lake of Aral in Tartary has, says the same author, a hundred leagues in length and fifty in breadth.

[p. 72]

Japan, and the frequent earthquakes of those islands, of China, of Persia, of Syria, of Turkey, etc., show that the old world is not yet at rest in our days. (c)

“The veins of the metals,” adds M. de P., “which in some places are found at the surface of the earth, seem to indicate that that soil was flooded, and that the torrents carried away the surface.” But would it not be better to say that some violent eruptions of the subterranean fires, quite evident in the very numerous volcanoes of the Cordilleras, by ruining the surface of some lands, left the veins of the metals almost uncovered?

The finding of marine bodies, heaped up in some inland places of America, if it ever proved that alleged inundation, would rather prove a greater inundation of the old world; for whereas in America there are few places in which masses of shells and of other petrified marine bodies are found, Europe on the contrary is almost wholly full of petrifications of such bodies, which demonstrate with the greatest evidence that it was once flooded by the sea. (d) Everyone knows the wonders and the calculations that several French natural philosophers have made upon that immense quantity of shells that is seen in Touraine, and no one is ignorant that such petrified marine bodies are found in the Alps also. Why then must the inundation of those countries be concluded from the marine bodies found in some places of America, and must not rather the inundation of Europe be concluded from such bodies found

in

(c) The same M. de P., after mentioning Vesuvius, Etna, Hecla, and the volcano of Lipari, says thus: “Among the great volcanoes are counted the Paranucan in the island of Java, the Canapis in the island of Banda, the Balaluan in the island of Sumatra. The island of Ternate has a flaming mountain, whose eruptions yield nothing to those of Etna. … Of all the islands and islets that compose the empire of Japan, there is not one that has not its volcano, more or less considerable, as also in the Manila islands (that is, the Philippines), in the Azores, in the islands of Cape Verde, etc.” Recherch. philosoph. sur les Américains, Letter III, on the vicissitudes of our globe.

(d) M. de Bourguet in his Traité des pétrifications, and Father Torrubia in his Aparato para la historia natural española, give us a very long catalogue of the places in Europe and in Asia where petrified marine bodies are found. [p. 73] in far greater abundance in very many places in it? If the transport of these bodies to the inland places of Europe is ascribed to the waters of the universal flood, why must it not be ascribed to the same cause in America? (*) On the other hand, if it was not the waters of the flood that carried the aforesaid marine bodies to the inland places of Europe, but those of another, later inundation: if Europe in general is, as M. de Buffon says, (e) a new country: if it is not long since it was covered with forests and swamps, why are those stupendous effects of the inundation not seen in Europe, nor were they seen two thousand years ago, that these authors see in America? Why have the animals of Europe not degenerated, as those of America have? Why are the Europeans not cold like the Americans? Why are the women of the one and of the other part of the world not, at present, or at least were they not formerly, equally infecund? Why, Europe having been flooded as America was, and more than America, and for a longer time (as is evidently deduced from the reasons of M. de Buffon), did the soil of Europe remain fertile, and that of America sterile: the sky of Europe so benign, and that of America so niggardly: why were all goods granted to Europe, and all evils sent to America? Whoever wished to inform himself better of

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                  K                        these

(*) One of the highest mountains of America is the Descabezado, that is, Beheaded, situated in the Chilean Alps more than a hundred and fifty miles distant from the sea. Its perpendicular height above the surface of the sea is, according to Abbé Molina, an erudite and diligent historiographer of that kingdom, more than three miles. Now on the summit of this mountain, so high, a great quantity of petrified marine bodies has been found, which certainly could not be carried to that stupendous height by the water of a particular inundation, different from the general one that occurred in the time of Noah. Nor can it be said that that summit, having once been the bed of the sea, was afterwards raised little by little by the subterranean fires, elevating with it likewise those marine bodies; for although this is not improbable in some places that we now see not too elevated above the surface of the sea, and indeed we believe it to have often happened, as we have said elsewhere, nevertheless at so extraordinary a height it is altogether incredible: so that those marine bodies on that summit must be considered as certain and indubitable traces of the universal flood.

(e) Vol. 1, Théorie de la Terre. [p. 74] these difficulties, let him read what M. de Buffon wrote concerning the inundation of Europe.

The last argument of M. de P. is taken from the extinction, or end, of the great quadrupeds in America, which, he says, are the first to perish in the waters. This author believes that anciently there were in America elephants, camels, hippopotamuses, and other great quadrupeds, and that all perished in the supposed inundation. But who will not wonder that the elephants and camels perished there, being so swift, and that the sloth escaped, being so slow and so unable to move? That the elephants could not take refuge on the mountains, as men took refuge, escaping by swimming, at which they are most adept, or availing themselves of the speed of their feet, which is so great that in one day they cover, as M. de Buffon affirms, as much as a hundred and fifty miles, and that the sloths had leisure to climb to the tops of the mountains, who can scarcely, according to what the aforesaid author says, make a perch in an hour? Even if we granted that such quadrupeds had once been in America, we are not therefore obliged to believe that their destruction was caused by the supposed inundation: since it could be ascribed to other causes quite different. M. de P. himself (f) affirms that if elephants were transported to America, as the Portuguese have attempted, they would meet with the same fortune as the camels in Peru, which would not propagate, though they were left in the forests to their own instinct; for the change of food and of climate is infinitely more sensible to elephants than to all the other quadrupeds of the first size. He also protests elsewhere that the destructive causes of these animals, that is, of the great quadrupeds in the New World, are very great difficulties, and at the same time among the most interesting articles of the physics of the terraqueous globe. Why then does he decide so boldly that that imaginary inundation was the cause of their ruin?

M.

(f) Recherch. philosoph., pt. I.

[p. 75]

M. de Buffon strives to persuade us of the recent inundation of America with several arguments, to which we shall reply in a few words. If this continent, he says, speaking of America, is as ancient as the other, why were so few men found there? The men who were found there cannot be called few, except in respect of the vast country that they inhabited. Those who lived in society, like the Mexicans, the Michoacans, the Acolhuas, and others, who occupied all that vast tract of land that extends from 9 to 23 degrees of latitude, and from 271 to 294 of longitude, formed peoples as numerous as those of Europe, as we shall show in another Dissertation. (g) Those who lived scattered formed small nations, or tribes; because the scant multiplication is a necessary effect of savage life in all the countries of the world. “If the savages are herdsmen,” says Montesquieu, “they have need of a great country in order to be able to subsist in a certain number. If they are hunters” (as were the savages of America) “they are still fewer in number, and form, in order to maintain themselves, a smaller nation.”

“Why,” M. de Buffon asks again, “why were they almost all savage and scattered?” It is not so. How can it be said that they were almost all savage and scattered, when we know that the Mexicans, the Peruvians, and all the peoples subject to them lived in society? who, as the same M. de Buffon confesses, were very numerous, and cannot be called new. The other nations remained savage out of too great a love of their liberty, or for some other cause that we do not know. In Asia, although it is a most ancient country, there are also

K2                           today

(g) These arguments of M. de Buffon against the antiquity of America are found in vol. 6 of his Natural History; but a little before, in the same volume, he says thus: Polished men and civilized peoples, subject to laws and governed by kings, were found in Mexico and in Peru: they had industry, arts, and a kind of religion, they dwelt in cities, in which order and government were maintained by means of the authority of the sovereign. These peoples, being moreover very numerous, cannot be called new, etc. If there is anyone who doubts of such a contradiction, let him read the aforesaid volume of M. de Buffon. [p. 76] today savage and scattered peoples. Why, he says, did those who were united in society count scarcely two or three hundred years since they had gathered together? Here is another error. The Mexicans counted scarcely two hundred years from the founding of their capital, the Tlaxcalans somewhat more from the establishment of their republic; but both these nations, and the others subject to them, and the Toltecs, the Acolhuas, and the Michoacans, lived in society from time immemorial. Neither M. de Buffon, nor M. de P., nor Dr. Robertson, nor several other European authors know how to distinguish the establishment of those nations in Anahuac from that which many centuries before they had had in the northern countries of the New World.

Why, he says again, were even those nations that lived in society ignorant of the art of handing down to posterity the memory of facts by means of durable signs, although they had found the way of communicating from afar, and of writing by knotting cords? And what were the paintings and the characters of the Mexicans, and of the other polished nations of Anahuac, if not durable signs destined, like our characters, to perpetuate the memory of facts? See what Acosta says in bk. 6, ch. 7 of his History, and what we set forth in the Dissertation on the civilization of the Mexicans.

Why, he adds, had they not tamed the animals, nor made use of any other except the Llama (h) and the Paco; which were not like our domestic animals, steady, faithful, and docile? Because there were no other animals that could be tamed. Does M. de Buffon want them to tame tigers, pumas, wolves, and other such wild beasts? M. de P. reproaches the Americans with their little industry in not having made use of reindeer, as the Laplanders do; but these quadrupeds were found only in countries too far from Mexico, and those

## savages

(h) Llama (not Lama) was, according to Father Acosta, the generic name of the four species of quadrupeds of that kind; but today it is used to signify only that which the Spaniards call Carnero, that is, Ram of Peru. The other three species are the Paco, the Guanaco, or Huannaco, and the Vicuña. The name Llama is pronounced as in Italian Gliama. [p. 77] savages, in whose lands these animals were found, did not wish to make use of them; because they had no need of them, or it did not occur to them to tame them. Moreover the proposition of M. de Buffon, taken in that generality, is without doubt false; for he himself says that the Alco, or Techichi, a quadruped resembling a little dog, and common to both Americas, was tamed by the Indians. Likewise the Mexicans had made domestic animals of rabbits, ducks, turkeys, and others.

Finally, their arts, concludes M. de Buffon, were as new as their society, their talent imperfect, their ideas not yet developed, their organs rude, and their language barbarous; but the errors contained in these words of M. de Buffon will be effectively refuted in the following Dissertations.

We must therefore reject that alleged inundation of America as one of those philosophical chimeras invented by the restless minds of our century; for among the Americans there was no memory of any other inundation than the universal one, of which the sacred books make mention. Indeed I say that if it were ever true that the flood of Noah did not flood the whole earth, no other country would have greater reason than Mexico to believe itself spared that great calamity; because, besides its extreme elevation above the surface of the sea, there is no inland country where petrified marine bodies are rarer.

§ II.

On the Climate of Mexico.

If we wished to take upon ourselves the task of refuting all the absurdities that M. de P. wrote against the climate of America, it would be necessary to write, instead of a dissertation, a large volume. Suffice it to say that he has collected all that has been said, rightly or wrongly, by several authors against various

particular [p. 78] particular countries of America, to represent to his readers a monstrous and horrible whole, without perceiving that if we, walking in his tracks, undertook to do the same with the various countries of which the old continent is composed (which would not be difficult), we should make a portrait much more abominable than his; but passing over this, as foreign to our purpose, we shall content ourselves with reasoning about the climate of Mexico.

This country, being so vast, and divided into so many provinces very different in their situation, must necessarily be subject to different climates. Some lands, like the maritime ones, are hot, and for the most part humid and unwholesome; others are, like almost all the inland ones, temperate, dry, and wholesome. These are too high, and those too low. In some regions the south wind prevails, in others the east, and in others the north. The greatest cold of all the inhabited places does not reach that of France, nor even that of Castile, nor can the greatest heat be compared with that of Africa, nor with that of the dog-days in some countries of Europe. The difference between winter and summer is so slight everywhere that even the most delicate persons wear the same clothing in August and in January. All this, and what more we have already said in the History concerning the benignity and mildness of that climate, is so well known that we have no need of testimonies, nor of other arguments, to prove it.

M. de P., to demonstrate the malignity of the American climate, alleges 1. The smallness and irregularity of the animals of America. 2.
