The greatness and enormous multiplication of insects and other such little animals. 3. The diseases of the Americans, and particularly the venereal disease. 4. The defects of their physical constitution. 5. The excess of cold in the countries of America in comparison with those of the old continent situated at an equal distance from the equator.

Now the supposed smallness, and the lesser ferocity of the American animals, of which we shall speak elsewhere, rather than the malignity of the climate, demonstrate its mildness and goodness, if we

believe [p. 79] believe M. de Buffon, at whose fountain M. de P. drank, and whose testimony he has used against Dom Pernety. M. de Buffon, who in many places of his Natural History produces the smallness of the American animals as a certain argument of the malignity of the climate of America, reasoning then of wild beasts in vol. XI, says thus: “Since all things, even the freest creatures, are subject to physical laws, and animals, like men, are subject to the influence of the Sky and of the Earth: it seems that those same causes which have civilized and softened the human species in our climates have likewise produced similar effects in the other species. The wolf, which is perhaps the most ferocious of all the quadrupeds of the temperate zone, is yet incomparably less terrible than the tiger, the lion, and the panther of the torrid zone, and than the white bear, the lynx, and the hyena of the cold zone… In America, where the air and the land are milder than those of Africa, the tiger, the lion, and the panther are terrible only in name… They have degenerated, if indeed ferocity added to cruelty made up their nature, or, to put it better, they have done nothing but suffer the influence of the climate: under a milder sky, their natural disposition has been softened… From excessive climates are taken the drugs, the perfumes, the poisons, and all those plants whose qualities are excessive. The temperate earth, on the contrary, produces only temperate things: the sweeter herbs, the more wholesome vegetables, the more delightful fruits, the more tranquil animals, and the more humane men are proper to this happy climate. Thus the earth makes the plants; the earth and the plants make the animals; the earth, the plants, and the animals make man… The physical qualities of man, and those of the animals that feed on other animals, depend, though more remotely, on those same causes, which have an influence also on their natural disposition and on their customs. The greatest proof to show that in temperate climates everything is tempered, and in excessive climates everything is excessive, and that the

## size

[p. 80]

size and form, which seem fixed and determined qualities, depend nevertheless, like relative qualities, on the influence of the climate: the size of our quadrupeds cannot be compared with that of the elephant, the rhinoceros, and the hippopotamus: the largest of our birds are very small, if they are compared with the ostrich, the condor, and the cassowary.” So far M. de Buffon, whose text I have copied, because it is very important to my purpose, and quite contrary to what M. de P. writes against the climate of America, and what M. de Buffon himself writes in many other places.

Now then, if large and ferocious animals are proper to excessive climates, and the smaller and more tranquil animals to temperate climates, as M. de Buffon establishes in this place: if the mildness of the climate influences the natural disposition and the customs of animals, M. de P. wrongly deduces the malignity of the climate of America from the lesser size and the lesser ferocity of its animals; rather he ought to deduce from this antecedent the pleasantness of that climate. If, on the contrary, the lesser size and the lesser ferocity of the American animals, in comparison with those of the old continent, are proof of their degeneration, owing to the malignity of the climate, as M. de P. would have it, we shall likewise have to argue the malignity of the climate of Europe from the lesser size and the lesser ferocity of its animals, compared with those of Africa. If some philosopher of Guinea were to undertake a work on the model of that of M. de P., with this title, Recherches philosophiques sur les Européens, he could make use of the same argument of M. de P. to demonstrate the malignity of the climate of Europe, and the advantages of that of Africa. “The climate of Europe,” he would say in the very words of M. de P., “is too contrary to the generation of quadrupeds, which are found there incomparably smaller and more cowardly than ours. What are the horse and the ox, the largest of its animals, compared with our elephants, our rhinoceroses, our hippopotamuses, our camels, and our giraffes? What are its

lizards [p. 81] lizards, whether in size or in intrepidity, with our crocodiles? The wolves and the bears, the most feared of its wild beasts, seem puppies beside our lions and our tigers. Its eagles, its vultures, and its cranes, if compared with our ostriches, will look like so many hens.” I pass over other fine things that he could say against Europe, making use of the same materials and even of the very words of M. de P., so as not to make this dissertation tedious. What Messrs. de Buffon and de P. would answer to that African philosopher, we answer to these European philosophers; for their arguments either do not prove that the climate of America is bad, or else they convince us that that of Europe is bad, or at least that the African climate is better than the European.

From the scarcity and smallness of the quadrupeds M. de P. passes to the enormous size and prodigious multiplication of insects and other noxious little animals. “The surface of the earth,” he says, “infected by putrefaction, was inundated with lizards, serpents, reptiles, and insects monstrous for their size and for the potency of their venom, which they drew from the abundant juices of this uncultivated soil, vitiated and abandoned to itself, in which the nutritive juice turned sour, like the milk in the breasts of animals that do not exercise the propagative virtue. Caterpillars, bugs, butterflies, beetles, spiders, frogs, and toads were for the most part of a gigantic corporature in their species, and had multiplied beyond what can be imagined… Panama is infested by serpents, Cartagena by clouds of enormous bats, Portobello by toads, Surinam by kakerlaks, or cockroaches, Guadeloupe and other colonies of the islands by beetles, Quito by pichi, or nigue, and Lima by lice and bedbugs. The ancient kings of Mexico and the emperors of Peru found no other way of freeing their subjects from these insects, which were eating them, than that of imposing on them the tribute of a certain quantity of lice, which they had to pay every year. Hernán Cortés found sacks full

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.           L                       of [p. 82] of them in the palace of King Moctezuma.” But this argument, full besides of falsehoods and exaggerations, proves nothing against the climate of America in general, and much less against that of Mexico. That there are some lands in America in which, because they are hot and humid, or uninhabited, large insects are found, and multiply excessively, will at most prove that in some places of it the surface of the earth is infected, as he says, with putrefaction; but not that the soil of Mexico, or that of the whole of America, is foul-smelling, uncultivated, vitiated, or abandoned to itself, as M. de P. foolishly claims. If such a consequence were sound, we should say as well that the soil of the old continent is likewise spoiled and foul-smelling; since in many countries of it there is a prodigious multitude of monstrous insects, of noxious reptiles, and of vile little animals, as in the Philippine islands, in many of those of the Indian Archipelago, in a good many countries of southern Asia, in many of Africa, and even in some of Europe. The Philippine islands are seen to be infested by certain enormous ants and by monstrous bats, Japan by scorpions, southern Asia and Africa by serpents, Egypt by asps, Guinea and Ethiopia by armies of ants, Holland by field mice, the Ukraine by toads, as M. de P. himself affirms. (i) In Italy the Campagna of Rome (after so many centuries since it was peopled) is infested by vipers, Calabria by tarantulas, the coasts of the Adriatic Sea by clouds of gnats; and even in France itself, whose population is so great and so ancient, whose lands are so well cultivated, and whose climate is so celebrated by the French, there appeared a few years ago, according to the testimony of M. de Buffon, a new species of field mice larger than the common ones, called by him Surmulots, which have multiplied excessively to the great damage of the fields. M. de Bazin, in his abridgment of the History of Insects, counts seventy-seven species of bedbugs, all of which are found in Paris and its environs. That great court, according to what

M.

(i) Défense des Recherches philosoph. sur les Américains, ch. 13.

[p. 83]

M. de Bomare says, swarms with such loathsome insects. It is true that there are places in America where the multitude of insects and of filthy little beasts makes life tiresome; but we do not know that their multiplication has ever reached such an excess that they have depopulated any place: at least there could not be produced in the new continent as many examples of such depopulation as in the old continent, to which Theophrastus, Varro, Pliny, (k) and other authors bear witness. Frogs depopulated one place in Gaul, and locusts another in Africa; the island of Gyaros, one of the Cyclades, was left depopulated by mice; Amyclae, near the place of Terracina, by serpents; another place near Ethiopia by scorpions and venomous ants, and another by scolopendras; and closer to our own times, the island of Mauritius was on the point of being abandoned by its inhabitants on account of the extraordinary multiplication of rats, as far as I remember having read in a French author.

As for the size of insects, reptiles, and animals of this kind, M. de P. avails himself of the testimony of M. Dumont, who in his Memoirs on Louisiana says that there are frogs so large that they weigh thirty-seven French pounds, and whose horrible clamor imitates the lowing of cows. But who can trust that author, especially knowing what M. de P. himself says (in his reply to Dom Pernety, ch. 17), that all those who have written about Louisiana, from Hennepin, Le Clercq, and the Chevalier Tonti down to Dumont, have contradicted one another, now on this point, now on that article? For my part, I am amazed that M. de P. has had the boldness to write that such monsters do not exist in the rest of the world. I know very well that frogs of thirty-seven pounds exist neither in the old continent nor, for that matter, in the new; but there do exist in Asia and in Africa serpents, bats, ants, and other similar animals of such stupendous size that they far surpass all those that have been discovered in the New World. In

L2                     what

(k) Pliny, Natural History, bk. 8, ch. 29. [p. 84] what place of America has there ever been seen a serpent of fifty Roman cubits, like the one that Augustus showed to the Roman people in the spectacles, as Suetonius affirms, (l) or one as thick as the one that was killed in the Vatican in the time of the Emperor Claudius, concerning which Pliny, an author almost contemporary, testifies that an entire child was found in its belly? But above all, where has there ever been seen, even in the most solitary forests of America, a serpent that can in any way be compared with that most enormous and prodigious one of a hundred and twenty feet, seen in Africa at the time of the first Punic War, and killed with war machines by the army of Atilius Regulus, whose skin and jaws were preserved in a temple at Rome until the Numantine War, as Livy, Pliny, and other Roman historians testify? I know well that some historian of America says that in certain forests there is found a certain gigantic species of serpents which with their breath draw men to them and swallow them; but I know also that several historians, ancient as well as modern, relate the same of the serpents of Asia, and something more besides. Megasthenes, cited by Pliny, said that in Asia there were found serpents so large that they swallowed whole stags and bulls. (m) Metrodorus, cited by the same author, affirms that in Pontus there were serpents that with their breath drew down birds, however high and swift their flight. Among the moderns, Gemelli, in vol. 5 of his Giro del mondo, where he discourses of the animals of the Philippine islands, says as follows: “There are serpents in these islands of immense size. There is one called Ibitin, very long, which, hanging itself by the tail from the trunk of a tree

## waits

(l) In Octavian Caesar.

(m) “Megasthenes writes that in India serpents grow to such a size that they swallow whole stags and bulls. Metrodorus, that around the river Rhyndacus in Pontus they swallow birds flying over them, however high and swiftly, drawn down by their breath. Well known is the serpent of a hundred and twenty feet in length which, in the Punic wars, at the river Bagradas, was brought down by the commander Regulus with ballistae and engines, as if it were a town taken by storm. Its skin and jaws lasted at Rome in a temple until the Numantine war. Lending credence to these are the snakes called boas in Italy, which grow to such a size that, when one was killed in the Vatican in the principate of the deified Claudius, an infant was seen entire in its belly.” Pliny, Natural History, bk. 8, ch. 14. [p. 85] from a tree, waits for stags, wild boars, and even men to pass by, in order to draw them to itself violently with its breath and devour them whole, etc.” Whence it is seen that this most ancient fable has been common to the one continent and the other. (n)

M. de P. will perhaps try to get out of it by saying that those monstrous animals were seen in the old continent at a time when its climate had not yet been perfected. But who will there be who, comparing what the ancients wrote with what we know at present of Asia and Africa, does not see that the climate of those countries is for the most part today such as it was two thousand years ago: the same heat, the same dryness or humidity, the same kind of plants, animals, and men, etc.? Besides, even in our own times there are seen in those regions various sorts of monstrous animals that greatly surpass their analogues in the new continent. In what country of America will M. de P. be able to find ants that can be compared with those called Sulum in the Philippine islands, of which Dr. Hernández affirms (o) that they are six fingers long and one broad? Who has ever seen in America bats as large as those of the islands of Bourbon, of Ternate, of the Philippines, and of the whole Indian Archipelago? The largest bat of America (peculiar to certain hot and shady lands), which is the one that M. de Buffon calls the Vampire, is, as the same author says, of the size of a pigeon; the Rougette (one of the species of Asia) is as large as a crow, and the Roussette (another species of Asia) is as big as a large hen. (p) Its wings spread out measure from one tip to the other three Paris feet, and according to Gemelli, who measured it (q) in the Philippine islands, six palms. M. de Buffon admits the excess in size

of

(n) See what M. de Bomare reports of the Minia of Africa and of the Rimberah of Ceylon.

(o) Hernández, Hist. Insector. N. Hisp., ch. 30.

(p) Buffon, Natural History, vol. 19.

(q) Gemelli, vol. 5. [p. 86] of the Asiatic bats as compared with the American; but he denies it in number. Gemelli, an eyewitness, says that those of the island of Luzon were so many that they covered the air, and that the noise they made with their teeth in eating the fruits of the forests was heard at a distance of two miles. (r) M. de P. himself, speaking of serpents, (s) says that it cannot be affirmed that serpents larger than those which M. Adanson saw in the deserts of Africa have been found in the New World. The largest serpent found in Mexico after the most diligent researches made by Dr. Hernández was eighteen feet long; but this is not to be compared either with that of the Moluccas, of which M. de Bomare says that it is thirty-two feet in length, (t) or with the Anacandaja of Ceylon, which, according to what the same author says, is more than thirty-three feet, (u) or with others of Asia and Africa mentioned by the same author. Finally, the argument drawn from the multitude and size of the American insects is almost as ineffectual as the other, drawn from the smallness and scarcity of the quadrupeds, and in the one as in the other the same ignorance is shown, or else the same voluntary forgetfulness, of the things of the old continent.

As for what M. de P. says about the tribute of lice in Mexico, he reveals in this, as in many other things, his bad faith. It is true that Cortés found sacks of lice in the storehouses of the palace of King Axayacatl. It is likewise true that Moctezuma imposed such a tribute; not on all, but only on beggars, and not because the extraordinary multitude of such insects was devouring them, as M. de P. affirms; but because Moctezuma, who could not endure idleness in his subjects, wished even that miserable class of people, who could not work, to occupy themselves at least

in

(r) What Gemelli says of the stupendous noise of the bats of the island of Luzon is confirmed to me by several persons worthy of belief who were some years in that island.

(s) Défense des Recherch. philosoph., ch. 22.

(t) Bomare, Dictionn. univ. d’Histoire natur., s.v. Couleuvre.

(u) Ibid., s.v. Anacandaja. [p. 87] in delousing themselves. (x) This was the true reason for so extravagant a tribute, as Torquemada, Vetancurt, and other historians affirm, and no one until now has ever thought of what M. de P. affirms solely because it suits his preposterous system. For the rest, those loathsome insects abound as much in the hair and the garments of the American beggars as among the miserable and unclean people of any country in the world, and there is no doubt that if some sovereign of Europe were to exact such a tribute from the poor of his state, he could fill with it not merely sacks but even ships.

Finally, reserving for another dissertation the examination of the proofs of the bad climate of America founded on diseases and on the defects of the physical constitution of the Americans, in which we shall demonstrate the errors and childish prejudices of M. de P., let us now see what he says on the excess of cold in the countries of the New World as compared with those of the old, situated at an equal distance from the equator. “Comparing,” he says, “the experiments made with thermometers in Peru by Messrs. de la Condamine and Juan de Ulloa (his name is not Juan, but Antonio) with those of the indefatigable M. Adanson in Senegal, one can easily understand that the air is less warm in the New World than in the old. Calculating with the greatest possible exactness the difference of temperature, I believe that there will be twelve degrees of latitude; that is, it is as hot in Africa at thirty degrees from the equator as at eighteen degrees from the same line in America. The liquor has not risen to so great a height in the thermometer in Peru, in the center of the torrid zone, as it has risen in France in the greatest heat of summer. Quebec, although it is almost at the same polar altitude as Paris, has nevertheless a climate incomparably harsher and colder

than

(x) It is certain that Moctezuma was as much inclined to cleanliness as he was an enemy of idleness; whence it is to be believed that he was induced by both motives to impose that extraordinary tribute. [p. 88] than Paris. The difference is likewise perceptible in Hudson’s Bay and in the Thames, which have the same latitude.”

Even if we conceded all this to M. de P., it would avail him nothing to demonstrate the malignity of the American climate. For if from the excess of cold in the American lands one wishes to deduce its bad climate, should one not rather deduce the bad climate of the old continent from the excess of heat in the countries situated at an equal distance from the equator? M. de P. will not be able to form in this matter any argument against America that the Americans cannot effectively turn against Europe, or against Africa. But to tell the truth, all the observations that have been made are not sufficient to establish, as a general principle, that the countries of the New World are colder than those of the old world situated in the same latitude, and much less to believe, as M. de P. believes, that it is as hot in the old continent at thirty degrees of polar altitude as in the new continent at eighteen degrees. If that were true, the cold would be as intense in America at 67 degrees of latitude as in the old continent at 80 degrees. Now M. de Pauw says (*) that the cold in the old continent must become in November, beyond the eightieth degree, so deadly to men that no one could live there: therefore neither could one live in America beyond the sixty-seventh degree. How then does he himself affirm in the same place that in the countries of the Eskimos inhabitants are found beyond the seventy-fifth degree? And if the feeble Americans can subsist in that latitude, we must believe that the very strong Europeans would be capable of withstanding the cold of the eightieth degree. Moreover, if that principle were true, it would be as hot in Jerusalem, situated at a little less than 32 degrees, as in Veracruz, situated at a little less than 20 degrees: which no one other than M. de P. is capable of thinking. Similarly one could deduce other equally preposterous consequences

## especially

(*) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 3, sect. 1, p. 304 (in my copy). [p. 89] especially if one were to adopt the calculation of Dr. Mitchell, who, according to what Dr. Robertson says, concluded after thirty years of observations that the difference between the climate of the New World and that of the old is from 14 to 15 degrees; that is, that it is as hot in the countries of the old continent that lie at 29 or 30 degrees as in the countries of the new continent that lie at 15 degrees. It is certain that, just as there are many countries in America colder than others of the old continent equally distant from the equator, so too there are many others that are warmer. Agra, capital of the Mogul, and the port of Loreto in California are found at almost the same latitude, and yet the heat of that Asiatic city is not comparable with that of this American port. Hue, capital of Cochin China, and Acapulco are almost equally distant from the equator, and yet the air of Hue is cool in comparison with that of Acapulco. Far more false and improbable is that other proposition of M. de P., namely, that in the center of the torrid zone the liquor of the thermometer does not rise to so great a height as it rises in Paris in the greatest heat of summer. If that were true, the difference between the American climate and the European would not be of twelve degrees only, as M. de P. will have it, but rather of 49, that is, the difference of latitude between the center of the torrid zone and Paris. It is true that, according to the observations made at Quito and compared with those that were made in Paris, the heat of that equinoctial city never reaches that of Paris in summer; but it is equally certain, according to the observations made by the same Academicians with the same thermometers in the city of Cartagena, which is not at the center of the torrid zone but is a full 10 degrees distant from the equator, that the ordinary heat of this city is equal to the greatest heat of Paris, as Don Antonio de Ulloa, one of those observers, attests. (y)

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.               M                       There

(y) In the year 1735 the liquor of M. de Réaumur’s thermometer was kept regularly at Cartagena at 1025½, without any other variation, sometimes, than that of falling to 1024 or rising to 1026. In Paris that same year it did not rise [p. 90] There are many causes which, besides that of nearness or distance from the equator, make the heat or the cold of a country. The elevation of the ground, the nearness of some high mountain covered with snow, the abundance of rains, etc., contribute much to the coldness of the atmosphere, and on the contrary the lowness of the ground, the scarcity of water, sands, etc., increase the heat. Ciudad Real, capital of the diocese of Chiapas, because it is situated in a high place, is cold, and the city of Chiapa of the Indians, not far from it, is very hot, because it is situated in a low place. Chalchicomula, a large town situated at the foot of the very lofty mountain of Orizaba, is cold, and Veracruz, placed in the same latitude, is very hot; and what is still more, though the air of Ciudad Real, in the latitude of 16½ degrees, is cold, that of Loreto in California, in the latitude of 25½ degrees, is very hot.

The very observations alleged by M. de P. prove that the climate of America is not so variable as that of Europe, and that the inhabitants of the New World are not, like those of the greater part of Europe, compelled to pass from the extreme of an excessive cold to that of an intolerable heat. The more uniform the climate, the more easily men grow accustomed to it, and avoid those pernicious effects which the succession of the seasons causes. In Quito the liquor in the thermometer does not rise as high as in Paris in summer, but neither does it fall as low as in the more temperate countries of Europe in winter. What can be more desirable in a climate than such a temperateness in the air, equally distant from the one extreme and the other, as is that of Quito and of the greater part of Mexico? What climate is sweeter and more agreeable to life than that in which one enjoys all the year the delights of the countryside, and the earth is seen always adorned with grasses and with flowers, the fields are covered with grain, and the trees laden with fruit; the herds and the flocks

sparing

to more than 1025½ in the greatest heat of July and August. Ulloa, A Voyage to South America, pt. 1, vol. 1. [p. 91] sparing labor to man; they need not his providence to maintain themselves, nor his roof to withstand the inclemency of the season; neither does snow or frost force man to stay by the fire, nor does the burning heat of summer drive him from inhabited places; but, experiencing nature ever kind toward him, he enjoys indifferently in all seasons either the company of men in the city or the innocent pleasures of the countryside? This is the idea that men have of a mild climate, and therefore the poets, wishing to exalt certain countries with praises in their verses, used to say that a perpetual spring reigned there, as Virgil said of his Italy, (z) and Horace of the Fortunate Isles, (A) where he invited his compatriots. Thus the ancients represented the Elysian Fields, and even in the holy books, to give us some idea of the felicity of the heavenly Jerusalem, it is said that there is neither cold nor heat there.

Father Acosta, whose History M. de P. calls an excellent work, who was acquainted with the climates of both continents, and was otherwise neither partial to America nor had any interest in aggrandizing it, speaking of the climate of America talks thus: “When I behold the sweetness of the air and the pleasantness of the climate of many countries of America, where one does not know what winter is that pinches, nor summer that distresses with heat; where with a mat they defend themselves against any inclemency of the weather; where there is hardly need to change one’s clothing in the whole year: considering, I say, all this, it has seemed to me often, and seems to me even today, that if men were willing to rid themselves of the snares that avarice lays for them, and to leave aside certain useless and tiresome pretensions, they might lead in America a very tranquil and pleasant life; for what those poets sang of the Elysian Fields and of famous Tempe, and what Plato related, or feigned, of that island of his Atlantis, all this men would find in the aforesaid

M2                          lands,

(z) “Here is perpetual spring, and summer in months not her own; twice the flocks are with young, twice the tree yields its fruit.” Virgil, Georgics, 2.

(A) “Where Jupiter grants a long spring and mild winters.” Horace, bk. 2, ode 4. [p. 92] lands, etc.” (B) Other historians say the same as Acosta of America, and particularly of Mexico and the neighboring provinces, whose inland countries, from nearly the isthmus of Panama up to the fortieth degree of latitude (since those that lie beyond that degree have not yet been discovered), enjoy a mild air and a climate favorable to life, except a few places which either because of their lowness are hot and humid, or because of their great height are of a harsh climate. But how many places in the old world are not harsh or noxious?

## § III

On the Quality of the Land of Mexico.

“It is certain,” says M. de Pauw, “that America in general has been, and even today is, a country far too sterile”; but it is rather certain that this in general is a great falsehood, and if M. de P. wishes to make sure of it, he can inform himself from many Germans lately come from America, where they stayed some years, who are at present in Austria, in Bohemia, in the Palatinate of the Rhine, and even in Prussia itself; or else let him reread the excellent work of Father Acosta, and he will find there in bk. 2, ch. 14, that if there is any land in the world to which the name of Paradise is suited, it is that of America. This is what a learned, judicious, and impartial European says, born in Spain, one of the best countries of Europe, and, speaking in bk. 3 of the countries of the Mexican empire, he says that New Spain is the best country of all those that the sun encircles. Surely Father Acosta would not speak thus of America in general, and of New Spain in particular, under whose name he comprehends the whole continent of Spanish North America, if America were in general a sterile country. Very many other Europeans speak no differently of America, and above all of Mexico, whose testimonies I omit so as not to

weary

(B) Natural and Moral History, bk. 2, ch. 14. [p. 93] weary the readers. (*) For the same reason we shall likewise leave aside what M. de P. writes against other countries of the New World; for it would be impossible to examine the reasons he alleges against each of them without making a large volume, and we shall content ourselves with what pertains to the land of Mexico.

Messrs. de Buffon and de P. have persuaded themselves that the whole soil of America comes down to inaccessible mountains, impenetrable forests, and flooded and marshy plains. These philosophers read in the descriptions of America that the famous Andes, or American Alps, formed two very long chains of very lofty mountains, covered for the most part with snow; that the vast desert of the Amazons is composed of dense forests; that Guayaquil and some other place is humid and marshy; and this sufficed them to say that in America there is nothing but mountains, forests, and swamps. M. de P. read in the History of Gumilla what this author says concerning the method the Indians of the Orinoco had of preparing that terrible poison of their arrows, and in the History of Herrera, or of other authors, that the Cannibals and other barbarous nations used poisoned arrows; and this sufficed him to affirm that the new continent produces a greater number of poisonous herbs than the rest of the world. He read that in lands too hot wheat does not grow, nor even the fruits of Europe, and this sufficed him to say that peaches and apricots have borne fruit only in the island of Juan Fernández (C), and that wheat

and

(*) Thomas Gage, oracle of the English and of the French in all that concerns America, speaking of Mexico says thus: “There is nothing in Mexico and about it wanting which may make a city happy; and certainly had those that have so much extolled with their pens the parts of Granada in Spain, Lombardy and Florence in Italy, making them the earthly paradise, had they been acquainted with the New World and with Mexico, they would have recanted their untruths.” Pt. 1, ch. 22. Thus speaks of Mexico this author, who knew how to speak well of no one.

(C) So that it may be seen how far M. de P. has departed from the truth, it is necessary to know that in the miserable island of Juan Fernández, where he says that peaches grow so well, very few and poor ones grow, as I have heard from Abbé Don José García, a Valencian, who stayed there six months [p. 94] and barley have not taken root, except in some districts of the north. Such is the Logic employed by M. de P. throughout his work, as we shall show on occasion in these dissertations.

But nothing is true with regard to Mexico of all that he says against the land of America. There are certainly in Mexico very lofty mountains, eternally covered with snow; there are great forests, and there are also some marshy places; but incomparably greater is the fertile and cultivated land, as is known to all who have been in those countries. In all that immense expanse of land where wheat, barley, maize, and other species of grain and legumes are now sown, in which that kingdom abounds infinitely, there used to be sown maize, pepper, beans, cacao, chia, cotton, and such plants, which served for the sustenance, clothing, and delights of those peoples; and these being as numerous as we have indicated in the History, and shall demonstrate elsewhere, the land could never have provided for their needs if there had been nothing but mountains, forests, and swamps. M. de Buffon, who in his vol. 1 says that America is nothing but a continuous swamp, and in vol. 5 affirms that the inaccessible mountains of America scarcely leave small spaces for agriculture and for the habitation of men, in the same vol. 5 confesses that the peoples of Mexico and Peru were very numerous. But if these peoples, who occupied a very great part of America, were very numerous, and lived, as he says, in society and under the direction of laws, America is certainly not a continuous swamp; if these so numerous peoples sustained themselves, as is certain, on the grain and fruits that they cultivated, the spaces are not small that

the

and was there in the season of the fruit. On the contrary, in almost all the temperate and cold countries of Spanish America, where he believes that peaches do not bear fruit, they grow very well, and in many countries, as in those of the kingdom of Chile and in some of New Spain, much better than in Europe. [p. 95] the inaccessible mountains leave to agriculture and to the habitation of men.

The multitude, the variety, and the goodness of the plants of Mexico leave no doubt of the prodigious fertility of those lands. In pastures, says Father Acosta, New Spain is excellent, and therefore an innumerable multitude of horses, cows, sheep, and other beasts is raised there. It is also very abundant in fruit as well as in every sort of grain. In fact there is no grain, legume, vegetable, or fruit that does not do well in that happy land. Wheat, which M. de P. scarcely concedes to some districts of the north of America, does not for the most part grow in the too hot lands of New Spain, as neither in the greater part of Africa and in many other countries of the old continent; but in the cold and temperate lands of that kingdom it grows excellent, and more abundant than in Europe. Suffice it to say that what is harvested in the diocese of Puebla is so much that, from what remained over after all its innumerable inhabitants had been supplied, provision was made for the islands of the Antilles and for the fleet of vessels that used to be at Havana under the name of Armada de Barlovento. In Europe there is no more than one sowing and one harvest; in New Spain there are several. In those lands, says Torquemada, a European author, who stayed many years in that kingdom and traveled all over it, in those lands in which wheat is cultivated, one sees in every season of the year wheat that is being cut, other that is ripening, other that is still greener, and other that is being sown; and now, in the month of November, we see this verified in the temporal wheat that is being cut, and in that of irrigation (D) that is growing at Atlixco and in other places, and in that which is being sown: which shows the marvelous fertility of the

land

(D) The wheat called de riego is sown in October, November, or December, and is harvested in May or June. The Temporal is sown in June and cut in October, and the wheat called Aventurero is sown in November, and is harvested sooner or later. [p. 96] land. (E) The same author makes mention of several lands that yielded now sixty, now eighty, now a hundred for one, and in our own days there has been seen so great a multiplication of wheat in some fields of those countries, (F) which, speaking in general, is certainly greater than that of Europe with far less cultivation, as is notorious to Europeans expert in agriculture who have been in that part of America. What we say of wheat we can say also of barley, although this is not sown except in proportion to the consumption that is made of it in the sustenance of horses and stable mules and of hogs. Much more could be said of maize, as being the grain most proper to America.

M. de P. claims that all the plants of Europe have degenerated in America, except the aquatic and juicy ones, and to prove so preposterous a claim he says that peaches and apricots have borne fruit only in the island of Juan Fernández. Even if we conceded to him that in no country of America do those fruits grow, it would avail him nothing to prove what he wishes; but this particular proposition is as false as the universal one. Father Acosta, reasoning of such fruits in particular, says thus: Peaches, quinces, and apricots also do well (in America), though more in New Spain. (G) In all New Spain,

except

(E) Torquemada, bk. 1 of the Monarquía indiana, ch. 4. See also what this author says of the variety and abundance of fruit that are had there in all seasons, and Herrera in many places of his History.

(F) I have been in a country in which the land used to yield fifty for one, and I have learned of another that sometimes yielded up to a hundred. In Sinaloa, although it is a very hot country, the land is wont to yield two hundred for one, according to what a trustworthy and most credible person who stayed there some years informed me. My learned friend Abbé Don Juan Ignacio Molina says in his compendious history of the kingdom of Chile, which he published a few years ago at Bologna, that in those countries wheat is wont to yield 150 for one. So great is the abundance of this grain that the fanega is sold there at five paoli, and every year about 30 vessels go to Peru laden with it, and still there is a surplus.

(G) Acosta, bk. 4, ch. 31. So great is the abundance of peaches in New Spain that they are ordinarily sold by the score, and two, three, and even [p. 97] except in the too hot lands, those fruits and all the others, transplanted from Europe, have taken root very well, and come in great abundance, as all the Europeans who have been in those countries attest. (H) Finally, says Acosta, speaking of America in general, almost all the good that is produced in Spain is there, in part better, in part not: wheat, barley, salads, vegetables, legumes, etc. (I) If he had spoken only of New Spain, he would have omitted that “almost.”

There is also another advantage, says Acosta, namely, that the things of Europe do better in America than those of America in Europe. And will such an advantage seem small to M. de P.? This alone would suffice to show that if there is any excess, it is on the side of America. In New Spain there grow very well, as many European authors bear witness, and as all those who have been there know, wheat, barley, rice, and all the other grains of Europe; chickpeas, peas, broad beans, and all other legumes; lettuces, cabbages, turnips, asparagus, and other salads and roots, and every sort of vegetable; peaches, apples, pears, quinces, and the other fruits; carnations, roses, violets, jasmines, basil, mint, marjoram, balm, and other flowers and fragrant plants transplanted from Europe; but in Europe the American plants for the most part do not take root, nor can they. Maize grows in the lands of Europe, but smaller, and much less good than that of America. Of the many delicious fruits of the New World some, such as the Musa, and

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                 N                           the four scores for the smallest coin there is there. In the kingdom of Chile they count up to twelve species of peaches, and there are some so large that some weigh a Spanish pound, or 16 ounces. Molina, History of Chile. See also what Father la Feuillée says of their most delicate taste.

(H) Pears too are sold by the score in Mexico; and there are more than fifty species of them. The Italian Gemelli testifies that the Carmelites of San Ángelo, a village seven miles distant from Mexico, drew every year 6500 sequins from the European fruits of their garden, and that the Dominicans got 3,000 sequins from the vegetables of their small garden of San Jacinto, in a suburb of Mexico. Gemelli, Giro del mondo, vol. 6, bk. 1, ch. 8.

(I) Acosta, bk. 4, ch. 31. [p. 98] the pineapple have succeeded in the gardens of the princes of Europe by means of hothouses and of great care and diligence; but neither so well ripened, nor in the abundance of their own country; others, moreover, more prized than these, such as the chirimoya, the mamey, and the chicozapote, I do not know to have been able as yet to take root in spite of European industry. The cause of this great difference between America and Europe is that which the aforesaid Acosta indicates, namely, because in America there is a greater variety of temperaments than in Europe: whence it is easier to give each plant the temperament that suits it. Now just as it is no argument of the sterility of Europe that the plants proper to America do not take root in it, so neither is it an argument of the sterility of some countries of America that some plants of Europe do not take root in them; for not every land bears everything. Here crops, there grapes come up more happily; nay, those hot countries in which wheat does not grow, nor do the European fruits succeed, are nevertheless the most pleasant and the most fertile, as men experienced in those countries well know.

For my part, I do not doubt that if the comparison is made of America with the whole of the old continent, they will be found almost equal in their productions; since in Asia and in Africa there are lands and climates proportioned to all the plants of America, which on account of the diversity of climate cannot succeed in Europe. But what use can the abundance of Asia at so great a distance ever be to the European gentlemen? On the contrary, the Mexicans, surrounded by countries of every sort of climate, enjoy all their different fruits. The marketplace of Mexico (like those of very many other cities of America) is the center of all the gifts of Nature. Here are found apples, peaches, apricots, pears, grapes, sour cherries, cherries, camotes, xicamas, walnuts, and other innumerable fruits, roots, and savory herbs, which the cold and temperate lands yield; the pineapple, bananas, coconuts, anonas, chirimoyas, mameys, chicozapotes, black zapotes, and very many others, which the hot lands bear; melons, cucumbers, oranges, pomegranates, ahuacates, the

white [p. 99] white zapotes, and others that grow indifferently in the hot countries and in the cold. In all seasons of the year that market is seen abundantly supplied with various excellent fruits, even at the time when the Europeans make do with their chestnuts, or at most with the apples and grapes that their industry preserves. All year round, even in the rigor of winter, there enter that marketplace by one of the innumerable canals boats laden with such a variety of fruits, of flowers, and of vegetables that it seems that all the seasons of the year have come at once, the most prized plants of Europe concurring in that place with the native vegetables of that country: which all those Europeans can attest who have had the pleasure of seeing it.

No less is the abundance of that land in medicinal plants. It suffices for this to see the work of the celebrated naturalist Hernández, in which are described and drawn more than nine hundred plants (produced for the most part in the neighborhood of Mexico) whose virtue has been known by experience, besides another three hundred the use of which is not indicated; and there is no doubt that very many, indeed innumerable, are lacking. M. de P., on the contrary, says that America produces a greater number of poisonous plants than all the rest of the world. But what does he know of the plants that grow in the inland countries of Africa and Asia, that he can make such a comparison? The fertility of the American soil being so great, it would be no wonder if there were an abundance of everything. But to tell the truth, I do not know that up to now there has been discovered in New Spain even a twentieth part of those poisonous plants born in the old continent, of which the naturalists and physicians of Europe often make mention in their books.

As for gums, resins, oils, and other juices that the trees send forth, either spontaneously or aided by human industry, New Spain is excellent, according to Acosta. In fact there are whole forests of acacia, which yields the true gum arabic, which because of its abundance is not prized there. There is besides balsam, incense, copal of many

N2                  species, [p. 100] species, of liquidambar, of tecamaca, of fir oil, and many other juices prized for their most delicate fragrance or for their medicinal virtue.

Even those very forests with which, according to Messrs. de Buffon and de P., the land of America is covered, demonstrate its fertility. There have always been, and there still are, great forests in those vast regions; but they are not so many that one cannot make a journey of five or six hundred miles without finding even one. And what forests! For the most part they are either of fruit trees, such as bananas, mamei, chicozapoti, apples, oranges, and lemons, like those of Coatzacoalcos, the Mixteca, and Michoacán, or of trees valued for their wood or for their resins, such as those that separate the Valley of Mexico from the diocese of Puebla, and those of Chiapas, of the Zapotecs, etc. Besides pines, oaks, ash trees, walnuts, firs, and very many others common to both continents, there are, in much greater number, the trees proper to that land and more precious. Of cedar there are, as we have said elsewhere, entire forests. The Conqueror Cortés was accused by his rivals before the Emperor Charles V of having put into the palace that he had built in Mexico seven thousand beams of cedar, and he excused himself by saying that it was a common wood in that country. Indeed it is so common that of this precious wood they make the pilings for the foundations of the houses in the marshy soil of the capital. Of the famous and justly celebrated ebony there are likewise forests in Chiapas, in Yucatán, and in Cozumel, of brazilwood in the hot lands, and of the fragrant aloe wood in the Mixteca. The Tapincerán, the granadillo or red ebony, the camote, and the others that we mentioned in the History furnish woods far better than those used in Europe. Finally, not to dwell on a long and tedious enumeration, I refer the reader to Father Acosta, to Dr. Hernández, to Ximénez, and to other European authors who have been in New Spain, although all that they say is not enough to give a complete idea of the fertility of that land. Father Acosta affirms that both

in [p. 101] in number and in variety of the trees produced by nature itself, there is a greater abundance in America than in Asia, in Africa, and in Europe. (K)

Here is a new argument suited to convince us of the advantages of the land of America over that of Europe. The nature and quality of a soil are better revealed by the plants that it produces of itself, without the aid of art. Let us compare, then, the native productions of Europe with those not of all America, but only of New Spain. “The reason,” says Montesquieu, (L) “why there are so many savages in America is that the land there produces of itself many fruits on which they can feed… I believe that these advantages could not be had in Europe if the land were left without cultivation: it would produce nothing but forests of oaks and other useless trees.” “Examining,” says M. de P., “the history and origin of our legumes, our vegetables, our fruit trees, and even our grains, one learns that all are foreign, and that they were transplanted from another climate into ours. It is easy to understand how great must have been the misery of the ancient Gauls, and also that of the Germans, in whose land no fruit tree was produced even in the time of Tacitus… If Germany had to return the foreign plants that do not belong originally to its soil or its climate, almost nothing would remain to it, nor would it keep among the seeds that serve for sustenance anything but the wild poppy and the wild oat.” (M) What M. de P. frankly confesses of the Gauls and of Germany might also be said of the other countries of Europe, and even of Greece and of Italy, which supplied the other countries. If Italy were obliged to return all those fruits that do not belong originally to her soil, what would remain to her, acorns

excepted?

(K) Acosta, bk. 4, ch. 30.

(L) Montesquieu, The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 18, ch. 9.

(M) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1. [p. 102] excepted? Those names, Malum persicum, Malum medicum, Malum assyrium, Malum punicum, Malum cidonium, Malum armeniacum, Nux pontica, etc., serve to remind her that such fruits came to her from Asia and from Africa. “It is known,” says M. Büsching, (N) “that the finest and best fruits of trees passed from Italy into those countries where they are now produced. Italy received them from Greece, from Asia, and from Africa. The apple came to her from Syria, from Egypt, from Greece, etc.; the apricot from Epirus, the pear from Alexandria, from Syria, from Numidia, and from Greece, the lemon and the orange from Media, Assyria, and Persia, the fig from Asia, the pomegranate from Carthage, the chestnut from Castania in Magnesia, a province of Macedonia, the cherry from Cerasus in Pontus, the almond from Asia to Greece and thence to Italy, the walnut from Persia, the hazelnut from Pontus, the olive from Cyprus, the plum from Armenia and from Syria, the peach from Persia, the quince from Cydonia in Crete to Greece, and thence to Italy.”

Pliny says that men at first sustained themselves on nothing but acorns. (O) This, although a false report as regards men in general, appears certain as regards the first settlers of Italy; at least such was the opinion of the ancients, as is evident from their writings. Pliny adds that even in his own time many peoples, for want of grain, considered themselves rich in proportion to the quantity of acorns they had, from whose flour they made bread, just as today in Norway they make it of pine bark, and in other northern countries of Europe of fish bones: which is without doubt the greatest misery. M. de Bomare protests that all the beauties of European gardens are foreign, (P) and that the finest flowers they have came from the Levant. (Q) The same M. de P. makes a more general confession of the ancient misery of the Europeans, where he affirms

that

(N) Büsching, Geograf., vol. 1.

(O) Pliny, Hist. nat., bk. 2, ch. 56.

(P) Bomare, Dictionn. univ. d’hist. natur., s.v. Plante.

(Q) Ibid., s.v. Fleur. [p. 103] that the useful plants they now have passed from southern Asia into Egypt, from Egypt to Greece, from Greece into Italy, from Italy into the Gauls, and from the Gauls into Germany, (R) so that the soil of Europe, as regards its own and original productions, is one of the poorest and most sterile in the world. On the contrary, how fertile and abundant is not the American soil, and above all that of Mexico, in plants of its own, useful for sustenance, for clothing, and for other uses of life! Let the works of the European authors who have written on the natural history of that New World be consulted.

Here, then, is the answer that the Americans might give to that ridiculous comparison which the chronicler Herrera makes in his first Decade, of which we made mention at the beginning of this dissertation. “In America,” he says, “there were not, as in Europe, lemons, nor oranges, nor pomegranates, nor figs, nor quinces, nor melons, nor grapes, nor olives, nor sugar, nor rice, nor wheat.” The Americans will say, then: 1. that not even in Europe was there any of these fruits until they were transplanted there from Asia and from Africa; 2. that today they are in America as in Europe, and for the most part are better there and in greater abundance, especially oranges, lemons, melons, and sugar canes; 3. that if America had no wheat, neither had Europe maize, which is no less useful nor less wholesome; if America had no pomegranates, lemons, etc., at least today it has them; but Europe has not had, has not, and cannot have chirimoye, ahuacati, bananas, chicozapoti, etc.

Finally, Messrs. de Buffon and de P. and other European philosophers and historians, who make such an outcry over the sterility, the forests, the swamps, and the deserts of America, should be reminded, if you please, that the wretched countries of Lapland, Norway, Iceland, Novaya Zemlya, and Spitsbergen, and the vast and dreadful deserts of Siberia, Tartary, Arabia, Africa, and others are likewise countries of the old

continent,

(R) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1. [p. 104] continent, and they make up at least a fourth part of its extent. But what countries! Let one see the description that Europeans themselves give of them. Let one see at least the eloquent description that M. de Buffon makes of the deserts of Arabia. “A country,” he says, “without verdure and without water: a sun forever burning, a sky forever dry, sandy plains, mountains still more arid, over which the sight extends as far as it can without meeting any living object: a land, so to speak, dead and flayed by the winds, which presents nothing but bones, scattered stones, and cliffs upraised or overturned: a wholly bare desert, in which the traveler never breathes beneath the shade, where nothing keeps him company, and there is nothing to remind him of living nature: an absolute solitude far more frightful than that of the forests; for at least the trees are living creatures that bring some relief to man, who finds himself alone, isolated, more naked and more lost in these empty and boundless places. All the ground that presents itself to him he sees as his sepulchre: the light of day, more melancholy than the shadows of night, is born again only to show him his nakedness and his impotence, and to set before his eyes his horrible situation, pushing back from his sight the limits of the void, and widening around him the abyss of immensity that separates him from the inhabited earth; an immensity so great that he would seek in vain to cross it; for hunger, thirst, and the burning heat shorten those moments that remain to him between despair and death.” (S)

## Dissertation

(S) Buffon, Hist. nat., vol. 22.

[p. 105]

## D I S S E RTAT I O N I V

O N T H E A N I M A L S O F M E X I C O.

O    ne of the things most insisted on by Messrs. de Buffon and de

Pauw to demonstrate the unhappiness of the American soil and the malignity of its climate is that of the alleged degeneration of animals, both of those that are native to that land and of those that were transported there from the old continent. In this dissertation we shall examine their reasons and shall demonstrate some of their many errors and contradictions.

§ I.

On the Animals Native to Mexico.

All the animals that are found in the New World passed there from the Old World, as we established in the first dissertation, and this is confessed by M. de Buffon himself in volume 29 of his Natural History, and must be confessed by all who hold the sacred books in respect. We therefore call native to Mexico those animals that the Spaniards found there, not because they in fact draw their first origin from that land, as M. de P. gives us to understand throughout his work, and M. de Buffon in the first twenty-eight volumes of his History; but only to distinguish those beasts that from time immemorial were raised in those countries from the others that were afterward transported thither from Europe: we shall therefore call these European, and those American.

The first head of reproach against America is, with Count de Buffon, the small number of its quadrupeds compared with those of the old continent. He counts two hundred

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.         O                  species [p. 106] species of quadrupeds in the whole earth discovered thus far, of which a hundred and thirty are found in the old continent, and only seventy in the New World; and if from this number are removed those that are common to both continents, we shall scarcely have, he says, forty species of quadrupeds properly American. From this antecedent he deduces that in America matter has been prodigiously scarce. (a)

But why does he wish to take from America, of the seventy species of quadrupeds it has, those thirty that are common to both continents, since these, by reason of their most ancient habitation in those countries, are as properly American as the others? Moreover, if those beasts that he calls properly American had been created in America from the beginning, he might perhaps with somewhat less improbability assert the alleged scarcity of matter in that part of the world; but since all the beasts were Asiatic in their first origin, as he himself confesses, I do not know how he dared to draw such a conclusion. “Every animal,” says the same Count de Buffon, “left to its instinct, seeks out a zone and a region proportioned to its nature.” (b) Here, then, is the cause of the smaller number of species of quadrupeds in America: that, left to their instinct, after they came out of Noah’s ark, they sought out and found in the countries of the old continent a zone and a region suited to their nature, so that they had no need to make that long journey to America. If Noah’s ark, instead of stopping on the mountains of Armenia, had stopped on the American Alps, then for the same reason the number of species of quadrupeds on the old continent would have been smaller, and the American philosopher who from such an antecedent claimed to argue the prodigious scarcity of matter, and the niggardly sky of what we now call the old continent, would be worthy of blame.

But

(a) Hist. nat., vol. 23.

(b) Hist. nat., vol. 29.

[p. 107]

But even if all those quadrupeds were truly native to America, the alleged scarcity of matter ought not therefore to be deduced from it; for matter cannot be said to be scarce in a country that has a number of species of quadrupeds proportioned to its extent. The extent of America is a third part of that of the whole earth: therefore it cannot be said that matter is scarce there, so long as there is a third part of the species of quadrupeds. Now the species of quadrupeds are, by Count de Buffon’s reckoning, two hundred, and America has seventy, that is, a little more than a third part: it cannot therefore be said that matter is scarce there.

Thus far we have reasoned on the supposition that what Count de Buffon says concerning the number of the species of quadrupeds is true; but who knows, since the true distinguishing character of species has not yet been found? Count de Buffon, like a good many other naturalists who have written after him, believes that the one indubitable proof of the specific diversity of two animals otherwise similar in many accidents and properties is that of the male’s being unable to cover the female and produce, by generation, an individual that is fertile and like them. But this argument for diversity, besides failing in some animals, is in the case of others very difficult to verify. To show its uncertainty, let us put on one side an ass and a mare, and on the other a mastiff and a greyhound bitch, two very different breeds of dogs. From this pair is born a dog that has something of the mastiff and of the greyhound; from that one is born a mule that likewise has something of the ass and of the mare. Now I want to know why the ass and the mare are two different species of quadrupeds, and the mastiff and the greyhound are only two different breeds of one and the same species. Because this pair, says Count de Buffon, generates a fertile individual, and that one does not. But how? The same Count de Buffon, in volume 29 of his History, affirms frankly that the failure of mules ordinarily to conceive is not at all due to some absolute impotence, but only to the excessive heat and to the extraordinary convulsions that they suffer

O2                        in [p. 108] in coition. M. de Bomare, (c) after citing the testimony of Aristotle, who in the History of Animals relates that in his time the mules of Syria, born of horses and she-asses, begot little mules like themselves, adds: “This fact, reported by a philosopher well worthy of credit, proves that mules are animals specifically fertile in themselves and in their posterity.” Similar facts demonstrating the fertility of mules are found attested by many authors, equally worthy of credit, ancient as well as modern, and some have even occurred in our own days in Mexico. (d) There is therefore no other disparity between those two pairs of quadrupeds, except that the litters of the bitches begotten by that pair of dogs are more frequent than those of the mules.

Besides, from whom did Count de Buffon learn that the Gibbon and the Magot, the Mammon and the Pappion (four sorts of monkeys) do not couple with one another and generate a fertile individual? This author has made no experiment on the matter, nor does he cite any other naturalist who has made one, and nevertheless he decides that all the aforesaid quadrupeds are so many different species. The division of the species of quadrupeds made by him is therefore in great part doubtful and unfounded, and we do not know whether certain quadrupeds that he sets down as different species are in fact one single species, or, on the contrary, whether others that he believes to be of one species are truly specifically different.

But leaving this aside, it would suffice to cause great distrust of the division that Count de Buffon makes of the quadrupeds, to take note of the contradictions found in this as in other points of his History, otherwise highly estimable. In the discourse that he gives in volume 29 on the degeneration of animals, he affirms that if one wishes to make the enumeration

of

(c) Dictionn. d’hist. natur., s.v. Mulet.

(d) Among others, the repeated delivery of a mule begotten of an ass and a mare is worthy of particular mention; it occurred on the large estate called El Salto de Zurita, near the city of Lagos, belonging to the Knight Don Fulgencio González Rubalcaba. This mule conceived by an ass and gave birth to a little mule in 1762, and to another in 1763. [p. 109] of the quadrupeds proper to the new continent, we shall find fifty different species, and in the enumeration that he makes of the quadrupeds of both continents, he says that those of America scarcely make forty species. In the aforesaid enumeration he sets down as different species those of the domestic goat, the chamois, and the bouquetin, or wild he-goat, and in volume 24, treating of the aforesaid animals, he says that these three quadrupeds, and the other six or seven species of goats that the nomenclators distinguish, are all of one and the same species; so that these eight or nine species will have to be removed from the hundred and thirty that he numbers on the old continent. In the aforesaid numbering he counts the dog, the rat, and the marmot, and adds that none of these quadrupeds was in America; but then, discussing the animals common to both continents, he says that marmots and rats are species common to the one and the other continent, although it is difficult to decide whether such American quadrupeds are of the same species as those of the old continent; and in volume 16 he affirms that rats were brought to America in the ships of Europe. As to the dogs, which in the aforesaid enumeration he denies to America, he grants them to it in volume 30; for he affirms that the Xoloitzcuintli, the Itzcuintepotzotli, and the Techichi were three different breeds of the same species as the dogs of the old continent. This sample suffices to show that Count de Buffon, in spite of his great talent and his supreme diligence, forgot at times what he had written.

Among the 130 species of quadrupeds of the old continent he numbers seven species of bats common in France and in other countries of Europe, of which the five that were formerly unknown or confused were very recently discovered and distinguished by M. Daubenton, as he affirms in volume 16 of his History. Now if in learned France, where for so many centuries the history of nature has been studied, five species of bats have until now been unknown, what wonder is it that in the vast regions of America, where there have not been such able naturalists, and it is not even long since such a study has been held in esteem, there

should [p. 110] should likewise remain unknown many species of quadrupeds. I have no doubt that if there were some Buffons and Daubentons in the New World, many more species of quadrupeds could be counted than those he numbers from Paris, where he cannot have regarding American animals all the information that he has regarding European ones. It truly moves me to pity that a philosopher so celebrated, so ingenious, so erudite, and so eloquent, who has set himself to write of all the quadrupeds of the world, distinguishes their species, families, and breeds, describes their character, their disposition, and their habits, counts their teeth, and even measures their tails, should show himself nevertheless ignorant of the most common animals of Mexico. What beast is more common and better known in Mexico than the Cojote? Of it all the historians of that kingdom make mention, and Dr. Hernández, whose History we find very frequently cited by Count de Buffon, gives an exact and minute description of it; and yet this author says not a word of it, under that name or under any other. (e) Who does not know that the rabbit was a most common quadruped in the countries of the Mexican empire under the name of Tochtli, that its figure was one of the four characters of the Mexican years, and that of the fur of its belly jerkins were woven for the use of the lords in winter? Notwithstanding, Count de Buffon will have it that the rabbit is one of those quadrupeds that were transported from Europe to America; but among so many European historians of Mexico I have not found even one who says so; on the contrary, all suppose that it has inhabited those countries from time immemorial, and I do not doubt that the Mexicans, on reading this singular anecdote of Count de Buffon, will laugh at him.

Dr. Hernández numbers in the History of Quadrupeds four Mexican animals of the class of dogs, which we mentioned

in

(e) The animals of the old continent that the Cojote most resembles are the jackal, the adive, and the isatis; but it is different from them. The jackal is of the size of a fox; the Cojote is twice as large. Jackals go almost always accompanied by thirty or forty; the Cojote goes for the most part alone. The adive is even smaller and weaker than the jackal. The isatis is proper to the cold zone and flees the woods; the Cojote loves the woods and inhabits the hot and temperate countries. [p. 111] in bk. I of the History: the first, the Xoloitzcuintli, or hairless dog; the second, the Itzcuintepotzotli, or humped dog; the third, the Techichi, or edible little dog; and the fourth, the Tepeitzcuintli, or mountain little dog. These four very different species of quadrupeds have been reduced by Count de Buffon to one. He says that Dr. Hernández was mistaken in what he wrote of the Xoloitzcuintli; because no other author makes mention of it, and it must therefore be believed that that quadruped was transported there from Europe; since the same Hernández affirms that he had first seen it in Spain, and it had no name in Mexico; whereas Xoloitzcuintli is the proper name of the wolf, imposed by Hernández on that other quadruped: that all those dogs were known in Mexico by the generic name of Alco. Here, in a few words, is a mass of errors. The name Alco, or Allco, is not Mexican, nor was it ever used in Mexico, but in South America. That of Xoloitzcuintli is not even the name of the wolf, nor do I know that there was ever anyone in Mexico who so called it. The Mexicans call the wolf Cuetlachtli, and in some places, where Mexican is not spoken with the greatest propriety, they call it Tecuani, which is the generic name of wild beasts. It is moreover evident from the very text of Hernández, which we set down here, (f) that the Xoloitzcuintli was not transported from Europe to Mexico, nor was that name imposed on it by Hernández, but it was the name by which the Mexicans called it. Hernández had seen that quadruped in Spain because it had been transported there from Mexico, as he himself indicates, just as he had seen in the gardens of Philip II several Mexican plants. But why has no other author made mention of the Xoloitzcuintli? Because no other, neither before nor after him, has undertaken the history of the Mexican quadrupeds, and the historians of that kingdom have

## contented

(f) “Besides the dogs known to our world, which, almost all brought over by the Spaniards, are today raised by the Indians in these regions, you will find three other kinds, of which the first, before I betook myself hither, I saw in my own country; the others I had not seen, nor do I think they have been carried thither even yet. The first, called Xoloitzcuintli, surpasses the others in size of body, etc.” Hernández, Hist. Quadrup. Novae Hisp., ch. 20. [p. 112] contented themselves with making mention of the most common animals. For the rest, every wise and impartial man must give greater credence to Dr. Hernández in the natural history of Mexico, as to one who for so many years was employed in it by order of King Philip II, and who with his own eyes observed the animals of which he wrote, and informed himself by word of mouth from the Mexicans themselves, whose language he learned, than to Count de Buffon, who, though more ingenious and more eloquent, had no other information about Mexican animals than what he procured from the work of the same Hernández, or from the accounts of some other author not so worthy of our trust as that learned and practical naturalist.

Count de Buffon would have the Tepeitzcuintli of Hernández be nothing other than the glutton, a quadruped common in the most northerly countries of both continents; but whoever will compare the description that Count de Buffon gives of the glutton with that which Dr. Hernández gives of the Tepeitzcuintli will at once perceive an enormous diversity between those two quadrupeds. (g) The glutton is, according to Count de Buffon, proper to the cold countries of the North; the Tepeitzcuintli is of the torrid zone: the glutton is, by Count de Buffon’s account, twice as large as the badger; the Tepeitzcuintli is, as Hernández says, of the size of a small dog. The glutton is so called on account of its stupendous and unheard-of voracity, which carries it to the excess of digging up corpses to eat them; Hernández says nothing like this of the Tepeitzcuintli, and he would not have omitted it, this being the principal character of the glutton; on the contrary, he affirms that the Tepeitzcuintli is domesticated and feeds on egg yolks and on bread softened in hot water, and a beast so avid of flesh as the glutton could certainly not sustain itself on that. Finally, leaving aside other arguments of their diversity, the skin of the glutton is, as Count de Buffon says, almost

as

(g) Buffon, Hist. nat., vol. 27. Hernández, Hist. Quadrup. N. Hispaniae, ch. 21. [p. 113] as precious as the sable’s; (h) and we do not know that that of the Tepeitzcuintli has ever been prized or used.

Since, then, the Xoloitzcuintli is distinct from the wolf, and the Tepeitzcuintli from the glutton, and since those four American quadrupeds of the class of dogs differ greatly among themselves in size, in disposition, and in many other notable accidents, and since moreover it is not established that they can couple with one another and produce a fertile third individual, we must conclude that they are four different species; and therefore those three species wrongly removed by Count de Buffon must be restored to America.

We should never finish if we wished to point out all the mistakes of this author concerning American quadrupeds; but to demonstrate still further that the number of seventy species prescribed by him to America is not correct, but quite deficient, and contrary besides to what he himself wrote in the course of his History, we shall give at the end of this dissertation a list of the American quadrupeds drawn from the aforesaid History, to which we shall add the quadrupeds confused by him with others very different, and also those entirely omitted by him, whereby we shall show how far he has strayed from the truth in saying that in America matter has been prodigiously scarce. Besides, to conclude such a scarcity it does not suffice to make known that the species are few, but it would also be necessary to demonstrate that the individuals of such species are few; for if the individuals of the seventy species of American quadrupeds are more than those of the hundred and thirty species of the old continent, nature will indeed be less varied in America, but matter not more scarce. It would likewise be necessary to demonstrate that the species of reptiles and of birds are equally few and scantily numerous, since these no less than those serve to show the scarcity or the abundance of matter; but who is so ignorant of the things of America as not to have knowledge of the

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.             P                 incredible

(h) M. de Bomare says that the skin of the glutton is more prized by the Kamchatkans than the sable’s, and that in Sweden it is much sought after and very dear. [p. 114] incredible variety, and of the astonishing multitude of American birds? Now let someone tell me why, Nature having been so niggardly in quadrupeds toward America, as Count de Buffon and M. de Pauw will have it, she has been so prodigal in birds?

Not content with diminishing the species of American quadrupeds, these authors strive also to shrink their stature. “All the animals of America,” says Count de Buffon, (i) “both those that were transported by men, such as horses, asses, bulls, sheep, goats, hogs, dogs, etc., and those that passed there of themselves, such as wolves, foxes, deer, and elks, are considerably smaller there than in Europe: and this,” he adds, “without any exception.” This stupendous effect he ascribes to the niggardly sky of America, and to the combination of the elements and other physical causes. “There was not,” says M. de P., “under the torrid zone of the new continent any great quadruped. The greatest quadruped (among those proper to those countries) that exists at present in the New World between the tropics is the tapir, which is of the size of a calf.” (k) “The bulkiest beast of the New continent,” says Count de Buffon, “is the tapir, which is as large as a small mule, and after it the Cabiai, which is of the size of a middling hog.”

We have already shown in the preceding dissertation that even if we granted these philosophers the alleged smallness of American quadrupeds, nothing would follow against the land or the climate of America; for according to the principles established by Count de Buffon, and cited by us elsewhere, the largest animals are proper to extreme climates, and the less large to temperate and mild ones: and if the advantages of a climate had to be argued from the size of quadrupeds, we should say without more ado that the climate of Africa and of southern Asia is much better than that of Europe. But if in America, when it was discovered

by

(i) Hist. nat., vol. 18.

(k) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 3, sect. 2. [p. 115] by the Europeans, there were no elephants, rhinoceroses, hippopotamuses, camels, or giraffes, at least there were at one time, if we give credence to Messrs. de P., Sloane, du Pratz, Lignery, and several other authors, who affirm the ancient existence of those great quadrupeds in America, grounded on the finding of fossil bones and of entire carcasses of immeasurable size in various places of that New World; indeed, if we believe what Count de Buffon wrote in volume 18 of his History, there was once in America a quadruped six times bulkier than the elephant, called Mammout by M. Müller; (l) but in Europe there never was, nor can there be, any quadruped of the first size. In America there were no horses, nor asses, nor bulls, (m) before they were transported there from Europe; but neither were they in Europe before they were transported or brought from Asia. All animals draw their origin from Asia, and from there spread to other countries: the nearness of Europe, and the commerce of the Asiatic peoples with the Europeans, facilitated the passage of those quadrupeds into Europe, and with them were also brought some customs and inventions useful to life, of which the Americans were deprived by reason of the distance of the countries and the lack of commerce.

When Count de Buffon asserted that the largest quadruped of the New World was the tapir, and after it the Cabiai, he had entirely forgotten the walruses, the seals, the bisons, the reindeer, the elks, the bears, and the huanachi. He himself

P2                   confesses,

(l) According to what M. Müller says of his Mammout, this quadruped would have been 133 feet long and 105 feet high. Count de Buffon speaks of it thus in volume 16: “The prodigious Mammout, whose enormous bones we have often considered, and which we have judged to be at least six times larger than the largest elephant, exists no more.” In volume 22 he says that he has made sure that those immense bones were once those of elephants seven or eight times larger than that whose carcass he had observed in the Royal Cabinet at Paris; but in his new work entitled The Epochs of Nature he again affirms the ancient existence of that enormous quadruped in America.
