(m) When we say that there were no bulls in America, we speak only of the common breed, which is employed in agriculture; for there were bisons, which Count de Buffon sometimes believes to be of the same species as the common bulls, and sometimes doubts. [p. 116] confesses, (n) that the seal seen by Lord Anson and by Rogers in America, and called by them sea lion, was incomparably larger than all the seals of the Old World. Who will dare to compare the Cabiai, which is no larger than a middling hog, with the bisons and the elks? The bisons are for the most part equal to the common bulls of Europe, and often exceed them in size. Let one see the description that M. de Bomare (o) gives of one of these quadrupeds, transported from Louisiana to France, and exactly measured by the same naturalist at Paris in 1769. There is an innumerable multitude of these great animals in the temperate zone of North America. The elks of New Mexico are of the size of a good horse. There was a knight in the city of Zacatecas who used them for his carriage in place of horses, as Vetancurt attests, (p) and at times they have been sent to the court of Spain to be presented to the Catholic King.

The universal proposition in which Count de Buffon affirms that all the quadrupeds common to both continents are smaller in America without any exception has been given the lie by several European authors, who saw those animals with their own eyes, and even by Count de Buffon himself in other places of his History. Of the Miztli, or American lion, Dr. Hernández says that it is larger than the lion of the same species of the old continent. (q) Of the tiger

of

(n) Hist. nat., vol. 27.

(o) Dictionn. d’hist. natur., s.v. Bison. M. de Bomare calls that American beast, on account of its size, a colossal quadruped: he says that its length from the muzzle to the root of the tail, measured along the flanks, was nine feet and two inches; its height from the top of the hump to the hoof, five feet and four inches; its girth, measured at the hump and at the dewlap, ten feet in circumference. He adds that he had learned from the owner of that beast that the females were even larger.

(p) Those elks must have been very large to be able to draw the carriages that were used in that kingdom in the last century.

(q) “The Miztli is either the same as our lion, though by no means maned, or of the same kind; dusky in infancy, and tawny in youth, sometimes reddish or whitish, yet rising to a greater bulk, which may happen because of the difference of region.” Hist. Quadrup. N. Hisp., ch. XI. [p. 117] of Mexico he affirms the same. (r) Neither Count de Buffon nor M. de P. has a just idea of that beast. I saw one, among others, killed a few hours before with nine arquebus shots, much larger than what Count de Buffon would have us believe. These authors, since they do not trust the report of the Spaniards, ought at least to give credence to M. de la Condamine, a learned and truthful Frenchman, who says that the tigers seen by him in the hot countries of the New World did not seem to him different from the African tigers, either in the beautiful colors of the skin or in size, etc. Of the Mexican wolf the aforesaid Hernández says that in figure, color, and inclinations, as in size, it is like the European wolf, except that the latter has a larger head. (s) The same he affirms of the common deer, and Oviedo of the deer and the fallow deer. Count de Buffon himself, in spite of the generality of the principle established by him, without any exception, on the smaller size of American quadrupeds, reasoning afterward in volume 29 on the degeneration of animals, says that the fallow deer and the roe deer are, among the quadrupeds common to both continents, the only ones that are larger and stronger in the New World than in the Old; and discoursing in volume 27 of the otter of Canada he confesses that it is much larger than those of Europe, and says the same of the American beaver: so that he who admitted no exception to his principle admits it after all in fallow deer, roe deer, otters, beavers, and seals. If to these are then added the tigers, the maneless lions, and the deer, according to the testimony of Hernández and of Oviedo, we shall have at least eight species of quadrupeds common to both continents that are larger in the New World than in the Old. To the foregoing must likewise be added those quadrupeds that are equally large in the one and the other continent; since these, as well as

those,

(r) “The tiger is common in this world, but larger than ours.” Hist. Quadr. N. Hisp., ch. X.

(s) “The Cuetlachtli is like our wolf in form, color, habits, and bulk of body, and so of the same species, as it seems to me, but with a larger head.” Ibid., ch. XXIII. [p. 118] those show the principle of Count de Buffon to be false. Dr. Hernández affirms that the Mexican wolf is of the same size as the European. Count de Buffon says that between the one and the other there is no difference, except that the Mexican wolf has a finer skin, and five toes on the forefeet and four on the hind. As for the bears, there are at present in Italy very many Europeans who have seen the bears of Mexico and those of the Alps. I do not believe that among so many witnesses there is even one who has recognized any excess in the European bears. I at least sincerely protest that all those that I have seen in Mexico seemed to me larger than those that I have seen in Italy. (t)

It is therefore false that all the animals of the New World are smaller than those of the Old World without any exception. It is also most false that they are all much smaller, and that Nature has made use in the New World of a different scale of size, as Count de Buffon affirms in another place. (u) In like manner the error of M. de Pauw can be shown in saying that all the quadrupeds of America are a sixth part smaller than their analogues of the old continent. The Tuza of Mexico is the analogue of the European mole, and is larger than it, according to what Count de Buffon says. That Mexican quadruped which Count de Buffon calls Coquallino, and we Tlalmototli, is analogous to the squirrel of Europe, and is, by the same author’s account, twice as large: the shrew of Brazil is analogous to the European shrew, and yet is larger than it, as the aforesaid author confesses. The Cojote, analogue of the jackal, is twice as large. The Llama, or sheep of Peru, analogue of the sheep of Europe, is incomparably larger, etc. But these philosophers, too bent on vilifying America and discrediting its animals, find even

## something

(t) Count de Buffon distinguishes the species of black bears from that of the brown, and affirms that black bears are not at all ferocious; but the Mexican bears, which are all black, are very ferocious indeed, as is notorious in Mexico; and I can be a witness to it.

(u) Hist. nat., vol. 18. [p. 119] something to censure in their tails, their feet, and their teeth: “Not only,” says Count de Buffon, “is matter prodigiously scarce in the new continent, but the forms of its animals are also imperfect, and seem to have been neglected. (x) The animals of South America, which are those that properly belong to this new continent, are almost all without tusks, horns, and tails; their figure is bizarre, and their limbs disproportioned and ill put together; and some of them, like the anteaters and the sloths, are of so wretched a nature that they have hardly the power to move and to eat.” (y) “The animals native to the New World,” says M. de Pauw, “are for the most part of an ungainly form, and in some of them so ill arranged that the first draftsmen could make their characters recognizable only with difficulty. It has been observed that most of the species lack a tail, and that they have a certain irregularity in the paws: which is notable in the tapir, the anteater, the Glama of Margraf, the sloth, and the Cabiai. The ostriches, which on our continent have no more than two toes united by a membrane, have all four toes separate in America.” (z)

These speeches, to tell the truth, are rather a censure of the conduct of the Creator than of the climate of America, not unlike that blasphemy which some attribute to King Don Alfonso the Wise concerning the arrangement of the heavenly bodies. If the first individuals of those species of animals did not come thus from the hand of the Creator, but the climate of the New World was the cause of their alleged irregularities, then, were such animals transported to Europe, their form, their disposition, and their instinct would be perfected: at least after ten or twelve generations those wretched animals, from which the malignant climate of America has taken away the tail,

the

(x) Hist. nat., vol. 18.

(y) Hist. nat., vol. 23.

(z) Recherch. sur les Américains, pt. 1. [p. 120] the horns, and the tusks, would recover them under a more beneficent climate. No, those philosophers will say, because it is not so easy to recover in nature what one loses as to lose what one has: so that even if those animals could not regain their tail, horns, or tusks on the old continent, it might nevertheless be said that the climate of America was the cause of such a loss. Be it so; and therefore let us not now discuss the irregularities that consist in some defect, but those that are such through an excess of matter. Let us discuss the ostriches, which through a fault of Nature, according to M. de Pauw, (*) have two toes more on each foot; or, so as not to leave the quadrupeds, let us discuss rather the Unau, a species of American sloth, which among its other irregularities has that of having forty-six ribs. “The number of forty-six ribs in an animal of so small a body,” says Count de Buffon, “is a kind of error, or excess, of Nature; for no animal, not even the largest, or those whose body is longest in proportion to their bulk, has so many. The elephant has no more than forty ribs, the horse thirty-six, the badger thirty, the dog twenty-six, and man twenty-four.” Now if the first Unau that existed in the world had from the hand of God the same number of ribs that its descendants have at present, the discourse of Count de Buffon is a censure of the Creator; and to say that that excessive number of ribs was an error of Nature will mean that it was an error of God, who is the true efficient Nature. I am quite sure that such a blasphemy is far removed from the sublime mind and the Christian heart of Count de Buffon; but the philosophical spirit that reigns everywhere in his works has led him at times to make use of such expressions as, when well examined, will not please good Christians.

(A)

(*) M. de Pauw was mistaken in the number of toes of the Touyou, or American ostrich; for it has no more than three; but at the back of the foot it has a round, callous tubercle, which serves it in place of a heel, and by the common people has been taken for a toe.

[p. 121]

(A) If, on the contrary, these philosophers believe that the Unau in its first origin had a number of ribs proportioned to the size of its body, and that the malignant climate of America afterwards went on increasing it, we must persuade ourselves that, if that species of quadruped were transported to the old continent and reared under a more favorable climate, it would in the end return to its pristine perfection. Let the experiment be made, then: let two or three males of that ungainly species be transported to the Old World, and as many females, and if after twenty or more generations it is observed that the number of their ribs begins to diminish, we shall at once confess that the land of America is the most unhappy, and its climate the worst, in the world. If it turns out otherwise, we shall say then, as we say from this very moment, that the logic of these gentlemen is more wretched than that quadruped, and that their reasonings are mere paralogisms. For the rest, it is truly a wonder that in a country where matter has been so scarce, Nature should have sinned by an excess of it in the ribs of the sloths and in the toes of the ostriches.

But to show that these philosophers, bent on making the climate of the New World appear malignant, had entirely forgotten the miseries of their own continent, let us ask them: which is the most wretched animal of America? The sloth, they will answer at once; because this quadruped is the most imperfect in its organization, the most unfit for motion, the most unprovided with arms for its defense, and above all the one that seems to have less sensation than all other animals: a truly unhappy animal, condemned by Nature to inertia, to hunger, and to weeping, with which it ever arouses in other animals pity and horror. But this class of quadrupeds, so famous for their wretchedness, is

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                   Q                      common

(A) Wishing to give the reason why man resists the influences of climates more than the animals do, Count de Buffon says thus in volume 18: Man is in everything the work of Heaven; the animals are in many respects nothing but productions of the earth. This proposition seems a little too harsh; but far harsher ones are read in his Epochs of Nature. [p. 122] common to both continents. Count de Buffon will not believe it, because it does not suit him, and he says that if any sloth is found in Asia, it was transported there from America; but whatever he may say, it is certain that the Unau, one of the species of sloths, is an Asiatic animal, as Klein, Linnaeus, Brisson, the publisher of Seba’s Cabinet, and above all Vosmaër, a learned and diligent Dutch naturalist, bear witness. (B) The Unau of Bengal, seen, reared, and exactly described by this author, cannot have been transported from America; because there has never been any commerce between South America and Asia by which it could have been transported. Besides, the Unau of Bengal is different from the American one: this has only two toes on its paws, and that five. If Count de Buffon persuades himself that the climate of Asia could increase the number of toes in the American quadruped, we shall say then that the climate of the old continent would also be capable of restoring the tail, the horns, and the tusks to those quadrupeds from which the deadly climate of America had taken them. For the rest, whoever wishes to read and compare the eloquent description that Count de Buffon gives of the American sloths, and that which M. de Vosmaër gives of the pentadactyl sloth of Bengal, will soon recognize that this Asiatic quadruped is as wretched as those American ones.

But let us examine philosophically what these authors say about the alleged irregularity of those quadrupeds. True irregularity in animals is the disproportion in the limbs, or the unsuitability in the form or the disposition of certain individuals with respect to the common run of the species, not that which is observed in a new species compared with another that is known. He would surely be a fool who should deem the Techichi irregular because it does not bark. This is an American quadruped, which, because it resembles the small dogs of Europe, was called a dog by the Spaniards, not because it was of the same species: and hence arose that fable, spread by not a few authors

of

(B) Description de plusieurs Animaux. A work printed at Amsterdam. [p. 123] of Europe, that in America the dogs were mute. Wolves are very like dogs, and yet they do not bark. If the first Spaniards who went to Mexico had never seen wolves in Europe, on seeing those of Mexico they would have announced that there were large dogs there that could not be tamed, and that instead of barking howled: and behold here a new argument, of which Count de Buffon and M. de Pauw would have availed themselves to prove the degradation and irregularity of the American animals.

In fact the argument of M. de Pauw about the American ostriches is of no other caliber. The Touyou (*) is an American bird specifically different from the ostrich; but because it is quite large, and very like that great African bird, it has commonly been called an ostrich. This suffices M. de P. to affirm that there is irregularity in those American birds; but even if we granted him as a favor that the Touyou is a true ostrich, it could never prove what he wishes. He wants us to believe the American ostrich irregular because, instead of having only two toes united by a membrane, like the African, it has four separate ones. But an American could say that the African ostrich is rather the irregular one, because instead of having four separate toes it has only two, and those united by means of a membrane. “No,” M. de P. would reply, all in a rage, “it is not so: the irregularity is certainly in your ostriches, because they do not conform to the ostriches of the Old World, which are the exemplars of the species, nor to the portrait of such birds that the most famous naturalists of Europe have left us.” “Our world,” the American would answer, “which you call new, because three centuries ago it was not yet known to you, is as ancient as your world, and our animals are likewise coeval with yours. Neither are those under any obligation to conform to your animals, nor are we at fault

Q2                          that

(*) In Peru the ostrich is known by the name of Suri; but I use here that of Touyou, in deference to our naturalists. [p. 124] that the species of our animals have been unknown to your naturalists, or confused through the scantiness of their lights. So that either your ostriches are irregular, because they do not conform to ours, or at least ours ought not to be called irregular, because they do not conform to yours. Until you demonstrate to me with undeniable documents that the first ostriches came from the hand of the Creator with only two toes united by a membrane, you will never persuade me of the irregularity of our Touyou.” This reason, undoubtedly most effective, serves also to dispel other such discourses of our philosophers, caused by the imperfection of their ideas, or by their prepossession in favor of the old continent.

Our philosophers are in truth no happier in their discourses on the tails of quadrupeds than in what they wrote about the feet of ostriches. They say frankly, and without any regard for truth, that the greater part of the quadrupeds of the new continent are altogether without tails: which, like all the other effects they have observed in those unfortunate countries, they ascribe to the niggardliness of the American sky, to the infancy of Nature in that part of the world, to the bad climate, and to I know not what combination of the elements. Thus reason these celebrated philosophers of the enlightened age. But since, according to Count de Buffon, the species of American quadrupeds number seventy, at least forty would have to be without tails for it to be true that the greater part lack that member, as M. de P. says, and much more would be needed to verify that almost all quadrupeds are destitute of tails, as Count de Buffon affirms. Now such quadrupeds in America are at most six, as we shall see below: therefore their proposition is a monstrous hyperbole, not to say a gross lie.

It appears that in the time of Pliny no animals without tails were known to the European naturalists other than man and the ape. (C) If since then there had not been discovered in the old

continent

(C) Tails, apart from man and the apes, belong to almost all animals, including those that lay eggs, according to the needs of their bodies. Pliny, Natural History, bk. 11, ch. 50. [p. 125] continent several other quadrupeds likewise unprovided with that member, Count de Buffon and M. de P. would indeed be right to reproach the American quadrupeds with it; but from Count de Buffon’s own History it is established that the species of quadrupeds without a tail are more numerous in the old continent than in America. Here is the list of both, taken from the aforesaid History.

Quadrupeds without a Tail in the Old continent.

1. The Pongo, or the Orang-outang, or Satyr, or savage man.

2. The Piteco, or true ape. 3. The Gibbon, another, different species of ape. 4. The Cynocephalus, or Magot. 5. The Turkish dog. 6. The Tanrec of Madagascar. 7. The Loris of Ceylon. 8. The guinea pig. 9. The Roussette } 10. The Rougette } two species of large bats of Asia. 11. The golden mole of Siberia.

To which the three following must be added. 12. The pentadactyl sloth of Bengal, described by M. de Vosmaër.

13. The Klipda, or bastard marmot of the Cape of Good Hope, de‐

scribed by the same M. de Vosmaër. 14. The Capiverd, or Capivard of the Cape of Good Hope, described

by M. de Bomare.

In America.

1. The Unau, a species of sloth. 2. The Cabiai, or amphibious hog. 3. The Aperea of Brazil. 4. The guinea pig.

5. The

[p. 126]

5. The Saino, Pecar, or Cojametl. 6. The Tapeto.

So that in the old continent there are at least fourteen species (D) of quadrupeds unprovided with a tail, and in America only six, from which we might remove the last two, because they are uncertain. (E) In all the thirty small volumes of Count de Buffon’s History of the quadrupeds I have found no other American animal without a tail except the aforesaid. And notwithstanding this, he dares to affirm that in the New World almost all the animals were without a tail. From which one sees that such universal propositions are as easy to utter as they are difficult to prove.

If the climate of America is so pernicious to the tails of animals, why is it that, while four species of apes of the old continent, namely the Pongo, the Piteco, the Gibbon, and the Cynocephalus, are altogether without that member, all the species of monkeys of the New World have it, and some, like the Sakis, have a tail so long that it is sesquiduple their body? Why do squirrels, Coquallins, anteaters, and other such quadrupeds, furnished with an enormous tail in proportion to their bodies, abound so in America? Why has the marmot of Canada, although of the same species as that of the Alps, a larger tail, as Count de Buffon himself confesses? Why do the deer and the roe deer of America, although smaller than those of the old continent, likewise have a

longer

(D) To the fourteen aforesaid species I could add the two-toed Unau of Ceylon, of which several authors make mention, and the Porte-musc, or Porta-mosco, described by MM. Daubenton and Bomare; but I omitted the first because I am not sure that it is different from the Loris of Count de Buffon; I omitted the second too, because it may be that it has some little tail, although the diligent M. Daubenton could not find it: so that those last two species of American quadrupeds ought likewise to be removed as uncertain.

(E) The Pecar is found described by Oviedo, Hernández, and Acosta under the names of Saino and Cojametl, and these authors say nothing of any lack of a tail. I, however, inquired of discerning and truthful persons who have seen many Sainos, and they told me that they had their tail, though a small one. Concerning the Tapeto, Count de Buffon believes that it is the Citli of Hernández. Now all Mexicans know that the Citli of Hernández is the hare of Mexico, and we are sure that it has its tail, like the common hare of Europe. [p. 127] longer tail, according to what the same author affirms? (F) If ever there were in America some principle destructive of the tails of animals, those that Columbus transported there from Europe and from the Canary Islands in 1493 would by now have remained entirely tailless, especially the hogs, which brought tails so small; or at least they would have been notably shortened after 288 years; but among so many Europeans who have seen the sheep, horses, oxen, etc. born in America, and those that are at present raised in Europe, there will not be even one who can find any difference between the tails of the one and of the other.

This same argument holds equally against what Count de Buffon says about the lack of horns and tusks in the greater part of the American quadrupeds, since the oxen, sheep, and goats preserve their horns unchanged, the dogs and hogs their teeth, and the cats their claws, as all those know who have seen them and compared them with those of Europe. If the American climate were so deadly to the teeth and to the horns of animals, the descendants of those European quadrupeds that were transported there almost three centuries ago would already have lost at least a good part of them, and much more the posterity of the wolves, the bears, and other similar quadrupeds that passed there from Asia perhaps as early as the first century after the universal Flood. If on the contrary the temperate zone of Europe is more propitious to the teeth of animals than the torrid zone of the New World, why did Nature give to the latter, and not to the former, the tapirs and the crocodiles, which in the number, the size, and the ferocity of their teeth exceed all the quadrupeds and reptiles of Europe?

Finally, if there are some animals in America without horns, without teeth, (G) and without a tail, it is not by reason

of

(F) Hist. nat., vol. 18.

(G) Among all the quadrupeds of the New World there are no others without teeth except the anteaters, just as there are in the old continent the pangolin and the Phatagin, quadrupeds of East India covered with scales instead of hair. All these quadrupeds, since they feed on nothing but ants, have no need of teeth to sustain themselves; but otherwise they are [p. 128] of the climate, or of the niggardly sky of America, or of that imaginary combination of the elements; but because the Creator, whose works are perfect, and whose counsels we must humbly revere, willed to make them so, that such variety might serve the general embellishment of the universe, and make his wisdom and his power shine forth the more. The very thing that makes some animals beautiful would make others deformed. In the horse it is a perfection to have a large tail, in the deer to have a small one, and in the Pongo to be altogether without one.

As to what our philosophers say about the ugliness of the American animals, it is true that among so many there are some whose form does not correspond to the idea that we have of the beauty of beasts. But who has assured us that such an idea is just, rather than imperfect and caused by the limitation of our mind? And how many other animals might we not find in the old continent still worse formed than all the American beasts? (I speak here according to the ideas of those philosophers; for the rest, I respect the hand of the Creator in all his works.) What quadruped is there in America that can be compared in deformity and in disproportion of limbs with the elephant, called a Monster of matter by Count de Buffon himself? (H) That vast mass of flesh, higher than it is long; that loathsome skin, bare of hair and furrowed with wrinkles; that enormous trunk in place of a nose; those long teeth thrust out of that most ugly mouth and turned upward, contrary to what is observed in other animals, to increase still more the deformity of its face; those vast and polygonal ears; those thick, crooked, and disproportionately small legs; those

feet, provided by the Creator with a very long tongue, with which they deftly catch the ants and swallow them.

(H) “Considering this animal” (says M. de Bomare of the elephant) “in relation to the idea we have of the correctness of proportions, it seems ill proportioned, because of its thick, short body, its stiff and ill-formed legs, its round and crooked feet, its big head, its small eyes, and its large ears: one might also say that the garment in which it is covered is still worse cut and worse made. Its trunk, its tusks, its feet make it as extraordinary as the greatness of its size, [p. 129] feet, shapeless, their toes barely outlined, and finally those minute little eyes and that ridiculous little tail on so immoderate a body, do they not make the elephant the most irregular quadruped? I challenge our philosophers to find me in the New World a quadruped more disproportioned, and whose form is more ungainly. Similar reflections might also be made on the camel, on the giraffe, on the Macaco, of which Count de Buffon says that it is of a frightful deformity, and on other animals of the old continent, and yet we do not dare to blame the climate that rears them, nor even to censure the supreme Artificer who formed them.

What our philosophers say, moreover, about the lesser ferocity of the American wild beasts, instead of helping them to prove the malignity of that climate, serves only to demonstrate its mildness and goodness. “In America,” says Count de Buffon, (I) “where the air and the earth are milder than those of Africa, the tiger, the lion, and the panther are not terrible except in name… They have degenerated, if indeed ferocity added to cruelty made up their nature, or rather, they have done nothing but suffer the influence of the climate: under a gentler sky their disposition has grown gentler.” What more can be desired in favor of the climate of America? How, then, is the lesser ferocity of the American beasts alleged as an argument of their degeneration, caused by the malignity of that climate? If the climate of the old continent must be reckoned better than that of the New World because the most terrible wild beasts are reared under it, then for the same reason the climate of Africa will have to be believed incomparably more excellent than that of Europe. This argument, which we have used elsewhere, ought to be pressed home for the greater confusion of our philosophers.

But these authors have no just idea of the American wild beasts. It is true that the Miztli, or Mexican lion, is not comparable with the celebrated lions of Africa: that species either never passed into the New World, or was exterminated by men; but it does not

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.          R                    yield,

(I) Hist. nat., vol. 12. [p. 130] yield, that American beast, to those of its species, that is, to the maneless lions of the old continent, as Hernández attests, who knew well both the one and the other. The Mexican tiger, whether or not it is of the same species as the Royal Tigers of Africa, since that matters not at all to us, is of a surprising strength and ferocity. There is no quadruped, either among the European or among the American, that can withstand it. It boldly assails and tears to pieces men, deer, horses, bulls, and even the most horrible crocodiles, as Acosta attests. (K) This learned author vaunts the intrepidity and the swiftness of that wild beast. Gonzalo de Oviedo, who had traveled through many countries of Europe and was not ignorant of natural history, speaking of those American tigers, says (L) thus: “They are animals very strong in the legs, well armed with claws, and so terrible that in my judgment there is no Royal Lion, even among the largest, that can vie with them in strength and ferocity.” The tiger is the terror of the forests of America: it is not capable of being tamed, nor of letting itself be taken when adult: those that are taken while still small cannot be kept without danger unless shut up in very strong cages of wood or of iron. Such is the character of those beasts which are called cowards by M. de P. and by other authors who did not know how to distinguish the species of quadrupeds with spotted skin.

It is moreover certain that those authors show themselves as ready to believe everything they find written about the size, strength, and intrepidity of the Royal Tigers of the old continent as they are obstinate in denying credit to what several eyewitnesses say of the American tigers. Count de Buffon believes, on the faith of I know not what authors, that the Royal Tiger is as much as thirteen or fourteen feet in length and five in height: that it fights together with three

elephants:

(K) Natural and Moral History, bk. 3, ch. 17.

(L) Sumario of the Natural History, ch. 11. See also what Abbé Gilij says of it in vol. 1 of the History of the Orinoco, bk. 5, ch. 6. [p. 131] elephants: that it kills a buffalo, and drags it easily wherever it wishes, and other such marvels, which cannot be believed by those who are not so prepossessed in favor of the old continent. If some trustworthy authors related of the American tigers a small part of what they say of the Asiatic tigers, they would at once be rejected without any examination as boasters. (M) The account that Pliny gives (N) of the hunters’ cunning in stealing her cubs from the tiger, and of the composure with which the tiger recovers them one by one, and that which M. de Bomare gives (O) of the combat that took place in the year 1764 in the forest of Windsor in England between a deer and a tiger brought from India to the Duke of Cumberland, in which the deer remained the victor, show that the ferocity of those Asiatic beasts is not so great as Count de Buffon and M. de P. represent it.

The American wolves are neither less strong nor less bold than those of the old continent, as all know well who have experience of both. Even the deer, which are, according to Pliny, (P) the most peaceable of animals, are in Mexico so bold that they often attack hunters, as Hernández attests (Q), and it is notorious in that kingdom. I have seen with my own eyes the carnage made in my own house by a deer, become almost domesticated, upon a poor American woman.

But let the American quadrupeds be smaller, more ungainly, and more pusillanimous. Let us even concede to those philosophers that from such an antecedent the goodness of the climate of the old continent may be deduced; yet they will never be able to persuade us that this is a complete proof and a certain argument of the malignity

(N) Nat. Hist., bk. 8, ch. 18.

(O) Bomare, Dictionn. d’hist. nat., s.v. Tigre.

(P) Nat. Hist., bk. 8, ch. 32.

(Q) Hist. Nat., bk. 9, ch. 14. [p. 132] of the American climate, so long as they do not show us in the reptiles and in the birds of America (R) that same degradation which they suppose in the quadrupeds. M. de P. says of the American crocodiles, whose ferocity is so notorious, that it appears from the observations of M. du Pratz and of some others that they do not have the fury and impetuosity of those of Africa; but Dr. Hernández, who knew well both the one and the other, found no difference between them. (S) Acosta says that the American ones are very ferocious, but slow; but this slowness is not in progressive motion in a straight line, in which they are very swift and agile, but only in turning or bending to one side or the other, as also happens in the African crocodiles, doubtless on account of the inflexibility of their vertebrae. Dr. Hernández affirms that the Acuetzpalin, or Mexican crocodile, flees from those who assail it and pursues those who flee (though the latter happens more commonly than the former). Pliny says the same of the African crocodiles. (T) Finally, if one compares what Pliny relates of the latter with what Hernández says of the former, it will be found that not even in size is there any difference between them. (V)

As to birds, M. de P. makes no mention of them except of the ostriches, and that as ineptly as

we

(R) M. de Buffon might say, as he intimates in volume 18, that no account should be taken of birds as regards the climate; because, being able easily to pass from one continent to the other, it would be almost impossible to distinguish which belong properly to the one or to the other. But since the cause of the journeys that birds make is the cold or the heat of the seasons, which they seek to avoid, the American birds have no need to leave their continent; because they have there countries of every sort of climate in which to defend themselves from the season harmful to them and to seek their food. We are altogether sure that the Mexican birds do not make journeys to the old continent.

(S) Hern. Hist. Nat., bk. 9, ch. 3.

(T) Against those who flee this beast is terrible; against those who pursue it, it flees. Pliny, Natural History, bk. 8, ch. 25.

(V) Pliny says that the African crocodile often has more than eighteen cubits in length, or twenty-seven Roman feet. Dr. Hernández affirms that the Mexican crocodile is usually more than seven paces long. If he speaks of Castilian paces, they make almost twenty-eight Roman feet; if of Roman paces, they will be thirty-five feet: so that the difference is small, or if there is some excess, it is on the side of the American crocodile. [p. 133] we have seen. He certainly chose to be silent, because he recognized his cause as lost on this side; for whether in the number or the variety of the species, or in intrepidity, or in the beauty of the feathers, or in the excellence of song, the birds of the old continent certainly cannot vie with the American birds. Of their surprising multitude we have spoken elsewhere. The fields, the woods, the rivers, the lakes, and even the inhabited places are full of innumerable species. Gemelli, who had made the circuit of the world and had been in the best countries of Asia, Africa, and Europe, declares that there is no country in the world that can be compared with New Spain in the beauty and variety of its birds. (U) See also what the historians of New France, of Louisiana, of Brazil, and of other countries of the New World say of them.

Many European authors, well worthy of credit, bear witness to the strength and spirit of the American birds. Dr. Hernández, who had had so much experience of birds of prey at the court of Philip II, king of Spain, when falconry was there more than ever in esteem, and who had also observed those of Mexico, confesses, when he speaks of the Quauhtotli, or Mexican saker, that all the birds of this class are better and more courageous in New Spain than in the old continent. (X) Because the excellence of the American falcons was known from the beginning, it was ordered by Charles V that every year fifty falcons should be sent to the court from New Spain and as many from the island of Hispaniola, as the historian Herrera attests, (Y) and Father Acosta relates (Z) that the falcons of Mexico

and

(U) So great is the beauty and the variety of the birds of New Spain that there is no country in the world that has their equal. Giro del mondo, vol. 6, bk. 2, ch. 9.

(X) I confess that every kind of hawk found in this New Spain, or in the province of Yucatán, is more excellent and more spirited than those born in the Old World. Hernández, De Avibus N. Hisp., ch. 92.

(Y) Herrera, Dec. 3, bk. 6, ch. 1.

(Z) Nat. and Moral Hist. of the Indies, bk. 4, ch. 35. [p. 134] and of Peru, because they were highly prized, were sent to be presented to the grandees of Spain. The same Acosta says (aa) that the condors, or American vultures, are of an immense size, and have such strength that they tear to pieces not only a ram but even a calf; and Don Antonio de Ulloa testifies (bb) that with a blow of the wing they lay a man flat. (cc) Dr. Hernández says that the Itzquauhtli, or royal eagle of Mexico, attacks men and even the fiercest quadrupeds. (dd) If the climate of America had taken from the quadrupeds their strength and courage, it would without doubt have caused the same effect in the birds; but from the testimony of the aforesaid authors, and of others, all Europeans and worthy of credit, it is established that they are not weak and pusillanimous, but that they surpass in strength and intrepidity those of the old continent.

As regards the beauty of birds, those authors who have otherwise been bent on running down that New World do not dispute its advantages to America. Whoever wishes to form some idea of them should see the works of Oviedo, Hernández, Acosta, Ulloa, and other European authors who have seen the American birds with their own eyes. In New Spain, says Acosta, there is a great abundance of birds adorned with feathers so excellent and so fine that their like is not found in Europe. (ee)

It is true, say some European authors, that the American birds are superior to ours in the beauty of the feathers, but not in the excellence of song, in which they are surpassed by ours.

So

(bb) A Voyage to South America, pt. 1, bk. 6, ch. 8.

(cc) The condor is so large that it measures from fourteen to sixteen feet from one tip of its extended wings to the other. M. de Bomare says that it is common to both continents, and that the Swiss call it Laemmer-geyer; but be that as it may, it is certain that no bird of prey has yet been found in the old continent that can equal the condor of America in size and strength.

(dd) Hernández, De Avibus N. Hisp., ch. 100.

[p. 135]

So think two modern Italians, (ff) as learned in certain speculative matters as they are ignorant of the things of America. The testimony of Dr. Hernández, which we copy here below, (gg) would suffice to confound these authors: who, after having heard the best nightingales at the court of Philip II, heard for many years the Centzontli, or polyglots, the cardinals, the Tigretti, the Cuitlaccochi, and other innumerable species of songbirds common in Mexico and not known in Europe, besides the nightingales, the goldfinches, the calandra larks, and others common to both continents. Among all the songbirds the most prized in Europe is the much-renowned nightingale, and yet this is far better in America, according to what M. de Bomare affirms. “The nightingale of Louisiana,” he says, “is the same as that of Europe; but it is more familiar, sings all year, and its song is more varied.” Behold three great advantages of the American over the European. But even if there were in America neither nightingales, nor goldfinches, nor any other bird of those that are esteemed in Europe for their song, the Centzontli or polyglot alone (*) would suffice to leave it nothing to envy any country in the world. I protest to our anti-American philosophers that what Dr. Hernández says about the great excess of merit of the polyglot over the nightingale is quite true, and in agreement with the judgment of the Europeans who have been in Mexico, and with that of the Mexicans who have been in Europe. Besides the singular sweetness of its song, the

## prodigious

(ff) The author of a certain metaphysico-political Dissertation on the proportion of talents, and their use, in which he wrote such nonsense about America, and showed himself as ignorant of the land, the climate, the animals, and the men of that New World as a child. The other is the author of certain pretty little Italian fables, in one of which he puts an American bird conversing with a nightingale.

(gg) In its cages, in which it is kept, it sings most sweetly; and there is no bird or animal whose voice it does not reproduce most splendidly and imitate most exquisitely. What more? It surpasses by a long interval our nightingale, whose most sweet harmony the ancient authors so greatly praise and celebrate, and whatever little birds are heard in our world with sweetest song. Hernández, De Avibus N. Hisp., ch. 30, on Centzontlatole, or Centzontli.

(*) Linnaeus calls the Centzontli Orpheus. Other authors call it Mocqueur, that is, mocker. [p. 136] prodigious variety of its tones, and the graceful aptness in imitating the different voices of the birds and quadrupeds that it hears, (hh) it has over the nightingale the advantage of being less rustic and more common; for its species is one of the most numerous. Were I to reason after the manner of M. de Pauw, I could add, to demonstrate the goodness of the climate of America, that some birds that are not prized in Europe for their song sing quite well in America. The sparrows, says Valdecebro, a European author, which in Spain do not sing, are in New Spain better than goldfinches. (ii)

What we say of songbirds we may likewise say of those that imitate human speech; for there are not in Asia and Africa so many species of parrots, nor so numerous, as in America. (kk)

But since we are on the subject of birds, I wish before putting an end to this article to make an opportune reflection. There is no American animal over which our philosophers make a greater clamor than over the sloth, on account of its stupendous slowness and inability to move. Now what would they say if there were a bird of that kind? This would be without doubt the most irregular animal in the world; since such tardiness or inertia is more unbecoming in a bird than in a quadruped. But where is this bird? In the old continent, and it has been described by Count de Buffon himself, who says that the Dronte, a bird of the East Indies larger than the swan, is among birds what the sloth is among quadrupeds: “It seems

he

(hh) Mr. Barrington, vice-president of the Royal Society of London, says in a curious work of his on the song of birds, presented to that learned academy, that he observed a polyglot that in the space of a single minute imitated the song of the lark, the chaffinch, the blackbird, the sparrow, and the thrush.

(ii) Valdecebro, in the Spanish work entitled Gobierno de las Aves, bk. 5, ch. 29. But we have already said in bk. 1 of the History that the Mexican sparrows, although resembling true sparrows, are of a different species.

(kk) There is in America a great abundance of parrots, especially in the Andes of Peru, and in the islands of Puerto Rico and Santo Domingo. Acosta, bk. 4, ch. 35. On the Mexican coasts of the Pacific Ocean the number of parrots is even greater than in those islands, [p. 137] he says, a tortoise clothed in the spoils of a bird, and Nature, in granting it these useless ornaments (of the wings and of the tail), seems to have wished to add encumbrance to heaviness, and the irregularity of its movements to the inertia of its body, and to make its heavy bulk more repulsive by reminding it that it is a bird.”

From what we have said up to this point it is evidently concluded that neither is the sky of America niggardly, nor is its climate contrary to the generation of animals: that neither has matter been scarce there, nor has Nature made use of a different scale of size: that it is an error, or rather a heap of errors, all that Count de Buffon and M. de Pauw say about the smallness, the irregularity, and the defects of the American quadrupeds, and even if it were true, nothing of it would help them to demonstrate the malignity of the climate of America; but now let us see whether they do less wrong to the New World in what they say about the alleged degradation of the quadrupeds transported there from Europe.

§ II.

On the European Animals Transported to America.

“All the animals transported to America, such as horses, asses, bulls, sheep, goats, hogs, and dogs,” says Count de Buffon, (ll) “are considerably smaller there than in Europe, and this without any exception.” If we seek the proof of so universal an assertion, we shall find no other, in all the Natural History of that philosopher, than that of the cows, sheep, goats, hogs, and dogs being smaller in Canada than in France. “The European or Asiatic animals,” says M. de Pauw (mm), “transported to America, immediately after

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                 S                        its

(ll) Hist. nat., vol. 18.

(mm) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1. [p. 138] its discovery, have degenerated, their bodily frame has shrunk, and they have lost a part of their instinct and of their character: the cartilages and the fibers of their flesh have become stiffer and coarser.” Such is M. de Pauw’s general conclusion; let us now see the proofs. 1. Beef is so fibrous that it can hardly be eaten on the island of Hispaniola. 2. The hogs on the island of Cubagua changed their form in a short time to such a degree that they could not be recognized: their hooves grew so much that they were half a palm in length. 3. Sheep suffered a great alteration in Barbados. 4. Dogs brought over from our countries lose their voice and cease to bark in the greater part of the regions of the new continent. 5. The cold of Peru disordered the organs of generation in the camels brought there from Africa. Such are the arguments these philosophers rely on to promote the degradation of the animals of the old continent in America: arguments such that, even if they were true, they would not suffice to prove so universal a conclusion; for what does it matter that beef is so fibrous on the island of Hispaniola, if in almost all the other countries of America it is good, and in many, as in all those of Mexico situated on the coasts of the Pacific Ocean, it is as excellent as the best in Europe, and perhaps even more so? What does it matter that sheep have suffered some alteration in Barbados and in some countries that are too hot, if in the temperate countries of Mexico and of South America they are preserved just as they were when they passed over from Spain? What does it matter that hogs have been disfigured in Cubagua, a wretched little island, without water and without everything necessary to life, if in the rest of America they have acquired, as the same M. de Pauw says, an extraordinary bodily frame, and their flesh has been so greatly perfected that physicians prescribe it to their patients in preference to every other meat? Now, if the disfigurement of the hogs in Cubagua does not prove that the climate of America is not the most suitable for them, why should the damage suffered by the sheep in Barbados, the greater fibrousness of the flesh of oxen in Hispaniola,

and [p. 139] and the slight shrinking of some quadrupeds in Canada, prove that the climate of America in general is contrary to the generation of animals, to their bodily frame, and to their instinct?

If such logic were tolerable, we could employ far better arguments against the climate of the old continent, without having to use any other materials than those which Count de Buffon himself furnishes us in his Natural History. Camels, as the same author says, have not been able to multiply in Spain, although that climate, among all those of Europe, is the least contrary to their nature. Oxen have degenerated in Barbary, and in Iceland they have lost their horns. Sheep, says Count de Buffon, have degenerated from their first state in our countries, and in all the hot countries of the old continent they change their wool into hair. Goats have grown smaller in Guinea and in other countries. Dogs in Lapland have become very small and very deformed, and those of temperate climates, when they are carried into cold climes, cease to bark, and after the first generation are born with erect ears. “From the accounts of travelers it is established that mastiffs, greyhounds, and other breeds of European dogs, carried to Madagascar, to Calicut, to Madura, and to Malabar, degenerate after the second or third generation, and that in excessively hot countries, such as Guinea and Senegal, this degeneration is more rapid; for hardly have three or four years passed when they lose their hair and their voice.” Deer in hot, dry, mountainous countries, such as Corsica and Sardinia, have lost half of their bodily frame. If to these and other facts which Count de Buffon gives us we wished to add those furnished by a great many other authors, what an abundance of examples of the degeneration of animals in the old continent we should have, far greater and truer than that of our philosophers! But so that the exaggeration and falsity in their examples may be seen, let us examine one by one all the species of Asiatic and European animals transported into the New World which they say have degenerated.

S2                        Of

[p. 140]

## OF THE CAMELS

“Of all the quadrupeds transported to America,” says M. de P., “those that have succeeded least have undoubtedly been the camels. At the beginning of the sixteenth century some were brought from Africa to Peru, where the cold disordered their organs of reproduction, and they left no posterity.” But, passing over for the moment the chronological error into which he falls, as one that matters little to our purpose, (nn) if it was the cold that destroyed the species of camels in America, the same would happen in Europe, especially in the northern countries, where the cold is beyond comparison greater than in any country of Peru. If the cold was the cause of their extinction, let M. de Pauw blame those who established those quadrupeds in places not suited to their nature, and not America, in which there are hot and dry lands such as are required for the subsistence of camels. The same experiment that was made in Peru with camels was also made in Spain, and proved similarly fruitless; and yet there will be no one to doubt that the climate of that peninsula is among the most temperate and the mildest in Europe. Count de Buffon says that those quadrupeds could be propagated no less in America than in Spain, if all the necessary precautions were taken; and I do not doubt that in the districts of New Galicia they would do quite well. For the rest, it is false that the camels transported to Peru left no posterity; for Father Acosta, who went there some years afterward, testifies that he saw them multiplied, though little. (oo)

O F T H E OX E N .

This is one of those species of animals which our philosophers

(nn) The transport of camels to Peru was not made, nor could it have been made, at the beginning of the sixteenth century, because at that time that country had not yet been discovered; but toward the middle of that century, as Herrera attests in his Decades.

(oo) Nat. and Mor. Hist., bk. 4, ch. 33. [p. 141] believe to be degraded in America, and to which the climate is supposed to be contrary. But even if in Canada oxen have perhaps lost a part of their bodily frame, as Count de Buffon affirms, and if in Hispaniola their flesh has become more fibrous, as M. de Pauw would have it, at least this is not so in the greater part of the countries of the New World, in which the multitude and the size of those animals and the goodness of their flesh show how favorable those climates are to their generation. Their prodigious multiplication in those countries is attested by a great many European authors, ancient as well as modern. Father Acosta relates (pp) that in the fleet which came from New Spain to Old Spain, and in which he returned to Europe in 1587, that is, about sixty years after the first bulls and cows had been transported to Mexico, 64,360 ox hides were carried from that country, and from Hispaniola alone, which M. de Pauw believes so contrary to the generation of these quadrupeds, 35,444. I do not doubt that if one compared the number of bulls and cows carried from the old continent to the new with the number of hides that America has sent in return to Europe, more than five million hides would be found for each of those animals. Valdecebro, a Spanish Dominican who lived some years in Mexico toward the middle of the last century, relates as a notorious thing that Don Juan Orduña, a Mexican knight, was given thirty-six thousand calves in one year for his cows: (qq) which could not have happened except in a herd of two hundred thousand bulls and cows. Today there are private individuals who own 50,000. But nothing else shows more clearly the stupendous multiplication of such quadrupeds than their selling so cheaply in those countries, where they are necessary for the sustenance of men and for the labors of the field, and where, because of the abundance of

## silver

(pp) Bk. 4, ch. 33.

(qq) Valdecebro, in the Spanish work entitled Gobierno de animales, bk. 4, ch. 34. [p. 142] silver everything sells dear, (rr) and, to put it in a few words, bulls have multiplied in Mexico, in Paraguay, and in other countries of the New World more than in herd-abounding Italy. (ss)

As for the size of American oxen, it is quite easy to obtain information about it, since ships loaded with ox hides often put in at Cadiz and at Lisbon. (*) Let M. de P., then, or someone else of those who maintain the degradation of European animals in the New World, have fifty or a hundred hides measured; and if they are found smaller than the common oxen of Europe, we shall at once confess that the climate of America has shortened their bodies and has been sparing of matter there; otherwise they will have to confess that their information is false, their observations ill founded, and their system fantastic: and so that it may be seen how much reason we have for not trusting their information, Gonzalo de Oviedo, who was one of the early settlers of the island of Hispaniola, or Santo Domingo, and lived there some years, speaking of the oxen of that island, whose flesh cannot be eaten, according to M. de P., because it is too fibrous, says (tt) that the herds

are

(rr) In the environs of Mexico, capital of New Spain, although they are very populous, a good pair of oxen for the plow sells for ten sequins, and bulls wholesale for 45 paoli each; in the environs of Guadalajara, capital of New Galicia, a good pair of oxen is worth from 6 to 7 sequins, a cow 25 paoli, and a calf of more than two years 10 or 12 paoli. In many other countries of that kingdom all these animals sell for much less. In very many places in the provinces of the Río de la Plata a cow is to be had for five paoli. According to the account given me by a very experienced and truthful person, in the provinces of the aforesaid river there will be nearly five million oxen reduced to herds, and it is believed that of wild ones there will be about two million.

(ss) Timaeus, a Greek author, and Varro, cited by Aulus Gellius (Noct. Attic., bk. 11, ch. 1), said that Italy was so called because of the abundance of oxen, which in the ancient language of the Greeks were called ἰταλοί: whence Gellius affirms that Italia means most abounding in herds.

(*) Everyone knows that there is no country that does a greater trade with Spain in ox hides than Paraguay, whence ships come loaded with them. I too know, from information received from a person familiar with that country and quite worthy of trust, that the hides which are carried from there to Spain are at least three varas (a Spanish measure) long, and many as much as four, that is, more than ten Paris feet. I do not believe there are three countries in Europe in which oxen sometimes reach so immoderate a size.

(tt) Sumario of the Natural History of the Indies, ch. 2. [p. 143] are here larger, and much finer than all those of Spain; and since the air in those parts is mild and never cold, the oxen never grow lean, nor is their flesh ever of bad taste. Count de Buffon affirms that cold countries are more suitable to oxen than hot ones; but this is not so in New Spain; for although the oxen of cold and temperate countries are good there, those of the hot countries are nonetheless better. The flesh of these animals in the maritime lands, which are very hot, is so excellent that it is sent as a gift to the capital even from places 250 and 300 miles distant from it.

O F T H E S H E E P.

Count de Buffon confesses (uu) that sheep have done very well both in the hot countries and in the cold ones of the new continent; but he adds that although they have multiplied greatly, they are nevertheless leaner, and their flesh is less juicy and less tender, than in Europe: in which he shows that he was badly informed. In the hot countries of the New World sheep for the most part do not do well, and the flesh of wethers is bad: at which there is no cause for wonder, since the hot climate is so contrary to them even in the old continent, as Count de Buffon himself says, that under it they are covered with hair instead of wool. In the cold and temperate climes of New Spain they have multiplied in proportion even more than bulls: their wool is in many places as fine as that of the sheep of Spain, and their flesh as good as the best in Europe, as those Europeans can testify who have been in those countries. The multiplication of sheep in America has been astonishing. Father Acosta testifies (vv) that before he went there there were well-to-do men in America who possessed seventy and even a hundred thousand sheep, and today

## there

(uu) Hist. nat., vol. 18.

(vv) Nat. and Mor. Hist., bk. 4, ch. 33. [p. 144] there are in New Spain some who have four hundred, five hundred, and even seven hundred thousand. (xx) Valdecebro says (yy) that Don Diego Muñoz Camargo, a Tlaxcalan noble, of whom we have made mention in the Notice of the writers of the Ancient History of Mexico, had from ten sheep alone forty thousand in ten years. Now how could those animals multiply so excessively if the climate were contrary to their generation? As for their size, I protest sincerely that I have so far seen in Europe no rams larger than those of Mexico.

## O F T H E G OAT S 

Count de Buffon himself, although he commits himself so strongly to disparaging the animals of America, nevertheless confesses that goats have done well in the American climates, and that their multiplication there is much greater than in Europe; (zz) for whereas in Europe they bear in each birth a single kid, or at most two, in America they bear three, four, and sometimes five. M. de P., who worthily gives Count de Buffon the title of Pliny of France, and wants one to defer to his authority in the matter of animals, as to one who has made a review of all the animals of the earth, ought to have weighed these and other confessions of that learned philosopher before setting himself to write about American animals.

## OF THE HOGS

Our philosophers are not in agreement on this article; for whereas Count de Buffon puts hogs among the animals

degraded

(xx) Those Europeans who have never been in America do not wish to believe what we tell them about the number of oxen, horses, sheep, and goats that many American lords have on their estates; but if it were not true, we would not dare to publish it before so many who could give us the lie.

(yy) In the work entitled Gobierno de animales, bk. 4, ch. 34. Valdecebro’s report on the multiplication of Camargo’s sheep has been confirmed by several other historians of Mexico.

(zz) Hist. nat., vol. 18. [p. 145] degraded in America, M. de P. on the contrary affirms that these are the only animals which have acquired in the New World an extraordinary bodily frame, and whose flesh has been perfected. This contradiction arose undoubtedly from not distinguishing, as they ought, the different countries of America. It may be that there are some, though I do not know of them, in which hogs have lost a part of their size; but it is certain that in New Spain, in the Antilles, in Tierra Firme, and in other countries of America they are as large as those of Europe, and on the island of Cuba there is a breed of hogs twice as large as the European: which is constant and notorious to all those who have been in those countries. Our philosophers can, if they please, get information from several European authors who have seen the hogs of Toluca and of Puebla in New Spain, of Cartagena, of Cuba, etc., about their excessive multiplication and the excellence of their flesh. (Aa)

## OF THE HORSES AND THE MULES

In nothing else of all that Count de Buffon and M. de Pauw say against the American animals do they do a greater wrong to America and to the truth than in supposing the horses too to be degraded there. Of these Father Acosta says (Bb) “that in many countries of America, or in the greater part, they have turned out, and do turn out, quite well, and there are some breeds as good as the best of Spain, no less for the course and for show than for journeys and toils.”

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                T                         Such

(Aa) It is enough to read what Father Acosta wrote in bk. 4, ch. 38 of his History: “It is certain,” he says, “that hogs have multiplied in great abundance everywhere in America. In many countries their flesh is eaten fresh, and is esteemed as wholesome as that of mutton, as in Cartagena.… In some places they are fattened with maize, and become excessively fat. In others excellent lards and hams are made of them, as in Toluca of New Spain, and in Paria.” Count de Buffon, in the same vol. 18, in which he puts hogs among the animals degenerated in America, says expressly that the hogs transported to America have done very well there.

(Bb) Nat. and Mor. Hist., bk. 4, ch. 33.

[p. 146]

Such testimony of a European so critical, so impartial, and so familiar with the affairs of America and of Europe is worth far more than all the declamations of those philosophers against the New World. Lieutenant General Don Antonio de Ulloa, a learned Spanish mathematician still living, (Cc) speaks with amazement of the American horses that he saw in Chile and in Peru, and praises highly the Chilean ones for their gait, those that are called Aguilillas for their extraordinary speed, and those called Parameros for their stupendous agility in the running they do in the hunting of deer, with the horsemen on their backs, over the slopes and over the roughest and steepest places of the mountains. He testifies that he made many times, on one of the horses called Aguilillas, which, he adds, was not among the swiftest of its breed, more than fifteen miles in 57 or 58 minutes. In New Spain there is an indescribable quantity of horses and mules. Their multitude may be conjectured from their price, for whereas in the time of the conquest an ordinary horse was worth as much as a thousand scudi, today a good one is acquired for ten or twelve scudi. (Dd) Their size is that of the common horses of Europe; seldom is a horse seen in Mexico as small as the Slavonian ones that we see in Italy, and much less like those of Iceland and of other northern countries according to Anderson, or those of India according to Tavernier and other authors. Their strength is such that it is quite common among the inhabitants of those countries to ride seventy or eighty miles, and sometimes even more, going all the while at a good pace, without ever stopping or changing horses, over roads that are often difficult. Saddle horses, although for the most part geldings, have a stupendous fire. The mules, which in all that kingdom serve for coaches and for the pack, are also, as regards size

## such

(Cc) A Voyage to South America, pt. 1, bk. 6, ch. 9.

(Dd) In New Galicia a middling horse is had for two sequins, a mule for three, or two and a half; and a herd of twenty-four mares with their stallion for twenty-five sequins. In Chile a trotting horse, of those most prized by the countrymen for their strength and their great agility in running, can be had even for half a sequin, that is, for one scudo, and a mare is usually bought for twenty-five baiocchi. [p. 147] such as the common ones of Europe. Those for the pack, which travel in a train, carry about five hundred pounds on their backs; they do not make more than twelve or fourteen miles a day, according to the custom of those carriers; but in this manner they make journeys of eight hundred, a thousand, and even fifteen hundred miles. Those for the coach go at the pace of the posts of Europe, although the coaches carry a much greater weight because of the baggage of the passengers, and the same mules do so on very long journeys, going at least thirty miles each day. Saddle mules are used for the longest journeys. It is common to make on a mule the journey from Mexico to Guatemala, of about a thousand miles, over a road that is largely mountainous and bad, covering thirty or more miles each day. All this that I have adduced to expose the error of our philosophers concerning the alleged degradation of those quadrupeds is public and notorious in that kingdom, and agrees with the report that several European authors give of it. But nothing, in my opinion, shows more clearly the multitude and the excellence of American horses than an observation I have made. Among the many things that the Spaniards settled in America send for from Spain at any cost, out of the love they keep for their native land, I do not know (at least with regard to Mexico) that in the last two hundred years they have ever had horses brought from Spain; and on the contrary I am sure that they have often sent American horses to Spain to be presented to the grandees of the court, and even to the Catholic King himself.

## OF THE DOGS

Among the great absurdities published by M. de Pauw, which are not few, a very great one is that which he writes about dogs. Dogs, he says (Ee), transported from our countries lose their voice at once, and cease to bark in the greater part of the regions

T2                         of

(Ee) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1. [p. 148] of the new continent. Americans have, on other counts, too much to laugh at in M. de Pauw’s work; but on reading this passage about dogs they will laugh uproariously. Even if we conceded to M. de P. that for the most part dogs have degenerated, nothing would be concluded against that new continent that could not equally be concluded against the Old World; since, as Count de Buffon affirms, dogs transported from temperate climates into the cold ones of the old continent lose their voice, and transported into excessively hot countries lose, besides their voice, their hair also. This assertion of Count de Buffon rests on the experiment made with European dogs transported to Asia and to Africa, whose degeneration, the aforesaid philosopher says, is so rapid in Guinea and in other excessively hot countries that after three or four years they remain quite mute and hairless. M. de P. does not dare to say as much of dogs transported to America; but even what he does affirm is entirely false; for which are those countries of America where dogs have lost their voice? On the faith of what authors has he dared to publish such a fable? The greater part of the American countries to which European dogs were transported are subject to the Catholic King, and in none of them has such a misfortune befallen dogs. Neither among the European authors who have noted the particularities of America, nor among the very many Americans who are here at present, come from all the countries of Spanish America, have I found even one who confirms M. de P.’s anecdote. What we do know, however, both from several writers on America and from several persons acquainted with those countries, from whom we have informed ourselves, is that dogs never have rabies in Peru, in Quito, in Chile, and in other districts of that New World. If perhaps in the dominions of England or of France there is some country (which I do not believe) in which dogs have become mute, must it nevertheless be said that they have lost their voice in the greater part of the regions of the new continent? Did M. de P. perchance read that in some countries of America there were dogs that did not bark, and was that enough for him to publish

## that

[p. 149]

that European dogs transported to America promptly lost their voice. He might similarly say that figs transported from Europe to America at once become thorny, because the Nochtli, or prickly pear (Tuna), which by some resemblance to the fig was called by the Spaniards fig of the Indies, has thorns, just as the Techichi was called by them little dog of Mexico because it resembles little dogs; but neither is this quadruped a true dog, nor is that fruit a true fig. It is far too easy to fall into such errors when ideas are not regulated, nor passions moderated. Count de Buffon on the contrary affirms (Ff) that European dogs have done well both in the hot countries and in the cold ones of the New World: in which he undoubtedly grants a great advantage to the climate of America over that of the old continent.

O F T H E C AT S .

Our philosophers say nothing in particular about the degeneration of cats in America; but they must be understood as included in their universal assertions. Nevertheless Count de Buffon, who in the passage cited above admits no exception to what he says about the degeneration of animals in America, speaking afterward of cats in particular, after having praised those of Spain as the best of all, affirms that these Spanish cats transported to America have kept their fine colors, and have not degenerated at all (Gg).

These are the quadrupeds transported (Hh) from the old to the new continent, all of which, except the camels, have multiplied excessively and have preserved unaltered their bodily frame, their figure, and all the perfections

of

(Ff) Hist. nat., vol. 10.

(Gg) Hist. nat., vol. 11.

(Hh) Count de Buffon adds to the aforesaid quadrupeds transported to America the Guinea hog and the rabbit; but he affirms that these two species have done well. As for rats, it would certainly be a great good for America if they could not live under that climate. [p. 150] of their ancestors, as is established partly from the confession of those same philosophers, partly from the deposition of European authors who are impartial, judicious, and familiar with those countries, and partly also from the notoriety to which we appeal without fear of being given the lie. We do not doubt that impartial readers will recognize, from what we have sincerely set forth thus far, the errors and contradictions of our philosophers, caused by the ridiculous determination to defame the New World, the falsity of their observations, the groundlessness of their reasonings, and the rashness of their censure.

(c) Chinche in Spanish is the name of the bedbug: whence it may be that the name of this insect was also given to the polecat on account of that intolerable stench which it gives off from its hind parts; but I do not doubt that Count de Buffon rather altered the name Chinghe, by which the polecat is known in Chile; for we do not find the name Chinche used in any country of America to signify that quadruped.

(d) See what we said in bk. 1 of the History regarding the name Coaso.

(e) The anteater is called by the Spaniards Oso ormiguero, that is, ant-bear, although it is perhaps more different from the bear than the dog is from the cat. Count de Buffon distinguishes three species of it in America: the first is called by him simply Fourmillier, or anteater; the 2nd Tamanoir, and the 3rd Tamandua. The Peruvians call them Hucumari, the people of Quito Huauniri and Cuckichi, the Tamanacs on the Orinoco Uaracà. In Brazil they call the large anteaters Tamanduà-guazu, and the small ones Irara and Guatimondè.

V2                    Vison

(o) I willingly use the name Tapir, because it is already in use among modern zoologists, and moreover is not equivocal. That of Granbestia is proper to the elk; that of Anta, or Danta, is also given to the Zebu, a quadruped of Africa, quite different from the tapir.

(p) I do not know whether the Tuza is of the same species as that quadruped which the Peruvians call Tupu-tupu.

(q) Count de Buffon doubts whether the Aluata, a cercopithecus also large, is of the same species as the Uarina; but I grant him that it is in fact of the same species, and therefore I do not put the Aluata (in Count de Buffon Alouate) in this catalogue.

[p. 156]

Vison, or American beech marten. 27. Uistiti, a species of small cercopithecus. 30. Unau, a species of sloth without a tail (r). 26. Fox, common. 14. Urson, a quad. of the cold countries similar to the beaver, but different. 25. Zorrillo, or Zorriglio, a species of polecat. (s) 27.

Thus Count de Buffon, who found in all America no more than seventy species of quadrupeds, in the course of his Natural History recognizes and distinguishes at least ninety-four. I said at least; for to the aforesaid there ought to be added those of the common hog, of the ermine, and others, which, although denied to America by Count de Buffon in one place of his History, have nevertheless been granted to it in other places.

§ II.

Species Confused by Count de Buffon with Other, Different Ones.

The Guanaco with the Llama, or Gliama. (t) The Vicugna with the Paco. The Citli with the Tapet, or Tapiitì. (u) The Huitztlacuatzin, or Mexican porcupine, with the Cuandù, or porcupine of Guiana. (x) The Tlacocelotl with the Ocelotl. (y)

The

(r) Count de Buffon rightly distinguishes two species of sloths, the one furnished with a tail, and the other without it; for besides this they have other different characters. In Quito they call sloths Quillac, or Quiglac, and on the Orinoco Proto. The Spaniards call them Pereza, that is, Sloth, and Perico ligero, that is, Swift Dog, by antiphrasis.

(s) Zorrillo, that is, Little Fox, is the generic name which the Spaniards, and other countries, give to polecats. The Mexicans call them Epatl. In Chile Chinghe, and in other countries of South America Mapurito, Agnatuja, etc.

(t) Besides other grounds of difference among the Gliama, the Guanaco, the Vicugna, and the Paco, they have never been seen to couple with one another, even when put together in one place. Now if this suffices to conclude the specific difference between the dog and the wolf, quadrupeds otherwise very similar both in their external features and in their internal organization, what shall we say of these four quadrupeds, which differ from one another more than the dog does from the wolf?

(u) To make oneself certain of the difference between the Citli and the Tapete it is enough to compare the descriptions which Dr. Hernández and Count de Buffon give of the one and the other.

(x) See what we said in bk. 1 of the History regarding the difference between the Mexican porcupine and that of Guiana.

(y) Count de Buffon wants to persuade us that the Tlacocelotl and the Ocelotl are one single [p. 157] The Tepeitzcuintli, or mountain dog of Mexico, with the glutton. (z) The Xoloitzcuintli, or hairless dog, with the wolf. The Itzcuintepotzotli, or humped dog, with the Alco, or Techichi. These eight species, therefore, which he has confused with others, must be

added to the ninety-four above, and they will make a hundred and two.

Brisson, and others make mention.

Cojote animal: that this is the male and that the female; that Ocelotl is the same name as Tlacocelotl contracted. Thus we might say that the Latin Canis is not different from Semicanis, and that Tygris is the same as Semitygris; since the Mexican Ocelotl means the same as Tygris, and Tlacocelotl means nothing other than Semitygris. Count de Buffon cannot be blamed for not knowing Mexican; but neither can he be excused for having dared to decide on what he does not know. Dr. Hernández, who had those two beasts before his eyes and observed them as a naturalist, deserves without doubt greater credence.

(z) On the difference among these last three quadrupeds, see what we said in Dissertation IV.

(A) I said in a note of mine placed in bk. 1 of the History that the Amiztli seemed to me to be the same quadruped that Count de Buffon calls Saricovienne; but having reflected on it anew, I have found those two quadrupeds specifically different.

[p. 158]

Cojote (in Mexican Cojotl), a beast described by us. Rabbit, common, called by the Mexicans Tochtli. Cui, or Peruvian rabbit, a small quad. very similar to the guinea pig, of which

several historians of Peru make mention. Culpeu, a particular species of large fox of Chile. Hist. of Chile. Degu, or dormouse of Chile. Hist. of Chile. Hog Seal, or sea hog, an amphibious animal, a particular species of seal of

Chile. Hist. of Chile. Ferret of Chile and of Paraguay, called in Guaraní Jaguarobape. Nat. Hist. of

Chile, and MS. in my possession. Honey Cat. So the Spaniards call a quad. of the province of Chaco in South

America, similar in form to the cat, which hunts birds in the trees and is
