very fond of the honey of the bees. MS. in my possession.

Guanque, a species of blue field mouse of Chile. Nat. Hist. of Chile. Horro, a large cercopithecus of Quito and of Mexico, entirely black except

the neck, which is white. It cries loudly in the forests, and standing upright

on two feet it has the height of a man. MS. in my possession. Huemul, or cloven-hoofed horse of Chile. Hist. of Chile. Jaguaron, in Guaraní Jagua-rú, an amphibious beast of Paraguay, called by

some the water tiger. MS. in my possession. Kiki, a quad. of Chile of the weasel kind. Hist. of Chile. Majan, a quad. similar to a little pig, which has a round body and curly bris‐

tles. It lives in Paraguay. MS. in my possession. Pisco-Cushillo, that is, bird cercopithecus, a cercopithecus of Quito,

which is covered from the neck to the tail with a certain kind of feathers.

MS. in my possession. Hedgehog, common in Paraguay. MS. in my possession. Mouse, most common in America before the Spaniards landed there, and

called by the Mexicans Quimichin. Hist. of Mexico. Field Mouse, common in Mexico and in other countries of America. Tajè, a quad. of California, of which mention is made both in the printed

History and in the manuscript accounts of that peninsula. The Tajè is with‐

out doubt the ibex of Pliny, described by Count de Buffon under the name

of Bouquetin. Taitetù, a quad. of Paraguay of the hog kind, whose female always bears two

young, which are born joined to each other by the umbilical cord. MS. in

my possession. White Badger of New York, described by M. de Brisson. Thopel-Lame, an amphibious quad. of the sea of Chile, a species of seal

much more like the lion than the one seen by Lord Anson. Nat. Hist. of

Chile.

Tlalcojote

[p. 159]

Tlalcojote, in Mexican Tlalcojotl, a quad. common in Mexico, de‐

scribed by us in bk. 1 of the History. White Rat, a field rat common in Mexico. Rat, common, a field rat common in Mexico and in other countries of

America. Maule Rat, a quad. of that province in the kingdom of Chile very simi‐

lar to the marmot, but twice as large as it. Nat. Hist. of Chile. Trefle, that is, Trefoil, a large quad. of North America, described by M.

de Bomare. Viscacha, field, a quad. similar to the rabbit, but furnished with a great

tail bent back upward. Acosta and other historians of South America. Viscacha, mountain, a very handsome quad. of the same kind as the

field viscacha, but of a different species. MS. in my possession. Usnagua, or nocturnal cercopithecus of Quito. MS. etc.

These forty species added to the hundred and two mentioned above make a hundred and forty-two species of American quadrupeds. If to these, then, are added those of the horses, asses, bulls, sheep, goats, common hogs, Guinea hogs, dogs, cats, and domestic rats, brought there after the conquest, we shall have at present in America as many as a hundred and fifty-two species. Count de Buffon, who in all his Natural History never counts more than two hundred species of quadrupeds in the countries of the world discovered up to now, enumerates three hundred in his new work on the Epochs of Nature. So much did that number grow in a few years! But let them be three hundred: behold, America, although it makes up no more than a third of our globe, has nonetheless at least half the species of quadrupeds. I say again, at least; for I have left out all those of which I doubt whether they are different from those described by Count de Buffon. The principal aim I set myself in the compiling of this Catalogue was not to demonstrate the error of Count de Buffon in his enumeration of American quadrupeds, and the falsity of what he wrote about the imaginary scarcity of matter in the New World; but rather to serve European naturalists, by pointing out to them some quadrupeds hitherto unknown, and by smoothing somewhat the difficulties that a misunderstood nomenclature has caused. These gentlemen would wish that the names of those quadrupeds were accompanied by an exact description, and I would gladly oblige them as far as possible, were it not foreign to my purpose. In making this catalogue, besides the great study I have devoted to it, I have obtained written information from learned, accurate, and experienced persons of various countries of America, to whose singular kindness I confess myself exceedingly obliged.

Dissertation

[p. 160]

## D I S S E RTAT I O N V

## ON THE PHYSICAL AND MORAL

## CONSTITUTION OF THE MEXICANS

F    our classes of men may be distinguished in Mexico and in other

countries of America. 1. The Americans proper, commonly called Indians, that is, those who descend from the ancient settlers of that New World and have not mixed their blood with that of the peoples of the old continent. 2. The Europeans, Asiatics, and Africans established in those countries. 3. The children or descendants of these, who are called by the Spaniards Criollos, and whom we shall call Creoles, although that name is given chiefly to those children or descendants of Europeans whose blood has not been mixed with that of Americans, Asiatics, or Africans. 4. The mixed races, called castas by the Spaniards, that is, those who are born of, or descend from, a European man and an American woman, or a European and an African woman, or an African and an American woman, etc. All these classes of men are defamed and abused by M. de P. He supposes, or feigns, the climate of the New World to be so malign that it causes to degenerate not only the Creoles and the Americans proper born there, but even the Europeans who inhabit those countries, although they were born under a milder sky and a climate more favorable, as he believes, to all animals. If M. de P. had written his Recherches philosophiques in America, we might rightly suspect the degeneration of the human species under the American climate; but since we see that this work, and very many others of the same caliber, are produced in Europe, we are the more confirmed in the truth of that Italian proverb taken from the Greeks, All the world is one country. But leaving aside now the absurdities of that philosopher and of his partisans against the other classes of men, we shall argue only about those that he wrote against the Americans proper, since these people

are [p. 161] are both the most abused and the most defenseless. If in writing this dissertation we were moved by any passion or interest, we should rather have undertaken the defense of the Creoles, as that which, besides being far easier, ought to concern us more. We are born of Spanish parents, and have no affinity or consanguinity with the Indians, nor can we hope for any reward from their misery. So that no other motive than the love of truth and zeal for humanity makes us abandon our own cause to defend another’s with less danger of erring.

§ I.

On the Bodily Qualities of the Mexicans.

M. de P., who censures the stature, the form, and the alleged irregularities of the American animals, has not been more indulgent toward men. If the animals seemed to him a sixth part smaller than those of Europe, men are still, by his account, smaller than the Castilians. If in the animals he noted the lack of a tail, in men he likewise censures the lack of hair. If in the animals he found notable deformities, in men he reviles the color and the features. If he believed that the animals there were less strong than those of the old continent, he likewise affirms that the men there are extremely weak, and that they are subject to a thousand maladies caused by the corruption of that air and of that stinking soil.

Concerning the stature of the Americans he says in general that, although it is not equal to that of the Castilians, there is little difference. But we are entirely sure, and it is notorious throughout New Spain, that the Indians who inhabit those countries, that is, those that lie from the ninth degree to the fortieth of north latitude, as far as the discoveries of the Spaniards have reached, are more than five Paris feet in height, and that those who do not exceed that measure are far rarer among the Indians than among the Spaniards. We are moreover certain that many

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.           X                       of [p. 162] of those nations, such as the Apaches, (*) the Yaquis, the Pimas, and the Cochimís, are at least as tall as the tallest Europeans, and we do not know that in all the vast extent of the New World there is any people, except the Eskimos, whose stature is as small as that of the Laplanders, the Samoyeds, and the northern Tartars in the old continent. Hence in this respect the inhabitants of both continents are equal.

As to the regularity and proportion of the limbs of the Mexicans, there is no need to say more than we have said in bk. I of our History. We are persuaded that among those who will read this work of ours in America, there will be none to contradict the description of the form and character of the Indians, unless he has his eyes in the back of his head or his wits turned. It is true that Don Antonio de Ulloa says (a), speaking of the Indians of Quito, that it has been observed “that among them the imperfect abound, either because they have bodies irregular and monstrous by reason of their smallness, or because they become witless, mute, or blind, or because some limb is lacking to them;” but having made inquiries into this singularity of the people of Quito, I have learned, from information given me by trustworthy persons well acquainted with those countries, that such defects are caused neither by bad humors nor by the climate, but by the misguided and cruel humanity of their parents, who, in order to spare their children the burdens and labors to which the healthy Indians are subjected by the Spaniards, deliberately make them useless or imperfect: which does not happen in other countries of America, nor even in those places of the same kingdom of Quito where the Indians are free from those labors. M. de P., and after him Dr. Robertson, say that among the savages of America no deformed are to be found; because they put to death, as the Lacedaemonians once did, those infants

who

(*) What we say of those nations of North America could also be said of the Chileans, the Patagonians, and other peoples of South America.

(a) A Voyage to South America, etc. [p. 163] who are born hunchbacked, blind, or lacking some limb; but that in those countries in which they have been brought into society, and where the vigilance of those who govern them does not permit such infanticides, the number of irregular individuals is greater than in any country of Europe. This would be a fine way out of the difficulty, if it were true; but if by chance there has been in America some tribe of savages that imitated the barbarous example (**) of the much-renowned Lacedaemonians, it is certain that those authors have no right to impute such inhumanity to the rest of the Americans; since it has not been in use, at least among the greater part of those nations, as can be demonstrated by the testimony of the writers best instructed in their customs. Moreover, in all the countries of Mexico, or New Spain, which make up at least a fourth part of the new continent, the Indians live united in society, and gathered in cities, towns, and villages, under the care of Spanish or Creole magistrates and parish priests: no examples of cruelty toward tender children are ever seen or heard of there, and notwithstanding this, the deformed are so rare there that all those Spaniards and Creoles who came from Mexico to Italy in 1768 were then, and are still today, amazed to observe in the cities of this most civilized peninsula so great a number of blind, hunchbacked, lame, crippled, etc. The cause, then, of that phenomenon observed by so many writers among the Americans is quite different from what the aforesaid authors imagine.

As for the color of the American peoples, no argument can be drawn from it against the New World; because that color is less distant from the white of the Europeans than from the black of the Africans and of a great part of the Asiatics. The hair of the Mexicans, and of the greater part of the Indians, is, as we have

X2                        said

(**) That inhumanity of killing children who were born deformed was not only permitted in Rome but was even prescribed by the law of the Twelve Tables: Let the father quickly kill a boy conspicuous for deformity. [p. 164] said elsewhere, thick and coarse, their hair sparse on the face, and for the most part (b) none on the arms and legs; but it is an error to say, as M. de P. says, that they are entirely without hair on all the other parts of the body. This is one of those many passages of the Recherches philosophiques at which the Mexicans and other American nations will not be able to restrain their laughter, on seeing a European philosopher so intent on stripping them of what they have received from nature. He no doubt read that ignominious description which Señor Ulloa gives of some peoples of South America, (c) and from this particular premise drew, according to his logic, that universal conclusion.

The mere sight of some Angolan, Mandinga, or Congo ought to have frightened M. de P., and deterred him from the censure that he passes on the color, the features, and the hair of the Americans. What can be imagined more opposed to the idea that we have of the beauty and perfection of the human body than a stinking man whose skin is black as ink, his head and face covered with black wool instead of hair, his eyes yellowish or bloodshot, his lips thick and blackish, and his nose flattened? Such are the inhabitants of a very great part of Africa, and of several islands of Asia. What men are more imperfect than those who are no more than four feet in stature, with a long, flat face, a flattened nose, the iris of the eyes blackish yellow, the eyelids drawn back toward the temples, the cheeks excessively raised, the mouth too large, the lips thick and prominent, and the lower part of the face too narrow? Such are, according to Count de Buffon, (d) the Laplanders,

the

(b) I said for the most part; because there are in Mexico American peoples who are bearded and furnished with hair on the arms and legs.

(c) Señor Ulloa, in the description he gives of the Indians of Quito in vol. 1 of the Account of his Voyage in America, says that hair does not grow on either the men or the women, as it does on the rest of mankind, when they reach puberty; but whatever may be the truth of this singularity of the people of Quito, and of its cause, there is no doubt that among the generality of Americans puberty is accompanied by the same symptoms as among the other nations of the world.

(d) Hist. nat., vol. 6. [p. 165] the Zemblans, the Borandians, the Samoyeds, and the Eastern Tartars. What objects can be more deformed than men whose face is too broad and wrinkled even in their youth, the nose flattened and thick, the eyes small and sunken, the cheeks too high, the upper part of the jaws depressed, the teeth long and disunited, the eyebrows so heavy that they encumber the eyes, the eyelids thick, a few bristles on the face instead of a beard, the thighs large, and the legs small? Such is the portrait that Count de Buffon draws of the Tartars, that is, of those peoples who, as he himself says, inhabit a tract of country in Asia more than twelve hundred leagues long and more than seven hundred and fifty wide. Among these the Kalmyks are the most notable for their deformity, which is so great that they are, in Tavernier’s words, the ugliest men in the whole universe. Their face is so broad that there is a space of five or six fingers between the two eyes, according to the same Count de Buffon. In Calicut, in Ceylon, and in other countries of India there is, say Pyrard and other writers of those regions, a race of men who have one of the legs, or both, as thick as the body of a man, whose deformity is hereditary among them. The Hottentot women have, besides other great imperfections, that monstrous irregularity of a callous appendage which extends downward from the pubic bone, as the historians of the Cape of Good Hope bear witness. Marco Polo, Struys, Gemelli, and other travelers affirm that in the kingdom of Lambry, in the island of Formosa, and in that of Mindoro there are found men furnished with tails. M. de Bomare (e) says that such a tail in those men is nothing but an elongation of the coccyx bone; but what is the tail in quadrupeds if not an elongation of that bone, though divided into several articulations? (f) Whatever the case may be, it is certain that that elongation is as good as a true tail for making those Asiatics too irregular.

If

(e) Dictionn. d’Histoire natur., s.v. Homme.

(f) See Heister in his Comp. Anat., de Ossibus trunci.

[p. 166]

If we wished to run through the other nations of Asia and Africa in the same manner, we should hardly find among them any but a small part that is not of a darker color, and in which there are not to be seen more enormous irregularities and greater defects than any that M. de P. censures in the Americans. The color of the latter is far lighter than that of almost all the Africans and of the inhabitants of southern Asia. Scantiness of beard is common to the inhabitants of the Philippine Islands and of the whole Indian Archipelago, to the famous Chinese, to the Japanese, to the Tartars, and to many other nations of the old continent, as is known to all those who have some acquaintance with the variety of the human species in the different countries of the earth. The imperfections of the Americans, however great they may wish to represent them, are certainly not to be compared with the defects of those immense peoples whose character we have sketched, and of others which we pass over. (g) All this ought to have held back the pen of M. de P., but he had forgotten it, or else maliciously dissembled it.

M. de P. represents the Americans as weak and sickly; Señor Ulloa, on the contrary, affirms (h) that they are healthy, robust, and strong. Which of the two will deserve greater credence from us: M. de P., who from Berlin set himself to philosophizing about the Americans without knowing them, or Señor Ulloa, who for some years saw them and associated with them in various countries of South America? M. de P., who took it upon himself to vilify and debase them in order to establish his absurd system of degeneration, or Señor Ulloa, who, though otherwise little favorable to the Indians, did not trouble himself to form any system, but only to write what he judged true? The impartial reader will decide this doubt.

To demonstrate the weakness and the disorder of the physical constitution of the Americans, M. de P. alleges several reasons,

which

(g) See vol. 6 of the Nat. Hist. of Count de Buffon; likewise the historians of Asia and of Africa.

(h) A Voyage to South America, vol. 1, bk. 5, ch. 5. [p. 167] which we must not conceal, and they are these. 1. That the first Americans taken to Europe became enraged on the voyage, and their rage lasted until death. 2. That grown men in many countries of America have milk in their breasts. 3. That the American women give birth with too great ease, have an extraordinary abundance of milk, and that in them the periodic evacuation of blood is scanty and irregular. 4. That the least vigorous European could beat any American whatever in wrestling. 5. That the Americans could not bear the weight of a light burden. 6. That they were subject to venereal disease and to other endemic maladies.

As for the first proof, we deny it as absolutely false and unsound. M. de P., on the authority of the Fleming Dappers, says that the first Americans whom Columbus brought with him in 1493 tried to kill themselves during the voyage; but that, when they were tied up in order to preserve them, they became enraged, and their rage lasted as long as they lived; and that when they entered Barcelona they so terrified the citizens with their howls, their contortions, and their convulsive movements that they were taken for madmen. I have not seen the work of Dappers; but I do not doubt that his whole account is a heap of fables; for I have found no one who mentions that event, either among the contemporary authors or among those who wrote in the years that followed; indeed, from what these testify, the falsity of that account can be demonstrated. Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo, who was in Barcelona at the time when Columbus arrived there, and saw and knew those Americans, and was an eyewitness of what took place, says nothing of their rage, or of their howls and contortions, and would not have omitted it had it been true, being rather unfriendly to the Indians, as we shall say elsewhere, and speaking so particularly of their entry into that city, of their baptism, of their names, and in part of their end. He says, then, (i) that Columbus brought with him from the island of Hispaniola ten Americans,

of

(i) General History of the Indies, bk. 2, ch. 7. [p. 168] of whom one died on the voyage; three remained ill at Palos, a port of Andalusia, where, as he conjectures, they died soon after, and the other six went to Barcelona, where the Court then was: that they were well instructed in the Christian doctrine and solemnly baptized, the Catholic Kings and Prince Don Juan being their godfathers: that the chief of them, who was a relative of King Guacanagarí, took in baptism the name of the Catholic King, and was called Don Fernando de Aragón, and the second was given the name of the Prince, being called from then on Don Juan de Castilla, who was lodged by the Prince in his palace, and the latter had him instructed: that he learned the Spanish language quite well, and died two years afterward. Peter Martyr d’Anghiera, who was likewise in Spain when Columbus arrived there, also makes mention (k) of the Indians whom that famous Admiral brought with him, and says not a word of their rage; indeed, he relates that when Columbus returned to Hispaniola he took back with him three of those Indians, since all the others were already dead from the change of air and of food, (l) and that he availed himself of one of them to inform himself of the state in which the Spaniards were whom he had left on that island. Ferdinand Columbus, a learned and diligent writer of the life of Christopher Columbus his father, who was likewise at that time in

Spain,

(k) Summary of the History of the West Indies, ch. 4.

(l) To the causes of the death of those Americans adduced by Peter Martyr should be added the extraordinary hardships they suffered on that dreadful voyage, the circumstances of which may be read in the same letters of the Admiral Columbus reported by his learned son Don Ferdinand. From the number of the dead mentioned by Peter Martyr must be excepted that American whom Prince Don Juan kept with him; for he did not die until two years afterward, as Oviedo bears witness. But even if they had all died on the voyage, or become frenzied or maniacs, it would not be a matter for wonder, considering what M. de Pauw himself relates in pt. 3, sect. 2 of his Recherches: The French Academicians, he says, carried off beyond Torneo two Laplanders, who, beset and martyred by those philosophers, died of despair on the way. Now neither the country that those Laplanders were leaving, nor the journey they had to make, can be compared with the country and the voyage of those Americans; nor can I believe the Spanish sailors of the fifteenth century as humane as the French Academicians of the eighteenth.

[p. 169]

Spain, gives a minute account of the voyages and actions of his glorious father, speaks of those Indians seen by him, and says nothing other than what Peter Martyr relates. Either, then, the account of Dappers is false, or else we shall say that the Catholic Kings wished to hold enraged men at the baptismal font, that the Prince wished to have an enraged man with him in order to amuse himself with his frightful howls, that an enraged man learned the Spanish language quite well, and finally that the prudent Columbus made use of an enraged man to inform himself of all that had happened in Hispaniola while he was absent.

The anecdote of the milk in the breasts of the Americans is one of the most curious that are to be read in the Recherches philosophiques, and one of the most worthy of being celebrated with our laughter and with that of all the Americans; but it must be confessed that M. de P. showed himself in this more moderate than other authors cited by him. The celebrated naturalist Jonston affirms in his Thaumatographia, on the authority of I know not what travelers, that in the New World almost all men abound in milk in their breasts. In all Brazil, says the author of the Historical Researches, the men alone nurse the infants; because the women have scarcely any milk. Oh, what fine materials for a Thaumatography! For my part I truly do not know which I ought to admire more, the rashness and effrontery of those travelers who peddle such fables, or the excessive simplicity of those who adopt them. If there were ever in the New World some people among whom such a phenomenon had truly been observed (which M. de P. will never be able to verify), that certainly would not be sufficient to say that in many places of America milk abounds in the breasts of men, and much less to affirm it, as Jonston affirms it, of almost all the men of the New World.

Those singularities that M. de P. notes in the American women would be most agreeable to them if they were true; for what could they desire more than to see themselves free of those great pains and torments that they suffer in giving birth, of abounding in that liquor with which they must nourish their children, and of sparing themselves

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.         Y                        in [p. 170] in great part the discomforts that that periodic and loathsome evacuation causes them? But what they would regard as a great happiness is reckoned by M. de P. an argument for degeneration; for that ease in giving birth shows, he says, the expansion of the vaginal canal, and the relaxation of the muscles of the womb on account of too copious fluids: the abundance of milk can derive only from the moisture of their complexion, and besides, they do not conform to the women of the old continent; since this, according to the legislation of M. de P., must be the model of the whole world. But who will not be astonished that, whereas the author of the Historical Researches notes in the American women such a scarcity of milk that on account of it the men are obliged to nurse their children themselves, the author of the Recherches philosophiques, on the contrary, finds in them so extraordinary an abundance of that liquor? And who will there be that, in reading these and other like contradictions and tall tales published in Europe, especially for a few years past, does not perceive that the European travelers, historians, naturalists, and philosophers (*) have made America the storehouse of their fables and their idle chatter, and, to render their works more entertaining by the wondrous novelty of their supposed observations, attribute to all the Americans what has been observed in some individuals, or in none?

The American women, being subject to the common sentence, do not give birth without pain; but neither with all the apparatus of the European women; because they are less delicate and more accustomed to hardships. Thévenot says that the Mogul women give birth with the greatest ease, and that the next day they are seen walking through the streets of the cities, and yet their fecundity is not on that account to be contradicted, nor their complexion blamed.

The quantity and the quality of the milk of the American women are

well

(*) What I say of the European writers on the things of America I do not wish to be understood of all; for among them there are men truly wise and lovers of the truth. [p. 171] well known in Mexico and in other countries of America to European and Creole ladies, who commonly take them as nurses for their children; because they know well that these women are healthy, and very faithful and diligent in that office. Nor is it of any avail to say that one is speaking of the ancient American women and not of the modern ones, as M. de P. sometimes replied to his adversary Dom Pernety; for, besides the fact that his propositions against America concern almost entirely the present, as is known to those who have read his work, that distinction has no place in many countries of America, and particularly in Mexico. The Mexicans use for the most part the same foods that they used before the conquest. The climate, if it has perhaps changed in other regions through the felling of the woods and the draining of stagnant waters, is in Mexico without doubt the same. Those who have compared, as I have done, the accounts of the first Spaniards with the present state of that kingdom know with the greatest certainty that the same lakes, the same rivers, and for the most part the same woods still exist.

Concerning the menses of the American women I can give no account, nor do I know that there is anyone who can give it. M. de P., who from Berlin has seen so many things in America that the very inhabitants of those countries do not see, will perhaps have found in some French author the way of knowing what we can neither inquire into nor wish to. But granted that the menstrual evacuation of the American women has been as scanty and irregular as M. de P. will have it, nothing could be concluded against their complexion; for the quantity of such evacuation depends, as Count de Buffon rightly says, (m) on the quantity of nourishment and on insensible perspiration. Women who eat too much and take little exercise have very abundant menses. In warm countries, where perspiration is more copious than in cold ones, that evacuation is scantier. Now if the scantiness of such an evacuation can proceed from sobriety in diet, from the heat of the climate, and from exercise, why

Y2                          is

(m) Nat. Hist., vol. 6. [p. 172] is it to be offered as an argument of a bad complexion? Besides, I do not know how to reconcile that scantiness of the menses with that superabundance of fluids which M. de P. supposes in the American women, as a source of the disorder of their physical constitution.

The aforesaid proofs of the weakness of the Americans are no more effective. M. de P. says that they were beaten by any European whatever in wrestling, and that they gave way under a moderate weight: that, the computation having been made, it was found that two hundred thousand Americans perished in a single year in the transport of baggage. As to the first, it would be necessary that the experiment of wrestling had been made among many individuals of the one continent and the other, and that the victory were attested no less by the Americans than by the Europeans. But however that may be, I do not claim to make the Americans appear stronger than the Europeans. They may be less strong without being positively weak, and without the human species having degenerated in them. The Swiss are stronger than the Italians, and yet we shall not believe the Italians degenerate, nor shall we blame the climate of Italy. The example of the two hundred thousand men dead in a single year under the weight of baggage, if it were ever true, would not so much prove the weakness of the Americans as the inhumanity of the Europeans. As those two hundred thousand Americans perished, so would two hundred thousand Prussians have perished, had they been forced to make a journey of three hundred, of four hundred, and more miles with a hundred pounds of weight on their backs: had they, with iron collars put on them and joined by thick chains, been obliged to carry that load over mountains and cliffs, and had those who grew tired or broke their feet had their heads cut off, so that they should not delay the pace of the others: had they been given nothing but a very scanty food to sustain so intolerable a toil. The same author (*) from whom M. de P. took that computation of two hundred thousand Americans dead under the weight of baggage reports also all the aforesaid circumstances: wherefore, if he gives him credence

in

(*) Monsignor de las Casas. [p. 173] in that, he must give him it in this also. But a philosopher who so vaunts the physical and moral qualities of the Europeans above those of the Americans ought rather to refrain from mentioning those deeds that are so disgraceful to those very Europeans. It is true that neither Europe nor any nation of it can be charged with those excesses into which some of its individuals fall, especially in countries so far from the metropolis, and against the express will and the repeated orders of their sovereigns; but if the Americans wished to use the logic of M. de Pauw, they could deduce from such particular antecedents universal consequences against the whole old continent, since he forms at every third word arguments against the whole New World from what has been observed in some people, or in some individual, as may be seen in reading his work.

He concedes to the Americans great agility and swiftness in running, because as boys they accustomed themselves to that exercise; he must therefore not deny them strength either; since, as is evident from their History and from their very paintings, scarcely did the children begin to walk when they were put to the exercise of carrying burdens, in which they were to employ themselves all the time of their life: indeed, according to his principles no other nation ought to be more vigorous at carrying burdens; because no other exercised itself so much as the Americans in bearing loads on their backs, on account of their lack of the beasts of burden (n) which other nations use. If M. de Pauw had seen, as I have seen, the enormous weights that the Americans carry on their shoulders, he would not have had the courage to reproach them with weakness.

But nothing shows so clearly the robustness of the

Americans

(n) Although the Peruvians had beasts of burden, they were not such that they could be used for the transport of those great stones that were found in some of their edifices, as also in those of Mexico: so that, since they had no machines either to ease such transport, it had to be done by the strength of men alone.

[p. 174]

Americans as those many and great labors in which they are continually employed. M. de Pauw says (o) that when the New World was discovered nothing was to be seen there but thick woods: that today there are some cultivated lands there, but by Africans and Europeans, not by Americans; and that the cultivated land, in relation to the uncultivated, is in the proportion of two thousand to two millions. (p) These three assertions are so many errors; but reserving for another dissertation what pertains to the labors of the ancient Mexicans, and speaking only of later times, it is certain that since the conquest the Americans alone have been those who have borne all the labors of agriculture in all the vast countries of the continent of North America, and in the greater part of those of South America, subject to the Crown of Spain. A European is never seen employed in the labors of the field. The Blacks, who in the vast kingdom of New Spain are very few in comparison with the Americans, are charged with the cultivation of sugar cane and tobacco, and with the manufacture of sugar; but the land destined for the cultivation of those plants is not, in relation to all the cultivated land, even in the proportion of one to two thousand. The Americans are those who work the land there. They alone are the plowmen, the sowers, the weeders, and the reapers of wheat, of maize, of rice, of the broad bean, of beans, and of the other grains and legumes; of cacao, of vanilla, of cotton, of indigo, and of all the other plants useful for the sustenance, the clothing, and the commerce of those provinces, and without them nothing is done, to such a degree that in the year 1762 the harvest of wheat was abandoned in many places because of the sickness there was, which did not permit the Indians to do the reaping. But this is little: they are those who cut and transport

## from

(o) Défense des Recherches, ch. 12.

(p) It would have been better to say, in the proportion of one to a thousand, since this is the same with simpler numbers. [p. 175] from the woods all the necessary timber; those who cut, transport, and work the stones, and those who make the lime, the plaster, and the bricks. They are the ones who build all the edifices of that kingdom, except in a few places where they do not dwell. They are the ones who open and repair the roads, those who make the ditches and the weirs, and those who clean the cities. They work in very many mines of gold, of silver, of copper, etc. They are the shepherds and the herdsmen, the weavers, the potters, the bread sellers, the bakers, the couriers, the porters, etc. In a word, they are the ones who bear the whole weight of the public labors, as is notorious in all the provinces of that great kingdom. This the weak, the idle, and the useless Americans do, while the vigorous P. and other tireless Europeans occupy themselves in writing invectives against them.

These labors, in which the Indians are continually employed, show their health and robustness; for they could not bear such great fatigues if they were sickly, and if a spoiled blood ran through their veins, as M. de P. will have it. To make their complexion believed vicious, he alleges all that, whether true or false, which he could find in the writers on America concerning the diseases that reign in certain particular countries of that great continent, and especially concerning the venereal disease, which he believes to be truly American. Now as to the venereal disease, we shall discuss it at length in another dissertation; as for the other diseases, I grant him that in the vast extent of America there are some countries in which men are more than elsewhere exposed to certain maladies caused either by the inclemency of the air or by the bad quality of the foods; but it is certain, on the testimony of many weighty authors experienced in the New World, that the American countries are for the most part healthy, and that if the Americans wished to pay M. de P. back in kind, and the other Europeans who write as he does, they would have a far greater abundance of good materials for discrediting the climate of the old continent, and the complexion of its inhabitants, in so many maladies

endemic [p. 176] endemic there, such as elephantiasis and the leprosy of Egypt and Syria, (q) the Verben of southern Asia, the dracunculus, or Medina worm, the Pircal of Malabar, the Yaws, or Guinea disease, the Tiriasi, or pedicular disease of Little Tartary, scurvy, and the boreal dysentery of the northern countries, the Plica of Poland, the goiter of the Tyrol and of many Alpine countries, the itch, rickets, smallpox, (r) and above all the plague, which so many times has depopulated very many cities and whole provinces of the old continent, and which every year makes a great slaughter in the East: a terrible scourge, from which the New World has so far been preserved.

Finally, the alleged weakness and vicious habit of body of the Americans cannot be reconciled with their long life. Among those Americans whom great labors and excessive toils, or else epidemic diseases, do not carry off early, there are not a few who reach eighty, ninety, and a hundred years, and, what is more astonishing, without showing in them that ravage which time commonly makes in the hair, the teeth, the skin, and the muscles of the human body. This phenomenon, so much admired by the Spaniards inhabiting Mexico

cannot

(q) Elephantiasis, a disease endemic in Egypt and wholly unknown in America, was so common in Europe in the thirteenth century that there were, according to Matthew Paris, an exact writer of that time, nineteen thousand hospitals.

(r) Smallpox was brought to America by the Europeans, as everyone knows, and it has wrought there a far greater slaughter than the venereal disease has wrought in Europe. Rickets, or knotting of the joints, is not known in the New World, and this is in my judgment the principal reason why there is not there that number of imperfect men which is to be seen in Europe. The itch either does not exist there or is so rare that, having been so many years in several countries of Mexico, I never saw anyone with the itch, nor did I learn that there was any. The vomito prieto, which seems likewise an endemic ailment, is quite modern, and is suffered only in some ports of the torrid zone frequented by Europeans. The first to feel it were the sailors of some European ships, who, after the bad food of the voyage, ate the fruit of the country greedily in those ports and drank brandy. Don Antonio Ulloa affirms that in Cartagena, one of the most unwholesome places in America, the vomito prieto was not known before the year 1729, and that it began with the seamen of the European fleet which arrived there that year under the command of Don Domingo Justiniani. [p. 177] cannot be ascribed to anything but the soundness of their constitution, their sobriety in diet, and the salubrity of the climate. The historians, and other persons who have lived there some years, report the same of other countries of the New World. But if perhaps there is some region in which their life is not so greatly prolonged, at least there is none in which it is so shortened as in Guinea, in Sierra Leone, at the Cape of Good Hope, and in other regions of Africa, where old age commonly begins at forty, and he who reaches fifty is regarded as an octogenarian would be among us. (f) Of these, indeed, it might rightly be said that they have corrupted blood and a physical constitution thrown into disorder.

§ II.

On the Souls of the Mexicans.

So far we have examined what M. de Pauw says on the bodily qualities of the Americans. Let us now see the absurdities he has written against their souls. In them he has been able to find nothing but a memory so weak that today they do not remember what they did yesterday, an intelligence so dull that they are incapable of thinking or of putting their ideas in order, a will so cold that they do not feel the stimuli of love, a worthless spirit, and a dull-witted and indolent genius. Finally, with such colors he paints the Americans, and so debases their souls, that although at times he inveighs

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                Z                      against

(f) The Hottentots, says Count de Buffon, live a short time, for they scarcely pass the age of forty. Drake testifies that certain peoples who inhabit the borders of the deserts of Ethiopia, because of the scarcity of provisions feed on salted locusts, and that so bad a diet causes them a terrible effect, namely that when they approach the age of forty certain flying insects are generated in their bodies, which soon bring them death by devouring first the belly, then the breast, and finally even the bones. These insects, like those by which the inhabitants of Little Tartary are devoured, according to the admission of the same M. de Pauw, are enough for the Americans to repay him more than amply for those ascarid worms which he says he found among I know not what peoples of America. [p. 178] against those who called their rationality in question, I do not doubt that if he had then been consulted, he would have declared against the opinion of the Rationalists. I know well that many other Europeans, and, what is more astonishing, many even of those sons or descendants of Europeans who were born in America itself, think as M. de Pauw does, some from ignorance, others from want of reflection, and others from a certain passion or hereditary prejudice. But all this, and much more besides, would not suffice to belie our own experience and the testimony of other Europeans, whose authority is worth much more, both because they were men of great judgment, learning, and acquaintance with those countries, and because they testified in favor of strangers against their own countrymen. The testimonies and the reasons we could adduce in favor of the souls of the Americans are so many that a large volume could be made of them; but leaving aside for now the greater part, so as not to make this dissertation too diffuse and tedious, we shall content ourselves with a few witnesses, who are worth a thousand.

Juan de Zumárraga, first bishop of Mexico, a prelate of happy memory, and held in the highest esteem by the Catholic Kings for his learning, for his spotless life, for his pastoral zeal, and for his apostolic labors, in his letter written in the year 1531 to the General Chapter of the Franciscan Fathers assembled at Toulouse, speaks thus of the Indians: “They are chaste, and very ingenious, especially in the art of Painting. They have been endowed with good souls. Praised be the Lord in all things.”

If M. de P. does not value the testimony of this most venerable prelate, whom he calls Sumarica and barbarian by reason of that authority which he has arrogated to himself to insult those whose sentiments are not in conformity with his absurd system of degeneration, let him read what Monsignor Bartolomé de las Casas, first bishop of Chiapas, wrote of the Americans; he knew them very well, as one who had had great familiarity with them for so many years in various countries of America. This prelate, then,

in [p. 179] in a memorial of his presented to Philip II speaks of them thus: “They are likewise (the Americans) of clear and lively minds, very docile and capable of every good doctrine, most apt to receive our holy Faith and virtuous customs, and, of all the peoples of the world, those who have the least impediment thereto.” Almost the same terms he uses in his refutation of the replies of Dr. Sepúlveda: “The Indians,” he says, “have a mind so good and an intelligence so acute, and so much docility and capacity for the moral and speculative sciences, and are for the most part so reasonable in their political government, as is seen in many of their most just laws, and they have so advanced in the things of our holy Faith and Religion, in good customs, and in correction, wherever they have been instructed by religious and persons of good life, and they advance today as much as any other nation since apostolic times.” Now since M. de Pauw believes all that this learned, exemplary, and indefatigable prelate wrote against the Spaniards, although he was not present at the greater part of the facts he relates, he will have to believe much more what the same bishop, as an eyewitness and one so experienced, deposed in favor of the Americans; for far less is needed to persuade us that the Americans are of good intelligence and good disposition than to make us believe those horrible and unheard-of outrages of the Spanish conquerors.

But if he still will not admit the testimony of that famous Bishop, because he considers him, though quite wrongly, an intriguer and ambitious, let him read the deposition made by Monsignor Julián Garcés, first bishop of Tlaxcala, a most learned man, and justly esteemed and praised by his famous master Antonio de Nebrija, restorer of letters in Spain. This distinguished prelate, in his weighty Latin letter to Pope Paul III, written in 1536, after ten years of continual familiarity with the Americans and eyewitness observation of them, among the many praises with which he celebrates their good disposition and the endowments of their souls, praises their intelligence, and in some fashion raises it above

Z2                    that [p. 180] that of his Spaniards, as can be seen in the passage of that letter which we copy below. (t) Who, then, will not give greater credence to these three venerable bishops, who, besides the merits of their probity, their learning, and their character, had also that of long familiarity with the Americans, than to so many other writers, who either never saw the Americans, or saw them without reflection, or deferred more than was fitting to the information of men who were ignorant, or prejudiced, or interested?

But if M. de P. in the end rejects the deposition of these three witnesses, however authoritative, because they were religious, of whom he believes weakness of mind to be characteristic, he cannot fail to yield to the judgment of the famous Bishop of Puebla, Monsignor Palafox. M. de P., although a Prussian and a philosopher, himself calls that prelate a venerable Servant of God. (u) Now if he gives so much credence to this Venerable servant of God in what he wrote against the Jesuits in his own cause, why should he not believe him in what he wrote in favor of the Americans? Let him read, then, the work composed by that prelate to demonstrate the disposition, the intelligence, and the virtues of the Indians. (x)

Despite the implacable hatred that M. de Pauw bears toward the ecclesiastics of the Roman Church, and above all toward the Jesuits, he nevertheless praises Acosta’s Natural and Moral History

and

(t) “But now, concerning the intelligence of these men one by one, whom we have seen for the past ten years, during which, living in their country, I have been able to examine their manners and search into their talents, testifying before you, Most Blessed Father, who act as the Vicar of Christ on earth, to what I have seen, what I have heard, and what our hands have handled concerning these children begotten to the Church through whatever ministry of mine in the word of life: that, referring each to each, that is, like to like, they are possessed of excellent reason and of sound sense and mind; but moreover the boys of these people are found to surpass those of our own country in vigor of spirit and in a more adroit liveliness of the senses, in everything that is to be done and to be understood.” This letter is found in Latin in the first volume of the Mexican Councils published in Mexico in the year 1769, and in French in the very History of America of Father Touron, which M. de P. cites against the Americans.

(u) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 6, letter 4.

(x) A work of Monsignor Palafox entitled Las virtudes del Indio, o Naturaleza, y costumbres de los Indios de la N. Esp., and often printed. [p. 181] and rightly calls it an excellent work. (y) Now this judicious, impartial, and most learned Spaniard, who saw and observed with his own eyes the Americans both in Peru and in Mexico, devotes the whole sixth book of that excellent work to demonstrating the good reason of these same Americans by an exposition of their ancient government, of their laws, of their histories in paintings and cords, of their calendars, etc. To be informed of his judgment on this matter it suffices to read the first chapter of that book. I beg both M. de Pauw and my readers to read it attentively; for there are things in it worth knowing. In it M. de P. will recognize the origin of the error into which he and very many Europeans have fallen, and he will perceive the great difference there is between seeing things with eyes darkened by some passion and examining them with judgment and impartiality. M. de P. holds the Americans to be brutish; Acosta, on the contrary, holds those who think so to be foolish and presumptuous. M. de P. says (z) that the shrewdest Americans were inferior in industry and sagacity to the crudest nations of the old continent; Acosta extols with praises the political government of the Mexicans above that of many republics of Europe. M. de P. finds in the reasonable and political conduct of the Americans nothing but barbarity, extravagance, and brutishness, and Acosta finds in it admirable laws, worthy of being preserved even in their Christianity. Which of these two authors shall we believe? The impartiality of our readers will decide this problem.

Meanwhile I cannot excuse myself from copying here a passage of the Recherches philosophiques, in which this author shows himself no less slanderous than an enemy of the truth. “At first,” he says, “the Americans were not believed to be men, but rather Satyrs or great apes, who could be killed without remorse or reproach. At last, to add the ridiculous to the calamities of these times, a Pope issued

an

(y) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1.

(z) Recherch. philos., pt. 5, sect. 1. [p. 182] an original bull, in which he declared that, desiring to found bishoprics in the richest regions of America, it pleased him, and the Holy Spirit, to recognize the Americans as true men: so that without this decision of an Italian the inhabitants of the New World would still today be in the eyes of the faithful a race of equivocal men. There is no example of such a decision since this globe has been inhabited by men and by apes.” God grant that there were not in the world another example of such calumnies and insolences as those of M. de P.; but in order that his malignity may be rendered more manifest, we shall give a copy of that papal decision after setting forth the reason for it.

Some of those first Europeans who settled in America, no less powerful than greedy, wishing to enrich themselves ever more at the expense of the Americans, kept them continually occupied and used them as slaves; and to avoid the reproaches that the bishops and missionaries made to them, that they should treat those peoples with humanity and leave them at least some time to be instructed in religion and to satisfy their obligations toward the Church and toward their families, they promoted the notion that the Indians were servants by their nature, and that they were incapable of instruction, and other such absurdities, of which the Chronicler Herrera makes mention. Now since those zealous ecclesiastics could not, either by their authority or by their preaching, withdraw those wretched neophytes from the tyranny of those greedy men, they had recourse to the Catholic Kings, and at length obtained from their equity and clemency those laws so favorable to the Americans, and so honorable to the Court of Spain, which are read in the Indian Code (*), and which were chiefly owing to the indefatigable zeal of Monsignor de las Casas. On the other hand Monsignor Julián Garcés, Bishop of Tlaxcala, knowing that those Spaniards, in spite of their wickedness, bore great respect to the decisions of the Vicar of Jesus Christ, had recourse in the year 1536 to Pope Paul III with that famous

letter

(*) Nueva recopilación de las leyes de Indias. [p. 183] letter, of which we have made mention, representing to him the evils that those wicked Christians made the Indians suffer, and begging him to interpose his authority. The Pope, moved by such grave remonstrances, dispatched the following year that original bull, a faithful copy of which we give here below: (A) which was not made, as is manifest, to declare the Americans true men; for this would be a folly far removed from such a Pontiff, and from any other; but only to uphold the natural rights of the Americans against the attempts of their persecutors, and to condemn the injustice and inhumanity of those who, under the pretext that those men were idolaters, or incapable of instruction, took from them their goods and their liberty, and made use of them as of beasts. The Spaniards, in truth, would have been even more witless than the crudest savages of the New

## World

(A) “Pope Paul III, to all the faithful of Christ who shall see these present Letters, health and apostolic benediction. — Truth itself, which can neither deceive nor be deceived, when it appointed the Preachers of the Faith to the office of preaching, is known to have said: Going, teach ye all nations: all, it said, without any selection, since all are capable of the discipline of the Faith. Seeing and envying which, the enemy of the human race, who always opposes good works that they may perish, devised a method hitherto unheard of, to hinder the Word of God from being preached to the Gentiles that they might be saved: and he stirred up some of his satellites, who, desiring to satisfy their own cupidity, presume to assert everywhere that the Indians of the West and of the South, and other peoples who in these times have come to our knowledge, are, under the pretext that they are strangers to the Catholic Faith, to be reduced to our service like brute animals, and they reduce them to servitude, pressing them with such afflictions as they scarcely inflict on the brute animals that serve them. We therefore, who hold on earth the place of the same our Lord, though unworthy, and with all our effort seek the Sheep of His flock committed to us that are outside His Fold, to bring them into the same Fold, considering that the said Indians, as being truly men, are not only capable of the Christian Faith but, as has been made known to us, run most promptly to the Faith itself, and wishing to provide suitable remedies in this matter, by Apostolic Authority, through these present letters, decree and declare that the aforesaid Indians, and all other peoples who shall hereafter come to the knowledge of Christians, although they be outside the faith of Christ, may use, possess, and enjoy freely and lawfully their liberty and dominion of this kind, and ought not to be reduced to servitude, and that whatever may happen otherwise is null and void, and that the same Indians and other peoples are to be invited to the said Faith of Christ by the preaching of the Word of God and by the example of a good life, notwithstanding the foregoing and any other things to the contrary whatsoever. — Given at Rome in the year 1537, on the fourth day before the Nones of June, in the third year of our Pontificate.” This, and no other, is that famous bull on account of which so great an uproar has been made.

[p. 184]

World, if in order to recognize the Americans as true men they had had to wait for the decision of Rome. It is certain that long before the Pope dispatched that bull, the Catholic Kings had warmly recommended the instruction of the Americans, had given the most solicitous orders that they should be well treated, and that no wrong should be done them in their goods or in their liberty, (B) and had sent to the New World several bishops and some hundreds of missionaries at the expense of the royal treasury, to preach to those Satyrs the Faith of Jesus Christ and to instruct them in the Christian life. In 1531, six years before that bull came out, the Franciscan missionaries alone had baptized in Mexico more than a million of those Satyrs, as Monsignor Zumárraga attests, (C) and in 1534 the Seminary of Santa Cruz had already been founded in Tlatelolco for the instruction of a good number of Little Apes, where they learned the Latin language, Rhetoric, Philosophy, and Medicine. (D) If the Americans were at first thought Satyrs, no one can say so better than Christopher Columbus, their discoverer. Hear, then, how that celebrated Admiral speaks, in his report to the Catholic Kings Ferdinand and Isabella, of the first Satyrs seen by him in the island of Haiti, or Hispaniola: “I swear to Your Highnesses,” he says, “that there is not in the world a better people than this, nor one so loving, affable, and gentle. They love their neighbors as themselves: their speech is the sweetest, the softest, the gayest, for they always speak smiling, and although they go naked, believe me, Your Highnesses, that they have very praiseworthy customs, and that their King is served with great majesty, and has manners so pleasing that it gives great pleasure to see him, as also to consider the great

## retentiveness

(B) The orders given by the Catholic Kings concerning the conversion of the Americans before that bull, and the laws published by them in favor of those nations, may be seen in the Decades of Herrera and in the Indian Code.

(C) Letter written by Monsignor Zumárraga to the General Chapter of the Franciscans assembled at Toulouse.

(D) Torquemada, in bk. 15, ch. 43 of the Monarquía indiana, relates the solemn founding of the Seminary of Santa Cruz, made by the first Viceroy of Mexico with the participation of two bishops. [p. 185] retentiveness of that people, and the desire to know everything, which impels them to ask the causes and effects of things.” (E) How much better it would be for us if the world were inhabited by such Satyrs, rather than by liars and slanderers! For the rest, since M. de P. spent ten continuous years in researching the things of America, he ought to know that in the countries of the New World subject to the Spaniards no other bishoprics have ever been founded than those which the Catholic King has willed. To him belongs, by the right of patronage that he has in the American churches, granted him as early as 1508 by Pope Julius II, the founding of the bishoprics and the presentation of the bishops. To affirm, then, that Paul III wished to recognize the Americans as true men in order to found bishoprics in the richest regions of the New World is a rash calumny of an enemy of the Roman Church: otherwise, if he did not have his mind so blinded by hatred, he ought rather to praise the zeal and humanity that make that Pope stand out in the aforesaid bull.

Dr. Robertson, who adopts in great part the distorted sentiments of M. de Pauw, speaks thus of the Americans in bk. 8 of his History of America: “Astonished equally at their slowness of comprehension, and at their insensibility, some of the early missionaries pronounced them a race of men so brutish, as to be incapable of understanding the first principles of religion.” But who those missionaries are, and how much weight their verdict deserves, can be learned from no one better than from Monsignor Garcés in the aforesaid letter to Pope Paul III. Let the passage of it, then, which we copy below, (F) be read, in which it is seen that the causes of such an error are the ignorance and the sloth of those missionaries; and I

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.               Aa                          add

(E) Ch. 32 of the History of Don Christopher Columbus, written by his son Don Ferdinand.

(F) “Who with so shameless a mind and so brazen a face dares to assert that those are incapable of the faith whom we see most capable of the mechanical arts, and whom, even when reduced to our service, we find to be of good disposition, faithful, and skilled? And if ever, Most Blessed Father, Your Holiness shall hear that any religious man leans toward this [p. 186] add also the false ideas instilled in them from their earliest age. Monsignor de las Casas, Acosta, and other weighty writers on America say almost the same as Monsignor Garcés.

“A council held at Lima,” continues Dr. Robertson, “decreed, that, on account of this incapacity, they ought to be excluded from the sacrament of the Eucharist. And though Paul III. by his famous bull, issued in the year 1537, declared them to be rational creatures, entitled to all the privileges of christians; yet, after the lapse of two centuries, during which they have been members of the church, so imperfect are their attainments in knowledge, that very few possess such a portion of spiritual discernment, as to be deemed worthy of being admitted to the holy communion… Even after the most perfect instruction, their faith is held to be feeble and dubious; and though some of them have been taught the learned languages, and have gone through the ordinary course of academic education with applause, their frailty is still so much suspected, that no Indian is either ordained a priest, or received into any religious order.”

Behold, in a few words, at least four errors: 1. that a Council of Lima excluded the Indians from the Sacrament of the Eucharist on account of their weakness of mind. 2. that Paul III declared the Indians rational creatures. 3. that very few Indians possess such a portion of spiritual discernment that they can

be

opinion, even if he seems to shine with exceptional integrity of life or with dignity, let Your Holiness not on that account grant him any authority in this matter, but judge it most certain that he has toiled little or not at all in their conversion, and consider that he has devoted little effort to learning their language or to investigating their talents: for those who have labored in these things with Christian charity affirm that they do not cast the nets of charity in vain upon them; but those who, given to solitude or hindered by sloth, have led no one back to the worship of Christ by their industry, in order not to be blamed for having been useless, ascribe to the weakness of mind of the infidels what is the vice of their own negligence, and defend their own real sloth by the false imputation of incapacity, and commit in the excuse no less a fault than that from which they strive to be freed. For that kind of men who assert such things does the greatest harm to this most wretched throng of the Indians: for they hold back some religious men, so that they may not set out to instruct them in the faith: wherefore some of the Spaniards who go to subdue them, relying on their judgment, are accustomed to think that to neglect, destroy, and slaughter them is not a crime.” From the letters of Julián Garcés, Bishop of Tlaxcala, to Paul III, Supreme Pontiff. [p. 187] be judged worthy of approaching the holy table. 4. that no Indian is ever ordained a priest.

As to the first, it is true that in a congregation of ecclesiastics held in Lima in the year 1552, which was called the First Council of Lima, but was neither a Council nor ever had conciliar authority, it was ordered that the Eucharist should not be administered to the Indians until they were perfectly instructed and convinced in the matters of the faith; because that sacrament is the food of the perfect, and not because they were thought weak-minded. This is shown by the testimony of the first Provincial Council (commonly called the Second) held in Lima in the year 1567, which ordered the parish priests to administer that sacrament to all those Indians whom they should find well disposed. (G) But that order not sufficing to bend those ecclesiastics, of which Father Acosta rightly complained, the second Council of Lima, held in 1583, over which St. Toribio de Mogrovejo presided, sought to remedy such disorders by the decrees which we give here below: (H) in which it is seen that equally, and for the same motives, they denied the Eucharist to the Indians and to Black slaves

Aa2                     brought

(G) “Although all adult Christians of either sex are bound to receive the Most Holy Sacrament of the Eucharist every year at least at Easter, yet the prelates of this province, observing that this nation of the Indians is both recent and infantile in the faith, and judging that it was expedient for their salvation, decreed that until they should hold the faith perfectly they should not be communicated with this divine sacrament, which is the food of the perfect, except if anyone should appear sufficiently fit to receive it…. It has pleased this Holy Synod to admonish, as it earnestly does admonish, all parish priests of the Indians, that to all the Indians whom, after hearing their confession, they have perceived to distinguish this heavenly food from other bodily food, and to desire and ask for it devoutly, they administer it at the time when they are accustomed to administer it to other Christians, since we cannot without cause deprive anyone of the divine nourishment.” Council of Lima I, commonly II, ch. 58.

(H) “The heavenly viaticum, which Mother Church denies to no one departing from this life, the Council of Lima many years ago ruled ought to be given to Indians and Ethiopians and other wretched persons. But nevertheless, through the negligence of very many priests, or through a certain preposterous and untimely zeal, it is today no more given to them. Whence it happens that weak souls are deprived of so great and so necessary a good. The Holy Synod, therefore, wishing to carry into execution what has been ordained under Christ’s leadership for the salvation of the Indians, strictly commands all parish priests not to omit to administer the viaticum to Indians and Ethiopians who are in extremity, provided they recognize [p. 188] brought from Africa: that the true causes of denying it were, in the judgment of the Council, the negligence or sloth, and the indiscreet and ill-understood zeal, of those parish priests, and that the Council believed itself obliged to remedy so grave a disorder with new decrees and with severe punishments. I know well that not even these respectable decrees were exactly carried out, and that it was necessary that they be inculcated anew by the diocesan synods of Lima, of La Plata, of La Paz, of Arequipa, and of Paraguay; but this shows more the obstinacy of those ecclesiastics than the incapacity of the Americans.

Concerning the bull of Paul III we have already shown that he did not undertake to declare the Americans men, but, presupposing their rationality, which none could doubt except the beasts, if they were capable of doubts, condemned the injustice of their oppressors.

As for the third error of Robertson noted by me above, leaving aside now what concerns the other countries of America, since it is not necessary, it is certain and notorious that throughout New Spain the Indians, like the Spaniards, are obliged to receive the Holy Eucharist at Easter, except the neophytes of the remote regions, who are admitted, or not, to the holy table according to the judgment of their missionaries. Now in the three audiences into which New Spain is divided there are, says Robertson, at least two millions of Indians. (I) I am sure that such a number is far below the truth; but let it be so much, and no more. It is not, then, very few Indians who possess such a portion of spiritual discernment

as

in them the due disposition, namely faith in Christ and repentance toward God after their own fashion…. Furthermore, let parish priests who from the first promulgation of this decree shall have been negligent know that, besides the judgment of divine vengeance, they will also pay penalties at the discretion of the Ordinaries, whose consciences are burdened with it: and in visitations special inquiry is to be made of them concerning the observance of this statute.” Council of Lima II, commonly III, act 2, ch. 19.

“Let the parish priest not omit to administer the eucharist at least at Easter to those whom he shall have judged both sufficiently instructed and fit by correction of life, lest otherwise he himself also be guilty of a violated ecclesiastical precept.” Ibid., ch. 20.

(I) History of America, bk. 8. [p. 189] as to be judged worthy of approaching the holy table; unless two millions seem very few to Robertson, or he considers rash those many bishops and parish priests who not only admit but even oblige those Indians to communicate. And what will it be if to that number are added the Indians of many provinces of South America, who are likewise obliged to receive the holy Eucharist?

No less gross is his fourth error, in affirming that no Indian is ever ordained a priest. It is astonishing that a writer who amassed so great a library of writers on America, and to whom so many reports on the things of the New World were furnished from Madrid, should have been so ill informed on this point as on others. Let Dr. Robertson know, then, that although the first Provincial Council celebrated in Mexico in the year 1555 forbade that the Indians be ordained, not at all on account of their incapacity, but because it was believed that from the abasement of their condition some infamy would redound to the ecclesiastical state; (*) nevertheless the third Provincial Council, held in 1585, which was the most celebrated of all, and whose decisions are in force, permitted them to become priests, provided great care were taken in admitting them to holy orders. (K) But it must be known that the decrees of the one Council and of the other include equally, and under the same terms, both the Indians and the Mulattoes, that is, those who are born or descend of a European father and an African mother, or the reverse: and yet no one doubts the great talent and capacity of the Mulattoes for learning all the sciences. Torquemada, who wrote his History in the first years of the last century, says (L) that Indians were not wont to be admitted into the religious orders, nor ordained priests, on account of their violent inclination to drunkenness; but he himself testifies that in his time some Indian priests were very sober and exemplary:

so

(*) Council of Mexico, First Provincial, ch. 44.

(K) Council of Mexico III, bk. 1, tit. 4.

(L) Monarquía indiana, bk. 17, ch. 13. [p. 190] so that it has been at least a hundred and seventy years since the Indians began to be made priests. Since then there have been so many American priests in New Spain that they could be counted by the thousands, among whom there have been some hundreds of parish priests, a good many canons, and doctors, (M) and even, as is believed, a most learned Bishop. (N) At present there are very many priests of them, and not a few parish priests, among whom are three or four of my pupils. Now if in a point of this kind Robertson erred so grossly, what will it be with those that cannot so easily be cleared up by an author who writes so far from those countries without ever having seen them.

I, on the contrary, associated intimately with the Americans: I lived some years in a seminary intended for their instruction: I saw the founding and the progress of the Royal College of Guadalupe, established in Mexico by a Mexican Jesuit for the education of Indian girls: I then had some Indians among my pupils: I dealt with many American parish priests, many nobles, and very many artisans: I observed attentively their character, their genius, their inclinations, and their manner of thinking: and besides this I have examined with the greatest diligence their ancient history, their religion, their government, their laws, and their customs. After so great experience, and so lengthy a study, by which I believe myself in a position to decide on these matters with less danger of erring, I protest to M. de Pauw, and to all Europe, that the souls of the Americans are not in the least inferior

to

(M) Among these American doctors, Don Sebastián Grijalva deserves particular mention, a native of Ocozoquauhtla, a large place in the diocese of Chiapas. Having come to Spain, he took a doctorate in Sacred Theology at the famous University of Salamanca, and there acquired a great reputation for his learning. Returning afterward to America, he was made parish priest of his native place, and made there such regulations for the civil and Christian conduct of his fellow citizens that his parish might be the model of all those of America, and the effects are seen to this day. He wrote a learned theological work on the Immaculate Conception of the Blessed Virgin, the original of which was preserved in the library of the College of the Jesuits at Ciudad Real, the capital of that diocese.

(N) Monsignor Juan de Merlo, Bishop of Honduras, and most learned in the sacred canons, who was Vicar General to Monsignor Palafox. I have not been able to find here any author who mentions the birth of that Bishop: but the general opinion holds him to be an Indian. [p. 191] to those of the Europeans: that they are capable of all the sciences, even the most abstract; and that if their education were seriously taken in hand, if from boyhood they were brought up in seminaries under good masters, and if they were protected and encouraged with rewards, there would be seen among the Americans philosophers, mathematicians, and theologians who could vie with the most famous of Europe. But it is very difficult, not to say impossible, to make great progress in the sciences in the midst of a miserable and servile life, and continual hardships. Whoever contemplates the present state of Greece could not persuade himself that there had once been those great men we know of, if he were not assured of it both by their immortal works and by the consent of all the centuries. And yet the obstacles that the Greeks have at present to overcome in order to become learned are not comparable with those that the Americans have always had, and still have. Despite all this I should like M. de Pauw, and as many others as think as he does, to be present, without being observed, at those councils or assemblies which certain Americans who have some appearance of superiority in their villages hold on certain days to deliberate on current affairs, and to hear how those Satyrs of the New World harangue and discourse.

Finally, the whole ancient history of the Mexicans and of the Peruvians shows that they know how to think and to order their ideas: that they are sensible to the passions of humanity, and that the Europeans have had no other advantage over them than that of being better instructed. The political government of the ancient Americans, their laws, and their arts demonstrate evidently their good intelligence. Their wars show that their souls are not insensible to the stimuli of love, as M. de Buffon and M. de Pauw think; since at times they took up arms for amorous interests.

As for their courage, we have candidly set forth, where we discoursed on their character, what we have observed in the present Americans, and what we judge of the ancients. But since M. de Pauw alleges the

conquest [p. 192] conquest of Mexico as a convincing proof of their cowardice, it is fitting to enlighten his ignorance, or rather to convict his bad faith.

“Cortés,” he says, (*) “conquered the empire of the Mexicans with four hundred and fifty vagabonds and fifteen ill-armed horses; his wretched artillery consisted of six falconets, which would not today be capable of frightening a little fort defended by invalids. During his absence he kept the capital in awe with half of his troops. What men! What events!

“It is certain,” he adds, “on the testimony of all the historians, that the Spaniards entered Mexico the first time without firing a single shot of their artillery. If the title of hero belongs to him who has the misfortune to cause the death of a great number of rational animals, Fernando Cortés might claim it: for the rest I do not see what true glory he has acquired in throwing into confusion a tottering monarchy, which likewise might be overthrown by any assassin of our continent.” These passages of the Recherches philosophiques make plain that M. de Pauw was ignorant of the history of the conquest of Mexico, or, what is more likely, maliciously suppressed what openly refuted his system: since all those who have read the aforesaid history know well that the conquest of Mexico was not made with four hundred and fifty men, but with more than two hundred thousand. Cortés himself, to whom it mattered more than to M. de P. to diminish the number of the conquerors, so as to make his prowess appear greater and his conquest more glorious, confesses likewise the excessive number of the allies who were under his orders in the siege of the capital, and fought with greater fury against the Mexicans than the Spaniards themselves. (O) It is evident from the account given by

Cortés

(*) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1.
