(O) See the letter of Cortés to Charles V, written from Coyoacán in the year 1522, as also the True History of Bernal Díaz.

[p. 193]

Cortés to the Emperor Charles V, that the siege of Mexico began with eighty-seven horses, eight hundred and forty-eight Spanish foot soldiers, armed with muskets, crossbows, swords, and lances, and more than seventy-five thousand Tlaxcalan, Huexotzinca, Cholulan, and Chalcan allies, armed with various sorts of arms: with three large cannon of iron, and fifteen small ones of bronze, and with thirteen brigantines. In the course of the siege there joined them the numerous nations of the Otomis, the Cohuixcas, and the Matlatzincas, and the troops of the populous cities of the lakes: so that the army of the besiegers not only exceeded two hundred thousand but reached two hundred and forty thousand, as appears from the same letter of Cortés, and besides this three thousand boats, or canoes, which came to their aid. I ask, then, M. de P., whether it seems to him cowardice to have sustained for fully seventy-five days the siege of an open city, fighting daily with so great an army, and one in part armed with such superior arms, and above all fighting with hunger and with thirst? Do those deserve the reproach of cowards who, after having lost seven of the eight parts of the city, and a hundred and fifty thousand or thereabouts of their citizens, partly killed by the sword and partly dead of hunger and of disease, continued to defend themselves until they were furiously assailed and overpowered in the last corner that remained to them? (P)

It is certain, says M. de P., on the testimony of all the historians, that the Spaniards entered Mexico the first time without firing even a single shot of their artillery. Oh, what a fine argument, certainly proper to the logic of M. de P. If the Mexicans were cowards, because the Spaniards entered Mexico the first time without firing a single shot of their artillery, we could likewise say that the Prussians are cowards, because the ambassadors of several courts of Europe enter Berlin without firing even a musket. Who does not know that the Spaniards were then admitted in that

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.              Bb                      capital

(P) All that we say here concerning the siege and the conquest of Mexico is taken from the letter of the Conqueror Cortés to Charles V. [p. 194] capital as ambassadors of the monarch of the East? See what the historians relate of it, and above all Cortés himself, who feigned himself ambassador of the Catholic King. If the Mexicans had wished then to oppose them, as they opposed them the second time, how could the Spaniards ever have been capable of entering it with only six thousand men, when the second entrance was so difficult for them with two hundred thousand? (Q)

As to what M. de P. adds against Cortés, I neither wish to make an apology for this conqueror, nor can I endure that panegyric which Solís wrote in place of a history; but any impartial man, well instructed in the history of his military actions, will have to confess that in courage, constancy, and military prudence he can vie with the most famous generals, and that he had that species of heroism which we recognize in the Alexanders and the Caesars, in whom magnanimity is praised in spite of the vices with which they were otherwise infected.

The causes of the rapidity with which the Spaniards conquered America have been in part indicated by M. de P. himself. I confess, he says, that artillery was a destructive and omnipotent instrument, which must necessarily subdue the Mexicans. If to the artillery are added the other superior arms, the horses, and the better military discipline on the side of the conquerors, and the division on that of the conquered, it will be seen that there is no reason to charge the Americans with pusillanimity, nor to be astonished at the violent overthrow of the New World. Let M. de P. imagine that in the times of the clamorous and cruel factions of Sulla and Marius, the Athenians had invented artillery and the other firearms, and furnished with them

no

(Q) “It is no less certain,” says Acosta, “that in New Spain the aid of the Tlaxcalans was that which gave Cortés and his men the victory and the conquest of Mexico, and without them it would have been impossible not only to make themselves masters of that land, but even to remain there for any length of time. Those who make little account of the Indians, and persuade themselves that the Spaniards could conquer any country and nation by the sole advantage of their persons, their horses, and their offensive arms, deceive themselves greatly.” Nat. and Mor. Hist., bk. 7, ch. 28. [p. 195] no more than six thousand men, and, joining these not to the whole army of Marius but only to a part of his troops, had undertaken the conquest of Italy. Does M. de P. believe that they would not have succeeded, in spite of the power of Sulla, the courage and discipline of the Roman troops, the number of the legions and of the cavalry, the multitude of their arms, of their engines, and of the fortifications of their cities? What terror would the dreadful din of the artillery have struck into the hearts of the most courageous centurions, and the destructive violence of the balls, with which whole ranks were seen to be swept away! What, then, must it have been among those nations of the New World, who had neither the arms, nor the cavalry, nor the discipline, nor the engines, nor the fortifications of the Romans? What is truly to be wondered at, on the contrary, is that the valiant Spaniards, with all their discipline, their artillery, and their firearms, have not been able in more than two centuries to subdue in South America the Araucanian warriors, armed only with lances and clubs, nor in North America the Apaches, armed with bow and arrows; and above all, what seems incredible but is quite certain, that five hundred men alone of the nation of the Seris have been for many years the scourge of the Spaniards of Sonora and Sinaloa.

Finally, passing over many other absurdities of M. de Pauw against the Americans, so as not to tire the patience of the readers, I cannot conceal the atrocious injury that he does them in the matter of customs. There are four principal vices with which he brands all the Americans: gluttony, drunkenness, ingratitude, and pederasty, or sodomy.

I, for my part, had never heard gluttony charged against the Indians until I came upon the passage of M. de la Condamine, cited and adopted by M. de Pauw. I have found no author at all informed in the affairs of America who does not praise the sobriety of the Americans in eating. Let anyone who wishes see what is said of it by Monsignor de las Casas, Monsignor Garcés, the Anonymous Conqueror, Oviedo, Gómara, Acosta, Herrera, Torquemada, Vetancurt.

Bb2                      etc. [p. 196] etc., etc. (R) Almost all the historians relate the wonder that the frugality of the Indians caused the Spaniards, and, on the other hand, the wonder of the Indians at seeing the Spaniards eat more in a day than they themselves in a week; and, to say it in a few words, the sobriety of the Americans is so notorious that any defense of them on this score would be superfluous. M. de la Condamine perhaps saw some famished Indians eating greedily on his journey along the Marañón River, and from this persuaded himself, as often happens to travelers, that all the Americans were gluttons. It is certain that Don Antonio de Ulloa, who was in America with M. de la Condamine, stayed there longer and informed himself better about the customs of the Indians, and he speaks quite to the contrary of that French mathematician.

Drunkenness is the dominant vice of those nations. I admit it candidly in the first book of my History; I set forth its excesses and point out its causes; but I add besides that it was not so in the countries of Anahuac before the Spaniards entered them, because of the great severity with which that vice was punished, a vice which in the greater part of the countries of the old continent goes unpunished, and even serves as an exception or excuse for other, graver crimes. It is likewise established by the testimony of the writers who made inquiries into the political government of the Mexicans, that there were severe laws against drunkenness, in Mexico as well as in Texcoco, in Tlaxcala, and in other states, which I have seen represented in ancient paintings. The sixty-third painting of the Collection of Mendoza represents two young people of both sexes

## condemned

(R) Monsignor de las Casas, in that memorial of his to Philip II which bears the title On the Destruction of the Indies, affirms that the fare of the Americans is such that that of the ancient holy Fathers of the Thebaid could not have been less delightful, nor more scanty, nor more wretched. Monsignor Garcés, in his letter to Pope Paul III, says that their sobriety cannot be sufficiently expressed. The Anonymous Conqueror asserts in his Relation that the Americans are, of all the peoples of the world, those who sustain themselves on the least. So speak all the eyewitnesses of their customs. We know from Torquemada that the first most abstemious religious who announced the Gospel to the Mexicans found not a little to admire, and even to learn, in their sobriety. [p. 197] condemned to death for having got drunk, and with them a septuagenarian old man, to whom the laws, out of regard for his age, permit to drink as much as he wishes. Few states will be found in the world in which the zeal of the sovereigns for the correction of excesses of this sort has been greater.

In the aforesaid first book of the Ancient History we likewise refuted the common error concerning the gratitude of the Indians; but since what was said there will not suffice to convince those who are prejudiced against them, we wish to relate here a singular example of gratitude, which will by itself be sufficient to dispel any contrary notion. In the year 1556 there died at Uruapa, a considerable place in the kingdom of Michoacán, while visiting his diocese at the age of ninety-five, Monsignor Vasco de Quiroga, founder and first bishop of that church, who, like St. Ambrose, was transferred from the secular judgeship to the episcopal dignity. This distinguished prelate, worthy to be compared with the first Fathers of Christianity, labored untiringly on behalf of the Michoacans, instructing them as an apostle and loving them as a father; he built churches, founded hospitals, and assigned to each village of Indians a principal branch of commerce, so that their reciprocal dependence might keep them united in charity, the arts might be perfected, and no one might lack the means of living. The memory of such benefits is preserved so vividly among those Americans after more than two centuries, as if their benefactor were still alive. The first care that the Indian women have, as soon as their children begin to have some judgment, is to make them acquainted with their Tata Don Vasco (so they call him even today, because of the filial love they preserve for him); they make them know him in his portraits, declaring to them what he did in favor of their nation, and they never pass in front of his portrait without kneeling. Moreover, that great prelate founded in 1540 a seminary in the city of Pátzcuaro for the instruction of youth, and charged the Indians of Santa Fe (a place founded by himself on the shore of Lake Pátzcuaro) to send every week a man to serve

the [p. 198] the seminarians. He was obeyed, and to this day, after 230 years and more, there has never been a lack of the Indian who must serve there, without any need ever having arisen to compel him or to call him, solely in order to repay with that homage the great good which that incomparable bishop did them. They possess his bones in the city of Pátzcuaro with such veneration that once, when the chapter of that cathedral attempted to transfer them to Valladolid, the Indians became disturbed and prepared to prevent it by force of arms, as in fact would have happened had the chapter not turned aside from its first resolution in order to avoid the disorders of that contest. Can a more conclusive proof of the gratitude of a nation be imagined? Similar demonstrations were made by the Indians in many other places of that kingdom to retain the missionaries who had instructed them in the Faith. Those of the two past centuries may be seen in vol. 3 of Torquemada, and in the Teatro mexicano of Vetancurt. Of those that have been made in our own day there are still living a great many eyewitnesses, and I too can testify to them. If at times the Americans do not show themselves grateful to their benefactors, it is because the continual experience of the evils that are done them makes benefits suspect to them; but whenever they are sure of the sincere good will of the benefactor, they are capable of sacrificing all their goods to gratitude, as is notorious to all who have dealt with them and observed them without prejudice.

But in nothing else of all that M. de P. has published against the Americans is he so injurious to them as in affirming that pederasty was in great vogue in the islands, in Peru, and in Mexico, and in the whole new continent. (S) I do not know how, after publishing so atrocious a calumny, M. de P. had the heart to say, as he says in his reply to Dom Pernety, that his whole work of the Recherches philosophiques breathes humanity. Is it humanity to defame wrongfully all the nations of the New World with a vice so enormous and so abominable to Nature? Is the indignation

that

(S) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1. [p. 199] that he displays against the Inca Garcilaso, because he defends the Peruvians from such an imputation, humanity? Even if there were grave authors who attributed that crime to all the peoples of America, since there are, as in fact there are, many authors equally grave who affirm the very contrary, M. de Pauw ought, in accordance with the laws of humanity, to have refrained from so grave an accusation. How much more ought he to refrain from it, when there is no authoritative writer on whose testimony he can rest so universal an assertion. He will find, indeed, some authors, such as the Anonymous Conqueror, Gómara, and Herrera, who have charged some Americans with that vice, or at most some people of America; but where will he ever find a reputable historian who has dared to say that pederasty was in great vogue in the islands, in Peru, and in Mexico, and in the whole new continent? On the contrary, all the historians of Mexico say with one voice that this vice was supremely abhorred by those nations, and they make mention of the terrible penalties prescribed against it by their laws, as may be seen in the works of Gómara, Herrera, Torquemada, Vetancurt, and others. Monsignor de las Casas testified in a writing of his presented to Charles V in 1542 that, having made diligent inquiries in the islands of Hispaniola, Cuba, Jamaica, and Puerto Rico, and in the Lucayas, he found that there was never any memory of that crime among those nations. He affirms the same of Peru, of Yucatán, and of all the countries of America in general, except in some place where it is said that there are some guilty of it; but not therefore, he adds, is that whole world to be blamed. (T) Who

## then

(T) “The Spaniards” (Monsignor de las Casas is speaking of some of them, not of all) “have defamed the Indians with the greatest crimes for no other reason than their temporal interest. . . . Since they perceived that their wealth consisted in seizing the goods and the persons of the Indians, they have a thousand times defamed them and accused them of being infected with sodomy; but such an imputation is a great falsehood, and a wickedness of the accusers; for in all the great islands of Hispaniola, Cuba, San Juan, and Jamaica, and in sixty Lucaya islands, in which there were very numerous peoples, there was never any memory of such a vice, as we can testify, having made inquiries about it from the very beginning. Nor in Peru, nor in Yucatán, was such a vice found, and so generally everywhere, except in some places where it is said that there are some, etc.” Casas, in the memorial On the Liberty Claimed by the Suppliant Indian, Reason 6. [p. 200] then has authorized M. de P. to defame the whole New World in so grave a matter? Even if the Americans were truly, as he believes, men without honor and without shame, the very laws of humanity would require him not to slander them. To such excesses he is carried by that ridiculous determination to debase America, and such are the consequences of his wicked logic, which often draws, as we have already said, universal conclusions from particular premises. If, because the Panucans or other American peoples were perhaps infected with that vice, it can be affirmed that pederasty was in great vogue throughout the whole New World, the Americans will likewise be entitled to defame the whole old continent with the same imputation, since pederasty was in great vogue among some ancient peoples of Asia, and far too common among the Greeks and the Romans. Besides, it is not known that any nation in America is at present infected with that vice; whereas we know from the deposition of several authors that some Asiatic peoples have not yet laid aside that abomination, and that even in Europe, if what Messrs. Locke and de P. say is ever true, there is common among those Turks who make a profession of sanctimony another, more execrable vice of the same kind, and that instead of being severely punished, they are held by that nation to be saints, and all vie in showing them the greatest demonstrations of respect and veneration. (U)

Among the crimes that M. de P. charges against the Americans, he also gives a place to suicide. It is indeed true that there were many who at the time of the conquest hanged themselves, or threw themselves from heights, or ended their bitter lives by starvation; but what wonder is it that men deprived of the light of the true religion, and driven to despair by the intolerable vexations which they suffered from the conquerors, should do what was done so frequently by the Romans, the Greeks, and the ancient Spaniards, and by the English, the French, and the Japanese of modern times, for a slight

## motive

(U) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 4, sect. 4. [p. 201] motive, for a ridiculous idea of honor, or for a whim? (X) Who would ever have believed that a European would reproach the Americans with suicide in a century in which it has become the fashion in England and in France, (Y) where, wiping from the mind those juster ideas that we have from Nature and from Religion, reasons are invented and books are published to justify it? So great is the determination to defame America and the Americans.

Such a determination seems to have been that of that Spaniard, whoever he was, who drew up the general index of the Decades of the chronicler Herrera, imputing heedlessly to all the Americans what Herrera says in his work of some particular ones, with various exceptions. I wish to copy here what is read in that index, so that men may be ashamed to write such absurdities: “The Indians,” it says, “are very lazy, most vicious, great drunkards by temperament, sluggards, feeble, liars, swindlers, lovers of novelty, inconstant, light-minded, cowards, filthy, seditious, thieves, ungrateful, incorrigible, vindictive more than any other nation: of so coarse a make that it was doubted whether they were rational: barbarous, brutish, led like brutes by their appetites,” etc., etc. This very same is the language of M. de Pauw and of other most humane Europeans; so that these men seem not to believe themselves obliged, in what concerns the peoples of the New World, to respect the truth, nor to observe the laws of fraternal charity, published by the very Son of God in the old continent.

But for any American endowed with a moderate talent and some erudition, who wished to pay these writers back in kind (as we have said elsewhere of a Guinean philosopher), it would be easy to compose a work with this

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.              Cc                         title

(X) Among the other memorable extravagances of those many who in these last years have killed themselves in England, I know from a person who was then in London that a man there, in dying, left it in writing that he was putting himself to death in order to free himself from the bother of dressing and undressing every day.

(Y) We know that in the single city of Paris, in one of these last years, about a hundred and fifty men killed themselves. [p. 202] title: Philosophical Researches on the Inhabitants of the Old Continent. He, following the same method as M. de P., would gather whatever he might find written of the barren countries of the Old World, of inaccessible mountains, of marshy plains, of impenetrable forests, of sandy deserts, and of bad climates; of loathsome and noxious reptiles and insects, of serpents, of toads, of scorpions, of ants, of spiders, of centipedes, of beetles, of bedbugs, and of lice; of irregular, small, tailless, defective, and pusillanimous quadrupeds; of degenerate peoples, of ugly color, of irregular stature, of deformed features, of bad complexion, of paltry spirit, of dull intelligence, and of cruel disposition. When he came to the article of vices, what an immense abundance of materials he would have for his work! What examples of baseness, of perfidy, of cruelty, of superstition, and of dissoluteness! What excesses in every sort of vice! The history of the Romans alone, the most celebrated nation of the Old World, would furnish him an incredible quantity of the most horrible crimes. He would admit, too, that such defects and vices were not common either to all countries or to all the other inhabitants of the old continent; but it does not matter, since he was to write on the very model of M. de P., and to use the same logic. This work would doubtless be far more valuable and more worthy of credit than that of M. de P., because whereas this philosopher cites against America and the Americans only European authors, that American writer, on the contrary, would avail himself for his curious work only of authors native to the very continent against which he would be writing.

## Dissertation

[p. 203]

## D I S S E RTAT I O N V I 

O N T H E C I V I L I Z AT I O N O F T H E M E X I C A N S .

M      . de Pauw, ever vexed and infuriated against the New World,

calls all the Americans barbarians and savages, and reckons them inferior in sagacity and industry to the crudest and rudest peoples of the old continent. If he had been content to say that the American nations were for the most part uncivilized, barbarous, and brutish in their customs, just as many of the most civilized nations of Europe were in ancient times, and as several peoples of Asia, of Africa, and even of Europe itself are at present: that the most civilized nations of America were far inferior in civilization to the greater part of the European nations: that their arts had not been so perfected, nor their laws so good and so well ordered: and that their sacrifices were inhuman, and some of their usages extravagant, we should have no reason to contradict him. But to treat the Mexicans and the Peruvians like the Caribs and the Iroquois, to set at naught their industry, to discredit their arts, to despise in everything their laws, and to put those industrious nations beneath the feet of the crudest peoples of the old continent, is not this to persist in the determination to debase the New World and its inhabitants, instead of seeking the truth, as the title of his work should require?

Barbarians and savages we call today those men who, led more by whim and natural desires than by reason, neither live gathered in society, nor have laws for their government, nor judges to settle their differences, nor superiors to watch over their conduct, nor practice the arts necessary to remedy the needs and miseries of life; those, finally, who have no idea of the Deity, or at least have not established the worship with which they ought to honor it. Now the Mexicans

Cc2                      and [p. 204] and all the other nations of Anahuac, as also the Peruvians, acknowledged a supreme and omnipotent Being, although their belief was, like that of other idolatrous peoples, vitiated by a thousand errors and superstitions. They had also a fixed system of religion: they had priests, temples, sacrifices, and rites ordained for the uniform worship of the Deity. They had kings, governors, and magistrates: they had so many cities, and populations so great and so well ordered, as we shall show in another dissertation. They had laws and customs, the observance of which the magistrates and governors zealously enforced. They had commerce, and took great care of equity and justice in contracts. They had distributed the lands, and had secured to each individual the property and possession of his own ground. They practiced agriculture and other arts, not only those necessary to life but even those that serve only for delights and luxury. What more, then, is required in order that those nations should not be reputed barbarous and savage? Money, says M. de P., the use of iron, the art of writing, and those of building ships, constructing stone bridges, or making lime. Their arts were imperfect and crude: their languages very scant of numeral words and of terms fit to express universal ideas, and their laws may be called none; because there cannot be laws where anarchy and despotism reign. All these articles require a particular examination.

§ I.

On the Lack of Money.

M. de P. decides that no nation of America was civilized and civil, because none made use of money, and to prove this assertion he alleges a passage of Montesquieu: “Aristippus,” says this politician, (a) “having been shipwrecked, swam to the neighboring shore; there he saw drawn in the sand

some

(a) The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 18, ch. 13. [p. 205] some figures of geometry, and was filled with joy, persuading himself that he had arrived among a Greek people and not in a barbarous nation. Imagine that by some accident you come to an unknown country: if you find some money there, do not doubt that you have arrived among a civilized people.” But if Montesquieu rightly concludes the civilization of a people from the use of money, M. de P. infers very badly the lack of civilization from the lack of money. If by money is meant a piece of metal coined with the stamp of the prince or of the public, it is certain that the lack of it in a nation does not demonstrate barbarism. “The Athenians,” says the same Montesquieu, “because they had no use of any metals, made use of oxen for money, as the Romans did of sheep,” and from this originated, as all know, the name pecunia, since the Romans put on the first money that they coined the stamp of the sheep which they had previously used for their contracts. The Greeks were without doubt a very civilized nation in the times of Homer, since it was not possible that in the midst of an uncivilized nation there should be raised up a man capable of composing the Iliad and the Odyssey, those two immortal poems which after twenty-seven centuries are still admired, but cannot be imitated. And yet the Greeks at those times did not know coined money, as appears from the works of that renowned poet himself, who wherever he wishes to signify the value of anything does not express it otherwise than by the number of oxen or sheep that it was worth: as he does in bk. 7 of the Iliad, where he says that Glaucus gave his arms of gold, which were worth a hundred oxen, for those of Diomedes, which were of copper, and were worth no more than nine oxen. Wherever, moreover, he makes mention of some acquisition by contract, he speaks of nothing but that of exchange or barter. And so in that ancient controversy which arose between the Sabinians and the Proculians, two sects of jurisconsults, the former maintained that a true sale and purchase could be made without a price, alleging for this certain passages of Homer, in which those who did nothing else than barter are said to buy and sell. The Lacedaemonians

## were

[p. 206]

were a civilized people of Greece, although they did not use money, and among the fundamental laws published by Lycurgus was that of not trading otherwise than by way of barter. (b) The Romans had no coined money until the time of Servius Tullius, nor the Persians until the reign of Darius Hystaspes, and yet they ought not to be called barbarous nations in the times that preceded those epochs. The Hebrews were civilized at least from the times of their Judges, and yet we do not know that stamped money was in use among them until the times of the Maccabees. Therefore the lack of coined money is not an argument of barbarism.

If by money is meant a representative sign of the value of all goods, as Montesquieu defines it, (c) it is certain and indubitable that the Mexicans, and all the other nations of Anahuac, except the barbarous Chichimecs and Otomis, made use of money in their commerce. What was cacao, which they constantly used to procure in the market all that they needed, if not a representative sign of the value of all goods? Cacao had its fixed value, and was given by number, but to spare themselves the trouble of counting whenever the goods amounted to many thousands of almonds, they knew already that each sack of a certain size contained three xiquipilli, or twenty-four thousand almonds. Now who does not see that cacao is far better to use in place of money than the oxen and sheep which the Greeks and Romans anciently used, and the salt which the Abyssinians use today? Oxen and sheep could not serve for acquiring small goods of little value, and any sickness or other misfortune that befell those animals might impoverish those who had no other capital. “Metal is used for money,” says Montesquieu, “so that the sign may be more durable.” “The salt that is used by

the

(b) He ordered that each thing be bought not with money, but by an exchange of goods. Justin, bk. 3.

(c) The Spirit of the Laws. [p. 207] the Abyssinians has the defect of continually diminishing.” Cacao, on the contrary, could serve for any goods, was more easily transported and kept, and was preserved with less danger and with less care.

The use of cacao in the commerce of those nations will seem perhaps to someone a mere exchange; but it was not so; for since there are several kinds of cacao, they did not use as money the Tlalcacahuatl, or small cacao, which they employed in their daily drinks, but rather other kinds of inferior quality and less useful for eating, which circulated incessantly like money, (d) and had hardly any other use than that of being employed in commerce. (e) Of this kind of money all the historians of Mexico make mention, Spaniards as well as Indians. The other four kinds, of which we have treated in bk. 7 of the History, are attested by Cortés and by Torquemada. Cortés affirms in his last letter to the Emperor Charles V that, having made inquiries into the commerce of those nations, he found that in Tlachco and in other provinces they traded with money. If he had not meant coined money, he would not have restricted the use of it to Tlachco and to some other province; since he well knew, without needing to make new inquiries, that in the markets of Mexico and of Tlaxcala, at which he had often been present, they used as money cacao, certain small cloths of cotton called by them Patolquachtli, and gold dust put inside goose quills. I suspect, however, notwithstanding what I have said in that place of the History, that there was also coined money, and that both those thin pieces of tin of which Cortés himself makes mention, and those of copper made in the form of a T of which Torquemada speaks, (f) as kinds of

money,

(d) Hernández, Rerum medicarum Novae Hispaniae thesauri, bk. 3, ch. 46.

(e) In that same capital, Mexico, in which up to eighteen or twenty millions of scudi (pesos fuertes) in gold and silver are coined annually, the poor people still use cacao to buy some trifles in the market.

(f) Monarquía indiana, bk. 14, ch. 14. [p. 208] money, had some stamp authorized by the sovereign or by the feudal lords.

To prevent, moreover, all fraud in commerce, nothing except ordinary provisions could be sold outside the marketplace, in which there was, as we have said on the deposition of many eyewitnesses, the finest order that can be imagined. There were measures prescribed by the magistrates, commissaries who went about incessantly observing what took place there, and judges of commerce charged with taking cognizance of the disputes that arose among the merchants, and with punishing the crimes that were committed there. And notwithstanding this, will it be said that the Mexicans were inferior in industry to the crudest peoples of the old continent, among whom there are some so rude, and so obstinate in their barbarism, that the example of the other nations of their continent has not sufficed in so many centuries to make them acquainted with the advantages of money?

§ II.

On the Use of Iron.

The use of iron is one of those things which M. de P. requires in order to call a nation civilized; and for the lack of it he believes all the Americans barbarians. So that if God had not created that metal, all men would of necessity have to be barbarians, according to the opinion of this philosopher. But in the very place of his work where he charges barbarism upon the Americans, he furnishes us with all the materials that could be desired to refute him. He affirms that in the whole extent of America very few mines of iron are found, and what there is, is so inferior in quality to that of the other continent, that it cannot even be used to make nails. He tells us that the Americans possessed the secret, already lost in the old continent, of giving to copper a temper equal to that which steel receives: that M. Godin sent in 1727 (he will perhaps mean to say in 1747; since in 1727 M. Godin had not yet gone to

Peru

[p. 209]

Peru) to the Count de Maurepas an old ax of hardened Peruvian copper, and that the Count de Caylus, having examined it, recognized that it almost equaled in hardness the ancient copper arms of which the Greeks and Romans once made use, who did not employ iron in many of those works in which we employ it at present, either because it was then rarer, or because their tempered copper was better in quality than their steel. Finally he adds that the Count de Caylus, astonished at that art, persuaded himself (although on this point he is contradicted by M. de Pauw himself) that that instrument was not the work of those brutish Peruvians whom the Spaniards found there at the time of the conquest, but of another nation, older and more industrious.

From all that M. de Pauw says of it I draw four important consequences. 1. That the Americans had the honor of imitating, in the use of copper, the two most celebrated nations of the old continent. 2. That they conducted themselves wisely in not making use of so bad an iron, which cannot be useful even for making nails, and in using a copper to which they gave the temper of steel. 3. That if they did not know the very common art of working iron, they possessed that most singular one of tempering copper like steel, which the European natural philosophers of the enlightened century have not been able to restore. 4. That the Count de Caylus was as much mistaken in the judgment that he made of the Peruvians as M. de P. in that which he has made of all the Americans. These are the legitimate consequences that must be deduced from the doctrine of our philosopher on the use of iron, and not that of the lack of industry which he claims to deduce from it. I should like to know from him whether more industry is required to work iron, as the Europeans work it, than to work without iron every sort of stone and wood, to fabricate several kinds of arms, and to make without iron, as the Americans did, the most curious works of gold, of silver, and of gems. The mere use of iron does not prove a great industry in the Europeans. Once invented by the first men, it easily passed from one to another, and as the modern Americans

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.            Dd                   received [p. 210] received it from the Europeans, so the ancient Europeans had it from the Asiatics. The first settlers of America doubtless knew the use of iron, since its invention was almost coeval with the world; but it may be believed that what happened was what we conjectured in the first Dissertation, namely, that since they did not at first find the mines of that metal in the northern countries of America, where they then established themselves, the memory of it was lost among their descendants.

But finally, if those are barbarous who have not the use of iron, what shall those be who lack the use of fire? Now in the whole vast extent of America there has not been found a nation, nor even a tribe however rude, which did not know the way of making fire and of using it for the common purposes of life; but in the Old World there have been found peoples so barbarous that they had neither use nor knowledge of fire. Such were the inhabitants of the Mariana Islands, to whom that element was wholly unknown before the Spaniards landed there, as the historians of those islands attest. And will M. de Pauw nevertheless persuade us that the American peoples are more savage than all the savages of the Old World?

For the rest, our Researcher is as much mistaken in what he says of American iron as in what he thinks of copper. In New Spain, in Chile, and in many other countries of America, infinite mines of good iron have been discovered, and if it were not forbidden to work them, in order not to prejudice the commerce of Spain, America could furnish Europe with all the iron necessary, as she supplies it with gold and silver. If M. de P. had known how to make his researches about America, he would have found in the chronicler Herrera, (g) that even in the island of Hispaniola there is iron better than that of Biscay. He would likewise have found (h) in the same author that in Zacatula, a maritime province of Mexico, there is

## copper

(g) Dec. 4, bk. 6, ch. 7.

(h) Herrera, Description of the West Indies, ch. 10. [p. 211] copper of two kinds: the one hard, which they used in place of iron to make axes, hatchets, and other instruments of war and of agriculture, and the other ordinary and pliable, which they employed in pots, basins, and other vessels for domestic uses; so that they had no need of the vaunted secret of hardening copper. My sincerity obliges me equally to defend the true progress of American industry and to reject those imaginary inventions that are attributed to the nations of that New World. The secret that the Americans truly possessed is that which is read in Oviedo, an eyewitness, and very experienced and knowledgeable in metals: “The Indians,” he says, (i) “know how to gild very well vessels of copper, or of low gold, and to give them so excellent and so glowing a color that it seems gold of twenty-two carats and more: which they do with certain herbs. This work succeeds so well that if some goldsmith of Spain or of Italy had this secret, he would think himself very rich.”

## § III

On the Arts of Building Ships and Bridges and of Making Lime.

If to other nations the ignorance of the art of constructing ships may perhaps be charged, this reproach ought certainly not to be made to the Mexicans; because, not having become masters of the seacoasts except in the last times of their monarchy, they had no need nor occasion to think of such construction. As for the nations, moreover, that occupied the shores of both seas before the Mexicans made themselves masters of them, those boats sufficed that were in use among them for fishing and for commerce with the neighboring provinces; because, being free from ambition and from avarice, which have been for the most part the causes of long voyages, they neither sought to usurp the states legitimately possessed by other nations, nor wished to transport

Dd2       from

(i) Sumario of the Natural History of the West Indies, ch. 84. [p. 212] from distant countries the precious metals of which they had no need. The Romans, although they had founded their metropolis so near the sea, nevertheless remained five hundred years without constructing ships, (k) until the ambition to extend their dominions and to make themselves masters of Sicily made them build vessels to cross that strait. What wonder, then, if those American nations, which felt no such stimuli to abandon their country, did not invent vessels by which to transport themselves with less risk into distant countries? It is certain that not having invented vessels does not argue a lack of industry in those who had no interest in such an invention.

Not so, however, in the invention of bridges. M. de P. affirms (l) that there was not a single stone bridge in the whole of America when it was discovered, because the Americans did not know how to build arches: and that the secret of making lime was absolutely unknown in all America. Here are three propositions, which are as many gross errors. The Mexicans knew how to make stone bridges, and among the remains of their ancient architecture there are seen even today in the river of Tula the great and strong piers which supported the bridge that was there. The remains, moreover, of the ancient palaces of Texcoco, and much more their Temaxcalli, or hypocausts, show the ancient use of arches and of vaults among the Mexicans and the other nations of Anahuac. Diego Valadés, who went to Mexico a few years after the conquest and dwelt there thirty years, shows us in his Rhetorica christiana the image of a small temple which he saw there, which leaves us no doubt in this matter.

Concerning the use of lime, one must have all the boldness

of

(k) “Appius had used all possible diligence to come to the aid of the Mamertines. In order to succeed it was necessary to cross the strait of Messina, and the enterprise was rash, indeed dangerous, and even, according to all the most prudent appearances, impossible. The Romans had no naval force, but only roughly built boats, which might be compared to the canoes of the Indians.” Rollin, Roman History, bk. 11.

(l) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 5, sect. 1. [p. 213] of M. de Pauw to be able to affirm, as he does, that the secret of making lime was absolutely unknown in all America; since it is established no less by the deposition of the Spanish conquerors than by that of the first missionaries that the nations of Mexico not only used lime, but that they whitened very well, and made curiously smooth and polished the walls of their houses and of their temples. It is established by the histories of Bernal Díaz, of Gómara, of Herrera, of Torquemada, and of others, that to the first Spaniards who entered the city of Cempoala the walls of the principal palace seemed of silver, because they were neatly whitened and shining. It is established, finally, by the paintings of the tributes that are in the Collection of Mendoza, that the cities of Tepeyacac, Tecamachalco, Quecholac, etc., were obliged to pay annually to the king of Mexico four thousand sacks of lime. But even if all these documents were lacking, the remains of the ancient edifices which are still seen in Texcoco, in Mictlan, in Huatusco, and in many other places of that kingdom would suffice to demonstrate the truth of what we say, and to confound the rashness of M. de P.

As for Peru, although Father Acosta confesses that lime was not in use there, and that that nation built neither arches nor stone bridges, and although this sufficed for M. de P. to say, according to his wicked logic, that the use of lime was unknown in all America; nevertheless the same Acosta, who was no vulgar man, nor an exaggerator, nor partial to the Americans, greatly praises the marvelous industry of the Peruvians in their bridges of totora, or rush, at the outlet of Lake Titicaca and in other places, where the great depth does not permit the building of stone bridges, or the extraordinary rapidity of the rivers renders the use of boats dangerous. He testifies that he passed over such bridges, and vaunts the ease and the security of the passage. M. de Pauw ventures to say that the Peruvians did not know the use of boats, that they did not know how to make windows in their buildings, and even suspects that their houses were without roof. The crudest absurdities that can

enter [p. 214] can leap into the head of a writer on America. He shows that he does not know what the bejucos of the Peruvian bridges are, and that he has formed no just idea of the rivers of South America. There are many things to be urged against M. de P. on this subject; but we pass them over in order to come to other, more essential articles.

§ I V.

On the Lack of Letters.

No nation of America knew the art of writing, if by that is meant the art of expressing on paper, on skins, on cloth, or on some similar material, any kind of words by the different combination of certain characters; but if the art of writing is taken to be that of representing, and of giving those absent and posterity to understand, anything whatever by means of figures, hieroglyphics, and characters, it is certain that such an art was known, and in great use, among the Mexicans, the Acolhuas, the Tlaxcalans, and all the other civilized nations of Anahuac. Count de Buffon, to prove that America was a truly new land, and its peoples likewise new, alleges, as we have said elsewhere, that even those nations which lived in society were ignorant of “the art of handing down facts to posterity by means of durable signs, although they had found the art of communicating from a distance, and of writing to one another by knotting cords.” But should not that same art, which they used in dealing with the absent, also have served for speaking to posterity? What were the historical paintings of the Mexicans, if not durable signs for handing down the memory of events to places as well as to centuries remote? Count de Buffon shows himself in truth as ignorant of the history of Mexico as he is learned in natural history. M. de P., although he grants to the Mexicans that art which Count de Buffon wrongly denies them, nevertheless, in order to discredit it, alleges several reasons and innumerable absurdities, which we cannot pass over in silence.

He

[p. 215]

He says, then, that the Mexicans did not use hieroglyphics: that their paintings were nothing but crude drawings of objects: that to represent a tree they painted a tree: that in their paintings not a trace of chiaroscuro is to be seen, nor any idea of perspective or of the imitation of Nature: that they had made no progress in that art, by means of which they strove to perpetuate the memory of past things and of events: that the only copy of Mexican historical painting rescued from the fire which the first missionaries made of them is the one which the first viceroy of Mexico sent to Charles V, which was afterwards published by Purchas in England and by Thévenot in France: that this painting is so crude and so badly executed that one cannot make out whether it treats, as its interpreter says, of eight kings of Mexico, or of eight concubines of Moctezuma, etc.

In all this M. de P. shows his ignorance, and from it springs his rashness in writing. But should greater credence be given to a Prussian philosopher, who has seen only the crude copies of Purchas, than to those who have seen, and diligently studied, a great many original paintings of the Mexicans? M. de P. does not want the Mexicans to have made use of hieroglyphics, lest it be thought that he grants them some resemblance to the ancient Egyptians. Father Kircher, that celebrated investigator and eulogist of Egyptian antiquities in his work entitled Oedipus Aegyptiacus, and Brian Walton in the prolegomena to the Polyglot Bible, are of the same opinion as M. de P., and their opinion has no other support than that of the aforesaid copy of Purchas; but Motolinía, (m) Sahagún, Valadés, Torquemada, Enrico

## Martínez

(m) Toribio de Motolinía in his manuscripts, especially in the exposition of the Mexican Calendar. Bernardino Sahagún in his universal dictionary of the Mexican language. Diego Valadés in his Rhetorica christiana, printed at Perugia and dedicated to Pope Gregory XIII in the year 1579. Enrico Martínez in his History of New Spain. Sigüenza in his Ciclografia mexicana, and in the work entitled Teatro de virtudes políticas. Torquemada in his Monarquía indiana. Valadés dealt with the Mexicans for thirty years, Torquemada for more than forty, [p. 216] Martínez, Sigüenza, and Boturini, who knew the Mexican language, conferred with the Indians, and saw and diligently studied a great many ancient paintings, say that among the various ways the Mexicans had of representing objects was that of hieroglyphics and symbolic paintings. The same is attested by Acosta and Gómara in their histories, by Dr. Eguiara in the learned preface to the Biblioteca mexicana, and by those learned Spaniards who published, with new additions, the work of Gregorio García on the origin of the Indians. Father Kircher was very well refuted by Dr. Sigüenza in his work entitled Teatro de virtudes políticas. It is certain that Kircher openly contradicts himself; for in the first volume of the same work, Oedipus Aegyptiacus, where he compares the religion of the Mexicans with that of the Egyptians, he frankly confesses that the parts of which the image of the god Huitzilopochtli was composed had many arcane and mysterious significations. Acosta, whose history is justly esteemed by M. de P., says this in his description of that image: All this ornament that we have described, and the rest, which was considerable, had its particular significations, as the Mexicans explained; and in the description of the idol of Tezcatlipoca he expresses himself in these terms: “His hair was bound with a little cord of gold, from the end of which hung an ear of the same metal with certain vapors of smoke painted on it, which signified the prayers of the afflicted and of sinners, who were heard by that god whenever they commended themselves to him… In his left hand he had a fan of gold, bordered with beautiful green, turquoise, and yellow feathers, so shining that it seemed a mirror: by which they gave to understand that in that mirror he saw everything that happened in the world… In his right he had four arrows to signify the punishment that he dealt out to wrongdoers for their misdeeds

etc. Motolinía forty-five, and Sahagún sixty. The last was the man best instructed in the secrets of that nation. It takes great pride to defer more to one’s own meager lights than to those of so many men incomparably more enlightened. [p. 217] ” etc. What are all these and other such insignia of the Mexican idols, of which we made mention in bk. VI of the History, if not symbols and hieroglyphics very much like those of the Egyptians?

M. de P. says that the Mexicans did nothing but paint a tree to represent a tree; but let him tell me, if he will be so good, what they painted to represent the Day, the Night, the Month, the Year, the Century, the names of those persons whom they wished to make known, etc.? How could they represent Time, and other things that have no shape, without making use of symbols or characters? “The Mexicans,” says the aforementioned Acosta, “had their figures and hieroglyphics, with which they represented things in this manner: namely, those things which had a shape they represented with their own proper figures; but for those which have no image of their own, they made use of other characters significant of them: thus they represented whatever they wished: and to indicate the time in which anything happened, they made use of those painted wheels, each of which comprised a century of theirs of fifty-two years, etc.” (n)

But here is another stumbling block of M. de P.’s ignorance. He makes sport of the secular wheels of the Mexicans, the exposition of which, he says, Careri dared to give, following a Castilian professor named Congara, who did not dare to publish the work that he had promised on this subject; because his relatives and friends assured him that it contained many errors. It seems that M. de P. cannot write without erring. That professor whom Careri, that is, Gemelli, follows was not a Castilian, but a Creole born in the very city of Mexico: nor was he called Congara, but Sigüenza and Góngora: he did not print his Ciclografia mexicana, which was the work that Gemelli used, not because he feared the censure of the public, but on account of the excessive expense of printing in those countries, which has likewise prevented the publication

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                     Ee              of

(n) Natural and Moral History of the Indies, bk. 6, ch. 7. [p. 218] of so many other excellent works, both by Sigüenza himself and by other most learned men. To say that Sigüenza’s relatives and friends dissuaded him from publishing that work because they found errors in it is likewise no slip caused by some inadvertence, but a manifest lie contrived on purpose to deceive the public. Who has communicated to M. de P. so strange an anecdote, wholly unknown in New Spain, where the memory of that great man is so dear and his fame so celebrated, and where men of letters lament the loss of that work and of other most valuable ones by the same author? What could Sigüenza fear from the publication of the Mexican wheels, which had already been published in Italy by Valadés more than a century before him, and described by Motolinía, Sahagún, Gómara, Acosta, Herrera, Torquemada, and Martínez, all of them Europeans, and by the Mexican, Acolhua, and Tlaxcalan historians Ixtlilxochitl, Chimalpahin, Tezozomoc, Niza, Ayala, and others? All these authors agree with Sigüenza in what concerns the Mexican wheels of the Century, of the Year, and of the Month, and differ only about the beginning of the Year and the names of some Months, for the reasons we indicated in bk. VI of the History. For the rest, all the authors who have written on this subject, both Spaniards and Americans, who are very many, agree in saying that the Mexicans and the other nations of those countries made use of such wheels to represent their Century, their Year, and their Month: that their century consisted of fifty-two years, their Year of three hundred and sixty-five days, distributed into eighteen months of twenty days each, and besides into five days which they called Nemontemi: that in their century they counted four periods of thirteen years, and that the days too were counted in periods of thirteen: that the names and characters of the years were only four, namely those of the Rabbit, the Reed, the Flint, and the House, which without interruption alternated continually with different numbers, etc.

It cannot be, says M. de P.; because such a use would presuppose

a [p. 219] a long series of astronomical observations, and very precise knowledge, to regulate the solar year, or else these cannot be reconciled with that prodigious ignorance in which those peoples were immersed. How could they have perfected their chronology, they who had no words for counting beyond ten? Very well. Then if the Mexicans in fact had that way of regulating time, they must not be called barbarians and savages, but rather civilized, and highly civilized; for only a highly civilized nation can be one that has a long series of observations and precise knowledge of astronomy. Now the certainty of such a regulation of time among the Mexicans is such that it cannot be doubted; for if the unanimous testimony of the Spanish writers concerning the communion of the Mexicans does not allow it to be doubted, as M. de P. affirms in another place, (o) how can one doubt the method which those nations had of computing the centuries and the years, and its conformity with the solar course, when both the one and the other are unanimously attested by all the Spanish, Mexican, Acolhua, and Tlaxcalan authors? Besides, the testimony of the Spaniards on this matter is of far greater weight, since they were committed rather, to use M. de P.’s phrase, to discrediting the American nations, even to the point of putting their rationality in doubt. One must therefore believe what the historians say of those wheels, and confess that the Mexicans were not immersed in that profound ignorance which M. de P. feigns. As to what he says of the scarcity of numerals in the Mexican language, we shall demonstrate his error and his ignorance elsewhere.

The contents of the paintings of the Mexicans cannot be known, M. de P. resumes; because the Spaniards could not understand them unless they were explained to them by the Mexicans, and none of

Ee2                        these

(o) “I confess to you that the consent of all the Spanish historians scarcely permits one to doubt that these two American peoples (the Mexicans and the Peruvians) had, in the immense sum of their gross superstitions, some usages that did not differ much from what is called Communion among us.” Rech. philos., vol. 2, letter I to Mr.** On the Religion of the Americans. [p. 220] these has known up to now enough to translate a book? Oh, how many absurdities in so few words! In order that the Spaniards might understand the Mexican paintings it was not necessary that the Mexicans should know the Spanish language; for it was enough that the Spaniards understood Mexican: nor does the explanation of a painting require as much as is needed to translate a book. M. de P. says that on account of the roughness of the Mexican language there has been up to now no Spaniard who could pronounce it, and that on account of the incapacity of the Mexicans none of them has up to now learned the Spanish language; but the one and the other are very far from the truth. Of the Mexican language we shall treat in its place. Castilian has always been very common among the Mexicans, and there are very many who speak it as well as the Spaniards themselves. Many of them wrote in Castilian their ancient history, and that of the conquest of Mexico as well, some of whom I have praised in the Catalogue of writers which I prefixed to my History. Others translated Latin books into Castilian, Castilian ones into Mexican, and Mexican ones into Castilian: among whom the following are worthy of particular mention: Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl, so often cited by us; Don Antonio Valeriano of Azcapotzalco, teacher of the Mexican language to the historian Torquemada and celebrated by him with great praise; Don Juan Berardo of Huexotzinco; Don Francisco Bautista Contreras of Cuauhnahuac; Fernando de Ribas and Esteban Bravo of Texcoco; Pedro de Gante, Diego Adriano, and Agustín de la Fuente of Tlatelolco. (p) We know from the history of the Conquest that the celebrated Indian woman Doña Marina learned the Castilian language with the greatest quickness and ease, and that she spoke very well both Mexican and Maya, which differ more from each other than French, Hebrew, and Illyrian. Since, then, there have been in all times very many Spaniards who have learned Mexican, as we shall show later, and very many Mexicans,

## who

(p) On what we say of those Indian translators, one may consult Torquemada in the Monarquía indiana, Pinelo in the epitome of the Biblioteca occidental, Dr. Eguiara in the Biblioteca mexicana, and Vetancurt in the Teatro mexicano. [p. 221] who have learned Spanish, why should the Mexicans not have been able to instruct the Spaniards in the meaning of their paintings?

As to the copies of the Mexican paintings published by Purchas and by Thévenot, it is true that in them neither the proportions nor the laws of perspective are seen to be observed; but since those crude copies were cut in wood, it may be that those authors increased the defects of the originals: nor should we wonder if they perhaps left out something belonging to the perfection of those paintings, since we know that they omitted altogether the copies of paintings 21 and 22 of that Collection, and the images of the cities in the greater part of the others, and moreover changed the figures of the years corresponding to the reigns of Ahuitzotl and of Moctezuma II, as we have said where we treated of the various collections of Mexican paintings in vol. I of the History. The Chevalier Boturini, who saw in Mexico original paintings of those annals, and of that register of tributes, which are contained in the copies published by Purchas and by Thévenot, complains of the great defects that crept into these editions. Indeed, it suffices to compare the copies published in Mexico in the year 1770 by Monsignor Lorenzana with those published in London by Purchas and in Paris by Thévenot to recognize the great difference between the figures of the one and of the others. But I do not undertake to maintain the perfection of the original paintings copied by Purchas; indeed, I do not doubt that they were imperfect, as almost all the historical paintings were, in which the painters, content with the outlines and the coloring of the objects, took no care for proportions, nor for chiaroscuro, nor for perspective. Nor was it possible for those laws of art to be observed, given the extraordinary speed with which they made such paintings, to which Cortés and Bernal Díaz, eyewitnesses, bear witness. But let us see the consequences that M. de P. draws from this. Here are his arguments: The Mexicans did not observe the laws of perspective in their paintings; therefore they could not by means of them perpetuate the memory of their events: The Mexicans were bad painters;

## therefore

[p. 222]

therefore they could not be good historians. But if one chooses to employ a logic of this sort, it will have to be said also that all those who in writing cannot make a good hand cannot be good historians; for what letters are to our historians, figures were to the Mexican historians: and just as good histories can be written in a poor hand, so facts can be well represented by crude paintings: it is enough that the historians of both kinds make themselves understood.

But this is precisely what M. de P. cannot find in the copies of Purchas. He protests that, having compared their figures in various ways with the interpretation added to them, he could never discover any correspondence: that just as they are interpreted as of eight kings of Mexico, so they might be interpreted as of eight concubines of Moctezuma. But he could say the very same thing if the book Chun-yum of the philosopher Confucius, written in Chinese characters, were presented to him with its interpretation beside it in the French language. He would compare those characters in various ways with the interpretation, and, supposing that he found no correspondence, he could say that just as they interpret that book as of the nine conditions that a good emperor must have, so they might interpret it as of nine concubines, or of nine eunuchs of some ancient emperor; since he understands Chinese characters about as well as Mexican figures. If I could meet M. de Pauw, I would show him the correspondence that those figures have with their interpretation; but since he is ignorant of it, he must stand to the judgment of those who understand.

He believes, or wishes us to believe, that only those paintings of which Purchas published the copy escaped the fire which the first missionaries made of them; but this is most false, as we showed against Robertson at the beginning of vol. I. The paintings that escaped that fire were so many that they furnished the greater part of the materials for the ancient history of Mexico no less to the Spanish writers than to the Mexicans themselves. All the works of Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl, of Don Domingo Chimalpahin,

of [p. 223] of Don Fernando Alvarado Tezozomoc, of Don Tadeo de Niza, of Don Gabriel de Ayala, and of the others named in the Catalogue of writers placed at the beginning of our first volume, were made with the help of a great number of ancient paintings. The indefatigable Sahagún made use of very many paintings for his History of New Spain. Torquemada often cites the paintings that he consulted for his work. Sigüenza inherited the manuscripts and the paintings of Ixtlilxochitl, and procured many others at great expense, and after making use of them left them at his death, together with his precious library, to the College of Saints Peter and Paul of the Jesuits of Mexico, in whose library I saw and studied some of the said paintings. In the last two centuries the Indians often presented ancient paintings in the courts of Mexico as titles of property or of possession of certain lands, and for that reason there were interpreters instructed in the meaning of such paintings. Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo mentions that practice in the courts in the time of Monsignor Sebastián Ramírez de Fuenleal, president of the Royal Audience of Mexico: and because the understanding of such titles was of great importance for the decision of a number of lawsuits, there was already in the University of Mexico a professor charged with teaching the science of the paintings, the hieroglyphics, and the Mexican characters. The many paintings collected a few years ago by the Chevalier Boturini, and indicated in the Catalogue of his Museum printed in Madrid in the year 1746, like those that we have cited elsewhere, show that not so few, as M. de Pauw and Dr. Robertson think, escaped the fire of the missionaries.

Finally, in order to confirm still further what we have written in our History, and to make M. de P. understand the variety of the Mexican paintings, we shall indicate here in brief what Dr. Eguiara (q) left written in the learned preface

of

(q) Dr. Eguiara, worthy to be perpetuated in our memory for his most amiable character, for his incomparable modesty, for his great learning, and for the zeal with which he labored until his death in the service of his country, was born in Mexico toward the end of the last century. He was for many years professor [p. 224] of his Biblioteca mexicana. There were, he says, among the Mexican paintings, almanacs, called by them Tonalamatl, in which they published their prognostications concerning changes of the weather. Dr. Sigüenza adduced one of these paintings in his Ciclografia mexicana, as he bears witness in the work entitled Libra astronómica. Others contained the horoscopes of children, in which were represented their names, the day and the sign of their birth, and their fortune: of this kind of painting Jerónimo Román makes mention in his Repúblicas del mundo, pt. 2, vol. 2. Others were dogmatic, containing the system of their religion; others historical, others geographical, etc. It is true, adds the author just cited, that those paintings which were made for common and familiar use were clear and easily understood by anyone; but those which contained the arcana of religion were full of hieroglyphics, whose sense could not be grasped by the common people. There was also a great diversity among the paintings, both as to their authors and as to what concerned the manner of making them, and their end and use. Those which were made for the adornment of palaces were perfect; but in others, which contained an arcane sense, certain characters and some monstrous and horrible figures were to be seen. The painters were many; but the writing of characters, the composing of annals, and the treating of matters concerning religion and politics were employments proper to the priests. So far Dr. Eguiara.

Let M. de P. know, then, that in the Mexican paintings some

were of theology in that University, and there printed some highly esteemed theological treatises in one folio volume. He was Rector, and finally Chancellor, of the same University, and a Dignitary of that Metropolitan Church, always loved and revered by every sort of person for his spotless life and for his learning. After declining the bishopric of Yucatán, to which he had been nominated by the Catholic King for his notable merits, he published in Mexico a folio volume of the Biblioteca mexicana: for which work, besides the immense labor of collecting, arranging, and perfecting the materials, he had a large printing press, well furnished with Roman, Greek, and Hebrew types, brought at great expense from Paris. His death, which occurred in 1763, did not allow us to see completed that work, which would have done great honor to his country. [p. 225] were mere images of objects; there were also characters, not indeed composing words, like ours, but significant of things, like those of the astronomers and of the algebraists. Some paintings were designed to express precisely things or concepts, and, so to speak, to write: and in these they took no care for proportions or for beauty, because they were made in haste, and with the aim of instructing the mind, not of pleasing the eyes: but in those in which they sought to imitate nature, and which were executed with that slowness which works of this kind require, the proportions, the distances, the attitudes, and the rules of the art were observed, though not with all that perfection which we admire in the good painters of Europe. For the rest, I should like M. de P. to show me some rude or half-civilized people of the old continent who has employed as much industry and diligence as the Mexicans in order to perpetuate the memory of its events.

Dr. Robertson, where he treats of the civilization of the Mexicans in bk. 7 of his History, sets forth the progress that human industry makes in arriving at the invention of letters, by the combination of which it can express all the different sounds of speech. These successive steps are, according to him, from an actual picture to the plain hieroglyphic; from this to the allegorical symbol; then to the arbitrary character; and finally to the alphabet. If anyone, then, seeks in his history to learn to what degree the Mexicans had advanced, he certainly will not be able to guess; for that historical reasoner speaks of it with such ambiguity that at times he seems to believe them scarcely arrived at the second step, that is, at the plain hieroglyphic; and at times he appears to judge them advanced as far as the fourth, that of the arbitrary character. But whatever he may say, it is certain that all the aforesaid ways of representing concepts, except that of the alphabet, were in use among the Mexicans. Their numeral characters, and those significant of night, of day, of the year, of the century, of heaven, of earth, of water, of voice, of song, etc., were they not perhaps true arbitrary characters, or of

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.         Ff             convention? [p. 226] convention? Here, then, the Mexicans had arrived at the point to which the famous Chinese have attained after so many centuries of civilization. There is no other difference between the one and the other, except that the Chinese characters have multiplied to such an excess that a man’s life does not suffice to learn them.

Dr. Robertson himself, far from denying, as M. de Pauw rashly does, the secular wheels of the Mexicans, acknowledges their method in the reckoning of time, and says that, having observed that in the eighteen months of twenty days each the course of the sun was not completed, they added the five days nemontemi. “This near approach to philosophical accuracy,” he adds, “is a remarkable proof that the Mexicans had bestowed some attention upon inquiries and speculations, to which men in a very rude state never turn their thoughts.” (r) What would he have said, if he had known, as we know no less from the weighty testimony of Dr. Sigüenza than from our own observations on Mexican chronology, that the Mexicans not only counted three hundred and sixty-five days in their year, but also, having noticed the excess of almost six hours of the solar year over the civil one, remedied such a discrepancy by means of the thirteen intercalary days which they added to their century of fifty-two years?

§ V.

On the Arts of the Mexicans.

After M. de P. has given an ignominious description of Peru and of the barbarism of its inhabitants, he speaks of Mexico, of whose state, he says, (f) as many falsehoods and marvels have been related as of Peru; but it is certain, he adds, that these two nations were about equal, whether one compares the

their

(r) History of America, bk. 7.

(f) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 5, sect. 1. [p. 227] their polity, or consider their arts and their instruments. Agriculture was neglected among them, and architecture mean: their paintings were crude, and their arts very imperfect: their fortifications, their palaces, and their temples are mere fictions of the Spaniards. “If the Mexicans,” he says, “had had fortifications, they would have put themselves under cover from the muskets, and those six wretched iron cannon that Cortés brought with him would not have ruined in a moment so many bulwarks and entrenchments. The walls of their buildings were nothing but large stones laid one upon another. The vaunted palace in which the kings of Mexico dwelt was a hut: wherefore Fernando Cortés, finding no suitable dwelling in the whole capital of that state which he had just conquered, was obliged to build in haste a palace, which still exists.” It is not easy to count the absurdities of M. de P. on this subject: leaving aside, however, those which concern Peru, let us examine what he wrote against the arts of the Mexicans.

Of their agriculture we have spoken in other places, where we showed that the Mexicans not only cultivated with the greatest diligence all the lands of their empire, but also, with marvelous industry, created for themselves new lands to cultivate, forming on the water those gardens and floating fields which have been celebrated with so much praise by Spaniards and foreigners, and which to this day are admired by all who sail on those lakes. We have likewise demonstrated, on the deposition of many eyewitnesses, that not only the plants useful for sustenance, for clothing, and for health, but flowers also, and other vegetables that serve only the delights of life, were cultivated by them with the greatest diligence. Fernando Cortés in his letters to Charles V, and Bernal Díaz in his History, speak with amazement of the famous gardens of Iztapalapa and of Huaxtepec, which they saw; and Dr. Hernández, who saw those gardens forty years afterwards, also makes mention of them in his Natural History. Cortés himself, in a letter to Charles V of October 30, 1520, says thus: So

Ff2                      great [p. 228] great is the multitude of inhabitants in these countries that there is not even a span of ground that is not cultivated. One would have to be far too headstrong to refuse credence to the unanimous testimony of the Spanish authors.

We have likewise set forth, on the authority of these same men, the great diligence of the Mexicans in raising every sort of animal: in which kind of magnificence Moctezuma surpassed, as we have said elsewhere, all the kings of the world. The Mexicans, moreover, could not have raised so stupendous a variety of quadrupeds, reptiles, and birds without having a great knowledge of their nature, their instinct, their manner of living, etc.

Their architecture was not comparable with that of the Europeans, but it was certainly far superior to that of the greater part of the Asiatic and African peoples. Who will dare to put on a level with the houses, palaces, temples, bulwarks, aqueducts, and roads of the ancient Mexicans not only the miserable huts of the Tartars, of the Siberians, of the Arabs, and of those wretched nations which live between Cape Verde and the Cape of Good Hope, but not even the buildings of Ethiopia, of a great part of India, and of the islands of Asia and of Africa, except those of Japan? It suffices to compare what those authors who saw them have written of the one and of the others, to give the lie to M. de P., who has had the audacity to publish that all the American nations were inferior in industry and sagacity to the crudest peoples of the old continent.

He says that the vaunted palace of Moctezuma was nothing but a hut; but Cortés, Bernal Díaz, and the Anonymous Conqueror, who saw it so many times, affirm the exact opposite. “He had,” says Cortés, speaking of King Moctezuma, “in this city (of Mexico) houses for his dwelling of such a kind, and so marvelous, that I should not believe I could ever express their excellence and grandeur: wherefore I shall say nothing else, except that there are none equal to them in Spain.” Thus this Conqueror writes to his king, without fear of being contradicted by his captains and soldiers, who had also

themselves [p. 229] themselves before their eyes the Mexican palaces. The Anonymous Conqueror, in his curious and truthful Relation, speaking of the buildings of Mexico, says thus: “There were fine houses of lords, so large, and with so many apartments and gardens, upper and lower, that they filled us with amazement and admiration. Out of curiosity I entered four times a palace of Moctezuma, and after wandering through it until I was tired, I never saw it all. They were accustomed to have around a great courtyard very large chambers and halls; but above all there was one so large that more than three thousand persons could stand inside it without discomfort: it was such that in the gallery above it there was formed a little square in which thirty men on horseback could have played at canes.” Similar expressions are read in the History of Bernal Díaz. It is established by the deposition of all the historians of Mexico that the army of Cortés, composed of six thousand, and more than four hundred, among Spaniards, Tlaxcalans, and Cempoalans, was lodged entirely in the palace which had been King Axayacatl’s, and there was room left over for the lodging of King Moctezuma and his household, besides the storehouses in which the treasure of King Axayacatl was kept. The magnificence and very beautiful arrangement of the palace of the birds is likewise established by the deposition of the same historians, and Cortés adds that in the apartments there two great princes could lodge comfortably with all their court, and he describes minutely its porticoes, its loggias, and its gardens. The same Cortés tells Charles V that in the palace of King Nezahualpilli in Texcoco he lodged with six hundred Spaniards and forty horses, and that it was so large that another six hundred could still have been lodged there comfortably. Similarly he speaks of the palace of the lord of Iztapalapa, and of those of other cities, praising their structure, their beauty, and their magnificence. Such were the huts of the Mexican kings and lords.

M. de P.’s statement that Cortés had that palace built in haste because he could find no suitable dwelling in that whole capital is an error, or rather, to speak with greater propriety, a great lie. It is
