true, [p. 230] true that Cortés, during the siege of Mexico, burned and ruined the greater part of that great city: as he himself bears witness, and to that end he asked for and obtained from his allies some thousands of peasants, who had no other employment than that of going about ruining the buildings as the Spaniards advanced, so that no house should be left at their backs from which the Mexicans could harm them. It would not be surprising, therefore, if Cortés had found no suitable dwelling in a city which he himself had destroyed; but the ruin was not so general that there did not remain a great number of good houses in the quarter of Tlatelolco, in which all the Spaniards, with a good number of their allies, could have lodged comfortably. Since it pleased Our Lord, says Cortés in his last letter to Charles V, that this great city of Tenochtitlan should be conquered, it did not seem good to me to reside in it, on account of many inconveniences; so that I went with all my people to stay in Coyoacán. If what M. de Pauw says were true, it would have sufficed to say that he did not remain in Mexico because there were no houses in which to stay. The palace of Cortés was built on the same site where that of Moctezuma had formerly stood. If Cortés had not ruined this palace, he could have lived in it comfortably, as that monarch lived in it with all his court. It is, moreover, false that the palace built by Cortés exists at present; for it was burned in 1692 in a popular sedition. But above all it is most false that the walls of the Mexican buildings were nothing but large stones laid one upon another without any binding, as is proved by the testimony of all the historians, and by the remains of the ancient buildings, of which we shall speak in their place. So that there is not, in the whole passage of M. de P. already cited, a single proposition that is not an error.

Not content with annihilating the houses of the Mexicans, M. de P. sets about attacking their temples as well, and, angered against Solís because he affirms that the temples of Mexico were no fewer than two thousand, large and small, says thus: “There has not

ever [p. 231] ever been so great a number of public buildings in any city from Rome to Peking: wherefore Gómara, less rash or wiser than Solís, says that, counting seven little chapels, there were not found more than eight places destined for placing the idols of Mexico.” (t) That it may be seen how great is M. de P.’s infidelity in citing authors, I wish to give here the passage of Gómara which he alleges: “There were,” says this author in ch. 80 of his Crónica de la Nueva España, “many temples in the city of Mexico, scattered through the parishes or districts, with their towers, in which were the chapels and the altars for placing the idols… Almost all had the same form: so that what we shall say of the principal temple will suffice to make all the others known,” and after making a minute description of that great temple, in which he extols its height, its breadth, and its beauty, he adds: “Besides these towers, which were formed with their chapels above the pyramid, there were forty and more other towers, small and large, on other lesser Teocalli, (u) which were within the precinct of that principal temple, all of which were of the same form as that… Other Teocalli or Cues there were in other places of the city… All these temples had their own houses, their own priests, and their own gods, with everything necessary for their worship and service.” So that the same Gómara who, according to M. de P., numbers in Mexico no more than eight places destined for placing the idols, including seven little chapels, clearly reckons more than forty temples within the precinct of the principal temple, besides many others scattered through the parishes or districts. Who can ever trust M. de P. after so manifest a falsification?

It

(t) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 5, sect. 1.

(u) Teocalli, that is, House of God, was the name which the Mexicans gave to their temples. Among the Spaniards some called them temples, others Adoratories, others Mosques, as men accustomed to the language of the Saracens, and others Cues, a word taken from the Haitian language. Besides these names, they also gave to the small temples those of Sacrificaderos and Humilladeros, that is, places of sacrifice and of adoration.

[p. 232]

It is true that Solís showed himself rather imprudent in putting down as certain that number of temples which the first historians expressed only by conjecture; but M. de P. also shows himself rather injudicious in including among the public buildings even those little chapels which the Spaniards called temples. Of these there were innumerable: all those who saw that country before the Conquest testify unanimously that both in inhabited places and on the roads and on the mountains such buildings were to be seen everywhere; which, though small and quite different from our churches, were nevertheless called temples, because they were consecrated to the worship of the idols. Thus from the letters of Cortés, as from the History of Bernal Díaz, we learn that the conquerors scarcely took a step in their expeditions without coming upon some temple or chapel. Cortés says that he himself counted more than four hundred temples in the city of Cholula alone. But there was a great difference in size between the one and the other temples. Some were nothing but little mounds of no great height, on which there was a little chapel for the titular idol. Others again were of a stupendous size and breadth. Cortés, where he speaks of the greatest temple of Mexico, protests to Charles V that it is not easy to describe its parts, its size, and the things contained in it, that it was so large that within the precinct of the strong wall which surrounded it a town of five hundred houses could be contained. Not otherwise do Bernal Díaz, the Anonymous Conqueror, Sahagún, and Tovar, who saw them, speak of this and of other temples of Mexico, of Texcoco, of Cholula, and of other cities, and the Mexican and Spanish historians who wrote afterwards and informed themselves well, such as Acosta, Gómara, Herrera, Torquemada, Sigüenza, Vetancurt, etc., etc. Hernández described one by one the seventy-eight parts of which the greatest temple was composed; Cortés adds that among the lofty towers of the temples that adorned that great capital there were forty so elevated that the smallest of them was not inferior in height to the famous Giralda

(*) [p. 233] (*) of Seville. Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl makes mention in his manuscripts of that tower of nine stories which his famous great-great-great-grandfather Nezahualcoyotl built to the Creator of Heaven, which seems to have been that famous temple of Tezcutzingo which Valadés extols with such praises in his Rhetorica christiana.

All this cloud of witnesses testifies against M. de P. Nevertheless he will not believe that great multitude of temples in Mexico; because Moctezuma I, he says, was the one who gave that village the form of a city: from the reign of this monarch to the arrival of the Spaniards no more than forty-two years had elapsed: which space of time certainly does not suffice to build two thousand temples. Here are three assertions, which are so many errors. 1. It is false that Moctezuma I gave Mexico the form of a city; for we know from history that that court had the form of a city from the times of the first king, Acamapichtli. 2. It is also false that from the reign of Moctezuma I to the arrival of the Spaniards no more than forty-two years had elapsed. Moctezuma began to reign, as we have shown in the second Dissertation, in the year 1436, and ceased to live in 1464, and the Spaniards did not arrive at Mexico before 1519: from the beginning of that reign, therefore, to the arrival of the Spaniards eighty-three years elapsed, and from the death of that king fifty-five. 3. M. de Pauw shows himself wholly ignorant of the construction of the Mexican temples, and does not know how great was the multitude of workmen who concurred in the building of the public edifices, and how great their speed in building them. There has sometimes been seen in New Spain an entire village (though composed of wooden huts covered with hay) built in a single night, and the new colonists conducting into it their families, their animals, and all their goods. (x) As for the fortifications, it is certain, and

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.              Gg                 indubitable

(*) The very lofty and renowned bell tower of the Cathedral of Seville.

(x) See what Torquemada relates in bk. 3, ch. 33 of the Monarquía indiana. [p. 234] indubitable from the testimony of Cortés and of all those who saw the ancient cities of that empire, (y) that the Mexicans and all the other nations living in society used walls, bulwarks, palisades, ditches, and entrenchments. But even if none of so many eyewitnesses bore witness to it, the ancient fortifications that still stand today at Cuauhtochco, otherwise Guatusco, and near Molcaxac, of which we have spoken elsewhere, would suffice to demonstrate the error of M. de Pauw. It is true that such fortifications were not comparable with those of Europe; for neither had their military architecture been so far perfected, nor had they any need to put themselves under cover from artillery, of which they had no knowledge; but they gave sufficient proof of their industry in devising so many kinds of defenses with which to protect themselves from their ordinary enemies. Whoever, moreover, reads the unanimous testimony of the conquerors will not doubt the great toil they had in storming the ditches and entrenchments of the Mexicans at the siege of the capital, although they had so excessive a number of allied troops, and the advantages of firearms and of the brigantines. The terrible defeat which the Spaniards suffered when they wished to withdraw from Mexico will never allow one to doubt the fortifications of that capital. It was not surrounded by walls, because its situation rendered it secure enough by means of those ditches which were on the three causeways by which enemies could assail it; but other cities, which were not placed in so advantageous a situation, had walls and other defenses for their protection. Cortés himself gives an exact description of the walls of Cuauhquechollan.

But why waste time in accumulating testimonies and other proofs of the architecture of the Mexicans, when these men have left us, in the three famous causeways which they built within

the

(y) Of the ancient fortifications very frequent mention is made by Cortés in his letters to Charles V, Pedro de Alvarado and Diego de Godoy in their letters to Hernán Cortés, Bernal Díaz in his History, the Anonymous Conqueror in his Relation, Alonso de Ojeda in his Memoirs, and Sahagún in his History, all eyewitnesses. [p. 235] the same lake, and in the very ancient aqueduct of Chapultepec, an immortal monument of their industry?

The same authors who bear witness to the architecture of the Mexicans testify also to the excellence of their goldsmiths, their weavers, their gem-engravers, and their workers in feather. Many Europeans saw such works, and marveled at the skill of the American artisans. Their cast-metal work was admired by the goldsmiths of Europe, as several European authors then living affirm, and among others the historian Gómara, who had those works in his hands and heard the opinion of the goldsmiths of Seville, who did not think themselves capable of imitating them. (z) And where shall one ever find a man capable of making those marvelous works that we mentioned in bk. VII, § 51 of our History, and that are attested in agreement by very many writers, such as that, for example, of casting a fish whose scales are alternately one of gold and the other of silver? Cortés says in his second letter to Charles V that the images of gold and of feather were so well made by the Mexicans that no artisan of Europe could make them better; that as for the jewels, it could not be understood with what instruments works so perfect had been made; and that the works of feathers were such that they could not be imitated either in wax or in silk. In his third letter to the same Charles V, where he speaks of the booty of Mexico, he tells him that among the spoils of the Mexicans he found certain shields of gold and of feathers, and other works of the same material so marvelous that, since it was not possible for him to give a just idea of them in writing, he sends them to His Majesty, so that with his own eyes he may assure himself of their excellence and perfection. I am certain that Cortés would not have spoken thus to his king of those works which he sent him, so that with his own eyes he might see them, if they had not been such as he represented them. Almost in the same terms as Cortés all those authors speak of them who saw such works, such as Bernal Díaz, the Anonymous Conqueror, Gómara, Hernández,

Gg2           Acosta,

(z) Crónica de la Nueva España, chs. 39 and 79.

[p. 236]

Acosta, and others, from whom we have taken all that we have written in the History on this subject.

Dr. Robertson (A) indeed acknowledges the unanimous testimony of the ancient Spanish historians, and believes that they had no intention of deceiving us; but he affirms that all were induced to exaggerate by the illusion of their own minds, caused by the heat of their imagination. Here is a fine solution, of which anyone might avail himself to deny credence to all human histories. All, then, were deceived, not excepting even the most illustrious Acosta, nor the learned Hernández, nor the goldsmiths of Seville, nor King Philip II, nor the Supreme Pontiff Sixtus V, all of them admirers and praisers of those Mexican works? (B) All had a heated imagination, even those who wrote a few years after the discovery of Mexico? Yes, all; only the Scot Robertson and the Prussian de Pauw have, after two centuries and a half, that temperament in the fancy which is required for forming a just idea of things, perhaps because the cold of their countries will have slackened the heat of their imagination. “It is not from those descriptions, but from considering such specimens of their arts as are still preserved, that we must decide,” adds Robertson, “concerning their degree of merit (of the Mexican works)… Many of their ornaments in gold and silver, as well as various utensils employed in common life, are deposited in the magnificent cabinet of natural and artificial productions, lately opened by the king of Spain; and I am informed by persons on whose judgment and taste I can rely, that these boasted efforts of their art are uncouth representations of common objects, or very coarse images of the human and some animal forms, destitute of grace and propriety.” And in the note he says thus: “In the armoury of the royal palace of Madrid, are shewn suits of armour, which are called Montezuma’s. They are composed of thin lacquered copper-plates. In the opinion of very intelligent judges

they

(A) History of America, bk. 7.

(B) Let one reread on this what we have written in bk. VII, § 51 of our History. [p. 237] they are evidently eastern. The forms of the silver ornaments upon them, representing dragons, &c. may be considered as a confirmation of this. They are infinitely superior in point of workmanship to any effort of American art… The only unquestionable specimen of Mexican art that I know of in Great Britain, is a cup of very fine gold, which is said to have belonged to Montezuma… A man’s head is represented on this cup. On one side the full face, on the other the profile, on the third the back parts of the head… The features are rude, but very tolerable, and certainly too rude for Spanish workmanship. This cup was purchased by Edward earl of Orford, while he lay in the harbour of Cadiz.” Thus far Robertson, to whose arguments we reply: 1. That there is no reason to believe that those rude works are truly Mexican. 2. That we do not even know whether those persons on whose judgment Robertson thought he could rely were such as to deserve our faith; for we have observed that Robertson often relies on the testimony of Gage, of Corral, of Ibagnez, and of other such authors, wholly unworthy of being believed. It might also be that those persons who judged of such works had a heated imagination; since it is far easier, according to the condition of our fallen nature, to heat the imagination against a nation than in its favor. 3. That it is far more probable that those copper arms, believed by intelligent judges to be evidently eastern, are truly Mexican; for we are certain from the testimony of all the writers on Mexico that those nations used such plates of copper in war, and that with them they sought to cover the breast, the arms, and the thighs to defend themselves from arrows, whereas we do not know that they were ever in use among the inhabitants of the Philippine Islands, (C) or among any other people,

## that

(C) Dr. Robertson says that the Spaniards probably had those arms from the Philippine Islands. [p. 238] that traded with them. The dragons represented on those arms, instead of confirming, as Robertson believes, the opinion of those who think them eastern, rather confirm our own; for there never was any nation in the world among whom the images of terrible animals were so much in use on their arms as among the Mexicans. Nor should it cause wonder that these people had an idea of dragons, since they had also that of griffins, as Gómara bears witness. (D) 5. That although the images formed in the works of gold and silver are clumsy, these might nevertheless be excellent, marvelous, and inimitable; because in those works two wholly distinct and unconnected arts must be considered, that of design and that of casting: so that the fish of which we spoke above might be badly formed as to its figure, and yet that alternation of scales of gold and silver, made by casting, be marvelous and astonishing. 6. Finally, the judgment of some wholly unknown persons upon those few doubtful works which are in the royal cabinet of Madrid cannot prevail against the unanimous testimony of all the ancient writers, who saw innumerable works that were certainly Mexican.

From all that we have set forth so far, the great wrong that M. de Pauw has done the Mexicans is made manifest, in believing them inferior in industry and sagacity to the crudest peoples of the old continent. Father Acosta, where he discusses the industry of the Peruvians, says thus: “If these men are beasts, let him judge who will; for I am sure that in that to which they apply themselves, they surpass us by far.” (E) Is not this candid confession of a European of so much critical sense, so much experience, and so much impartiality worth far more than all the invectives of a Prussian philosopher, than all the reasonings of a Scottish historian, both of them either ill informed about the things

of

(D) Some lords had in their coats of arms a griffin flying and carrying a deer between its claws. Crónica de la Nueva España, ch. 71.

(E) Natural and Moral History, bk. 6, ch. 8. [p. 239] of America, or prejudiced against the Americans? But even if we conceded to M. de Pauw that the industry of the Americans in the arts is inferior to that of the other peoples of the world, nothing should be concluded from it against the souls of the Americans, or against the climate of America; since it is certain and indubitable that the inventions and the progress of the arts are for the most part due rather to chance, to necessity, and to greed than to talent. The men most industrious in the arts are not always the most talented, but often the neediest, or those most drawn to gold. “The sterility of the land,” says Montesquieu well, (F) “makes men industrious… it is necessary that they procure for themselves what the earth does not yield them. The fertility of a country brings with it, together with the ease of sustaining oneself, idleness.” “Necessity,” says Robertson, “is the great prompter and guide of mankind in their inventions.” The Chinese would certainly not be so industrious if the excessive population of their country did not make their own support difficult for them; nor would so much progress have been made in the arts in Europe, if the enticement of rewards, or the hope in the artisans of bettering their fortune, had been lacking. Nevertheless the Mexicans can boast many inventions of their own capable of immortalizing their name, such as, besides those of their famous cast-metal works and of the mosaics of feathers and shells, that of paper; (G) that of dyeing in indelible colors, of spinning and weaving the finest fur of rabbits and hares; that of making razors of Itztli; (H) that of raising cochineal so industriously for use in dyes; that of enamel in the floors of their houses, and a thousand others no less estimable, that can

be

(F) The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 18, ch. 4.

(G) See what we say in bk. 7 of the History about the different kinds of Mexican paper, namely of cotton, of maguey, of mountain palm, and of silk. The invention of paper is without doubt older in America than in Egypt, whence it was communicated to Europe. It is true that the paper of the Mexicans was not comparable in fineness with that of the Europeans; but it should be noted that they did not make it for writing, but for painting.

(H) See what we say in bk. 7, § 56 of the History about that art. [p. 240] can be seen in our History and in the works of the other historians of Mexico, just as the arts of the Peruvians can in the works of Acosta and of the Inca Garcilaso, and in the Lettere americane of the learned Count Carli. But what wonder is it that such inventions were found among those civilized nations, when even among other American peoples not yet polished some very singular arts were found? What invention, for example, is more singular and marvelous than that of taming sea fish and using them to hunt other, larger fish, as the inhabitants of the Antilles used to do? Would not this art alone, of which Oviedo, (I) Gómara, and other authors make mention, suffice to give the lie to the insulting invectives of M. de Pauw against the industry of the Americans?

§ VI.

On the Mexican Language.

“The languages of America,” says M. de P., “are so restricted, and so scant of words, that it is not possible to express in them any metaphysical concept. There is not one of these languages in which one can count beyond three. (L) It is not possible to translate a book, not only into the languages of the Algonquins and of the Guaranís or Paraguayans, but not even into those of Mexico or Peru, because they do not have a sufficient abundance of proper terms to enunciate general notions.” Whoever reads these magisterial decisions of M. de P. will doubtless be persuaded that he decides so

after

(I) Oviedo, General and Natural History of the Indies, bk. 13, ch. 10, and Sumario, ch. 8. Gómara, Historia general de las Indias, ch. 30. The species of fish of which the Indians availed themselves to hunt large fish, as falcons are used in Europe to hunt other birds, was rather small, called by them Guaicàn and by the Spaniards Reverso. See in the history of Oviedo the manner of using it.

(L) In the same section 1 of part 5 of the Recherches philosophiques, in which he affirms that there is no American language in which one can count beyond three, he says that the Mexicans counted up to ten. [p. 241] after having traveled through all America, having dealt with all those nations, and having examined all their languages; but it is not so. M. de P., without leaving his study in Berlin, knows the things of America better than the Americans themselves, and in his knowledge of those languages surpasses those who speak them. I learned the Mexican language, and heard it spoken by Mexicans for many years; and yet I did not know that it was so scant of numeral words, and of terms signifying universal ideas, until M. de P. came to enlighten me. I knew that the Mexicans gave the name Centzontli (400), or rather that of Centzontlatole (he who has four hundred voices), to that bird so renowned for the singular sweetness and the incomparable variety of its song. I knew also how the Mexicans anciently counted by xiquipilli both the almonds of cacao in their commerce and their troops in war: that a xiquipilli was worth eight thousand; so that to say that an army was composed, for example, of forty thousand men, they said that it had five xiquipilli. I knew, finally, that the Mexicans had numeral words with which to express as many thousands and millions as they wished; but M. de P. knows quite the contrary, and there is no doubt that he will know it better than I, for I had the misfortune to be born under a climate less favorable to intellectual operations. Nevertheless, to gratify the curiosity of my readers, I wish to set down here below the series of numeral names which the Mexicans have always used. (*) From it one sees that those who, according to M. de P., had no words for counting beyond three, had them nevertheless, in spite of him, for counting at least up to forty-eight million. In the same way we could convict Messrs. de la Condamine and de P. of error in many other languages

Among the materials gathered for this work of mine I have the numeral names of the Araucanian language, which, although it is the language of a nation more warlike than civilized, has nevertheless words to express even millions. I said that they had words for counting up to 48 million at least; for there are still others for carrying the numeration further, but longer words must be used, and those cited above suffice to give the lie to M. de P.

(M) Mari in the Araucanian language means ten, Pataca a hundred, Huaranca a thousand, Patachuaranca a hundred thousand, Maripatacahuaranca a million. After finishing this Dissertation I acquired also the series of the numeral names in the Otomi language. Although this language is esteemed one of the rudest of Mexico, it has nevertheless words to express as many thousands as one wishes. [p. 243] the American languages are so scant that they are not capable of expressing a metaphysical concept; a lesson which he learned from M. de la Condamine. “Time,” says this philosopher, reasoning about the languages of the Americans, “Duration, Space, Being, Substance, Matter, Body, all these words, and many others, have no equivalent words in their languages: and not only the names of metaphysical beings, but not even those of moral beings, can be expressed by them, except improperly and by long circumlocutions.” But M. de la Condamine knew as much about the American languages as M. de P., and he took his information, no doubt, from some ignorant man, as often happens to travelers. We are quite sure that many American languages do not have that scantiness of words which M. de la Condamine imagines; but leaving aside now what concerns the others, let us discourse only of the Mexican, which is the principal subject of our dispute.

It is quite true that the Mexicans had no words to express the concepts of matter, of substance, of accident, and the like; but it is likewise certain that no language, either of Asia or of Europe, had such words before the Greeks began to subtilize, to abstract their ideas, and to create new terms to express them. The great Cicero, who knew the Latin language so well, and flourished in those times in which it was at its greatest perfection, although he esteemed it more copious than the Greek, nevertheless often struggles in his philosophical works to find words corresponding to the metaphysical ideas of the Greeks. How many times was he not compelled to create new words equivalent in some way to the Greek, because he did not find them among the words used by the Romans? But even today, since that language was enriched with many words invented both by Cicero and by other learned Romans who, following his example, gave themselves to the study of philosophy, it still lacks terms to express many metaphysical concepts, unless one has recourse to the barbarous language of the schools. None of those languages which the philosophers of Europe speak had words signifying matter, substance, accident, and other similar concepts;

Hh2                      and [p. 244] and so it was necessary that those who philosophized should adopt the Latin words, or else the Greek. The ancient Mexicans, because they did not engage in the study of metaphysics, are excusable for not having invented words to express those ideas; yet their language is not so scant of terms signifying metaphysical and moral things as M. de la Condamine affirms of those of South America; indeed I affirm that it is not easy to find a language more apt than the Mexican for treating metaphysical matters, since it is difficult to find another that abounds so much as that does in abstract nouns; for there are few verbs in it from which verbals corresponding to those in -io of the Latins are not formed, and few also are the substantives or adjectives from which abstract nouns are not formed, expressing the being, or as it is called in the schools the quiddity of things: the equivalents of which I cannot find in Hebrew, Greek, Latin, French, Italian, English, Spanish, or Portuguese; of which languages I seem to have that knowledge which is required for making the comparison. Now, to give some specimen of this language, and to gratify the curiosity of the readers, I shall set below before their eyes some words signifying metaphysical and moral concepts, and understood even by the rudest Indians. (*)

The excessive abundance of such words has been the cause that the highest mysteries of the Christian religion have been set forth without great difficulty in the Mexican language, and that there have been well translated into

it

Tlamantli Thing                      Nejolnonotzaliztli Reflection Jeliztli Essence                     Tlachtopaittaliztli Foresight Qualloti Goodness                    Nejoltzotzonaliztli Doubt Neltiliztli Truth                    Tlalnamiquiliztli Remembrance Cetiliztli Unity                     Tlalcahualiztli Oblivion Ometiliztli Duality                  Tlazotlaliztli Love Jeitiliztli Trinity, &c. Tlo-it [p. 245] some books of Holy Scripture, and among others those of the Proverbs of Solomon and of the Gospels; which, like the Imitation of Christ of Thomas à Kempis and other similar books likewise translated into Mexican, certainly could not be rendered into languages that are scant of terms signifying moral and metaphysical things. So many are the books published in Mexican on religion and on Christian morals that of them alone a good library could be formed. After this Dissertation we shall give a brief Catalogue of the principal authors whom we remember, no less to confirm what we say than to show our gratitude for their labors. Some of them have published a great number of works that I have seen. Others, to ease for the Spaniards the understanding of the Mexican language, have composed grammars and dictionaries of it.

What we say of Mexican we could in great part affirm of other languages that were spoken in the dominions of the Mexicans, such as the Otomi, the Matlatzinca, the Mixtec, the Zapotec, the Totonac, and the Popoluca; for grammars and dictionaries of all these languages have likewise been composed, and in

Those Europeans who have learned Mexican, among whom there are Italians, French, Flemings, Germans, and Spaniards, have celebrated that language with great praises, and have extolled it to such a degree that by some it has been esteemed superior to the Latin and the Greek, as we have said elsewhere. The Chevalier Boturini affirms that in urbanity, in polish, and in the sublimity of its expressions there is no language that can be compared with the Mexican. This author was not a Spaniard, but a Milanese; he was not a common man, but erudite and critical; he knew at least Latin, Italian, French, and Spanish quite well, and of Mexican he knew enough to be able to make the comparative judgment. Let M. de P., then, acknowledge his error, and learn not to decide on matters that he does not know.

Among the proofs on which Count de Buffon wishes to support his system of the recent organization of matter in the New World, he says that the organs of the Americans were coarse, and their language barbarous. “Let the list of their animals be seen,” he adds, “their names are so difficult to pronounce that it is a wonder that there have been Europeans who took the trouble to write them.” But I do not wonder so much at their trouble in writing them as at their carelessness in copying them. Among so many European authors who have written the civil or natural history of Mexico in Europe, I have not found even one who has not altered and disfigured the names of the persons, the animals, and the Mexican cities, and some have done so to such a degree that it is not possible to guess what they meant to write. The history of the animals of Mexico passed from the hands of its author, Dr. Hernández, to those of Nardo Antonio Recchi, who knew nothing of Mexican; from the hands of Recchi it passed to those of the Lincean Academicians of Rome, who published it with notes and dissertations; and of this edition Count de Buffon made use. Among so many hands of Europeans ignorant of the Mexican language,

could [p. 247] could not but have the names of the animals altered. To satisfy himself of the alteration which they suffered in the hands of Count de Buffon, whoever wishes need only compare the Mexican names that are read in the Natural History of that philosopher with those of the Roman edition of Hernández. For the rest, it is certain that the difficulty we find in pronouncing a language to which we are not accustomed, and especially if its articulation is too different from that of our own language, does not prove that it is barbarous. That same difficulty which Count de Buffon feels in pronouncing Mexican names, the Mexicans would feel in pronouncing French names. Those who are accustomed to the Spanish language feel great difficulty in pronouncing German and Polish, and these seem to them the harshest and roughest of all. The Mexican language was not that of my parents, nor did I learn it as a child; and yet all the Mexican names of animals produced by Count de Buffon as an argument for the barbarity of that language seem to me incomparably easier to pronounce than many others taken from certain European languages, which he employs (N) in his Natural History: and perhaps it will seem so to those Europeans who are accustomed neither to the one nor to the other languages; nor will there be wanting someone to wonder that Count de Buffon took the trouble to write those names, capable of frightening the most courageous writers. Finally, as to what concerns the American languages, one must abide by the judgment of those Europeans who knew them rather than by the opinion of those who know nothing of them.

## § VII

On the Laws of the Mexicans.

Wishing to impugn that antiquity which Gemelli attributes by mistake to the court of the Mexicans, M. de Pauw alleges the anarchy of their government and the scantiness of their laws; and treating of the government of the Peruvians he says “that there cannot be laws in a despotic state; and in case there once were, it is not possible at present to analyze them, because we do not know them; nor can we know them, because they were never written, and their memory must have failed with the death of those who knew them.”

No one had made mention of the anarchy of the kingdom of Mexico before M. de P. came into the world, whose brain seems to have a particular organization for understanding things contrary to all other men. There is no one so ignorant of the history of Mexico as not to know that those peoples were subject to particular lords, and the whole state to one supreme head, who was the king of Mexico. All the historians extol the great authority of that sovereign, and the supreme respect which his vassals bore him: if this is anarchy, all the states of the world will doubtless be anarchic.

Despotism was not introduced in Mexico until the last years of the monarchy. In earlier times the monarchs had always respected the laws promulgated by their predecessors, and had zealously watched over their observance. Even in the times of Moctezuma II, who was the only truly despotic king, the magistrates judged according to the laws of the kingdom, and Moctezuma himself severely punished transgressors, abusing his power only in what could serve to increase his opulence and his authority.

These laws were not written; but they were perpetuated in the memory of men no less by tradition than by paintings. There was no subject who did not know them; for fathers of families did not cease to instruct their children in them, so that

by [p. 249] by avoiding transgression, they might shun the punishment. The copies of the paintings of the laws were without doubt infinite; for although they were so furiously persecuted by the Spaniards, I have nevertheless seen many of them. The understanding of such paintings is not so difficult for one who has knowledge of the manner in which the Mexicans represented things, of the characters they used, and of their language; but for M. de P. they will be as unintelligible as the laws of the Chinese, expressed in the characters proper to that nation. Moreover, after the conquest many well-informed Mexicans wrote down in our characters the laws of Mexico, of Acolhuacan, of Tlaxcala, of Michoacán, etc. Among others Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl wrote in the Spanish language the eighty laws promulgated long ago by his famous great-great-great-grandfather King Nezahualcoyotl, as we have said in the History. The Spaniards, besides, sought out the ancient laws and customs of those nations with greater diligence than any other point of their history; because a knowledge of them was of great importance to Christian government, both civil and ecclesiastical, especially with regard to marriages, the prerogatives of the nobility, the quality of vassalage, and the condition of slaves. They informed themselves by word of mouth from the best-instructed Indians, and studied their paintings. Besides the first missionaries, who labored fruitfully in this enterprise, Don Alonso de Zorita, one of the principal judges of Mexico, learned in matters of law and familiar with those countries, made diligent researches into them by order of the Catholic King, and composed that most useful work of which we have made mention in the Catalogue of the writers on the ancient history of Mexico. Here is how the ancient laws of the Mexicans could be known without having been written by them.

But what laws! Many of them worthy, says Acosta, of our admiration, and according to which those peoples ought to be governed even in their Christianity. First, the constitution of their state, in what concerns the succession to the crown, could not have been better conceived, as one in which the inconveniences of hereditary succession were avoided alike, and those

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.         Ii                       of [p. 250] of the elective. One individual of the royal family had to be elected, so as thus to preserve the splendor of the crown, and to prevent the throne from ever being occupied by a man of low birth. Since it was not the son who succeeded, but the brother, there was no danger that so eminent and so important an office would be exposed to the indiscretion of an inexperienced youth, or to the malignity of an ambitious regent.

Had the brothers, moreover, been bound to succeed according to the order of their birth, the crown would necessarily have fallen at times to a man unfit for government, and it might also have happened that the presumptive heir would plot against the life of the sovereign in order to anticipate the succession. Both inconveniences were obviated by election. The electors chose, from among the brothers of the dead king, and failing them from among the sons of former kings, the one most fit to command the nation. Had it been in the king’s power to name the electors, he could have chosen those who were most favorable to his designs, and procured their votes in favor of that brother who was dearest to him, and perhaps even in favor of his son, heedless of the fundamental laws of the state; but it was not so; for the electors themselves were elected by the body of the nobility, which entrusted to them the votes of the whole nation. If the office of the electors had been perpetual, they might, abusing their authority, have become masters of the monarchy; but since their electoral voice ended with the first election, and new electors were then chosen for the next, it was not so easy for ambition to usurp authority. Finally, to avoid other inconveniences, the true electors were no more than four, men of the first nobility, of great prudence and of notorious probity. It is true that not even after so many precautions could all disorders be prevented; but what government was ever among men that was not exposed to greater evils?

The Mexican nation was warlike, and therefore needed a head who was knowledgeable and expert in the trade of war: now what counsel could be taken more suited to this end than

that [p. 251] that of not electing as king one who had not by his merits obtained the post of general of the army, and of not crowning one who, after his election, had not procured in war the victims which, according to their system of religion, had to be sacrificed at the festivals of the coronation?

That readiness with which the Mexicans shook off the yoke of the Tepanecs, and that glory which their arms won in the conquest of Azcapotzalco, were naturally bound to excite the rivalry and the distrust of their neighbors, and especially that of the king of Acolhuacan, who had been, and was even then, the greatest king of that land, and who, the throne of Mexico being moreover still unsteady, needed a strong support to sustain it. The king of Acolhuacan, who had recently recovered, with the help of the Mexicans, the crown formerly usurped from him by the tyrant Tezozomoc, had to fear that some powerful subject, following the footsteps of that tyrant, might incite to rebellion a part of his kingdom, and deprive him, as his father had been, of the crown and of life. The king of Tlacopan, who occupied a newly established and not very considerable throne, had more to fear. Each of these kings was by himself little secure, and had to distrust the other two; but united all three together they could form an invincible power. Now what do they do? They form a triple alliance, which makes each secure with regard to the other two, and all three with regard to their subjects. This was the alliance that strengthened the thrones of Acolhuacan and of Tlacopan, and that made their conquests easier for the Mexicans: an alliance so firm and so well ordered that it never fell apart until the arrival of the Spaniards. This single stroke of policy suffices to demonstrate the discernment and sagacity of those nations; but there were so many others like it that if we wished to relate them all, it would be necessary to copy a good part of the History.

The judicial system of the Mexicans and of the Texcocans furnishes us with several useful lessons of politics. That diversity of grades among the magistrates served good order; their assiduity in the tribunals from the break of day until evening shortened the course

Ii2                      of [p. 252] of the causes, and turned them away from many clandestine practices which could have swayed them in favor of one of the parties. The capital penalties prescribed against those who perverted justice, the promptness of their execution, and the vigilance of the sovereigns kept the magistrates in check, and the care taken to supply them at the king’s expense with everything necessary made them inexcusable. Those assemblies that were held every twenty days before the sovereign, and particularly that general assembly of all the magistrates every eighty days to conclude the pending causes, besides avoiding those great evils which the slowness of judgments occasions, brought it about that the magistrates communicated their lights to one another reciprocally; that the king came to know better those whom he had appointed depositaries of his authority; that innocence had more recourse; and that the apparatus of judgment rendered justice more respectable. That law which permitted appeal from the tribunal of the Tlacatecatl to that of the Cihuacoatl in criminal causes, and not in civil ones, shows that the Mexicans, respecting the laws of humanity, recognized that more was required to believe a man guilty than to declare him a debtor. In the trials of the Mexicans no other proof against the accused was admitted than that of witnesses. Torture was never seen employed among them to make the innocent guilty by force of torments, nor did they avail themselves of those barbarous proofs of the duel, of fire, of boiling water, and the like, which were once so frequent in Europe and are now read by us with astonishment in the histories. “There will be no one who does not marvel,” says Montesquieu on this subject, (O) “that our forefathers made the honor, the fortune, and the property of citizens depend on certain things which belonged less to the jurisdiction of reason than to that of chance; and that they constantly employed those proofs which proved nothing, and were connected neither with innocence nor with crime.” What we now say of those proofs, our posterity will say in time to come of torture, and

## will

(O) The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 28, ch. 17. [p. 253] will never cease to marvel that such a proof was generally in use for so many centuries in the most enlightened part of the world. The oath was a proof of great weight in the trials of the Mexicans, as we have said elsewhere; for just as they were persuaded of the terrible punishments that the gods would infallibly inflict on the perjured, so they believed that no one would dare to perjure himself; but we do not know that such a proof was permitted to the plaintiffs against the accused, but only to the accused, to clear himself of the crime.

The Mexicans severely punished all those crimes that are particularly repugnant to reason or prejudicial to the state: lese-majesty, homicide, theft, adultery, incest, and the other excesses of this kind against nature; sacrilege, drunkenness, and lying. They acted wisely in not leaving such misdeeds unpunished; but they erred in the measure of the penalty, which in some crimes was excessive and cruel. I do not claim to excuse the faults of that nation; but neither can I conceal that whatever is reprehensible in their legislation will find its examples among the most famous peoples of the old continent, and such as to make the laws of the Mexicans appear very mild and more conformable to reason. “The celebrated laws of the twelve tables,” says Montesquieu, (P) “are full of most cruel provisions… one sees in them the punishment of fire, and penalties always capital.” Yet this is that most praised compilation which the Romans made of the best that they found among the Greek peoples. Now if the best of the most civilized Greece was such, what must that have been which was not so good? What must have been the legislation of those peoples whom they called barbarians? What law more inhuman and cruel than that of the twelve tables, which permitted creditors to tear apart (Q) the debtor who did not pay, and for each to carry off his

share

(P) The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 14, ch. 15.

(Q) “If there were several to whom the accused had been adjudged, they permitted them, if they wished, to cut up and equally divide the body of the man handed over to them.” Aul. Gell., Noct. Att., bk. 20, ch. 1. I know well what several jurists say to justify this law, but I also know that they have not succeeded. [p. 254] share in satisfaction of his claim? And this law was not promulgated in Rome in the rude beginnings of that so renowned city, but three hundred years after its foundation. What law, on the contrary, is more iniquitous than that of the famous legislator Lycurgus, which permitted theft to the Lacedaemonians? The Mexicans punished this crime, so pernicious to society; but they did not proceed to capital punishment unless the thief was not in a condition to make satisfaction and to pay for the offense with his liberty and his goods. It was not so with regard to theft committed in sown fields; for these, being by their situation more exposed to plunder, had greater need of the protection of the laws; but that same law which prescribed capital punishment against him who stole there a certain number of fruits or plants permitted travelers in need to eat there as much as they required, to remedy their present necessity. How much more reasonable was this law than that of the twelve tables, which condemned to be hanged, without distinction, whoever took anything from the sown fields of others? (*)

Lying, that sin so pernicious to society, is for the most part left unpunished in very many countries of the old continent, and in Japan is often punished with death. The Mexicans kept equally far from the one extreme and the other. Their legislators, well aware of the genius and inclinations of the nation, perceived that if they did not prescribe grave penalties against lying and drunkenness, men would lack the judgment to satisfy their respective obligations, truth would be lacking in trials, and good faith in contracts. Experience has shown how harmful to those nations the impunity of these two sins is.

But in the midst of their severity the Mexicans took care not to involve the innocent in the punishment of the guilty. Many laws of Europe and Asia prescribed the same penalty for one guilty of high treason and for all his family. The Mexicans punished

this

(*) “Whoever by night secretly grazes or cuts down a crop raised by the plow, let him be hanged, a victim put to death for Ceres.” [p. 255] this crime with capital punishment; they did not, however, deprive the relatives of the offender of life, but only of liberty: and not all of them either, but only those who, being aware of the treason and having been unwilling to reveal it, had made themselves guilty as well. How much more humane is this law than those of Japan! Those laws of which Montesquieu says, (R) that they punish a whole family, or a whole quarter, for a single crime: those laws that cannot find innocent persons where there are guilty. We do not know that the Mexicans prescribed any penalty against those who spoke ill of the government: it seems that they did not set great store by that venting of the self-love of subjects which is so much feared in other countries.

Their laws concerning marriages were without doubt more honest and more decorous than those of the Romans, the Greeks, the Persians, the Egyptians, and other peoples of the old continent. The Tartars marry their daughters; the ancient Persians and the Assyrians took their own mothers; the Athenians and the Egyptians their sisters. In Mexico every marriage was strictly forbidden between persons related in the first degree of consanguinity and of affinity, except between brothers- and sisters-in-law, when the brother on dying left any child. That prohibition shows that the Mexicans judged better of matrimony than all the aforesaid nations. That exception demonstrates their sentiments of humanity. If a widow passes to second nuptials, she often has the displeasure of seeing her children little loved by a father who did not give them life; her new husband little respected by those same children, who regard him as a stranger; and the children of the one marriage and of the other so disunited and discordant among themselves as if they had been born of different mothers. Now what better counsel (I speak according to the rules of human politics, by which those nations were guided that had no knowledge of the holy laws of Christianity), what better counsel, I say, could the Mexicans take to remedy

those

(R) The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 14, ch. 15. [p. 256] those evils all too common than that of marrying the widow to her brother-in-law? Many ancient nations of Europe, imitated by not a few modern peoples of Asia and of Africa, bought their wives, and therefore exercised over them an authority far greater than that which the author of Nature granted them, and treated them more as slaves than as companions. The Mexicans acquired their wives only by means of lawful and decorous suits; and although they presented gifts to the parents, these were not on account of a price for the daughter, which they did not claim, but only a token of respect to win their goodwill and to bend their will to the contract. The Romans, though they had no scruple about lending their wives, (S) nevertheless had the right by law to take their lives whenever they were caught in adultery. This iniquitous law, which made the husband judge in his own cause, and even executor of his own sentence, instead of preventing adulteries increased parricides. Among the Mexicans that infamous traffic in their wives was not permitted to husbands, nor did they have any authority over their lives. He was punished with capital punishment who took the life of his wife, even though he caught her in adultery. This, they said, is to usurp the authority of the magistrates, to whom it belongs to take cognizance of crimes and to punish them according to the tenor of the laws. Before the Julian law De Adulteriis was made by Augustus, we do not know, says Vives, (*) that any trial for adultery was ever held in Rome: that is to say, that that celebrated nation lacked justice on a point so grave and so important for more than seven centuries.

If after making the comparison of the laws one wishes to make

that

(S) “At Rome,” says Montesquieu, “it was permitted to a husband to lend his wife to another. Plutarch says so expressly. It is known that Cato lent his wife to Hortensius, and Cato was not capable of violating the laws of his country.” The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 25.

(*) Notes on ch. 5, bk. 3, De civitate Dei. Many jurists say that this power over the lives of their adulterous wives was taken from husbands by the Cornelian law De sicariis; but however that may be, it is certain that this law was made by Sulla toward the end of the seventh century of Rome; so that as to time there is no great difference between this law and that of Augustus. [p. 257] that of the nuptial rites of these two nations as well, one will find much superstition among both, but for the rest a great diversity will be seen: those of the Mexicans were honest and decent, those of the Romans obscene and infamous, as we shall see elsewhere.

As for the laws of war, it is difficult for them to be just among a warlike people: the great esteem that it has for valor and military glory makes it regard as enemies those who are not, and the ambition to conquer drives it to overstep the bounds prescribed by justice. Nevertheless in the laws of the Mexicans such traits of equity are seen as would do honor to the most civilized nations. War could not be declared without having first examined its reasons in full council, and without their having been approved by the High priest. Moreover, embassies, often repeated, had to be sent beforehand to those against whom war was being resolved, in order to obtain peacefully, by way of some accommodation, what was wanted, before coming to a rupture. Such delays gave their enemies time to prepare for defense; but besides serving as their justification, they contributed likewise to their glory, since among them it was reckoned cowardice to make war on unprepared enemies, and without having first solemnly challenged them, so that the victory could never be ascribed to anything but their bravery. It is true that these laws were not always observed; but they were not for that reason less just: and if there was injustice in the conquests of the Mexicans, it was certainly no less in those of the Romans, the Greeks, the Persians, the Goths, and other celebrated nations. One of the great evils that war usually brings with it is that of famine, on account of the hostilities that are carried on in the fields. It is not possible to prevent this evil altogether; but if there was ever anything capable of moderating it, it was without doubt that custom of the Mexicans and of the other peoples of Anahuac of having in every province a place assigned as a field of battle. No less conformable to reason and humanity was that other custom of theirs of having in time of war, every five days, a whole day of truce and rest.

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.         Kk                    Those [p. 258] Those nations had formed a kind of Jus Gentium, by virtue of which, if the lord, the nobility, and the commons rejected the proposals made to them by another people or nation, and referred the decision to arms, and were defeated, the lord lost his sovereign right, the nobility the supreme dominion that it had over its possessions, the commons were subjected to personal service, and all those who had been taken prisoner in the heat of the fray were deprived, quasi ex delicto, of liberty and of the right to life. This is assuredly opposed to those ideas of humanity that we hold; but the general convention of those peoples made that inhumanity less blameworthy, and the far more atrocious examples of the most civilized nations of the old continent dispel that horror which the cruelty of those American peoples at first sight causes in us. “Among the Greeks,” says Montesquieu, (T) “the inhabitants of a city taken by force of arms lost their liberty, and were sold as slaves.” Certainly the inhumanity that the Mexicans exercised toward their enemy prisoners is not to be compared with that which the Athenians used toward their own citizens. “A law of Athens,” says the aforesaid author, “ordained that when the city was besieged, all the useless people should be put to death.” Neither among the Mexicans, nor among any other nation of the New World at all polished, will a law be found as barbarous as that of the most civilized people of ancient Europe; indeed the greatest concern of the Mexicans, and of all those nations of Anahuac, when some city of theirs was about to be besieged, was to place their children, women, and invalids in safety, either by sending them to other cities or else to the mountains. Thus they withdrew that weak folk from the fury of the enemies, and otherwise prevented the excessive consumption of provisions.

The tribute that was paid to the kings of Anahuac was excessive, and the laws that prescribed it were likewise tyrannical; but these laws were consequences of despotism, introduced there in the

last

(T) The Spirit of the Laws, bk. 20, ch. 14. [p. 259] last years of the Mexican monarchy; and even at its greatest increase it did not reach that excess of seizing the lands of the empire and the goods of subjects which we justly blame in Asiatic monarchs; nor was it ever heard that laws on tributes were published by the sovereigns of Anahuac as extravagant and harsh as very many that have been published in the ancient world, such as, for example, that of the emperor Anastasius, who imposed a burden even on breathing: that each one pay for his draught of air.

But if we censure in the laws on tributes the tyrannical ambition of those monarchs, we cannot help praising and admiring in their laws on commerce the civilization of those nations and the wisdom of their legislators. To have in every city or town a square destined for the trade in all the things that could serve the needs and the delights of life was useful for bringing all the merchants together there for the readiest sale of wares, and put them under the eyes of inspectors or commissaries, so that all fraud and disorder in contracts might be avoided. That every ware had its determined place contributed to good order and to the convenience of those who wished to provide themselves with it. The Tribunal of Commerce, established in the same square of the market to settle the differences that arose among the traders and to punish promptly any excess that might occur there, kept the rights of justice inviolable and ensured public tranquillity. To these wise provisions was owed that marvelous order which the first Spaniards admired there, in the midst of so excessive a number of traders.

Finally, in the laws on slaves the Mexicans were superior to the most civilized nations of ancient Europe. If one wishes to make a comparison of the laws of the Mexicans with those of the Romans, the Lacedaemonians, and other celebrated peoples, one will soon see in the latter such barbarity and cruelty as causes horror, and in the former great humanity and great respect for the law of Nature (I do not speak now of prisoners of war, of whom I shall treat later). What law is more humane than that which made free at birth

Kk2                      all [p. 260] all men, even of slave parents; which left to the slave the ownership of his goods and of what he acquired by his own industry or labor; which obliged the master to treat the slave as a man and not as a beast; did not allow him any authority over his life, and even deprived him of the power to sell him in the market, unless after having had his unruliness legally established? Now how different from these were the laws of the Romans! Those people, by the supreme authority granted them by the laws, were masters not only of all that their slaves acquired by their labor, but even of their lives, (V) of which they deprived them according to their caprice; they treated them with the greatest inhumanity, and made them endure the most atrocious torments: and, that the inhuman character of this nation may be seen, while they so extended the authority of masters against slaves, they restricted it likewise in what was in favor of these. The Fufia Caninia law forbade masters to manumit by testament beyond a certain number of slaves. In the Silanian law, and in others, it was prescribed that whenever a master was killed, all those slaves of his who lived within the same house, or in a place near it from which his voice could be heard, should likewise be put to death. If he was killed on some journey, all those slaves who had remained with him had to die, and likewise all those who had fled from him, however manifest their innocence might be. The Aquilian law included under one and the same action the wound inflicted on a slave and that inflicted on another man’s beast. To such an excess did the barbarity of the most civilized Romans go. Nor indeed were the laws of the Lacedaemonians more humane, which granted slaves no action at law against those who insulted or injured them.

If

(V) What wonder that the Romans granted that barbarous authority to masters over slaves, when they had granted it also to fathers of families over their lawful children? “Let the father have over his lawful children the power of life, of death, and of sale.” This single law, published in Rome by the first kings, and afterwards inserted by the Decemvirs in the twelve Tables, suffices to show that the legislation of the Mexicans was more humane.

[p. 261]

If, beyond what has been said so far, one wishes to compare the system of education that existed among the Mexicans with that of the Greeks, it will be recognized that the instruction which the Greeks gave their children in the arts and in the sciences was not so great as that which Mexican children and young people received in manners from their parents. The Greeks applied themselves more to enlightening the mind, the Mexicans to rectifying the heart. The Athenians prostituted their young men to the most execrable obscenity in those very schools that were destined to instruct them in the arts. The Lacedaemonians trained their children, according to the precept of Lycurgus, in stealing, to make them cunning and nimble, and whipped them severely when they caught them in some theft, punishing in them not that sin, but the little skill in committing it, whereby they were caught. But the Mexicans taught their children, together with the arts, religion, modesty, honesty, sobriety, the laborious life, the love of truth, and respect for their elders.

This is a brief but true sample of the civilization of the Mexicans, drawn from their ancient history, from their paintings, and from the accounts of the most exact Spanish historians. Thus were governed those peoples whom M. de Pauw believes the most savage in the world. Thus were governed those peoples inferior in industry and sagacity to the rudest peoples of the old continent. Thus were governed those peoples whose rationality some Europeans have wished to doubt.

## D I S S E RTAT I O N V I I 

O N T H E B O U N DA R I E S A N D P O P U L AT I O N O F

## T H E K I N G D O M S O F A N A H UAC 

T    he mistakes of many Spanish writers concerning the boundaries

of the Mexican empire, and the absurdities of M. de Pauw and of other foreign authors concerning the population of those countries, have obliged me to write this Dissertation in order to set the truth in a clear light: which I shall try to do with all possible brevity.

§ I.

On the Boundaries of the Kingdoms of Anahuac.

Solís, following several ill-informed Spanish writers, asserts that the Mexican empire extended from the Isthmus of Panama to Cape Mendocino in California. Father Touron, a French Dominican, wishing in his General History of America to extend those limits still further, says that all the countries discovered in North America were subject to the king of Mexico; that the extent of that empire from east to west was five hundred leagues, and from north to south two hundred or two hundred and fifty; that its limits were on the north the Atlantic Ocean, on the west the gulf of Anian, on the south the Pacific sea, and on the east the Isthmus of Panama; but besides the geographical errors in this description, there is also a contradiction; for if it were ever true that that empire extended from the Isthmus of Panama to the gulf, or rather strait, of Anian, its extent would not have been only five hundred leagues, but even a thousand, since no fewer than fifty degrees would have been included in it.

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.          Ll                     The [p. 266] The cause of such errors is that these authors were persuaded that in Anahuac there was no other sovereign than that of Mexico; that the kings of Acolhuacan and of Tlacopan were his subjects, and that the Michoacans and the Tlaxcalans, who also belonged to that Crown, afterwards rebelled. But it is not so: for none of the aforesaid states ever belonged to the kingdom of Mexico, as appears from the testimony of all the Indian historians and of all those Spanish writers who obtained information from them, such as Motolinía, Sahagún, and Torquemada. The king of Acolhuacan had always been an ally of the king of Mexico since the year 1424, but was never his subject. It is true that when the Spaniards arrived there, King Cacamatzin seemed to depend on Moctezuma his uncle; because, on account of the overbearing power of his brother Ixtlilxochitl, he needed the help of the Mexicans. The Spaniards afterwards saw Cacamatzin come to meet them as ambassador of the king of Mexico, and even serve this man as an arm-bearer. They saw also that he was taken prisoner to Mexico by order of Moctezuma. All this makes the error of the Spaniards excusable on many counts; but it is certain that those demonstrations made by Cacamatzin to Moctezuma were not services of a vassal toward his king, but courtesies of a nephew toward his uncle; and that Moctezuma, in having him seized to please the Spaniards, arrogated to himself an authority that did not belong to him, and did that king a most grievous wrong, for which he afterwards had cause to repent. As for the king of Tlacopan, it is true that he was created king by the king of Mexico; but there was granted to him perfect dominion and full sovereignty in his states, on the sole condition of being the perpetual ally of the Mexicans and of giving them aid with his troops whenever they should need it. The king of Michoacán and the republic of Tlaxcala were always rivals and capital enemies of the Mexicans, and there is no record that either state was ever subject to the Crown of Mexico.

We must say the same of many other countries which were believed by the Spanish historians to be provinces of the Mexican empire. How was it possible that a nation which had been reduced to

a [p. 267] single city under the dominion of the Tepanecs should subjugate in less than a century as many peoples as there were from the Isthmus of Panama to California? All that the Mexicans actually did, although far less than what the aforesaid authors say, was a truly astonishing thing, and the rapidity of their conquests would not be credible if it were not confirmed by so many undeniable documents. For the rest, neither from the narrative of the Indian historians, nor from the enumeration of the states conquered by the kings of Mexico that is found in the Collection of Mendoza, nor from the register of the tributary cities set out in the same Collection, can any foundation be had for confirming that arbitrary enlargement of the Mexican dominions; on the contrary, the very opposite is established by the account of Bernal Díaz. He says thus in ch. 93 of his History: “The great Moctezuma had many garrisons and men of war on the frontiers of his states. He had one in Soconusco to defend himself against Guatemala and Chiapas; another to defend himself against the people of Pánuco, between Tuzapan and that place which we call Almería; another in Coatzacoalcos, and another in Michoacán.” (a)

We are certain, then, in the first place, that the Mexican dominions did not extend toward the southeast beyond Soconusco, and that none of all those provinces which are today included in the three dioceses of Guatemala, Nicaragua, and Honduras belonged to the Mexican empire. In the fourth book of our History we said that Tliltototl, a celebrated Mexican general, in the last years of King Ahuitzotl carried his victorious arms as far as Cuauhtemallan; but here too we add that it is not known that that country then remained subject to the Crown of Mexico: on the contrary, history shows quite the opposite. Torquemada, in bk. 2, ch. 81, makes mention of the Conquest of Nicaragua by the Mexicans; but the very same writer who in the cited place affirms it of a Mexican army in the time of Moctezuma II attributes it, in bk. 3, ch. 10, to a colony that went out many years earlier by order of the

Ll2                      gods

(a) To understand better what we say concerning the boundaries of the kingdoms of Anahuac, it will be necessary to have our geographical maps before one’s eyes. [p. 268] gods from the neighborhood of Soconusco; for which reason no account is to be taken of his report.

The same Bernal Díaz, both in the place cited and in ch. 166, affirms expressly that the Chiapanecs were never subjugated by the Mexicans; but this cannot be understood of the whole country of the Chiapanecs, but only of one part of it; for we know from Remesal, the chronicler of that province, that the Mexicans had a garrison in Tzinacantlan, and it is established by the Register of the tributes that Tochtlan, (*) and other cities of that country, were tributary to the Mexicans.

On the northeast the Mexicans did not advance beyond Tuzapan, as is established by the cited passage of Bernal Díaz; and we know for certain that the Panucans were never subjected to the Mexicans. On the east we have fixed the boundaries at the river Coatzacoalcos. Bernal Díaz says that the country of Coatzacoalcos was not a province of Mexico; on the other hand we find among the tributary cities of that Crown Tochtlan, Michapan, and other places of the aforesaid province. We are therefore persuaded that the Mexicans possessed all that lay to the west of the river Coatzacoalcos, but not what lay to the east, and that that river was on that side the limit of their empire. Toward the north it was bounded by the country of the Huastecs, never subjugated by the Mexicans. Toward the northeast the empire did not extend beyond the province of Tula: all that great tract of land which lay beyond that province was occupied by the barbarous Otomis and Chichimecs, who had no settled population and obeyed no sovereign. On the west it is known that the empire ended at Tlaximaloyan, the frontier of the kingdom of Michoacán; but on the coastal lowlands it reached as far as the western extremity of the province of Colima, and no farther. In the Catalogue of the tributary cities are seen Colima and other places of that province, and none of those which are

beyond

(*) There were, and there are, at least three places called Tochtlan (by the Spaniards Tuxtla): the first in the province of Chiapas, the second in that of Xoconochco, or Soconusco, and the third in that of Coatzacoalcos. [p. 269] beyond it; nor is any mention of them made in the history of Mexico. The Mexicans had nothing to do with California, nor could they expect any advantage from the conquest of a country so distant, the most depopulated and the most wretched in the world. If that arid and rocky peninsula had ever been a province of the Mexican empire, some settlements would have been found there; but it is certain that not even a single house was found there, nor any remnant or trace of one. Finally, on the south the Mexicans had taken possession of all those great states that lay from the Valley of Mexico to the Pacific sea. Now, the Mexican coastal lowlands stretching from Soconusco to Colima, there precisely was the greatest length of their dominions.

Dr. Robertson says that “the territories belonging to the chiefs of Tezeuco and Tacuba, were hardly inferior in extent to those of the Mexican monarch.” (b) But this is too far from the truth, and contrary also to what all the historians of Mexico say about it. The kingdom of Texcoco, or rather of Acolhuacan, was bounded on the west partly by the lake of Texcoco and partly by Zumpango and other Mexican states, and on the east by the dominions of Tlaxcala; so that it could not have more than sixty miles from west to east; on the south it was bounded by the state of Chalco, which also belonged to Mexico, and on the north by the independent country of the Huastecs. Now from the frontier of this country to that of Chalco there are about two hundred miles. Here is the whole extent of the kingdom of Acolhuacan, which does not make even the eighth part of that of the Mexican dominions. The states of the petty king of Tlacopan, or Tacuba, were so small that they did not deserve the name of kingdom; for from the Mexican lake on the east to the frontier of Michoacán on the west there were not more than eighty miles, nor more than fifty from the Valley of Toluca on the south to the country of the Otomis on the north. Therefore the comparison

made

(b) The History of America, bk. 7. [p. 270] made by Robertson of the dominions of Acolhuacan and of Tlacopan with those of Mexico is an error.

The republic of Tlaxcala, surrounded by the Mexican and Texcocan dominions, and by the states of Huexotzinco and of Cholula, was so confined that from east to west it had scarcely fifty miles, and from south to north about thirty. I have found no author who gives greater extension to that state, except Cortés, who says that the dominions of Tlaxcala had ninety leagues of circuit; but this is a manifest mistake.

Concerning the kingdom of Michoacán, none, so far as I know, has indicated all the ancient boundaries, except the Chevalier Boturini. This author says that the extent of that kingdom, from the Valley of Ixtlahuacan near Toluca to the Pacific sea, was a hundred and fifty leagues, and from Zacatollan to Xichú a hundred and sixty; and that in the Michoacán dominions were included the provinces of Zacatollan and of Colima, and that which the Spaniards called the Province of Ávalos, situated to the northwest of that of Colima. But in all this the cited Chevalier was mistaken; for it is known for certain that the kingdom of Michoacán had its boundaries not in Ixtlahuacan but in Tlaximaloyan, which was as far as the Mexican dominions reached. It is known from the register of the tributes that the maritime provinces of Zacatollan and of Colima belonged to Mexico. Finally, the Michoacans could not extend their dominions as far as Xichú without first subjugating the barbarous Chichimecs who occupied that country; but of these we know that they were not subjugated until many years after the conquest of Mexico, and then only by the Spaniards. The kingdom of Michoacán was not, therefore, as great as the Chevalier Boturini believed. Its extent comprised only about three degrees of longitude, and a little more than two of latitude.

All that we have said so far serves to demonstrate the accuracy of our description, and of our geographical maps, concerning the boundaries of those kingdoms, founded on the history itself, on the register of the tributes, and on the testimony of the ancient historians.

§ II.

[p. 271]

§ II.

On the Population of Anahuac.

I do not intend to discuss here the population of all America, because such a subject would be too vast and moreover foreign to my purpose, but only that of Mexico, which belongs to my History. In America there were, and are, very populous countries, and there are also vast deserts; and those who imagine the countries of the New World to be as populous as those of China stray no less from the truth than those others who believe them to be as depopulated as those of Africa. The calculation of Father Riccioli is as uncertain as those of Süssmilch and of M. de Pauw. Father Riccioli counts three hundred millions of inhabitants in America. The political arithmeticians, says M. de Pauw, count no more than a hundred there; Süssmilch in one place of his work reckons a hundred millions, and in another a hundred and fifty. M. de P., who reports all these calculations, says that there are no more than thirty to forty millions of true Americans. But all these calculations are, I repeat, extremely uncertain, and rest on no foundation; for if the population is not known even approximately in those countries in which the Europeans have established themselves, such as Mexico, Guatemala, Peru, Quito, Tierra Firme, Chile, etc., who will be capable of guessing the number of the inhabitants of the immense regions little or not at all known to the Europeans, such as those to the north and northwest of Coahuila, of New Mexico, of California, and of the river Colorado, or Red River, in North America? Who can count the inhabitants of the New World, when it is not known, nor can it be known, even how many provinces and nations are contained in it? Leaving aside, then, such calculations, which cannot be undertaken without rashness, we shall content ourselves with examining what M. de Pauw and Dr. Robertson say on the population of Mexico.

The

[p. 272]

“The population of Mexico and of Peru,” says M. de Pauw, “has undoubtedly been exaggerated by the Spanish writers, accustomed to paint objects in immoderate proportions.
