Three years after the conquest of Mexico the Spaniards had need to bring people there from the Lucayan islands, and afterward from the coasts of Africa, in order to people the kingdom of Mexico. If this monarchy contained thirty millions of inhabitants in 1518, why was it depopulated in 1521?” I shall never deny that among the Spanish writers there have been some exaggerators, just as there have been among the Prussians, the French, the English, and the other peoples; for the immoderate desire to magnify the things one describes is a passion quite common to all the nations of the world, and M. de P. has certainly not been preserved from it, as he makes plain throughout his work; but to charge all the Spaniards indiscriminately with it is to do a very grave wrong to that nation, which has, like all the others, both good and bad. For my part, at least, after reading the best historians of the civilized nations of Europe, I have not found two who seem to me comparable in truthfulness (c) with the two Spaniards Mariana and Acosta, who are therefore held in the highest esteem, and justly praised even by the enemies of their nation and of their religion. Among the early historians of Mexico there have been some, such as Acosta, Bernal Díaz, and Cortés himself, whose truthfulness cannot be doubted. But even if none of those authors had been endowed with the qualities required to merit our belief, the uniformity of their testimonies would nevertheless form a most effective argument in favor of the truth of their account. Authors who are little truthful never agree with one another, except when they copy one from another; but this did not happen with our historians, who, intent solely on writing what they had seen with their own eyes, or had found true through their inquiries, did not trouble themselves about what

the

(c) I speak here only of truthfulness because it is to my purpose: for the rest, those two historians have other merits that make them exceedingly estimable. [p. 273] the others had written; indeed it is apparent from their very works that when they wrote they did not have the writings of others before their eyes. M. de P. himself, reasoning in a letter of his (d) on that rite which the Mexicans had of consecrating and eating the paste statue of the god Huitzilopochtli, whom he calls Vitzilipultzi, and on that of the Peruvians at the feast of Capac-raime, says thus to his correspondent: I confess to you that the unanimous testimony of all the Spanish writers does not allow us to doubt it, etc. Now if the agreement of the Spanish historians concerning that which they did not see with their own eyes does not permit one to doubt it, how can one doubt that which they set down as eyewitnesses?

Let us see, then, what the early Spanish writers say of the population of Mexico. They all agree in affirming that those countries were very populous, that there were very many great cities and countless towns and villages; that to the markets of the populous cities many thousands of merchants flocked; that they put numerous armies into the field, etc. Cortés in his letters to Charles V, the Anonymous Conqueror in his Relation, Alonso de Ojeda and Alonso de Mata in their memoirs, Monsignor de las Casas in the work that bears the title On the Destruction of the Indies, Bernal Díaz in his History, Motolinía, Sahagún, and Mendieta in their writings, all eyewitnesses of the ancient population of Mexico; Herrera, Gómara, Acosta, Torquemada, and Martínez, all are agreed concerning the great population of those countries. M. de Pauw cannot allege to me a single early author who does not confirm it with his testimony, whereas I can cite to him several writers who make no mention of that superstitious rite of the Mexicans, such as Cortés, Bernal Díaz, and the Anonymous Conqueror, the three oldest Spanish historians of Mexico. Nevertheless M. de Pauw affirms that one cannot doubt that rite by reason of the unanimous testimony of the other Spaniards: why, then, will he wish to doubt the great population

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                    Mm                               of

(d) Recherch. philosoph., vol. 2, letter I to M. **, on the Religion of the Mexicans. [p. 274] of Mexico, indeed to deny it boldly against the uniform deposition of all the early historians? But if the population of Mexico was so great in 1518, why in 1521 was it necessary to bring people there from the Lucayan Islands, and afterward from the coasts of Africa, to people it? I confess candidly that I cannot read this objection of M. de P. without indignation, seeing such boldness in affirming what is absolutely false and wholly contrary to the account of the authors. Where has M. de P. ever read that in order to people Mexico it was necessary to transport people there from the Lucayas? I challenge him to produce me a single author who says so; on the contrary, from many writers we know quite the opposite. We know from the chronicler Herrera and from other writers that from 1493, the year in which the Spaniards established themselves on the island of Santo Domingo, until 1496, a third of the inhabitants of that great island perished through the war and through other very grave hardships. (e) In 1507 there remained no more than a tenth part of the Indians who had been there in 1493, as Monsignor de las Casas, an eyewitness, attests; (f) and from then on the population of that island diminished to such a degree that in 1540 scarcely two hundred Indians were left there. For this reason, from the beginning of the sixteenth century the Spaniards began to draw thousands of Indians from the Lucayas to replenish the population of Hispaniola; but as these too perished, they began, before the conquest of Mexico, to bring settlers there from Tierra Firme and from other countries of the American continent, as these were discovered. We know from a letter written to the Council of the Indies by the first bishop of Mexico, and cited to the Emperor Charles V by Monsignor de las Casas, that the cruel Nuño de Guzmán, governor of Pánuco, sent from there twenty-eight ships laden with enslaved Indians to be sold in the islands; so that it is so far from the truth that the Spaniards

## brought

(e) Dec. 1, bk. 2, ch. 18.

(f) On the Destruction of the Indies. All that we say here is established no less by the testimony of the same Monsignor de las Casas in the work entitled The Suppliant Indian Slave, and in others, than by that of the chronicler Herrera in his Decades. [p. 275] brought people from the islands to people the continent of North America that, on the contrary, they drew people from the latter to people the islands, as the two authors cited say expressly, and others as well. It is true indeed that after the conquest of Mexico slaves were transported there from Africa; not, however, because there was need of settlers, but because the Spaniards wished to employ them in the making of sugar and in the labor of the mines, to which they could not compel the Americans, on account of the laws then recently published. It is therefore false, and contrary to the deposition of the aforesaid authors, that Mexico was so depopulated three years after the conquest that it was necessary to bring people there from the Lucayas and from Africa to people it again; on the contrary, we are certain that from the countries subject to the king of Mexico and to the republic of Tlaxcala colonies were sent, some years after the conquest, to people other countries, such as Zacatecas, San Luis Potosí, Saltillo, etc.

But let us see what those early writers say in particular of the population of Mexico. I do not know that any of them has had the boldness to state the number of the inhabitants of the Mexican empire: whether it contained thirty millions or not could be known only to the kings of Mexico and to their ministers; and although the Spaniards could have informed themselves of it from them, none, so far as I know, did so. What several of those authors affirmed is that among the feudatories of the crown of Mexico there were thirty, each of whom had about a hundred thousand subjects, and three thousand other lords who had a smaller number of vassals. (g) Laurentius Surius affirms (h) that this is established by documents which were in the royal archive of Charles V. Cortés, in his first letter to the same emperor, says to him thus: “So great is the multitude of inhabitants in these countries that there is not even a palm of

Mm2                      ground

(g) See Gómara in ch. 76 of the Crónica de la Nueva España, and Herrera in Dec. 2, bk. 7, ch. 12.

(h) Surius in Commentario brevi rerum in Orbe gestarum ab anno 1500 ad 1568. [p. 276] ground that is not cultivated; and yet there are many people who, for want of bread, go begging through the houses, through the streets, and through the markets.” A similar idea of the population of Mexico in general is given us by Bernal Díaz, the Anonymous Conqueror, Motolinía, and other eyewitnesses. Coming now to the particular countries of Anahuac, we are assured by the deposition of the aforesaid writers, and of almost all the early ones, of the great population of the Valley of Mexico, of the countries of the Otomis, the Matlatzincas, the Tlahuicas, the Cohuixcas, the Mixtecs, the Zapotecs, and the Cuitlatecs, of the province of Coatzacoalcos, of the kingdoms of Acolhuacan and Michoacán, and of the states of Tlaxcala, Cholula, Huexotzinco, etc.

The Valley of Mexico, although the lakes occupied a great part of it, was at least as populous as the most populous country of Europe. There were forty considerable cities, named by us elsewhere and mentioned also by the early writers; the other inhabited places in it were innumerable, and their names would be set down here by us, if we did not fear to weary our readers. The most truthful Bernal Díaz, describing in ch. 88 of his History what they saw on their way through the Valley of Mexico toward the capital, says thus: “When we saw things so marvelous, we did not know what to say to ourselves, nor whether what we had before our eyes was real; for we saw so many great cities standing on dry land, and many others in the lake, and everything full of boats.” He says, besides, that some of his fellow soldiers, exceedingly amazed at seeing so many and such fine settlements, doubted whether what they saw was dreams, or things of enchantment. This and many other candid confessions of Bernal Díaz suffice to answer Dr. Robertson, who made use of certain words of that author, misunderstood by him, to make his readers believe that the population of Mexico was not as great as is claimed.

Concerning the population of the ancient capital there is a great variety of opinions; nor can it be otherwise, whenever one wishes to judge by eye the population of a great city; but

all [p. 277] all the writers who saw it, or were informed by eyewitnesses, are agreed in saying that it was very large. The chronicler Herrera says (i) that it was twice as large as Milan; Cortés affirms that it was as large as Seville and Córdoba; (k) Laurentius Surius (l), citing certain documents that were in the royal archive of Charles V, says that the population of Mexico City was made up of 130,000 houses; Torquemada, following Sahagún and some Indian historians, counts 120,000 houses there, (m) and adds that in each house there were from four to ten inhabitants. The Anonymous Conqueror speaks of it thus: “This city of Tenochtitlan may have more than two leagues and a half, or nearly three, more or less, of circuit; most of those who have seen it judge that there are more than sixty thousand hearths in it, and rather more than less.” This calculation, adopted by Gómara and by Herrera, seems to me the one that comes nearest to the truth, considering the extent of the city and the manner in which those people lived.

But all this is contradicted by M. de Pauw. He calls “excessive and extravagant the description given us of this American city, which contained, according to some authors, seventy thousand houses in the time of Moctezuma II; so that it would then have had three hundred and fifty thousand inhabitants, whereas it is notorious that the city of Mexico, considerably enlarged under the dominion of the Spaniards, has at present no more than sixty thousand inhabitants, including twenty thousand Blacks and Mulattoes.” (n) Here is another passage of the Recherches philosophiques that will make the Mexicans laugh. But who will not laugh at seeing a Prussian philosopher, so intent on diminishing the population of that great American city, and so indignant against those who represent it as larger than he wishes

it

(i) Dec. 2, bk. 7, ch. 13.

(k) Letter 1 to the Emperor Charles V.

(l) Surius in Commentario brevi, etc.

(m) Monarquía indiana, bk. 3, ch. 24.

(n) Rech. philos., pt. 5, sect. 2. [p. 278] it to be? Who, besides, will not marvel to hear that the number of the inhabitants of Mexico is known in Berlin, when in Mexico it was not known, not long ago, even to the very parish priests, who every year made the count? I wish, however, to give M. de P. some certain information about that American city, so that in the future he may avoid those errors into which he has fallen in speaking of its population.

Let him know, then, that Mexico is the most populous city of all those that the Catholic King has in his vast dominions. From the list of births and deaths in Madrid and in Mexico, published in the journals of each of the two cities, it appears that the number of the inhabitants of Madrid is more than a quarter less than that of Mexico: (o) that is, if Madrid, for example, has 160,000 inhabitants, Mexico without doubt has more than 200,000. There has been a great diversity of opinions concerning the number of souls of the modern city of Mexico, as there was concerning the ancient one, and as there likewise is concerning other cities of the first rank; (p) but the count having been made in these last years with greater diligence, both by the parish priests and by the magistrates, it has been found that the inhabitants of that capital exceed two hundred thousand, although the exact amount of the excess cannot be known. One can form some idea of the population

of

(o) It is certain that in proportion to the excess of one city over another in the number of births and deaths, there will also be the excess in the number of inhabitants; and there is no surer means of finding approximately the number of inhabitants of a very large city than that of knowing the number of births and deaths in it, provided that the precautions required for this are employed.

(p) It is enough to know the diversity of opinions among modern writers concerning the population of Paris: one counts five hundred thousand inhabitants there, another seven hundred thousand, another a million. In the same way the opinions concerning modern Mexico have been diverse. Lionel Wafer, a celebrated English traveler of the last century, believed that there were three hundred thousand inhabitants there; to the traveler Gemelli there seemed to be a hundred thousand, and to the missionary Tallandier sixty thousand; a very recent European traveler, who went to Mexico after traveling through Europe and the principal countries of Asia, was of the opinion that there were no fewer than a million and a half inhabitants. The latter erred by excess, and Tallandier by defect. [p. 279] of that city from the quantity of pulque (q) and of tobacco that is consumed there daily. (r) Every day more than six thousand arrobas of pulque enter it, that is, a hundred and ninety thousand Roman pounds; in the year 1774 there entered 2,214,294½ arrobas, that is, more than seventy-three millions of Roman pounds; but this computation does not include what is introduced by contraband, nor what the exempt Indians sell in the principal square of the city. This so great a quantity of pulque is consumed almost entirely by the Indians alone and the Mulattoes, whose number is exceeded by that of the white Europeans and Creoles, among whom there are not many who habitually use that drink. The duty on it amounts annually, in the capital alone, to about two hundred and eighty thousand scudi (pesos fuertes). The consumption of smoking tobacco in that capital amounts every day to about 1250 scudi, which in a year makes the sum of four hundred and fifty thousand scudi and more. But it must be known that among the Indians those who use tobacco are rare; among the Creoles and the Europeans there are very many who do not use it, and among the Mulattoes some. Now who will wish to give more credit to the calculation of M. de Pauw than to the very registers of that capital, and to esteem more highly the judgment of a modern Prussian so extravagant concerning the ancient population of that court than that of so many early writers who saw it with their own eyes?

As for the city and court of Texcoco, we know from the letters of Cortés to Charles V that it had about thirty thousand houses; but this must be understood of the court alone; for taken together with the three other cities of Coatlichan, Huexotla, and Atenco, which, as Cortés himself testifies, seemed to form a single settlement, it was by far larger

than

(q) Pulque (in Italian we would say Pulche) is the wine, or rather beer, most commonly used by the Mexicans, made from the fermented juice of the maghei, as we said in the History. This beverage cannot be kept for another day; and therefore all that is brought in is consumed each day.

(r) The record of the daily consumption of pulque and tobacco in Mexico is taken from a letter of one of the principal accountants of that customs house, written on February 23, 1775. [p. 280] than Mexico. Torquemada, following Sahagún and the accounts of the Indians, affirms that the population of those four cities comprised a hundred and forty thousand houses; and if we wished to take half from that number, a very large population would nonetheless remain. No historian has told us how great the population of the court of Tlacopan was, although all affirm that it was considerable. Of that of Xochimilco we know that it was the largest of all after the courts. Of Iztapalapa Cortés affirms that it had from twelve to fifteen thousand hearths; of Mixcoac he says that it had about six thousand, of Huitzilopochco from four to five thousand, of Acolman and of Otompan four thousand each, and of Mexicaltzinco three thousand. Chalco, Azcapotzalco, Coyoacan, and Cuauhtitlan were incomparably larger than these last cities. All these, and very many other settlements, were comprised within the Valley of Mexico alone; the sight of them caused no less admiration than fear in the conquerors when they first beheld that delightful valley from the summits of the mountains. The same befell them when they saw the population of Tlaxcala. Cortés in his letter to Charles V speaks thus of that city: “It is so large and marvelous that, although I pass over much of what I could say of it, the little that I shall say, I believe, will be incredible; for it is much larger and more populous than Granada was when it was taken from the Moors, much stronger, with such good buildings, and much better supplied with everything.”

The Anonymous Conqueror speaks of it likewise: “There are,” he says, “great cities, and among others that of Tlaxcala, which in some respects resembles Granada, and in others Segovia; but it is more populous than any of them.” Of Tzompantzinco, a city of that republic, Cortés affirms, (s) that a count having been made there by his order, more than twenty thousand houses were counted in it. Of Huejotlipan, a place likewise of the same republic, he says that it had from three to four thousand

(s) Cortés speaks of this city without naming it; but from the context it appears that it was Tzompantzinco, and Torquemada says so expressly. [p. 281] hearths. Of Cholula Cortés himself affirms that it had about twenty thousand houses, and almost as many in the neighboring places, which were like its suburbs. Huexotzinco and Tepeyacac were rivals of Cholula in size. These are some of the settlements that the Spaniards saw before the Conquest, leaving aside many others whose size is established by the deposition of these and other writers.

The great population of those countries can no less be shown by the innumerable gatherings that were seen in the markets, by the very numerous armies that were raised whenever there was need, and by the astonishing number of baptisms that took place after the conquest. Concerning the gatherings in the markets and the armies we have said enough in the History, on the authority of many eyewitnesses. It might be suspected that the conquerors had exaggerated the number of the Indian troops in order to make their conquests more glorious, but this could be done by them in speaking of the enemy troops, not however in counting the troops confederated with them; for the more the number of these was increased, the less difficult and the less glorious their conquests must have appeared. Yet the conqueror Ojeda counted a hundred and fifty thousand men of allied troops from Tlaxcala, Cholula, Tepeyacac, and Huexotzinco in the review that was held at Tlaxcala in order to go and lay siege to Mexico. Cortés himself affirms that the allied troops who accompanied him to the war of Cuauhquechollan exceeded a hundred thousand, and that those who aided him in the siege of the capital exceeded two hundred thousand by far. On the other hand the besieged were so many that, although more than a hundred and fifty thousand of them died during the siege, as we have said in the History, nevertheless when that capital was taken by the Spaniards and all the Mexicans were ordered to leave it, the three streets were seen full for three days and three nights continuously of the people who were leaving it to take refuge in other places, as Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, attests. As for the number of baptisms, we are sure

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.       Nn                      by [p. 282] by the testimony of those same apostolic religious who employed themselves in the conversion of those peoples, that the children and adults baptized there by the Franciscan Fathers alone (t) from the year 1524 until 1540 were more than six millions; most of whom were inhabitants of the Valley of Mexico and of the surrounding provinces. Now in this number are not included those who were baptized by the priests, by the Dominicans, and by the Augustinians, among whom and the Franciscans that most abundant harvest was then divided; and besides, it is certain that those Indians were innumerable who remained obstinate in their heathenism, or did not receive the Christian faith until many years afterward. We know, moreover, from the noisy controversies stirred up there by some religious and referred to the Supreme Pontiff Paul III, that on account of the extraordinary and never before seen multitude of catechumens the missionaries were compelled to omit certain ceremonies of baptism, and among others that of the saliva, since from so much spitting their mouths, tongues, and throats became dry.

Since the discovery of Mexico the number of the Indians has been continually diminishing. Besides the countless thousands who perished in the first contagion of smallpox brought there in 1520, and in the war of the Spaniards, in the epidemic of 1545 eight hundred thousand died, and in that of 1576 more than two millions in the dioceses of Mexico, Puebla, Michoacán, and Oaxaca alone; which was learned from the list of the dead of every parish presented to the viceroy. Nevertheless the chronicler Herrera, who wrote toward the end of the sixteenth century, reports on the authority of authentic documents sent to him by the viceroy of Mexico that in the dioceses of Puebla and of Oaxaca alone, and in those provinces of the diocese of Mexico that were adjacent to the capital, 655 principal places of Indians were then counted, and innumerable other smaller ones dependent on them:

in

(t) Toribio de Benavente, or Motolinía, one of those apostolic religious, baptized more than four hundred thousand Indians, the count of which he left written in his own hand. [p. 283] in which there were nine hundred thousand families of tributary Indians. (u) But it must be known that among the tributaries are not included the nobles, nor the Tlaxcalans, nor other Indians of those who aided the Spaniards in the Conquest; for, in regard to their birth or to the services rendered to the conquerors, they were exempt from tribute. Herrera himself, very well instructed on this subject, affirms that at that time there were counted in the capital four thousand families of Spaniards and thirty thousand houses of Indians. From then on the number of the Indians went on continually diminishing, and that of the Spaniards, or Whites, increasing.

M. de P. will reply, according to his style, that all the documents we have adduced to demonstrate the great population of Mexico are worth less than nothing, because they were taken from rude and wicked soldiers, or from ignorant and superstitious religious; but even if all the writers we have cited were such, which is altogether false, their testimony would nevertheless be of great weight on account of their uniformity. Who, moreover, can be persuaded that Cortés and the royal officers who subscribed his letters with him dared to deceive their kings, when they could so easily be contradicted by so many hundreds of witnesses and by not a few enemies? Would it ever be possible that so many writers, Spaniards as well as Indians, should all agree in exaggerating the population of those countries, and that among them there should not be a single one who respected posterity? Of the truthfulness of the first missionaries one cannot doubt. They were men of exemplary life and of great learning, chosen from among many to plant the Gospel in that New World: some of them had been lecturers in the most celebrated universities of Europe, had obtained the first offices in their Order, and had deserved the favor and confidence of the Emperor Charles V. Those honors which they renounced in Europe, and those which they did not accept in America (x), clearly show

Nn2                         their

(u) Description of the West Indies, chs. 9 and 10.

(x) Among the first fifteen Franciscan missionaries there were six who, having been named bishops by Charles V, did not accept that dignity. [p. 284] their disinterested zeal; their voluntary and austere poverty, their continual converse with God, their unspeakable labors in so many journeys, so long and so difficult, made on foot and without provisions, and in so many such toilsome ministries, and above all their exceptional charity toward those afflicted nations, full of compassion and gentleness, will render their memory forever venerable in that kingdom, in spite of M. de P. and of any other malicious writer for whom it suffices to recognize in an author the quality of religious in order to despise and insult him. In the writings of those immortal men there is seen such a character of truthfulness as to permit no suspicion against the truth of their accounts. It is true that, in the judgment of M. de P., they committed a great sin in burning, as superstitious, the greater part of the historical paintings of the Mexicans. I prize those paintings more than M. de P. does, and their loss grieves me far more; but I do not for that reason despise the authors of that deplorable fire, nor blame their memory; because the evil to which they were then led by a zeal too ardent and not well informed is not to be compared with the great good that they did there besides; and moreover they themselves sought to repair that loss by their works, especially Motolinía, Sahagún, Olmos, and Torquemada.

But M. de P. is so zealous in diminishing the population of those countries that he has gone so far as to affirm (who would believe it?) in a decisive and magisterial tone that in all those regions there was no other city than that of Mexico. Let us hear him discourse, to amuse ourselves a little: “Since there are not found,” he says, “in the whole kingdom of Mexico any vestiges of ancient Indian cities, it is manifest that there was not more than a single place that had any appearance of a city, and this was Mexico, which the Spanish writers chose to call the Babylon of the Indies; but it is now a long time that the magnificent names given by them to the miserable villages of America do not deceive us.”

But all the authors who have written of Mexico unanimously affirm that all the nations of that vast empire

lived [p. 285] lived in society, that they had settlements many, large, and well ordered, and they name the cities seen by them (y); and those who have traveled through those regions two centuries and a half after the conquest have seen with their own eyes the aforesaid settlements in the very places indicated by those authors: so that either M. de P. persuades himself that those writers announced prophetically the future settlements, or he must confess that even then they were where they are at present. It is true that the Spaniards founded many settlements there, such as the cities of Puebla, Guadalajara, Valladolid, Veracruz, Celaya, Potosí, Córdoba, León, etc.; but the settlements founded by them within the bounds of the Mexican empire, in relation to those founded by the Indians, are not even in the proportion of one to a thousand. The Mexican names given to the settlements, which are preserved to this day, show clearly that it was not the Spaniards but the Indians who were their founders. Now that those places of which we make frequent mention in the History were not for the most part miserable villages, but cities and settlements large and well formed, like those of Europe, is established by the deposition of all those writers who saw them.

M. de Pauw would like to have shown to him the vestiges of the ancient cities; but we show him, and more besides, those very ancient cities still standing. Nevertheless, if he wants vestiges, let him go to Texcoco, to Otumba, to Tlaxcala, to Cholula, to Huexotzinco, to Cempoala, to Tula, etc., and he will find so many there that he cannot doubt the ancient greatness of those American cities.

This great number of cities and inhabited places, although

so

(y) Cortés in his four long letters to Charles V; Bernal Díaz del Castillo in his History of the Conquest; the Anonymous Conqueror in his curious Relation; Motolinía, Sahagún, and Mendieta in their manuscripts; Monsignor de las Casas in some of his works; Pedro de Alvarado, Diego Godoy, and Nuño de Guzmán in their letters, which are found in Ramusio’s Collection; all eyewitnesses, to whom must be added all the Mexican historians, Acolhuas and Tlaxcalans, especially those whom we have put in the catalogue of the authors of the ancient history of Mexico. [p. 286] so many thousands of men perished there every year in the sacrifices and in the continual wars of those peoples, shows clearly the great population of the Mexican empire and of the other countries of Anahuac; but if nothing of what we have said suffices to convince M. de P., I would charitably advise him to have himself taken to a hospital.

What we have adduced against M. de P. serves equally to refute Dr. Robertson, who, seeing so many witnesses contrary to his opinion, has recourse to a subterfuge entirely similar to that of the heat of imagination, of which he availed himself to deny credit to the Spanish writers concerning what they say of the excellence of the Mexican cast-metal work. Reasoning, then, on that wonder which the sight of the cities of Mexico caused the Spaniards, he says thus in bk. 7 of his History: “In the first fervour of their admiration, they compared Zempoalla, though a town only of the second or third size, to the cities of greatest note in their own country. When, afterwards, they visited in succession Tlascala, Cholula, Tacuba, Tezeuco, and Mexico itself, their amazement increased so much, that it led them to convey ideas of their magnitude and populousness bordering on what is incredible… For this reason, some considerable abatement ought to be made from their calculation of the number of inhabitants in the Mexican cities, and we may fix the standard of their population much lower than they have done.” So commands Robertson; but I am not disposed to obey him. If the Spaniards had written their histories, letters, and relations in the first fervor of their wonder, it might well then be suspected that astonishment had led them to exaggerate; but it did not happen so: Cortés, the oldest of those writers, did not write his first letter to Charles V until a year and a half after he had arrived in that country; the Anonymous Conqueror wrote some years after the conquest; Bernal Díaz after more than forty years of continual residence in those regions, and so the others. Is it possible that that first fervor of wonder should last one, twenty, and even forty years? But whence did their wonder arise? Let us hear it

from [p. 287] from Dr. Robertson himself: “The Spaniards,” he says, “accustomed to this mode of habitation (of isolated huts) among all the savage tribes with which they were then acquainted, were astonished, on entering New Spain, to find the natives residing in towns of such extent as resembled those of Europe.” But Cortés and his companions, before going to Mexico, already knew that those peoples were not savage tribes and that their houses were not huts: having already heard from all those who a year earlier had made that voyage with Grijalva that there were fine settlements, furnished with houses well built of stone and lime, and with lofty towers, as Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, attests. That was not, then, the cause of their wonder; but rather the real greatness and multitude of the cities which they saw there. “It is not surprising, then,” adds Robertson, “that Cortes and his companions, powerfully tempted to magnify, in order to exalt the merit of their own discoveries and conquests, should have been betrayed into this common error, and have raised their descriptions considerably above truth.” But Cortés was no fool, and saw well that to exaggerate the number of his allies, so far from exalting the merit, served to diminish the glory of his conquests. Yet he often confesses that he was aided in his conquests now by eighty, now by a hundred thousand, now by more than two hundred thousand men: and as these candid confessions make plain his truthfulness, so those so numerous armies demonstrate the great population of that country. Moreover Dr. Robertson supposes that whatever the Spanish authors wrote concerning the number of the houses of the Mexican cities was expressed by them only by conjecture and according to the judgment they formed by eye; but it did not happen so: for Cortés himself testifies in his first letter to the Emperor Charles V that he had caused a count to be made of the houses that were in the district of the republic of Tlaxcala, and had found more than a hundred and fifty thousand, and in the city of Tzompantzinco alone more than twenty thousand.

## Dissertation

[p. 288]

## D I S S E RTAT I O N V I I I 

## ON THE RELIGION OF THE MEXICANS

## I    have nothing to do in this dissertation, as in the others, with M.

de Pauw; for he candidly acknowledges the resemblance that there is between the delusions of the Americans and those of other nations of the old continent in matters of religion. “Since the religious superstitions of the peoples of America,” he says, (a) “have had a sensible resemblance to those which the nations of the old continent have practiced, these absurdities have been spoken of only in order to make the comparison, and to show that, in spite of the diversity of climes, the weakness of the human mind has been constant and invariable.” If he had reasoned with the same judgment on other points, he would have spared us several disputes, and would have preserved his work from those grave and strong censures that some wise men of Europe itself have made of it. I, however, address this dissertation to those who, from ignorance of what has passed and is passing at present in the world, or from want of reflection, have made such an outcry in reading in the History of Mexico of the cruelty and superstition of those peoples, as if they were things never heard of among mortals. I shall therefore make their error plain, and shall demonstrate that the religion of the Mexicans was less superstitious, less indecent, less puerile, and less irrational than that of the most civilized nations of ancient Europe, and that examples of cruelty like theirs have been seen, and perhaps even more atrocious ones, among almost all the peoples of the world.

The system of natural religion depends principally on that idea which one has of the Deity. If the Supreme Being is conceived

as

(a) In the preface to the Recherches philosophiques. [p. 289] as a father full of goodness, whose providence watches over his creatures, love and respect will be seen in the religious practices. If, on the contrary, he is imagined as an inexorable tyrant, the worship will be bloodthirsty. If he is believed omnipotent, veneration will be directed to one alone; but if his power is judged to be restricted, the objects of worship cannot but be multiplied. If the holiness and perfection of his being are recognized, his protection will be sought with a pure and holy worship; but if he is reputed subject to the imperfections and vices of men, the religion itself will consecrate crimes.

Let us compare, then, the idea that the Mexicans had of their gods with that which the Greeks, the Romans, and other nations, from whom the latter learned religion, had of their deities, and we shall soon recognize the advantages of the Mexicans in this matter over those ancient nations. It is true that the Mexicans divided the power among various gods, imagining the jurisdiction of each to be restricted within certain limits. “I do not doubt,” said King Moctezuma to the conqueror Cortés in a conference on religion, “I do not doubt the goodness of the God whom you adore; but if he is good for Spain, our gods are likewise good for Mexico.”

“Our god Camaxtli,” the Tlaxcalans said to the same Cortés, “grants us victory over our enemies; our goddess Matlalcueye sends the rain necessary to our fields, and defends us from the inundations of the Zahuapan. To each of our gods we are indebted for a part of the happiness of our life;” but they never believed their gods so impotent as the Greeks and Romans did theirs. The Mexicans had no more than one deity, under the name of Centeotl, for the care of the countryside and of the sown fields, and although they were so fond of their children, they were content with a single god for their protection. The Romans, besides the goddess Ceres, employed in grain alone a great crowd of gods, (b) and in the care and

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.             Oo                  education

(b) Seia was in charge of the sown grain, Proserpina of the grain once sprung up, Nodotus [p. 290] education of their children more than twenty, besides those many who were occupied in generation and in the birth of infants. (c) Who would believe that they needed three gods for the mere guarding of the door? Forculus was in charge of the doors, Carna of the hinge, and Limentinus of the threshold. Thus, exclaims Saint Augustine here, (d) thus Forculus could not guard at once the doors, and the hinge, and the threshold. So paltry, in the judgment of the Romans, was the power of their gods! Even the names by which some of them were called show the poor idea in which they were held by their worshippers. What names could be more unworthy of divinity than those of Jupiter Pistor, Venus Calva, Pecunia, Caca, Subigus, and Cloacina? Who would ever be persuaded that a statue found by Tatius in the principal sewer of Rome should become a goddess under the name of Cloacina? Is this not to mock their own religion, and to make vile and contemptible the very gods whom they worshipped? (e)

But in no other respect did the Greeks and the

Romans

of the knots of the stalk, Volatina of the eyes, or buds, Patelena of the leaves already unfolded, Flora of the flowers, Ostilina of the ear, Segesta of the new grains, Lattanzia of the grain still milky, Matuta of the ripe grain, Tutano and Tutilina of the grain kept in the granary; to which must be added the god Sterculius, who watched over the manuring of the fields; Priapus, who defended the grain from the birds; Rubigo, who preserved it from insects; and the Napaean Nymphs, who had care of the nourishing sap.

(c) The goddess Ops was charged with giving help to the newborn child and receiving it in her lap, Vaticanus with opening its mouth for its crying, Levana with lifting it from the ground, Cunina with guarding the cradle, the Carmentes with announcing its destiny, Fortuna with favoring it in its fortunes, Rumina with putting the nipple of the mother’s breast into the infant’s mouth, Potina with its drink, Educa with its pap, Faventia with making it say “bau,” Venilia with reviving its hope, Volupia with taking care of its pleasures, Agenoria with watching over its actions, Stimula with making it active, Strenua with giving it courage, Numeria with teaching it to count, Camena with instructing it in singing, Consus with giving it counsel, Sentia with making it come to decisions; Juventa had charge of the beginning of youth, and the bearded Fortuna had the most important office of making the hair grow on adults.

(d) Aug., City of God, bk. 4, ch. 8.

(e) What derision of religions is this? If I were their defender, what could I so gravely complain of as that the divinity of the gods has come to such contempt that it is held up to ridicule by the most shameful names? Who would not laugh at the goddess Fornax? Who could keep from laughing when he hears of the goddess Muta? There is worshipped also Caca, etc. Lactantius, Divine Institutes, bk. 1, ch. 20.

[p. 291]

Romans show better the opinion they had of their gods than in those vices which they attributed to them. Their whole mythology is a long series of crimes; the whole life of their gods came down to grudges, vengeances, incests, adulteries, and other base passions, capable of disgracing even the vilest of men. Jove, that omnipotent father, that source of all things, that king of men and of gods, as the poets call him, shows himself at times disguised as a man to deal with Alcmena, at times as a satyr to enjoy Antiope, at times as a bull to carry off Europa, at times as a swan to abuse Leda, and at times as a shower of gold to corrupt Danae; at times he takes other forms to accomplish his guilty desires. Meanwhile the great goddess Juno, enraged with jealousy, knows nothing to do but to take vengeance on her faithless husband. Of this same stamp were the other immortal gods, especially the greater gods, or chosen, as they were called by them. Chosen, says St. Augustine, (f) for the superiority of their vices, not for the excellence of their virtues. And to tell the truth, what good examples could those nations find among their gods, who, while they boasted of teaching men virtue, consecrated nothing in their gods but vices? What merits had Leaena among the Greeks, and Lupa, Faula, and Flora among the Romans, to obtain apotheosis, if not that of having been famous courtesans? Hence it came about that various gods were put in charge of the most infamous and shameful offices. (g)

But what shall we say of the Egyptians, who were the chief authors of superstition? (h) They gave worship not only to the ox, the dog, the wolf, the cat, the crocodile, the sparrow hawk, and other such animals, but even to leeks, onions, and garlic; which gave occasion to that fine saying of Juvenal: O holy nations, in whose gardens

Oo2                            such

(f) Aug., City of God, bk. 7, ch. 33.

(g) Whoever wishes to know the names and the offices of those gods will find them in bk. 6, ch. 2, and in bk. 9, ch. 9 of the City of God; for I have not the heart to set them here before the eyes of my readers.

(h) We Romans have received into our temples your Isis, and the half-canine gods, and the sistra that rouse to mourning. Lucan. [p. 292] such gods are born! And not content with this, they celebrated also the apotheosis of the most indecent things. That detestable custom of marrying their own sisters was held to be authorized by the example of their gods.

Very different from this was the idea that the Mexicans had of their gods. There is not to be found in all their mythology a single trace of those stupendous wickednesses with which those other nations defamed their gods. The Mexicans honored virtues, not vices, in their divinities: in Huitzilopochtli, prowess; in Centeotl, in Tzapotlatenan, in Opochtli, and in others, beneficence; and in Quetzalcoatl, chastity, justice, and prudence. Although they imagined gods of both sexes, they did not marry them off, nor did they think them capable of those obscene pleasures that have been so common among the Greek and Roman gods. The Mexicans supposed in them a supreme aversion to every sort of crime; hence their worship was directed to appeasing the wrath of the gods, provoked by the sins of men, and to winning their protection by repentance and by religious observances.

Wholly conformable to the idea of the gods were the rites used by those nations. Superstition was common to all; but that of the Mexicans was less, and less childish; one need only compare their auguries. The Mexican astrologers observed the signs or characters of the days for their marriages, for their journeys, etc., just as the European astrologers observed the position of the stars in order to foretell from it the fortunes of men. Both alike feared eclipses and comets, as forerunners of great calamity; for this superstition has been common to all the peoples of the world. All likewise were frightened on hearing the voice of the owl, or of some other such bird. These and other similar superstitions have been general, and are even today only too common among the common people of both continents, even in the heart of the most civilized Europe; but all that we know of those American nations in this matter is certainly not to be compared with what is told us of the ancient Romans by their own historians

and [p. 293] and poets. The works of Livy, of Pliny, of Virgil, of Suetonius, of Valerius Maximus, and of other judicious authors (which cannot be read without compassion) show to what excess the childish superstition of the Romans went in their auguries. There was no animal, whether among the quadrupeds, or among the reptiles, or among the birds, from which they did not take an augury of the future. If the bird flew toward the left hand, if the raven croaked, if the voice of the crow was heard, if the mouse tasted the honey, if the hare crossed the road, all this was held to be a presage of some great misfortune. The expiation, or lustration, of all Rome has been seen performed for no other reason than that an owl had entered the Capitol. (i) And not only animals, but even the most trivial and contemptible things were enough to cause them a superstitious fear, as for example if, while at table, the wine or the salt was spilled, or some particle of the food fell to the ground. Who would not have marveled on contemplating those gentlemen Haruspices, persons of such authority, seriously occupied in observing all the movements of the victims, the state of their entrails, and the color of their blood, in order to foretell from such signs the principal events of that famous republic? I wonder, said the great Cicero, (k) that a haruspex does not laugh on seeing another of the same profession. What in truth is more ridiculous than that kind of augury which they called Tripudium? Who could be persuaded that a nation on the one hand so enlightened, and on the other so warlike, should carry with it in its armies, as the thing most important for the success of its arms, a cage of chickens, and that without first consulting them it should not dare to give battle? If the chickens did not taste the mash that was set before them, it was a bad sign; if besides not eating it they escaped from the cage, worse; if on the contrary they ate it greedily, this was held the happiest augury. So that

the

(i) The owl is a bird of ill omen, and above all abominated in public auspices … it entered even the shrine of the Capitol, in the consulship of Sex. Papellius Istrus and L. Pedanius; on account of which the City was purified on the Nones of March of that year. Pliny, Natural History, bk. 10, ch. 12.

(k) I wonder that a haruspex does not laugh when he sees a haruspex. Cic., On Divination. [p. 294] the most effective means of assuring victory would have been to make the chickens suffer hunger before consulting them.

To such excesses the human mind is easily carried, when it abandons itself to its own lights. The experience of those crude errors, of that ridiculous childishness, and of those monstrous abominations into which the most civilized nations of gentilism have fallen, shows that we must not expect the true and holy religion from any other than that same God whom we adore. It is for him to reveal the truths that we must believe, and to prescribe the worship with which we must revere him. If the most weighty business of religion is entrusted to human reason, of whose weakness we have so much experience, the greatest absurdities will be presented to our mind as true dogmas; and the worship due to the Supreme Being will be defective through impiety, or excessive through superstition. Would to God that those very philosophers of our enlightened age, who so vaunt the powers of reason, did not give us in their own works so many and such clear proofs of their weakness of mind!

But in the end Americans, Greeks, Romans, and Egyptians were all superstitious and childish in the practice of their religion; not so, however, in the obscenity of their rites; for in the rites of the Mexicans there is not found the slightest vestige of those abominations so common among the Romans and other civilized nations of antiquity. What is more indecent than the Eleusinian festivals that the Greeks held, than those that the Romans celebrated in honor of Venus on the Kalends of April, and above all those most obscene games that were held in honor of Cybele, of Flora, of Bacchus, and of other such wicked gods, against which several Fathers of the Church, and even some of the Romans themselves, inveighed most strongly? What rite is more obscene than that which was performed upon the statue of Priapus amid the nuptial ceremonies? (l) How could they celebrate the festivals of those incestuous and adulterous gods, if not

with

(l) See what Lactantius Firmianus says about this and other such rites in his work Divine Institutes, and St. Augustine in the books of the City of God. [p. 295] with such abominations? How was it possible that they should be ashamed of those vices which they saw consecrated in their divinities?

It is true that although no obscenity entered into the rites of the Mexicans, there were nevertheless some which, supposing the divinity of their gods, would have been indecent, such as that of smearing the lips of the idols with the blood of the victims; but would it not have been far more indecent to slap them, as the Romans did to the goddess Matuta at the Matralia? Granting the error of the one and the other, the Mexicans were certainly less unreasonable in giving their gods to taste a liquor which, according to the principles of their religion, was pleasing to them, than the Romans in performing toward their goddess an action which is held to be gravely insulting among all the peoples of the world.

What we have said thus far, although sufficient to show that the religion of the Mexicans was less blameworthy than that of the Romans, the Greeks, and the Egyptians, may be called almost nothing if compared with what we omit so as not to weary the readers. But otherwise I see well that the comparison should not be made only in the aforesaid articles, but rather in that which concerns the quality of the sacrifices. I confess that the religion of the Mexicans was too bloody, that their sacrifices were most cruel, and their austerities exceedingly barbarous; but whenever I set myself to consider what the other nations of the world have done, I am confounded on recognizing the weakness of the human mind, and the deplorable errors into which it plunges when it is not guided by the light of the true religion; and I render infinite thanks to the Most High for having preserved me from so many evils.

There has been scarcely any nation in the world that has not at some time sacrificed human victims to the god it worshipped. We know from the holy books that the Ammonites burned some of their children in honor of their god Moloch, and that other peoples of the land of Canaan did the same, whose example was at times imitated by the Israelites. It is established from bk. 4 of Kings that Ahaz and Manasseh, kings of Judah, practiced that pagan

rite [p. 296] rite of passing their children through the fire. The expression of the sacred text seems to signify rather a mere lustration, or consecration, than a holocaust; but Psalm 105 does not allow us to doubt that the Israelites truly sacrificed their sons to the gods of the Canaanites, (m) the stupendous and evident miracles wrought by the almighty arm of the true God not sufficing to turn them from that barbarous superstition. Of the Egyptians we know, from the testimony of Manetho, a priest and celebrated historian of that nation, cited by Eusebius of Caesarea, that every day three men were sacrificed at Heliopolis to the goddess Juno alone. Now just as the Ammonites sacrificed human victims to their Moloch, and the Canaanites to their Beelphegor, so the Persians sacrificed them to their Mithras, or the Sun, the Phoenicians and Carthaginians to their Baal, or Saturn, the Cretans to Jupiter, the Lacedaemonians to Mars, the Phocians to Diana, the Lesbians to Bacchus, the Thessalians to the Centaur Chiron and to Peleus, the Gauls to Esus and to Teutates, (n) the Bards of Germany to Tuisto, and other nations likewise to their tutelary gods. Philo says that the Phoenicians in their public calamities

offered

(m) And they were mingled among the heathens, and learned their works, and served their idols, and it became a scandal to them. And they sacrificed their sons and their daughters to devils. And they shed innocent blood, the blood of their sons and of their daughters, whom they sacrificed to the idols of Chanaan: and the land was infected with blood. Ps. 105.

(n) A certain French author, driven by a blind love of his country, boldly denies that human victims were ever sacrificed by the Gauls; but he offers no reason by which to refute the testimonies of Caesar, of Pliny, of Suetonius, of Diodorus, of Strabo, of Lactantius, of St. Augustine, and of other weighty authors. The testimony of Caesar is enough to confound him, who, as one who had the most acquaintance with the Gauls, knew them better: The whole nation of the Gauls, he says, is greatly given to religious observances; and on that account those who are afflicted with graver diseases, and those who are engaged in battle and in dangers, either sacrifice human beings for victims, or vow that they will sacrifice them, employing the Druids as ministers of those sacrifices; because they judge that unless a man’s life be given for a man’s life, the divine will of the immortal gods cannot otherwise be appeased; and they have sacrifices of the same kind instituted publicly. Others have images of immense size, whose limbs, woven of wicker, they fill with living men; these being set on fire, the men, surrounded by the flames, perish. They hold that the punishment of those who have been taken in theft, or robbery, or some other offense, is more acceptable to the immortal gods; but when a supply of that kind fails, they descend even to the punishment of the innocent. Bk. 6 of the Gallic War, ch. 5. From which it is seen that the Gauls were still more cruel than the Mexicans. [p. 297] offered in sacrifice to their inhuman Baal the dearest of their children, and Curtius affirms that this sacrifice was in use among the Tyrians until the destruction of their famous city. The Carthaginians, their countrymen, did the same in honor of Saturn the cruel: so rightly named by them. We know that when they had been defeated by Agathocles, King of Syracuse, in order to appease their god, whom they believed to be angered, they sacrificed to him two hundred noble boys, besides three hundred young men who of their own accord offered themselves for the sacrifice to show their bravery, their piety toward the gods, and their love of country; and according to what Tertullian affirms, who, as an African, and not long after the epoch of which he spoke, must have known it well, those sacrifices were in use in Africa until the times of the Emperor Tiberius, as in Gaul until the times of Claudius, as Suetonius attests.

The Pelasgians, ancient inhabitants of Italy, sacrificed, in obedience to an oracle, the tenth part of their children, as Dionysius of Halicarnassus relates. The Romans, who were as bloodthirsty as they were superstitious, did not reject such sacrifices. All the time that they were under their kings they were accustomed to sacrifice boys to the goddess Mania, Mother of the Lares, for the happiness of their houses, induced, as Macrobius says, by a certain oracle of Apollo; and we know from Pliny (o) that it was not forbidden to sacrifice human victims until the year 657 of Rome; but it was not by this prohibition that the examples of that barbarous superstition altogether ceased, for Augustus, according to what some writers cited by Suetonius affirm, after the capture of Perugia, where the consul L. Antonius had fortified himself, sacrificed in honor of his uncle Julius Caesar, already deified by the Romans, three hundred men, part senators and part Roman knights, chosen from among Antonius’s people, on an altar erected to that new god. (p)

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                      Pp                     Lactantius

(o) At length, in the year of the City 657, in the consulship of Cn. Cornelius Lentulus and P. Licinius, a decree of the Senate was passed that no man should be sacrificed. Pliny, Natural History, bk. 30, ch. 1.

(p) When Perusia was taken, he punished very many; to those who tried to beg pardon or to excuse themselves he replied with a single word, “You must die.” Some write that three hundred chosen from among the prisoners, of both orders, were slaughtered like sacrificial victims on the Ides of March at an altar built to the Divine Julius. Suetonius, Octavian.

[p. 298]

Lactantius Firmianus, a man very well instructed in the affairs of the Romans, who flourished in the fourth century of the Church, says expressly that even in his own times those sacrifices to Jupiter Latialis were still made in Italy. (q) Nor did the Spaniards preserve themselves from that barbarous superstition. Strabo relates in bk. 3 that the Lusitanians sacrificed their prisoners, cutting off their right hands to consecrate them to their gods, observed their entrails, and watched them for their auguries; that all the inhabitants of the mountains were accustomed to sacrifice prisoners together with horses, offering such victims to the god Mars by the hundred; and speaking in general he says that it was characteristic of the Spaniards to sacrifice themselves for their friends. Not alien to this way of thinking is what Silius Italicus relates of the Baeticans, his ancestors: that after passing the age of youth, grown weary of life, they gave themselves death; which he praises as a heroic action. (r) Who would have believed that this ancient fashion of Baetica should in our own days be renewed in England and in France? Coming then to later times, Father Mariana, speaking of the Goths who occupied Spain, says as follows: (s) “Because they were persuaded that the war would not go well for them unless they offered human blood for the army, they sacrificed prisoners of war to the god Mars, to whom they were chiefly devoted; and they were accustomed also to offer him the first fruits of the spoils, and to hang from the trunks of trees the skins of those whom they killed.” If those Spaniards who wrote the history of Mexico had not forgotten what had happened earlier in their own peninsula, they would not have been so astonished at the sacrifices of the Mexicans.

Whoever wishes for more examples may consult Eusebius of Caesarea in bk. 4 of the Preparation for the Gospel, where he gives a long

detailed

(q) Nor were the Latins free from this inhumanity: for even to this day Jupiter Latialis is worshipped with human blood. Lactant., Divine Institutes, bk. 1, ch. 21.

(r) A people prodigal of life, and most ready to hasten death: for when a man has passed the years that flourish with strength, impatient of age he scorns old age as it comes, and the measure of his fate lies in his right hand. Silius.

(s) General History of Spain, bk. 5. [p. 299] detailed account of the nations among whom those barbarous sacrifices have been in use; for what we have indicated suffices us to show that the Mexicans did nothing but follow in the footsteps of the most celebrated nations of the old continent, and that their rites were no more cruel, nor more unreasonable. Is it not a greater inhumanity to sacrifice one’s own citizens, one’s own children, and oneself, as for the most part those nations did, than prisoners of war, as was the custom among the Mexicans? The Mexicans were never seen to sacrifice their own countrymen, except those who by their crimes were guilty of death, and at times the wives of the lords, so that they might accompany them also into the other world. That answer which Moctezuma gave to Cortés, who reproached him with the cruelty of their sacrifices, shows that although their sentiments were not just, they were still less unreasonable than those of other nations that had fallen into the same superstition. We, he said, have the right to take the life of our enemies: we could kill them in the heat of battle, as you do your enemies. Now what injustice is there in putting to death, in honor of our gods, those who are guilty of death?

The frequency of such sacrifices was certainly no less in Egypt, in Italy, in Spain, and in Gaul, than in Mexico. If in the single city of Heliopolis there were sacrificed annually, according to Manetho, more than a thousand human victims to the goddess Juno alone, how many must have been those that were sacrificed in the other cities of Egypt to the famous goddess Isis, and to the other innumerable gods worshipped by that so superstitious nation? How great must the frequency have been among the Pelasgians, who sacrificed to their gods the tenth part of their children? What number of men must have been consumed in those hecatombs, and centenary sacrifices of the ancient Spaniards? And what shall we say of the Gauls, who, after sacrificing prisoners of war and malefactors, made even innocent citizens die in sacrifice, as Caesar says? The number of the Mexican sacrifices, moreover, has certainly been exaggerated by the greater part of the historians of Mexico, as we have said elsewhere.

Pp2                     The

[p. 300]

The most humane Romans, who had scruples about looking upon human entrails, (t) although at the end of six centuries and a half after the founding of their famous metropolis they finally forbade the sacrificing of men, nevertheless permitted with all too great frequency the gladiatorial sacrifice. I so call those barbarous combats which, while serving for the amusement of that ferocious people, were moreover prescribed by their religion. Besides the great quantity of human blood that was shed in the Circus games and at banquets, there was in truth no little shed at the funerals of the well-to-do, either by gladiators fighting among themselves, or by the putting to death of some prisoners to appease the Manes of the dead; and they were so persuaded of the necessity of human blood for such an end, that when for lack of means the expenses of gladiators or of prisoners could not be borne, mourning women were paid, so that they might draw blood from their cheeks with their nails. How many, then, must have been the victims put to death by the superstition of the Romans at so many funerals, especially as there was emulation among them, each seeking to surpass the others in the number of gladiators and prisoners that were to serve for his funeral pomp? This sanguinary spirit of the Romans was what wrought such slaughters among the peoples of Europe, Asia, and Africa, and it likewise often flooded Rome with the blood of her own citizens, especially during those horrible proscriptions which darkened the glory of that famous republic.

Not only were the Mexicans inhuman toward their prisoners, but also toward themselves, with those barbarous austerities which we have set forth in the History. But drawing blood from the tongue, the arms, and the legs with the thorns of the maghei, as all did, and piercing the tongue with bits of cane, as some of the more austere were wont to do, will seem slight mortifications beside those frightful and unheard-of austerities performed by the penitents of the East Indies and of Japan, which cannot be read without horror. Who will dare to compare

the

(t) To look upon human entrails is held to be sacrilege. Pliny, Natural History, bk. 28, ch. 1. [p. 301] the inhumanity of the most famous Tlamacazqui of Mexico and of Tlaxcala with that of the priests of Bellona and of Cybele? (u) When was it seen that the Mexicans slashed their limbs, tore the flesh with their teeth, or castrated themselves in honor of their gods, as those priests did in honor of their Cybele?

Finally, the Mexicans, not content with sacrificing human victims, also ate their flesh. I confess that in this they were more inhuman than those other nations; but examples of such inhumanity have not been so rare in the old continent, even among civilized nations, that the Mexicans must on that account be numbered among the absolutely barbarous peoples. “That horrible custom,” says the historian Solís, “of men eating one another, was seen earlier among other barbarians of our hemisphere, as Galicia confesses in its Annals.” Besides the ancient Africans, whose descendants are even today in part cannibals, it is certain that many of those nations that were once known by the common name of Scythians were likewise so, and also the ancient settlers of Sicily and of the mainland of Italy, as Pliny and other authors say. Of the Jews who lived in the times of Antiochus the Illustrious, Apion, an Egyptian historian (not a Greek, as M. de Pauw says), wrote that they kept a Greek prisoner to eat him at the end of a year. Of the famous Hannibal Livy says that he had human flesh eaten by his soldiers in order to hearten

them

(u) The priests of the Great Goddess, who were called Galli, cut off their own virile parts, and, driven by frenzy, whirled their heads, and with knives lacerated their faces and the muscles of their whole body; they also attacked themselves with their teeth. Augustine, City of God, bk. 2, ch. 7. One cuts off his virile parts, another slashes his arms. Where do they fear angry gods, who thus earn propitious ones? … So great is the frenzy of a mind disordered and driven from its seat, that the gods are appeased in a manner in which not even the most savage men rage; the tyrants handed down in fables for their cruelty have mangled the limbs of some, but have ordered no one to mangle his own. For the pleasure of a king’s lust some have been castrated; but no one, at his master’s bidding, laid hands on himself so as not to be a man. These men cut themselves to pieces in the temples, they supplicate with their wounds and their blood. If anyone has leisure to look at what they do and what they suffer, he will find things so unbecoming to honorable men, so unworthy of free men, so unlike sane men, that no one would doubt that they were mad, if they were mad in company with fewer; as it is, the multitude of the insane is the defense of their sanity. Seneca, On Superstition. [p. 302] them for war. Pliny severely reproaches the Greeks for the custom of eating all the parts of the human body to cure various diseases. (x) Now what wonder that the Mexicans performed as a maxim of religion what the Greeks practiced for medicine? But no, I do not pretend to make an apology for the Mexicans on this point. Their religion in what concerns anthropophagy was without doubt more barbarous than that of the Romans, the Egyptians, and those other civilized nations; but for the rest it cannot be doubted, given what we have said, that it was less superstitious, less ridiculous, and less indecent.

## Dissertation

(x) Who first thought of devouring each separate human limb? By what conjecture was he led? How could that medicine have had such an origin? Who made poisonings more innocent than remedies? Be it so, let barbarous and foreign rites have invented them; did the Greeks too make these their arts? etc. Pliny, Natural History, bk. 28, ch. 1.

[p. 303]

## D I S S E RTAT I O N I X 

## ON THE ORIGIN OF THE FRENCH DISEASE

## I   n the present Dissertation we have to dispute not only with M. de

Pauw, but also with nearly all Europeans, who are already generally persuaded that the French disease had its origin in America: for after various nations of Europe had for more than thirty years laid the blame on one another for the origin of so shameful a disease, they finally agreed to blame the New World. We should without doubt be charged with rashness in wishing to combat so universal an opinion, if the arguments that we are about to oppose to it, and the example of two modern Europeans, did not render our boldness excusable. (a) Since among the supporters of the common opinion the principal one, the most renowned, and he who has written about it most copiously and most learnedly is M. Astruc, a learned French physician, it is he who will be chiefly attacked by us, availing ourselves for this purpose in large part of those very materials which he furnishes us in his work. (b)

§ I.

(a) These two authors are William Becket, a surgeon of London, and Antonio Ribeiro Sanches. Becket wrote three dissertations, which were inserted in vols. 30 and 31 of the Philosophical Transactions, to prove that the French disease was already known in England as early as the fourteenth century. Ribeiro wrote a dissertation, which was printed in Paris in 1765 with this title: Dissertation sur l’origine de la maladie vénérienne, dans laquelle on prouve qu’elle n’a point été portée de l’Amérique. Having read the title of this dissertation in the “Catalogue of Spanish Books and Manuscripts” added to vol. IV of Dr. Robertson’s History of America, we sought it here in Rome, in Genoa, and in Venice, and have not been able to find it; nor do we know whether the author is Spanish or Portuguese, as his surnames suggest, or born in France of Spanish parents.

(b) De morbis venereis, vol. 2. I have used the Venice edition.

[p. 304]

§ I.

Opinions of the Early Physicians on the Origin of the French Disease.

In the first thirty years after the French disease began to be felt in Italy, there was no author who attributed its origin to America, as we shall say later. All the authors who wrote about it before 1525, and some even of those who wrote afterward, attributed it to various causes, the knowledge of which will bring the readers both compassion and pleasure.

Some of the earliest physicians then living, such as Corradino Gillini and Gaspar Torrella, were persuaded, according to the ideas of those times, that the French disease had been caused by the great conjunction of the Sun with Jupiter, Saturn, and Mercury in the sign of Libra, which occurred in 1483.

Others, following the celebrated Niccolò Leoniceno, (c) attributed it to the most abundant rains and to the inundations that occurred in Italy in that year in which the contagion began.

Giovanni Manardi, a learned professor of the University of Ferrara, ascribed the origin of this disease to the impure intercourse of a Valencian knight infected with leprosy with a harlot; and Paracelsus to the intercourse of a French leper with a prostitute. Antonio Musa Brasavola, a learned Ferrarese, affirms that the French disease had its beginning from a harlot who was to be found in the army of the French at Naples, who had an abscess in the mouth of the womb.

Gabriele Falloppio, a celebrated Modenese physician, affirms that the Spaniards being few in the war of Naples, and the French countless, poisoned one night the water of the wells, from which their enemies were to drink, and that from this the contagion had its origin.

Andrea

(c) Therefore we say that this ailment, which they commonly call the Gallic disease, ought to be reckoned among the epidemics … This is sufficiently established, that in that year there was a great overflowing of waters throughout all Italy … and that the summer, moreover, came on to that [season] with an intemperance, namely a hot and humid one, etc. Opusc., On the Gallic Disease.

[p. 305]

Andrea Cesalpino, physician to Clement VIII, says that he had learned from those who took part in the war of Naples that when Somma, a place on Vesuvius where there is a great abundance of excellent Greek wine, was besieged by the French, the Spaniards slipped away secretly one night, leaving there a great quantity of that wine, mixed with the blood of the sick of Saint Lazarus, and that the French, entering immediately, drank of that wine, and soon began to feel the effects of the venereal disease.

Leonardo Fioravanti, a learned physician of Bologna, says in his work entitled Capricci medicinali that he had learned from the son of the man who had been sutler to the army of Alfonso, king of Naples, around the year 1456, that when provisions began to fail through the length of the war, both in the army of that king and in that of the French, the sutlers furnished both with seasoned human flesh, and that from this the French disease took its origin. The celebrated Chancellor Bacon of Verulam adds (d) that the flesh furnished them was that of men killed in Barbary, which they prepared like tuna.

Since no one knew, nor could know, who in Europe was the first to suffer that great disease, so neither can its cause be known; but let us see what could have happened.

§ II. The French Disease Could Have Been Communicated to Europe from Other

Countries of the Old Continent.

To show that the French disease could have been communicated by contagion to Europe from other countries of the same continent, it is necessary, and sufficient, to prove that the aforesaid disease was already suffered in some of those countries, and that they had commerce with Europe before the New World was discovered. The one and the other will here be fully demonstrated.

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.             Qq            Vatable

(d) Sylva sylvarum, cent. 1, art. 26.

[p. 306]

Vatable, Father Pineda, Father Calmet, and other authors maintained that among the diseases with which holy Job was afflicted, one was that of the French disease. This opinion is so ancient that as soon as that disease appeared in Italy, some called it Job’s disease, as Battista Fregoso, an author living at the time, attests. (e) Father Calmet strives (f) to prove his view with great erudition; but since we know nothing of Job’s infirmity except what is indicated in the sacred book, which can easily be understood of other known diseases, or of some wholly unknown to us, no account is to be made of this opinion.

André Thevet, a French geographer, (g) and other authors affirm that the French disease was endemic in the interior provinces of Africa situated on one bank and the other of the Senegal.

Andreas Cleyer, chief physician of the Dutch colony on the island of Java, says (h) that the venereal disease was proper and native to that island, and as common as the quotidian fever. Thuanus had affirmed the same. (i)

Jacob Bontius, physician to the Dutch in the East Indies, testifies (l) that that disease was endemic in Amboina and in the Moluccas, and that to contract it no previous carnal intercourse was needed. This was in part confirmed by the report of the companions of Magellan, the first who made the circuit of the world in the famous ship Victoria, who testified, as the chronicler Herrera says, (m) that they found in Timor, an island of the Moluccan archipelago, a great number of islanders infected with the French disease; which was certainly not brought there either by the Americans or by Europeans already infected.

Father

(e) In the work entitled Dicta factaque memorabilia, bk. 1, ch. 4.

(f) Dissertation on the Disease of Job.

(g) Cosmographie universelle, bk. 1, ch. 11.

(h) Letter to Christian Mentzel.

(i) History of His Own Time, ch. 71.

(l) In the Method of Healing to be used in the East Indies in the treatment of the diseases commonly and popularly raging there.

(m) Dec. 3, bk. 4, ch. 1.

[p. 307]

Father Foureau, a French Jesuit, learned, exact, and experienced in the affairs of China, being asked by M. Astruc (n) whether the physicians of China reputed the venereal disease to be native to their country, or else brought there from elsewhere, replied that the Chinese physicians consulted by him were of the opinion that this disease had been suffered in that empire from the most remote antiquity, and that in fact the books of medicine written in Chinese characters, which are esteemed by them as ancient, said nothing about the beginning of that malady, but rather made mention of it as of a most ancient evil even at the time in which the aforesaid books were written: that therefore it was not known, nor even probable, that this disease had been brought there from other countries.

Finally, M. Astruc himself says (o) that in his opinion, after having examined and weighed the testimonies of the authors, the venereal disease was not peculiar only to the island of Haiti or Hispaniola, but common also to many regions of the old continent, and perhaps to all the equatorial countries of the world, in which it had reigned from of old. This candid confession of a man so well instructed in this matter, and moreover so committed against America, goes far, besides the testimonies mentioned above, to show that even if we suppose the French disease to have anciently existed in that New World, nothing can however be alleged in this matter by the Europeans against America that cannot be said by the Americans against several countries of the Old World; and that if the blood of the Americans was spoiled, as M. de P. will have it, the blood of so many Asiatics and Africans was no healthier.

M. Astruc adds that from those countries of Asia and Africa in which the French disease was endemic, it might indeed be communicated through commerce to neighboring peoples, but not however to the Europeans; because, owing to the torrid zone’s having been believed uninhabitable and inaccessible, there was no commerce between those countries and Europe. But to whom is it not known that
