Qq2                         great

(n) Dissertation On the Origin of Venereal Diseases among the Chinese, at the end of vol. 1.

(o) De morbis venereis, bk. 1, ch. 11. [p. 308] great commerce which Egypt had for so many centuries, on the one hand with the equatorial countries of Asia, and on the other with Italy? Now why might not the Asiatic merchants have carried the venereal disease from India together with the drugs to Egypt, and thence the Venetians, the Genoese, and the Pisans, who had for a long time had continual commerce with the city of Alexandria, have carried it to Italy, just as other Europeans carried leprosy to Italy from Syria, and smallpox from Arabia? Moreover, among those many Europeans who from the twelfth century onward undertook the journey into the southern countries of Asia, such as Benjamin of Tudela, Carpini, Marco Polo, and Mandeville, among whom some, like Marco Polo, penetrated as far as China, could not someone of them have brought back with him on his return to Europe the contagion taken in those Asiatic countries? Here we are not discoursing of what in fact happened, but only of what might have happened.

Not only from Asia, but also from Africa could the French disease have passed into Europe before America was discovered; for the Portuguese, thirty years before the glorious expedition of Columbus, had already discovered a great part of the equatorial countries of Africa, and had opened commerce there. Now could not some Portuguese, there infected with the French disease, have infected in turn his countrymen, and subsequently other nations of Europe, as perhaps in fact happened, according to what we shall say below? Let M. Astruc see, then, in how many ways the French disease could have been communicated to Europe without the intervention of America, although the torrid zone was believed by the ancients to be inaccessible.

## § III

The French Disease Could Have Arisen in Europe without Contagion.

Before treating this argument it is necessary to say a word on the nature and the physical cause of that disease. The French disease is, according to the physicians, a kind of cachexy, in which the lymph, and especially its serous part, takes on a singular

thickness [p. 309] thickness and acrimony. The venereal poison, says M. Astruc, (p) is of a salty nature, or rather acido-salty, corrosive, and fixed. It causes the condensation and acrimony of the lymph, and from this arise the inflammations, the warts, the ulcers, the erosions, the pains, and all the other dreadful symptoms known to physicians.

This poison, communicated to a healthy man, must not be considered, says the aforesaid author, as a new humor added to the natural humors, but rather as a mere dyscrasia, or vicious quality of the natural humors, which, degenerating from their natural state, change into acido-salty ones.

Now nearly all physicians are persuaded that this evil cannot arise otherwise than by way of contagion, communicated through the seminal fluid, or the milk, or the saliva, or the sweat, or the contact of venereal ulcers, etc. But I, with the good leave of these gentlemen, maintain that the French disease can absolutely be generated in a man without any contagion or communication with the infected; because it can absolutely be generated in that same manner in which it was generated in the first man who suffered from it: now this man did not have it by contagion, for then he would not have been the first to suffer from it, but from another, quite different cause: therefore from a similar cause, whatever it was, that same cachexy could be produced without contagion in other individuals of the human species. This is true, says M. Astruc, in America, or in some other similar country, but not in Europe. And why is Europe so privileged? Because, replies the aforesaid author, those causes do not concur there which in the beginning could have caused that evil in America. And what are these causes? Let us examine them.

In the first place M. Astruc says (q) that the air is not to be counted among such causes, which could indeed cause other

maladies

(p) De morbis venereis, bk. 2, ch. 2.

(q) The air, indeed, seems to be struck from the number of causes: in Hispaniola it could perhaps bring on other diseases, but by no means the venereal pox. It is certainly established that the Europeans who have inhabited that island for 200 years (indeed, nearly 300) never contracted the venereal pox there except by contagion. Yet the Europeans there breathe the same air that the natives formerly breathed, and without doubt tempered and constituted in the same way. Astruc, De morbis venereis, bk. 1, ch. 12. [p. 310] maladies in Hispaniola, but not the venereal disease, because the Europeans who for two hundred years and more have lived on that island have never contracted that malady there except by way of contagion; and yet the air is not at present different from what it was three hundred years ago: and even supposing it is different at present, at least it was not so at the beginning of the fifteenth century. The air, therefore, is not to be taken into account where the question is to discover the first origin of the venereal disease. Nevertheless the same M. Astruc, after having excluded the air from the number of the causes of the French disease, has recourse to it, openly contradicting himself, as we shall see below.

Only two causes are indicated by M. Astruc: food and heat. As to food, he says that the inhabitants of Hispaniola, when they lacked maize, cassava, etc., fed on spiders, worms, bats, and other such small creatures. As to heat, he affirms that women in hot countries are apt to be troubled by menstrual discharges that are too acrid and almost virulent, especially if they eat unwholesome foods. This being supposed, the author in question reasons thus: “Therefore the natives of the island of Haiti must once have been afflicted with many and most grievous diseases, where no one kept away from menstruating women; where men, impotent through lust, were driven to venery after the manner of beasts; where the women, who were most shameless, admitted men promiscuously, as Gonzalo de Oviedo attests, Hist. Indiar., bk. 5, ch. 3; nay, more shamelessly provoked these same men, and more of them, at the time of menstruation, when, the womb then growing warm, they raged more madly with lust after the manner of cattle. What wonder, then, if the various, heterogeneous, acrid semen of many men, mixed together in one, blended with the most acrid and virulent menstrual blood, shut up within the seething and foul womb of most filthy women, putrefied in a short time through delay, heterogeneity, and the heat of the place, and formed the first seeds of the venereal disease, which spread to others, if any perhaps were more continent?”

Here is the whole argument of M. Astruc on the first origin of the venereal disease, full from head to foot of falsehood, as we shall show below; but supposing now that all were true,

I

[p. 311]

I affirm that this same thing which, by his account, happened in Hispaniola could likewise happen in Europe; for just as those Americans, lacking maize and other customary foods, fed on spiders, worms, etc., so the Europeans, lacking wheat and other good provisions, have at times been seen to eat mice, lizards, and other such small creatures, the excrement of several animals, and even bread made of the flour of human bones, bringing upon themselves the gravest illness. It is enough to recall the dreadful famines suffered in Europe in the past, caused partly by the weather and partly by war. Then there have always been men who, like beasts, have let themselves be carried by an unbridled lust to the most execrable excesses. There have always been shameless and most filthy women, and one could say of them what Plautus says: There are more harlots there than flies at the very time when it is hottest. For the rest, there has never been a lack of excessively acrid seminal fluids, nor of seething wombs, nor of virulent menses. Such causes, then, could produce the French disease in Europe, as they produced it in America, according to M. Astruc.

“No,” replies this author, “it is not so; because, the air being more temperate in Europe (here is the recourse to the air, after having excluded it from the number of the causes of the French disease) there is not the same acrimony in the semen of men, the same virulence in the menstrual blood, the same fervor in the womb of women, as were proved to have existed on the island of Haiti: (the proofs of M. Astruc are no others than those set out above) whence,” he adds, “those symptoms could never be produced there by the simultaneous concurrence of the causes. And to say it in a few words, one must judge of diseases and of their causes as of the generation of animals and plants. Now just as in Europe lions do not breed, nor do monkeys propagate, nor do parrots build nests, nor do many Indian or American plants come up in Europe, however much they may be sown there, so neither could the French disease ever be produced in Europe by those causes from which, as we have said, it was produced in Hispaniola; because each climate has its own

particular [p. 312] particular qualities, and those things which in one climate come of themselves cannot in another be brought about by any art; because, as the poet says, Not every land bears everything.”

I am willing to concede many things to M. Astruc that surely no one else would concede to him. I concede to him that there never were in Europe either that abuse of menstruating women, or that acrimony, or that virulence in the fluids of the human body, or that fervor in the womb, which he supposes on the island of Hispaniola, although from the books of medicine published these two thousand years the exact opposite is established. I concede to him that no examples of the most unbridled lust were ever seen there; for it seems to him too much to confess such examples in Europe, (r) and I concede to him besides that all the women and men of Europe have been most healthy and most chaste. All this I concede to him, although it is contradicted by history and by the common opinion of the Europeans themselves. Nevertheless I affirm that the French disease could absolutely be generated in Europe without contagion; because all those disorders which M. Astruc supposes on the island of Hispaniola could also have occurred in Europe, even though in reality they never did. Those most chaste women, led on by those evil passions which are common to all the children of Adam, could become as incontinent and shameless as that author believes the American women of Hispaniola to have been. Those men, so healthy, could feed on foods as harmful as were those of the Haitians. Human sperm, which is by itself very acrid, as the same M. Astruc says, could, by reason of unwholesome foods, become more and more acrid, until it took on that degree of acrimony which the venereal disease requires. The menses could become virulent either from their previous suppression, or from plethora, or from many other morbid causes, in the fluids as well as in the vessels.

The

(r) But so be it: let us grant that in Europe lust is practiced as impurely as in Hispaniola; for I do not wish to contend against it, although these things nevertheless seem excessive. Astruc, De morbis venereis, bk. 1, ch. 12.

[p. 313]

The uterus, moreover, could conceive an excessive ardor from blood heated with strong liquors or with foods that are too hot. I do not believe that there is any physician who would contradict these truths: and since M. Astruc confesses that the venereal poison is not a new humor added to the natural humors, but a mere depravation of those same humors, why could not those causes which, by his account, caused such depravation in Hispaniola have caused it also in Europe? Because in Europe, he says, the air is more temperate.

This is the only subterfuge that remains to M. Astruc; but it avails him nothing; for it is certain that in many countries of Europe, as in Italy, and particularly in the southern part of it, the air is hotter in summer than in Hispaniola, and there is moreover no reason to believe that the heat of the whole year is necessary, and that that of some months does not suffice to cause that depravation of the humors. But who ever thought that the external heat of the air is needed to cause that extraordinary acrimony and virulence in the humors? Scurvy is a cachexy very similar to that of the venereal disease, but more terrible, which brings with it a stupendous acrimony and corruption in the blood: now this kind of malady arises as much in the hot regions as in the northern countries and seas, and is more often caused by traveling in the temperate or cold zones than in the torrid: therefore hot air is not necessary for a stupendous acrimony and corruption to be generated in the humors.

Finally M. Astruc wants diseases and their causes to be judged as the generation of animals is; and he affirms that just as lions do not breed in Europe, nor do monkeys propagate there, so neither can the French disease be produced there from those causes that produced it in Hispaniola. But what would M. Astruc say if he saw lions growing stronger in Europe, and monkeys propagating there far more than in Africa? He would say without doubt, or at least ought to say, that the climate of Europe was more apt and more suitable than that of Africa to the generation of such animals, now the French disease

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.        Rr                     has [p. 314] has become much stronger in Europe than in America; as M. Astruc confesses, and also M. de Pauw, (s) and Oviedo (t); that is, that author who may be called the inventor of that malady in America, and moreover it has spread much more in Europe than in America, as is known to all those who have been in both these parts of the world, or are well informed about them: therefore, according to the principles of M. Astruc, the climate of Europe is more apt and more suited than that of America to the generation of the French disease.

Thus far we have reasoned on the supposition that what M. Astruc reports in his discourse was true; but besides some errors in matters of physics, on which it is not fitting to discourse, there are also facts arbitrarily supposed and contrary to the truth. He supposes 1. that the Indians of Hispaniola fed on worms, spiders, etc.; but this perhaps happened some years after the discovery of that island, when the Americans, fleeing the fury of the European conquerors, went wandering lost through the woods, and, lacking maize and cassava, because they had not sown them, out of hatred for their enemies, as Peter Martyr d’Anghiera bears witness (u), ate whatever they found there; but no ancient author affirms that they used such foods before the Spaniards landed there; and to show that the aforesaid foods had some influence on the French disease, it would have to be proved that the use of them was at least as ancient on that island as that infirmity was in the judgment of M. Astruc: which he has not done, nor could do. 2. He affirms that on the island of Hispaniola no one kept away from menstruating women; but I should have wished that to confirm this he had cited the testimony of some ancient author; for I do not find anyone who says it; on the contrary, I see that among the singular things noted by European writers in the Americans, even in the barbarous tribes, is that of not having intercourse with

women

(s) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1.

(t) General History of the Indies, bk. 10, ch. 2.

(u) Sumario of the History of the West Indies. [p. 315] women during their periodic evacuation. M. de Pauw, that capital enemy of the whole New World, and that great Researcher into American filth, speaks thus in part 1 of his Recherches: “There was a law among all the savage peoples of the New World not to approach women at the time of their courses, either because contact with the flux was held by them to be pernicious, or because instinct alone taught them such a regard.” 3. M. Astruc represents the men and women of Hispaniola as exceedingly heated and agitated by a violent and raging lust; Count de Buffon and M. de Pauw (x) represent, on the contrary, all the Americans as most cold and insensible to the stimuli of love. What does such a contradiction mean, if not that these systematic authors paint the Americans in whatever colors turn out to suit them? When they want to bring out the apathy or insensibility of those men, they say that they are most cold; but when they claim to discredit their customs, or to blame them for the French disease, then they affirm that they are beyond measure libidinous. M. Astruc cites the testimony of Gonzalo de Oviedo in bk. 5, ch. 3 of his History to convince us that the Haitian women were too shameless, and that they prostituted themselves indiscriminately to all men; but besides that the testimony of this author against the Americans is worth less than nothing, as we shall show below, he does not say what M. Astruc would have us believe. Here is what Oviedo says in the cited place: The women of this island were continent with their own men; but with the Christians they willingly made free of themselves. Here is what Herrera says of it: (y) The women were continent with their fellow natives, and dishonest with the Castilians. If they were continent with their fellow natives, their incontinence could not cause the French disease before the Spaniards landed there. If they were dishonest only with the Christians, it must be believed

## Rr 2                      that

(x) See what Count de Buffon says concerning the coldness of the Americans in various places of his Natural History, and M. de P. in pt. 1 of his Recherches.

(y) Dec. 1, bk. 3, ch. 4. [p. 316] that they were driven to such disorders more by the importunity or fear of their conquerors than by their own lust. Finally, what M. Astruc affirms concerning the acrimony of the spermatic humor, the virulence of the menstrual blood, the filthiness of the American women, and their uterine fervor is talk in the air, and without any foundation in history.

Before ending this article I cannot help mentioning the no less foul than extravagant opinion of Dr. Johann Linder, an Englishman, on the cause of the French disease, so that it may be seen how far the zeal to discredit the Americans in this matter has gone. He affirms, then, that that evil had its origin in the carnal conjunction of the Americans with satyrs, or large cercopitheci; (z) but, fortunately for the Indians of Hispaniola, there were no cercopitheci, either large or small, in that island, nor in any other of those islands.

§ I V.

The French Disease Did Not Come from America.

We have indicated above that in the first thirty years after America was discovered, no one ascribed the origin of the French disease to that New World. I, at least, after consulting a great many authors, physicians as well as historians, who wrote at that early time of such a disease and of its origin, have not found a single one who was of that opinion; nor could M. Astruc find one, although he looked among all the Italian, French, English, Spanish, and German writers for someone who would support his opinion. The first, into whose head it came to blame America for the French disease, was

Gonzalo

(z) He traced its origin to sodomy practiced at times between men and large cercopitheci, or the satyrs of the ancients. Exercitat. de venenis, chs. 1 and 10. With which fiction, says M. Astruc, as nothing could be more vain and absurd, so nothing more foul could be devised.

[p. 317]

Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo, who in the Sumario of the History of the West Indies, presented to Charles V in 1525, asserted that the Spaniards infected on the island of Hispaniola, who afterward returned to Spain with Admiral Columbus and then passed into Italy with the Great Captain, communicated that evil to the Neapolitan women, and these to the French, etc. As this author was a man of letters, and lived some years in America, holding an honorable office, his authority drew after it almost all the writers; for on the one hand everyone believed him well informed, and on the other it suited everyone that he should be believed, so that each might free his own nation from the imputation of so shameful a disease. But before examining his account, it is necessary to make this writer known, whose authority has been the chief, or rather the sole, support of the common opinion.

Monsignor Bartolomé de las Casas, who lived in America at the same time as Oviedo and knew him quite well, in his refutation of Dr. Juan Ginés de Sepúlveda, who alleged the authority of Oviedo against the Indians, says thus: “What most prejudices the person of the Reverend Doctor among prudent and God-fearing men who have eyewitness knowledge of the Indies is the allegation he makes of Oviedo as an irrefragable author in his most false and execrable History, he having been one of the tyrants, robbers, and destroyers of the Indies, as he himself confesses in the preface to the first part and in bk. 6, ch. 8, and for that reason a capital enemy of the Indians. Let wise persons judge whether such a writer is a fit witness against the Indians. And yet this man is called a grave and diligent chronicler by the Doctor, because he found him suited to his purpose; but it is certain that that History has little more leaves than lies, as we amply prove in other writings and in the Apologia.” In fact the chronicler Herrera, a judicious and impartial man, says that Monsignor de las Casas had reason to complain of Oviedo, and that this man was not too exact in some reports. He, moreover, promoted some extravagant opinions, induced by the spirit of flattery, or

by [p. 318] by vanity. It is enough to read the second book of his History, in which, besides saying that the Trojans descended from the Spaniards, he affirms that the Antilles are the Hesperides of the ancients, and that they were so called from Hesperus, twelfth king of Spain, who was lord of them 1658 years before the Christian era. “In this manner,” he adds, “with so ancient a claim, and by the aforesaid route, this dominion returned to Spain after so many centuries: and as its own possession, it seems, Divine Justice was pleased to restore it to her, that she might possess it through the good fortune of the two happy and Catholic Kings Don Ferdinand and Doña Isabella.” (A) Such is the author of the common opinion; let us now examine his account.

He speaks with some variation in the Sumario of the History and in the History itself; but since the latter is his principal work, the more extensive, published some years after the Sumario, and composed with greater study, we ought rather to stand by what he says in it, wherever there is any divergence. He says, then, in bk. 2, ch. 14 of the General History of the Indies, that the Spaniards who returned with Columbus to Spain in 1496 from their second voyage to America brought back from the island of Hispaniola the French disease together with samples of the gold of the famous mines of Cibao: and that some of them, already infected, who passed into Italy with the Great Captain Gonzalo Fernández de Córdoba, infected through the Italian women the French who had come with King Charles VIII to take possession of the kingdom of Naples: But this account is wholly untenable, and full of anachronisms; for Columbus returned to Spain from his second voyage on June 3, 1496, and we know from countless eyewitnesses that Europe was already infected with the French disease at least as early as 1495: therefore such infection could not have come from those Spaniards who then returned with Columbus. Then, to show with the greatest historical evidence that the French who were at Naples with King Charles

## VIII

(A) The learned Don Ferdinand Columbus, in ch. 9 of his History, reproaches Oviedo with the extravagance of his opinions and the infidelity of his citations.

[p. 319]

VIII, could not have been infected by the Spanish troops who came with the Great Captain into Italy, it suffices simply to set out the dates, as we find them in Guicciardini, Mariana, Mézeray, and other Italian, Spanish, and French historians. King Charles VIII marched with his army toward Italy in August 1494: he arrived at Asti, a city on the Tanaro, on September 9: entered Rome on December 31, and Naples on February 22, 1495. In this city he stayed no more than three months; for, aware of the great confederation formed against him, he hastened to return to France. He left Naples on May 20, as is attested by Guicciardini, Bembo, Mariana, etc., and having won on July 6 the famous battle of Fornovo against the Venetians, he withdrew precipitately to his court, leading his army infected with the venereal disease, as all the historians of those times testify. The Great Captain, held back at Majorca and in Sardinia by contrary winds, could not arrive with his fleet at Messina before May 24, 1495, that is, four days after King Charles had departed from Naples with his infected army: therefore this army was not, nor could it have been, infected by those Spanish troops, unless one is willing to say that those same contrary winds which prevented the Great Captain’s fleet from approaching Italy carried the contagion there. I wonder that the authors of the common opinion did not notice so manifest an anachronism. Someone might say that that contagion was not brought by the troops of the Great Captain, but by other Spanish troops that came earlier into Italy; but besides that both Oviedo, the inventor of the common opinion, and the other writers who follow him generally ascribe the contagion of Naples to the troops of the Great Captain, I have not been able to find, though I have made diligent researches, that from the discovery of America until the arrival of the Great Captain any other Spanish troops came to the mainland of Italy; on the contrary, from Mariana’s account the very opposite appears: it was not, therefore, the Spanish troops that caused the contagion of Naples.

From what we have said above it must not be thought

that [p. 320] that the French disease was only a few days earlier in Italy than the arrival of the Spanish troops; for we know from the deposition of the ablest physicians of that time that this malady began there at least some months before the Spanish fleet landed. Gaspar Torrella, a Valencian, physician to Pope Alexander VI, then reigning, (B) Wendelin Hock, a learned German, and professor of medicine in those times in this University of Bologna, (C) Giacomo Cattaneo of Lagomarsino, a learned Genoese physician, (D) Giovanni de Vigo, a Genoese, physician and surgeon to Pope Julius II (E): these four authors, besides others of much authority, because learned and well versed in diseases, and because they were eyewitnesses of it, testify that the French disease began to be felt in Italy as early as 1494. There is, moreover, no reason to wonder that there is some divergence among the authors about the beginning of that evil; since by some it was observed sooner than by others, because it was not felt at the same time in all the states of this peninsula.

But it might still be said that, although Oviedo made a mistake in the History, affirming that the first to carry the French disease to Spain were those who returned there with Columbus in 1496, it was not so in the Sumario of the same History,

published

(B) When the French entered Italy with a strong hand, and especially when the Parthenopean kingdom had been occupied, and were staying there, this disease was discovered. Tract. de dolore in pudendagra, published in 1500. From this it is seen that the French disease began in Italy from the time the French entered it, although its great increase was after they occupied the kingdom of Naples. The French entered Italy, as we have said, in September 1494.

(C) As has happened at this time, namely from the year 1494 up to the present year 1502, in which a certain contagious disease, which is called the French, etc. Opusc. de morbo gallico, printed in 1502.

(D) In the year of the Virgin’s childbirth 1494, when Charles VIII, King of the French, invaded the Parthenopean kingdom, and Alexander VI was at that time holding the Supreme Pontificate, there arose in Italy a monstrous disease, seen in no earlier centuries, etc. Tract. de morbo gallico, composed in 1505.

(E) In the year 1494, in the month of December, in which year that most serene Charles, King of the French, with a great retinue set out toward the parts of Italy to recover the kingdom of Naples, there appeared that same year a certain kind of disease, of a nature almost unknown throughout all Italy, which the different nations called by various and diverse names. In Praxis chirurgiae, printed in 1514, bk. 5, ch. 1. [p. 321] published some years earlier, in which he gives clearly to understand that even among those who had returned to Spain with the same Columbus in 1493 some came infected; but neither is this true, nor probable. It is established by the letters of Christopher Columbus himself, adduced by his learned son Don Ferdinand, that he first set foot on land on the island of Hispaniola on December 24, 1492, because a caravel of his wretched fleet was wrecked on a shoal for him: that all those days that he stayed there, from December 24 until January 4, were employed by his few people in extracting from the shoal the timber of the caravel in order to make of it a small fortress, in which, having left forty men, he embarked that same day with the rest of his people to return to Spain to carry the news of the discovery of that New World. All the circumstances of their arrival on that island do not permit us to suspect that the Spaniards had leisure to advance to such familiarity with the American women as was required to remain infected by the French disease. The reciprocal wonder caused in both parties by the sight of so many new objects, and the very brief stay of only eleven days, occupied in the great labor of extracting the timbers of the caravel and of building that fortress in such haste, after the hardships of a voyage the longest and most perilous that had been made until then, render this conjecture wholly improbable. No less improbable is it rendered by the silence of Columbus himself, of his son Don Ferdinand, and of Peter Martyr d’Anghiera, who, describing the great hardships of that navigation, say nothing of such a disease.

But even if we were to grant that those Spaniards who returned from the first voyage came infected with the French disease, we should nevertheless say that the contagion of Europe did not come from them, in view of the testimonies of some authoritative writers then living. Gaspar Torrella, the learned physician cited by us above, says in his work entitled Aphrodysiacum (F) that the French disease

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. IV.                    Sf                        began

(F) This malignant sickness began in Auvergne in the year 1493, and so came by contagion, etc. [p. 322] began in Auvergne, a province of France very far from Spain, in 1493. Battista Fulgosio, or rather Fregoso, Doge of Genoa in 1478, in his curious work entitled Dicta factaque memorabilia, printed in 1509, affirms (G) that the French disease began to be known two years before King Charles VIII came into Italy. Now he came into Italy in September 1494: therefore that evil was known as early as 1492, or at the latest at the beginning of 1493, that is, some months before Columbus returned from his first voyage. John Leo, formerly a Mohammedan, a native of Granada in Spain, commonly called Leo Africanus, in his Description of Africa, composed in Rome under the pontificate of Leo X, after he had been converted, says that the Jews, driven out of Spain in the times of Ferdinand the Catholic, carried the French disease into Barbary and infected the Africans: for which reason it was there called Spanish Evil. (H) Now the edict of the Catholic Kings concerning the expulsion of the Jews was published in March 1492, as Mariana says (I), granting them no more than four months in which to sell their goods, if they did not wish to carry them with them: and in the following month another edict was published by Brother Tomás de Torquemada, Inquisitor General; in which Christians were forbidden, under very heavy penalties, to deal with the Jews and to furnish them with provisions after that term prescribed by the king: so that all, except those who became, or pretended to become, Christians, were

## forced

(G) Two years before Charles (VIII) came into Italy, a new sickness was discovered among mortals, for which the physicians could find neither name nor remedies from the teaching of the ancients, and which was called variously, according to the regions. In Gaul they called it the Neapolitan disease, but in Italy they called it the Gallic. Bk. 1, ch. 4, last §.

(H) Not even the name of this evil was known to the Africans themselves before Ferdinand, King of the Spains, drove all the Jews out of Spain; and when these had by then returned to their own country, certain wretched and most wicked Ethiopians began to have commerce with their women, and so at length, as if from hand to hand, this plague spread over the whole region, so that there is hardly a family that has remained free from this evil. And they have persuaded themselves most firmly and indubitably that it migrated to them out of Spain. Wherefore they also gave that disease the name Spanish Evil (lest it should lack a name). Bk. 1.

(I) General History of Spain, bk. 26, ch. 1. [p. 323] forced to leave it, before Columbus went to discover America; since he did not sail from the port before August 3 of that year: therefore the French disease began in Europe before America was discovered. Moreover, we find among the poems of Pacifico Massimi, poet of Ascoli, published at Florence in 1479, some verses in which he describes the virulent gonorrhea and the venereal ulcers that he suffered, caused by his excesses. (K)

Not content with affirming that the French disease came from Hispaniola, Oviedo offers also to prove it. Here are his proofs: 1. With guaiacum (a wood abundant in Hispaniola) that dreadful infirmity of the pustules is cured better than with any other medicine: and the Divine Clemency, where it permits the evil for our sins, there in its mercy provides remedies. If this argument were good, it should be concluded that Europe, rather than the island of Hispaniola, is the native country of the French disease; for all know that the most efficacious remedy against such an evil is mercury, which, being common in Europe, was not found in Hispaniola, and was not even known to the Indians: it is certain that as soon as the Gallic disease appeared in Europe, they began to employ mercury there, and that it was used by Giovanni Berengario da Carpi, Gaspar Torrella, Giovanni de Vigo, Wendelin Hock, and other famous physicians of that time, although, discredited afterward by the indiscretion of some empirics, it fell for some time into disuse. Guaiacum did not begin to be employed until 1517, that is, twenty-five years after the discovery of the French disease. Sarsaparilla began to be used in 1535, china root about the same time, and sassafras a little after.

Oviedo’s other proof (for he alleges no more than two) is that among those Spaniards who returned with Columbus from his second voyage in 1496 there was Don Pedro Margarit, a Catalan knight, “who,” he says, “went about so ill,

Sf 2                      and

(K) Hecatelegium, bk. 3, To Priapus, and bk. 8, To Mentula. We do not copy the aforesaid verses here, because they are too indecent. [p. 324] and complained so much that I well believe he felt those pains which those are wont to feel who are touched by this passion, although I never saw any pustule on his face. Then, a few months later, in the same year ’96, this infirmity began to be felt among some courtiers; for in those beginnings this evil went among lowly persons… Now it happened afterward that the Great Captain was sent to Italy with a large and handsome fleet… and among those Spaniards who went in this fleet there were some infected with this infirmity: whence, by means of the women, etc.” (L) Such are the proofs of Oviedo, which hardly deserved that mention be made of them.

M. de Pauw thinks he has won the case, (M) and has demonstrated the truth of the common opinion by the testimony of Rodrigo Díaz de Isla, physician of Seville, who is called by him a contemporary author, as his testimony is held to be decisive; but Díaz was not a contemporary author, for he did not write until sixty years after the discovery of the French disease, and his account merits no credence. He says that the first Spaniards, returned from Hispaniola with Columbus in 1493, brought the contagion to Barcelona, where the court then was: that this city was the first to be infected: that the evil wrought such havoc there that recourse was had to public prayers, to fasts, and to alms to placate the wrath of God: that, King Charles of France having passed into Italy the following year, certain Spaniards who were infected there, or many regiments, according to M. de P., sent from Spain to oppose the invasion of King Charles, infected the French. But from history we know that no regiment, whether infected or healthy, nor any other Spaniards were sent into Italy before King Charles left Naples with his army already infected to return to France. As for the contagion of Barcelona, we know that at the arrival there of Columbus,

Oviedo

(L) General History of the Indies, bk. 2, ch. 14.

(M) Rech. philosoph., pt. 2, sect. 3.

[p. 325]

Oviedo was there. Now if what the Sevillian physician relates were true, Oviedo, who was seeking proofs to confirm his extravagant opinion, would without doubt have alleged that havoc seen by him, those prayers, those fasts, and those alms, and would not have availed himself of the wretched proofs of guaiacum and of the lamentations of Margarit. Besides, the French disease is still more ancient in Europe than that epoch, as we have already said.

It seems that the Sevillian physicians were at those times the worst informed about the origin of the French disease; for Nicolás Monardes, a physician too of that city, and a contemporary of Díaz, gives an account of it so full of fables that it cannot be read without indignation. He says, then, (N) that “in the year 1493, in the war that the Catholic King had at Naples with King Charles of France, there came Don Christopher Columbus from the first discovery that he made of the island of Santo Domingo, etc., and brought with him from that island a great multitude of Indian men and women, whom he took to Naples, where the Catholic King then was, the war being ended. And because there was peace between the two kings, and the armies mingled together, Columbus having arrived there with his Indian men and women, the Spaniards began to consort with the Indian women, and the Indians with the Spanish women, and in this way the Indian men and women infected the army of the Spaniards, Italians, and Germans,” etc. Who would be persuaded that a Spanish man of letters would disfigure to such a degree the public facts of his nation, which occurred not more than eighty years before, that there would not be in his account a single proposition that was not a gross error? But where it is a matter of discrediting America, no regard is had for truth. It is certain and notorious that there was no war between Spain and France in 1493: that the Catholic King was not then at Naples, but at Barcelona, not yet healed of the wounds that he received from a

madman

(N) On the Things That Are Brought from the West Indies Pertaining to the Use of Medicine. Pt. 1, ch. 9. [p. 326] madman: that Columbus did not bring with him a great multitude of Indian men and women, but only ten Indians: that Columbus never came to Italy after his glorious expedition: that the Indians led by him never saw Italy, etc.

I, on the contrary, after having made the most diligent researches, am so far from believing that the French disease came to Europe from America that, rather, I am persuaded that it, no less than smallpox, was carried to America by Europeans. 1. For neither Don Christopher Columbus in his Diary, nor Don Ferdinand Columbus in the Life of his famous father, who saw those countries recently discovered and noted their particularities, make any mention of the French disease, although they relate minutely the hardships and sufferings of those first voyages. Nor did Peter Martyr d’Anghiera make mention of it in his history of those same countries, a contemporary author of Columbus and well informed, as one who was Protonotary of the Council of the Indies and Abbot of Jamaica. Oviedo, the first who attributed that evil to America, did not go there until twenty years after the island of Haiti was inhabited by the Spaniards. What we say of the silence of those authors concerning the Antilles we can say also of that of the first historians of other countries of America. 2. If America had been the true native country of the French disease, and if the Americans had been the first to suffer from it, it would be suffered more in America than elsewhere, and the Americans would be subject to such an evil more than any other nation; but it is not so. Of the Indians of the Antilles we cannot now speak, because it is now two centuries since they perished entirely: but among the present inhabitants of those islands the aforesaid contagion is far rarer than in Europe, and is not felt except in those places where there is a great frequency of European soldiers and sailors. In the capital of Mexico there are some whites and Indians infected with the venereal disease, but very few in proportion to the great number of the inhabitants. In other great cities of that vast kingdom the infected are very rare, and in others there is none at all; but in those places of Americans in which there is no great concourse of whites, such a disease has not

been [p. 327] been seen or heard of. Concerning South America I have been well informed by exact persons, truthful and very conversant with those countries, and have learned that in the provinces of Chile as well as in those of Paraguay that evil is very rare among the whites and never seen among the Americans. Some missionaries who have dwelt twenty or thirty years among different nations of Americans agree in affirming that they never saw anyone infected with that evil, nor even knew that it existed. As to the provinces of Peru and Quito, Señor Ulloa says, (O) that although in those countries the venereal disease is so common among the whites and among the other races of men, it is nevertheless a very rare thing to see an infected Indian there. America, therefore, is not the native country of that evil, as has commonly been believed, nor is that evil to be considered, as M. de P. would have it, (P) as an affection of the spoiled blood and of the bad temperament of the Americans.

What, then, is the true native country of the French disease, since it had its origin neither in Europe nor in America? I do not know; but if in the midst of uncertainty I am permitted to make use of conjectures, I suspect that that contagion came from Guinea, or from some other equatorial country of Africa. Of this same opinion was the most learned English physician Thomas Sydenham (Q), and it is confirmed by what Battista Fregoso affirms, an eyewitness of the beginning of the French disease

in

(O) Voyage to South America, pt. 1, bk. 6, ch. 6. It seems that this writer has confused the French disease with scurvy, adopting the opinion of the common people; for I know that Dr. Giulio Rondoli of Pesaro, a famous physician of Lima, affirmed to a person of authority that among many sick persons believed to be infected with the French disease, and treated by him, he had found almost none who was afflicted by that evil; but almost all were scorbutic, and he had succeeded in their cure by using the remedies for scurvy.

(P) Recherch. philosoph., pt. 1.

(Q) Sydenham affirms in one of his letters (Epist. 2, resp.) that the French disease is as foreign in America as in Europe, and that it was brought there by Blacks led as slaves from Guinea; but it is not true that these same Blacks carried it to America; for before they were brought to Hispaniola that island had already begun to be infected with it. [p. 328] in Europe. This man says in the work already cited (R) that the French disease was carried from Spain into Italy, and from Ethiopia into Spain. M. Astruc claims that Fregoso meant to signify America by the name of Ethiopia. Here is a curious way of getting out of the difficulty. But who ever called America Ethiopia? We know, on the contrary, that it was common among the authors of that century to give the name of Ethiopia to any country inhabited by black men, and to call such men Ethiopians: so that the natural sense of Fregoso’s words is that the French disease was carried from the equatorial countries of Africa into Lusitanian Spain, that is, Portugal. I therefore suspect that the first country of Europe to be infected was Portugal; but I should not dare to affirm it without making new researches and procuring better documents.

T H E E N D.

(R) This plague (for so it appeared) was first brought from Spain into Italy, to the Spaniards from Ethiopia, and in a short time embraced the whole world. Fregoso, Dict. Fact. que memorab., bk. 1, ch. 4.

[p. 329]
